  14. Summary

Interrupt and exception handling is where the Linux kernel stops being an abstract resource manager and reveals itself as a real-time reaction engine. Every timer tick, every arriving network packet, every page fault, every breakpoint, every inter-processor reschedule request, and every machine-check report reaches the kernel through an entry path that must be correct even when the rest of the system is stressed, preempted, nested, or half asleep. Linux 7.0.12 inherits the classical Unix model of Linux 2.6, but the implementation now reflects SMP scaling, x86_64 and ARM64 hardware differences, generic IRQ domains, per-CPU entry areas, KPTI hardening, threaded interrupts, workqueue-based bottom halves, and a much stricter separation between low-level entry code and ordinary C code.

An interrupt is usually defined as an event that alters the sequence of instructions executed by a processor. Such events correspond to electrical signals generated by hardware circuits both inside and outside the CPU chip. Interrupts are divided into two fundamental classes: synchronous and asynchronous interrupts:

  • Synchronous interrupts (exceptions) are produced by the CPU control unit itself while executing instructions. They are called synchronous because the control unit issues them only after terminating the execution of an instruction — the event is a direct consequence of the code the processor just ran. Examples include division by zero, invalid opcodes, page faults, breakpoints, and explicit system-call instructions. On x86_64, Intel manuals further subdivide these into faults (restartable, saved RIP points to the faulting instruction), traps (saved RIP points to the instruction after the trapping one), and aborts (unrecoverable, such as machine-check exceptions). On ARM64, the architecture groups these under the Synchronous exception type, dispatched through the sync vector entry. The cause is identified by the Exception Syndrome Register (ESR_EL1), whose Exception Class field (EC) encodes over 40 distinct causes — including SVC (system call), data aborts, instruction aborts, FP/SIMD traps, breakpoints, watchpoints, BTI violations, and PAC failures.
  • Asynchronous interrupts (hardware interrupts) are generated by other hardware devices at arbitrary times with respect to the CPU clock signals. They bear no causal relationship to the instruction the processor happens to be executing when the signal arrives. Examples include timer ticks, keyboard presses, network packet arrivals, disk completion events, and inter-processor interrupts (IPIs). On x86_64, these arrive via the Local APIC as external interrupt vectors and are dispatched through interrupt gates in the IDT. On ARM64, the architecture defines two asynchronous interrupt types — IRQ and FIQ — each with its own vector entry, plus a third asynchronous category, SError (System Error), used for asynchronous aborts such as uncorrectable ECC errors or external data aborts.

Intel microprocessor manuals designate synchronous and asynchronous interrupts as exceptions and interrupts, respectively. We adopt this classification throughout this book, although we occasionally use the term “interrupt signal” to designate both types together (synchronous as well as asynchronous).

Table 4-1. Synchronous vs. asynchronous interrupts on x86_64 and ARM64

PropertySynchronous (Exceptions)Asynchronous (Interrupts)
CauseCPU execution of an instructionExternal hardware signal
TimingDeterministic — occurs at a specific instruction boundaryArbitrary — unrelated to current instruction
x86_64 dispatchIDT gates (vectors 0–31 reserved by architecture)IDT gates (vectors 32–255, via Local APIC)
ARM64 dispatchsync vector entry; cause in ESR_EL1irq / fiq / error vector entries
x86_64 subtypesFaults, traps, abortsMaskable (INTR, controlled by IF flag), non-maskable (NMI)
ARM64 subtypesData abort, instruction abort, SVC, BRK, FP trap, BTI, PAC, etc. (EC field)IRQ, FIQ, SError
Reproducible?Yes — re-executing the same instruction in the same state triggers it againNo — depends on external device timing
Masked by CLI/PSTATE?No — exceptions cannot be masked (except Debug on ARM64 via DAIF.D)Yes — IF flag on x86_64, DAIF.I / DAIF.F on ARM64; SError is masked by DAIF.A, while NMI cannot be masked at all

Interrupts issued by I/O devices are the classic example of asynchronous events: the arrival of a keystroke, the completion of a DMA transfer, or the expiration of a hardware timer each generates an electrical signal that reaches the CPU independently of whatever instruction is running. Exceptions, on the other hand, are caused either by programming errors or by anomalous conditions that must be handled by the kernel. In the first case (e.g., division by zero, illegal opcode), the kernel handles the exception by delivering to the current process one of the Unix signals familiar to every programmer (SIGFPE, SIGILL, SIGSEGV, etc.). In the second case (e.g., a page fault for a valid mapping, or an SVC/syscall instruction requesting a kernel service), the kernel performs all the steps needed to recover from the condition or provide the requested service, then returns to the interrupted code.

This distinction — synchronous exceptions generated by the CPU itself, and asynchronous interrupts generated by external hardware — is as fundamental in Linux 7.0.12 as it was in Linux 2.6. The ARM64 vector table at arch/arm64/kernel/entry.S makes it explicit: each exception level × source combination has four vector slots labeled sync, irq, fiq, and error:

/* arch/arm64/kernel/entry.S:519 */
SYM_CODE_START(vectors)
        kernel_ventry  1, t, 64, sync       // Synchronous EL1t
        kernel_ventry  1, t, 64, irq        // IRQ EL1t
        kernel_ventry  1, t, 64, fiq        // FIQ EL1t
        kernel_ventry  1, t, 64, error      // Error (SError) EL1t

        kernel_ventry  1, h, 64, sync       // Synchronous EL1h
        kernel_ventry  1, h, 64, irq        // IRQ EL1h
        kernel_ventry  1, h, 64, fiq        // FIQ EL1h
        kernel_ventry  1, h, 64, error      // Error (SError) EL1h

        kernel_ventry  0, t, 64, sync       // Synchronous 64-bit EL0
        kernel_ventry  0, t, 64, irq        // IRQ 64-bit EL0
        kernel_ventry  0, t, 64, fiq        // FIQ 64-bit EL0
        kernel_ventry  0, t, 64, error      // Error (SError) 64-bit EL0

        kernel_ventry  0, t, 32, sync       // Synchronous 32-bit EL0
        kernel_ventry  0, t, 32, irq        // IRQ 32-bit EL0
        kernel_ventry  0, t, 32, fiq        // FIQ 32-bit EL0
        kernel_ventry  0, t, 32, error      // Error (SError) 32-bit EL0
SYM_CODE_END(vectors)

The four groups correspond to the four ways an exception can reach EL1: from EL1 while the kernel was using SP_EL0 (EL1t), from EL1 while using SP_EL1 (EL1h, the normal kernel case), from 64-bit EL0, and from 32-bit EL0. The last group is present even on kernels built without CONFIG_COMPAT, because the table's geometry is fixed by the architecture — the slots simply route to a handler that reports an invalid entry.

On x86_64, the IDT encodes the same distinction through vector numbering: vectors 0–31 are reserved by the architecture for CPU exceptions, while vectors 32–255 (FIRST_EXTERNAL_VECTOR onward) carry device interrupts and IPIs. The correspondence is not perfect, and the exception proves instructive. Vector 2 is the non-maskable interrupt, which is asynchronous — it originates outside the CPU — yet it lives inside the architecture-reserved range rather than above 32. It is placed there because the NMI must remain deliverable when no interrupt controller is programmed and when maskable delivery is disabled, so it cannot depend on the APIC vector space at all. Vector numbering therefore reflects who owns the vector, not purely whether the event is synchronous.

The IDTENTRY and DEFINE_IDTENTRY_* macros in arch/x86/include/asm/idtentry.h generate separate entry stubs for each exception and interrupt type, ensuring that the entry path matches the nature of the event. The NMI gets a dedicated declaration, DECLARE_IDTENTRY_NMI, precisely because it can arrive while the kernel is midway through another entry sequence; the name is an alias for DECLARE_IDTENTRY_RAW, which is the point — NMI must bypass the generic entry bookkeeping and do it by hand.

Interrupt handling in Linux 2.6.11 was built around the i386 platform. That model is still worth remembering. The basic distinction between synchronous exceptions and asynchronous device interrupts is unchanged. The need to save processor state, dispatch through a hardware table, and restore the interrupted context is unchanged. The need to keep the immediate handler short and defer less urgent work is unchanged. What has changed is almost everything around those core ideas: the hardware controllers, the entry stacks, the generic IRQ layer, the affinity model, the interaction with RCU and preemption, and the mechanisms used for deferred work.

This chapter follows the life of an interrupt signal from several angles. We begin with the conceptual role of interrupts as nested kernel control paths, then classify interrupts and exceptions, with special attention to x86 vectors and ARM64 exception levels. From there we move downward into the controllers — the 8259A PIC, the xAPIC/x2APIC family, MSI/MSI-X, and ARM GICv3 — and into the tables through which the hardware finds a handler: the IDT on x86_64 and the vector table on ARM64. With the hardware understood, we walk the entry sequence itself, separating what the processor does automatically from what the entry stub must complete before a valid pt_regs frame exists.

The second half of the chapter is about software structure. We look first at exception handling, where the kernel must decide whether a fault is a program error, a recoverable condition, or a kernel bug. We then turn to interrupt handling proper: the generic IRQ descriptor layer, irq domains, flow handlers, and the dispatch path taken by an I/O interrupt. Two specialized cases follow, the timer interrupt and interprocessor interrupts, each with its own delivery mechanism and its own constraints. Deferred work — softirqs, tasklets, threaded IRQs, and workqueues — comes next, followed by the return path, which turns out to carry more logic than the entry path. The chapter closes by comparing Linux 7.0.12 to Linux 2.6.

Several neighbouring topics are deliberately left to their own chapters. The locking primitives that interrupt handlers rely on — and the rules for using them in atomic context — belong to Chapter 5, Kernel Synchronization. The clock sources, clock events, and timekeeping machinery that the timer interrupt drives are the subject of Chapter 6, Timing Measurements; this chapter covers only how the tick arrives and what it triggers. What the scheduler does once TIF_NEED_RESCHED has been set is deferred to Chapter 7, Process Scheduling. Page faults appear here as an exception type, but demand paging, copy-on-write, and VMA lookup are treated in Chapter 9, Process Address Space. System-call entry shares much of the mechanism described here, yet differs enough in its return path to warrant separate treatment in Chapter 10, System Calls.

The Role of Interrupt Signals

Interrupts are the mechanism by which the processor abandons its current sequential instruction stream and transfers control to code chosen by hardware-defined rules. When an interrupt signal arrives, the CPU must stop what it is currently doing and switch to a new activity. On x86_64, it does this by saving the current values of the program counter (RIP) and code-segment selector (CS) — along with the stack pointer (RSP), stack segment (SS), and processor flags (RFLAGS) — onto the kernel-mode stack or an interrupt stack, and then loading an address related to the interrupt type from the Interrupt Descriptor Table (IDT) into the program counter. On ARM64, the processor saves the exception return address into ELR_EL1, the processor state into SPSR_EL1, and branches to one of the sixteen vector-table slots — selected by the exception type (synchronous, IRQ, FIQ, or SError) together with the exception level and stack pointer of the interrupted context.

That description sounds simple. In practice, interrupt handling is one of the most sensitive and difficult tasks performed by the kernel. It must satisfy a demanding set of constraints that shape the entire design of the interrupt subsystem:

  • Minimal latency in the fast path. Interrupts can arrive at any time, even when the kernel is in the middle of something important. The kernel's goal is therefore to get the interrupt out of the way as soon as possible and defer as much processing as it can. For instance, suppose a block of data has arrived on a network interface. When the hardware interrupts the kernel, the handler could simply note the presence of new data, acknowledge the interrupt controller, give the processor back to whatever was running before, and schedule the rest of the processing — moving data into socket buffers, waking the recipient process, running the protocol stack — for later. The activities that the kernel needs to perform in response to an interrupt are thus divided into a critical urgent part that the kernel executes right away and a deferrable part that is left for later. This split is what Linux calls the “top half” and “bottom half” model.
  • Nested execution must be supported. Because interrupts can arrive at any time, the kernel might be handling one of them while another one (of a different type) occurs. This should be allowed as much as possible, because it keeps I/O devices busy and reduces the chance of lost events. As a result, the interrupt handlers must be coded so that the corresponding kernel control paths can be executed in a nested manner. When the last kernel control path terminates, the kernel must be able to resume execution of the interrupted process or switch to another process if the interrupt signal has caused a rescheduling activity.
  • Critical regions must be short. Although the kernel may accept a new interrupt signal while handling a previous one, some critical regions exist inside the kernel code where interrupts must be disabled. Such critical regions must be limited as much as possible, because the kernel and particularly the interrupt handlers should run most of the time with interrupts enabled. The macros local_irq_disable() and local_irq_enable() (defined in include/linux/irqflags.h) control the processor’s interrupt flag, while local_irq_save(flags) and local_irq_restore(flags) save and restore the flag state around critical sections.

Interrupts vs. Process Switching

There are things in this chapter that will remind you of the context switch described in the previous chapter, carried out when the kernel substitutes one process for another. But there is a key difference between interrupt handling and process switching: the code executed by an interrupt or by an exception handler is not a process. Rather, it is a kernel control path that runs at the expense of the same process that was running when the interrupt occurred. As a kernel control path, the interrupt handler is lighter than a process — it has less context and requires less time to set up or tear down. It does not have its own task_struct, does not have a user-mode address space, and is not independently scheduled.

In Linux 7.0.12, the kernel tracks whether it is in interrupt context by examining a per-CPU or per-thread counter called preempt_count. Where that counter physically lives is an architecture decision. ARM64 keeps it in the current thread’s thread_info, which since the change described in Chapter 3 is embedded directly inside task_struct (arch/arm64/include/asm/thread_info.h:30). x86_64 instead keeps it in a cache-hot per-CPU variable, DECLARE_PER_CPU_CACHE_HOT(int, __preempt_count) (arch/x86/include/asm/preempt.h:10), so that a preemption check costs a single %gs-relative load rather than a dereference through current. x86_64 also packs an extra flag into the same word: bit 31 holds PREEMPT_NEED_RESCHED in inverted form, which lets the kernel test “preemptible and rescheduling needed” with one comparison against zero. The preempt_count() accessor masks that bit off, so generic code never sees it.

Whichever storage an architecture chooses, the layout of the counted fields is generic. The word is divided into bit fields that separately track preemption depth, softirq state, hardirq nesting level, and NMI depth:

Table 4-2. Layout of the preempt_count field (include/linux/preempt.h:33-53)

Bit fieldWidthShiftMaskMeaning
Preemption count8 bits0PREEMPT_MASKNested preempt_disable() calls
Softirq count8 bits8SOFTIRQ_MASKBH disable depth; bit 8 set when serving softirqs
Hardirq count4 bits16HARDIRQ_MASKNested hardirq handler depth
NMI count4 bits20NMI_MASKNMI nesting depth (normally 0 or 1)

The kernel provides a family of macros to query the current execution context:

/* include/linux/preempt.h:126 */
#define in_nmi()            (nmi_count())
#define in_hardirq()        (hardirq_count())
#define in_serving_softirq() (softirq_count() & SOFTIRQ_OFFSET)
#define in_task()           (!(preempt_count() & \
                              (NMI_MASK | HARDIRQ_MASK | SOFTIRQ_OFFSET)))

/* deprecated, include/linux/preempt.h:140 */
#define in_softirq()        (softirq_count())
#define in_interrupt()      (irq_count())   /* NMI + hardirq + softirq */

These macros are used throughout the kernel to make decisions about what operations are safe in the current context. in_task() returns true only when the CPU is executing normal process context — not in any form of interrupt or softirq handling — which is the only context where sleeping, page faults, and full scheduling are legal. That is why callers in interrupt context must pass GFP_ATOMIC rather than GFP_KERNEL; the allocator marks the sleeping possibility with might_alloc(), which expands to might_sleep_if(gfpflags_allow_blocking(gfp_mask)) (include/linux/sched/mm.h:315) and so trips a debug splat if a blocking allocation is attempted where sleeping is illegal. A subtler use appears in set_active_memcg() (include/linux/sched/mm.h:496), which stores the active memory cgroup in current->active_memcg when in_task() is true but in a per-CPU variable otherwise — precisely because, as the rest of this section explains, interrupt context only borrows current and must not scribble on it.

Note the split in the listing above. Linux 7.0.12 explicitly marks in_interrupt() and in_softirq() as deprecated and directs new code to the four precise queries instead. The reason is that both deprecated macros conflate distinct situations. in_softirq() is true both when a softirq is actually running and when a task has merely called local_bh_disable(), and in_interrupt() folds NMI, hardirq, and softirq context into a single answer. Code that needs to know which of those it is in — and most code that disables preemption or picks a GFP flag does — gets the wrong answer often enough that the kernel now prefers the explicit form.

One caveat applies to Table 4-2 on PREEMPT_RT kernels. There, softirqs are served in task context and the softirq field is no longer read from preempt_count at all; softirq_count() is redefined to read current->softirq_disable_cnt instead (include/linux/preempt.h:111). The bit positions in the table are unchanged, but on PREEMPT_RT bits 8–15 of preempt_count itself are unused.

Interrupts as Nested Kernel Control Paths

An interrupt handler is not a process. It has no independent task_struct, no separate user address space, and no standalone scheduling identity. Instead it borrows the context of whatever execution was interrupted. If the CPU was running a user process, the handler runs on behalf of that process. If the CPU was already in the kernel, the handler nests inside that kernel control path. If the CPU was idle, the handler runs while the idle task is current. In all three cases the interrupted entity pays the accounting cost for the work until Linux has a chance to hand some of it to a deferred mechanism.

This is why interrupt handling is said to run "at the expense of" the interrupted process. The phrase is still accurate in Linux 7.0.12. CPU time spent in the hard interrupt top half is charged to the current CPU context, not to an invented temporary process. The current task's kernel stack is the first landing zone for the saved register frame. The current task's preemption and RCU state help determine what entry bookkeeping must be performed. Only later, if the kernel wakes an IRQ thread, a ksoftirqd thread, or a workqueue worker, does the remaining work become attached to a schedulable kernel task.

Figure 4-1 makes this concrete. Read it left to right: the left column shows three events arriving on one CPU in quick succession, the centre column shows the register frames they stack up on the single kernel stack, and the right column shows those frames being unwound in the reverse order. Nothing here is scheduled — each frame simply returns into the one beneath it.

Nested execution is not an odd corner case. It is fundamental to the design. On x86_64, Linux relies on interrupt gates, interrupt stacks, and the generic irqentry_enter() / irqentry_exit() helpers to make nested entry safe. On ARM64, Linux relies on exception level state, vector-specific prologues, and explicit calls to irq_enter_rcu() and irq_exit_rcu(). Either way, the key principle is the same: entry code must establish enough invariants that a second interrupt can arrive before the first has fully completed.

The nesting rules are architecture dependent. A maskable x86 interrupt gate clears IF on entry, so the earliest instructions run with further maskable interrupts disabled. Linux later re-enables interrupts when the path is ready, often only after a complete pt_regs frame exists and the RCU state has been updated. An NMI ignores IF entirely and therefore needs a stronger isolation strategy, which is why x86 assigns a dedicated IST stack to NMI and another to debug and machine check. ARM64 instead saves PSTATE into SPSR_EL1, masks selected exception classes through DAIF bits, and chooses a vector slot based on whether the exception came from the current EL or a lower one.

Three Classes of Interrupt Work

The classical Unix literature distinguished between work that must happen immediately and work that can wait. Linux 7.0.12 refines that into a practical three-class model. Some work is so urgent that the hard interrupt handler must do it before acknowledging the interrupt source or before any other CPU state can safely change. Some work is not urgent, but still belongs in interrupt context because it should run very soon and does not need to sleep. The rest is deferrable and can be migrated to a schedulable context such as an IRQ thread or workqueue worker.

Table 4-3. Three classes of interrupt-related work

Class Typical context Can sleep? Examples Why it exists
Critical hardirq / entry path No acknowledge controller, snapshot status register, mask source prevents interrupt loss, storming, or controller confusion
Noncritical hardirq or softirq No consume a few descriptors, raise NET_RX softirq, account statistics keeps latency low without delaying urgent hardware service
Noncritical deferrable IRQ thread / workqueue / ksoftirqd Usually yes copy data to user-visible buffers, retry I/O, wake waiters, heavy protocol work improves fairness, allows blocking, and shortens hardirq residency

The distinction is not aesthetic. It is what lets Linux remain responsive under load. A network card can generate tens or hundreds of thousands of interrupts per second. If each hardirq path tried to run a full protocol stack, wake multiple tasks, allocate memory under reclaim pressure, and perform lengthy bookkeeping, overall system latency would explode. By limiting the immediate top half to the minimal hardware-facing actions, Linux keeps the CPU available for both other interrupts and ordinary process execution.

This separation also explains why so much of the generic IRQ layer is built around handoff points. handle_irq_event() iterates the registered primary handlers. A primary handler may return IRQ_HANDLED and finish the matter. It may return IRQ_WAKE_THREAD to request a dedicated threaded follow-up. Or it may raise a softirq to schedule later processing. The hardirq top half is therefore not the whole interrupt story. It is the first, most time-constrained slice of it.

Why Interrupt Handlers Must Be Short

Every microsecond spent in hardirq context delays something else. If the interrupted task was in user mode, its forward progress stops. If the interrupted task held a spinlock that another CPU wants, lock hold time increases. If the interrupted code was itself in the kernel on a latency-sensitive path, that path is now longer. And because hardirq context cannot sleep, it is limited to atomic operations, per-CPU data, and carefully bounded critical sections.

Linux therefore adopts a consistent policy. Entry code makes the interrupted state safe. The flow handler masks or acknowledges the source if needed. The driver's primary handler does only the device-specific work that cannot be postponed. Everything else is deferred. That is the conceptual bridge from the classic top-half / bottom-half terminology to the modern menu of softirqs, tasklets, threaded IRQs, and workqueues.

The Interrupted Process and CPU Accounting

Because interrupts are not processes, they do not change current. A hard interrupt that arrives while process P is running still sees current == P. That is useful. It means entry code can consult the interrupted task's thread flags, stack, RCU state, memory context, and tracing state without inventing an intermediate software abstraction. It is also dangerous. It means interrupt code must never assume that current chose to run the handler or that the interrupted task's locks and invariants are in a calm state.

The modern kernel compensates with explicit context tracking. irqentry_enter() distinguishes user-mode entry from kernel-mode entry. irq_enter_rcu() and irq_exit_rcu() update the hardirq nesting count and decide whether softirqs may run on exit. If a handler wakes an IRQ thread, the expensive continuation is moved into a schedulable kernel task where sleeping and rescheduling are legal. The result is a layered execution model: borrowed context first, owned context later.

Interrupts and Exceptions

The word interrupt is often used loosely for every asynchronous or exceptional control transfer. Architecturally, however, Linux must respect the distinctions made by the CPU. On x86, exceptions arise synchronously from instruction execution, while interrupts proper arrive asynchronously from external agents such as devices or other CPUs. On ARM64, the architecture speaks in terms of exceptions at different exception levels, but the same practical split remains: some entries are caused by the current instruction stream, others by external interrupt lines or system events.

Table 4-4. Classes of x86 events entering the kernel

Class Timing Saved instruction pointer Typical source Linux consequence
Fault before instruction retires faulting instruction page fault, invalid opcode handler may repair state and retry
Trap after instruction retires next instruction breakpoint, overflow handler reports event without rerunning instruction
Abort architecturally severe undefined or unreliable machine check, some double faults kernel treats as fatal or nearly fatal
Programmed exception caused intentionally by software usually next instruction int3, int n, syscall-like trap used for debugging, emulation, or ABI entry
External interrupt asynchronous current point in interrupted stream device IRQ, timer, IPI dispatch through IRQ subsystem

IRQs and Interrupts

Each hardware device controller capable of issuing interrupt requests usually has one or more output lines designated as Interrupt ReQuest (IRQ) lines. All existing IRQ lines are connected to the input pins of a hardware circuit called the Programmable Interrupt Controller (PIC), which performs the following actions:

  1. Monitors the IRQ lines, checking for raised signals. If two or more IRQ lines are raised simultaneously, selects the one having the lower pin number (higher priority in the default scheme).
  2. If a raised signal occurs on an IRQ line:
    • Converts the raised signal into a corresponding vector number.
    • Stores the vector in an Interrupt Controller I/O port, allowing the CPU to read it via the data bus.
    • Sends a raised signal to the processor’s INTR pin — that is, issues an interrupt.
    • Waits until the CPU acknowledges the interrupt signal by writing into one of the PIC’s I/O ports; when this occurs, clears the INTR line.
  3. Goes back to step 1.

The IRQ lines are sequentially numbered starting from 0; therefore, the first IRQ line is usually denoted as IRQ0. Intel’s architectural convention placed the vector for IRQn at n + 32, immediately above the 32 vectors the CPU reserves for exceptions. Linux does not follow that convention exactly. On x86_64 with APIC, the vector for a device interrupt is not a fixed offset at all: it is allocated dynamically out of the external-interrupt pool when the interrupt is requested, and programmed into an I/O APIC redirection entry or an MSI message.

Each IRQ line can be selectively disabled. Thus, the PIC can be programmed to stop issuing interrupts that refer to a given IRQ line, or to resume issuing them. Disabled interrupts are not lost; the PIC sends them to the CPU as soon as they are enabled again. This feature is used by interrupt handlers to process IRQs of the same type serially.

Selective enabling/disabling of IRQs is not the same as global masking/unmasking of maskable interrupts:

  • Selective disabling (per-IRQ masking) tells the interrupt controller to hold back one specific source. On x86_64, this is done by setting the mask bit in the I/O APIC redirection table entry or by calling disable_irq() / enable_irq() in the Linux kernel (defined in kernel/irq/manage.c). On ARM64 GICv3, it is done through the enable/disable bits in the Distributor (GICD_ISENABLERn / GICD_ICENABLERn).
  • Global masking tells the CPU itself to ignore all maskable interrupts. On x86_64, the CLI instruction clears the IF flag in RFLAGS, suppressing all maskable interrupts; STI sets it again. The kernel wraps these in local_irq_disable() / local_irq_enable(). On ARM64, the DAIF bits in PSTATE control masking: bit I masks IRQs, bit F masks FIQs, bit A masks SErrors, and bit D masks debug exceptions. The kernel uses local_daif_mask() / local_daif_restore() to manipulate them.

Table 4-5. IRQ masking levels: selective vs. global

Levelx86_64 mechanismARM64 mechanismScopeLost?
Selective (per-IRQ)I/O APIC mask bit; disable_irq()GIC distributor enable bitOne interrupt sourceNo — latched until unmasked
Global (all maskable)CLI / STI (RFLAGS.IF)DAIF.I / DAIF.F in PSTATEAll maskable on this CPUNo — pending until IF/DAIF restored
NMI (non-maskable)Cannot be masked by IF; special IST stackGICv3 pseudo-NMI via priority maskingCritical system eventsNot maskable, but x86 blocks further NMIs until IRET, so at most one can be latched

Traditional PICs are implemented by connecting “in cascade” two 8259A-style external chips. Each chip can handle up to eight different IRQ input lines. Because the INT output line of the slave PIC is connected to the IRQ2 pin of the master PIC, the number of available IRQ lines is limited to 15. Linux must remap those lines away from vectors 0 through 31, which the CPU reserves for exceptions. The remapping is easy to state imprecisely, so it is worth reading the constants directly:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/irq_vectors.h:33 */
/*
 * IDT vectors usable for external interrupt sources start at 0x20.
 * (0x80 is the syscall vector, 0x30-0x3f are for ISA)
 */
#define FIRST_EXTERNAL_VECTOR           0x20

/*
 * Vectors 0x30-0x3f are used for ISA interrupts.
 *   round up to the next 16-vector boundary
 */
#define ISA_IRQ_VECTOR(irq)             (((FIRST_EXTERNAL_VECTOR + 16) & ~15) + irq)

FIRST_EXTERNAL_VECTOR is not where IRQ0 lands. It marks the bottom of the range that may be used for external interrupts at all — the first vector past the architectural exception block. The legacy ISA lines sit one 16-vector group higher: ISA_IRQ_VECTOR() rounds 0x20 + 16 up to the next multiple of sixteen, giving 0x30. IRQ0 is therefore vector 0x30 (48), IRQ1 is 0x31, and the fifteen usable ISA lines occupy 0x30 through 0x3f. Vectors 0x20 through 0x2f are deliberately left unassigned so that the ISA block starts on a priority-class boundary: the local APIC derives an interrupt’s priority from the top four bits of its vector, so keeping a related group inside one aligned block of sixteen keeps its members at one priority level. Table 4-36 later in this chapter shows the result on a running system, where IRQ0 appears as vector 0x30 and IRQ1 as 0x31.

The Advanced Programmable Interrupt Controller (APIC)

The previous description refers to PICs designed for uniprocessor systems. If the system includes a single CPU, the output line of the master PIC can be connected in a straightforward way to the INTR pin of the CPU. However, if the system includes two or more CPUs, this approach is no longer valid, and a more sophisticated interrupt controller is needed.

Being able to deliver interrupts to each CPU in the system is crucial for fully exploiting the parallelism of the SMP architecture. For that reason, Intel introduced the I/O Advanced Programmable Interrupt Controller (I/O APIC) and the Local APIC. All current x86_64 microprocessors include a local APIC integrated into the processor core itself. Each local APIC has 32-bit registers, an internal clock, a local timer device, two additional IRQ lines (LINT0 and LINT1) reserved for local APIC interrupts, and performance-monitoring and thermal interrupt inputs. All local APICs are connected to one or more external I/O APICs, giving rise to a multi-APIC system.

Figure 4-2 shows the resulting topology. Every core carries its own integrated local APIC, and one or more external I/O APICs collect device interrupts and forward them as messages to whichever local APIC the routing tables select.

The I/O APIC acts as a router with respect to the local APICs. The IRQ lines coming from external devices are connected to the I/O APIC, which translates each signal into an APIC message directed at one or more local APICs. In early Pentium III systems the APIC bus was a dedicated serial three-line bus; starting with the Pentium 4 the APIC bus was implemented via the system bus; modern x86_64 systems use MSR-based x2APIC mode, where all APIC register access is through RDMSR/WRMSR instructions rather than memory-mapped I/O, offering faster access and supporting more than 255 logical processors.

Table 4-6. Evolution of x86 interrupt controller architectures

GenerationControllerMax CPUsMax IRQ inputsBusKernel 7.0.12 status
LegacyCascaded 8259A PIC115ISA I/O portsEmulated for compatibility; CONFIG_X86_64 still builds PIC code for fallback
Pentium Pro–IIIxAPIC (memory-mapped)25524 per I/O APICDedicated APIC busSupported; still common in VMs
Pentium 4+xAPIC via system bus25524+ per I/O APICSystem busSupported
Modernx2APIC (MSR-based)2³²Unlimited (MSI)MSR + platform busDefault on modern hardware; X86_FEATURE_X2APIC

The I/O APIC consists of a set of IRQ input pins (typically 24), an Interrupt Redirection Table with one entry per pin, programmable registers, and a message unit for sending APIC messages. Unlike the 8259A, interrupt priority is not related to pin number. Each entry in the Redirection Table can be individually programmed to indicate:

  • The interrupt vector number (0–255) delivered to the target CPU
  • The delivery mode (fixed, lowest priority, SMI, NMI, INIT, ExtINT)
  • The destination mode (physical APIC ID or logical destination)
  • The trigger mode (edge-triggered or level-triggered)
  • The polarity (active high or active low)
  • A mask bit to block or allow delivery
  • The destination CPU or set of CPUs

Interrupt requests coming from external hardware devices can be distributed among the available CPUs in two ways:

  • Static distribution: The IRQ signal is delivered to the local APICs listed in the corresponding Redirection Table entry. The interrupt is delivered to one specific CPU, a subset of CPUs, or all CPUs at once (broadcast mode). This is the most common mode in Linux 7.0.12 — the kernel uses explicit affinity masks to pin each interrupt to specific CPUs.
  • Dynamic distribution: The IRQ signal is delivered to the local APIC of the processor that is executing the process with the lowest priority. Each local APIC has a programmable Task Priority Register (TPR) used to compute the priority of the currently running context. In practice, modern Linux 7.0.12 rarely uses hardware lowest-priority delivery; instead, the irqbalance daemon periodically redistributes interrupt affinities in software based on observed load, which is more predictable and controllable.

Besides distributing external interrupts, the multi-APIC system allows CPUs to generate Inter-Processor Interrupts (IPIs). When a CPU wishes to send an interrupt to another CPU, it stores the interrupt vector and the identifier of the target’s local APIC in the Interrupt Command Register (ICR) of its own local APIC. A message is then sent to the target’s local APIC, which issues a corresponding interrupt to its own CPU. IPIs are crucial for SMP operation — Linux uses them for TLB shootdowns, reschedule requests, function-call delivery, and stop/NMI signals (see “Inter-Processor Interrupts” later in this chapter).

ARM64 Interrupt Controller: GICv3/GICv4

ARM64 systems use the ARM Generic Interrupt Controller (GIC), typically GICv3 on server and mobile platforms. GICv3 solves the same problems as the APIC architecture but uses a different structure:

  • The Distributor (GICD_* registers) manages system-wide shared peripheral interrupts (SPIs) and configures priority, enable state, target CPU, and trigger mode for each interrupt. Affinity routing on GICv3 uses the 64-bit GICD_IROUTERn registers, which name a target PE by its full Aff3.Aff2.Aff1.Aff0 hierarchy or set the “any participating PE” bit; this is what gic_set_affinity() writes (drivers/irqchip/irq-gic-v3.c:1453). The older GICv2 GICD_ITARGETSR byte-per-interrupt CPU mask cannot express more than eight cores and is not used for SPI routing once affinity routing is enabled.
  • Each CPU has a Redistributor (GICR_* registers) that manages per-CPU Software Generated Interrupts (SGIs, used for IPIs) and Private Peripheral Interrupts (PPIs, such as the architected timer and PMU).
  • The CPU Interface is accessed through system registers (ICC_*_EL1) rather than memory-mapped I/O, similar in spirit to x86’s x2APIC transition. Key registers include ICC_IAR1_EL1 (acknowledge interrupt), ICC_EOIR1_EL1 (end of interrupt), and ICC_PMR_EL1 (priority mask).
  • The optional Interrupt Translation Service (ITS) translates MSI writes from PCIe devices into Locality-specific Peripheral Interrupts (LPIs), analogous to how I/O APIC redirection works for wired interrupts.

Figure 4-3 shows the equivalent ARM64 arrangement. GICv3 divides the same responsibilities across three components: a Distributor that routes wired SPIs, a per-CPU Redistributor that owns the private and software-generated interrupts belonging to one core, and a CPU interface reached through system registers rather than memory-mapped I/O.

Table 4-7. Interrupt controller comparison: x86_64 APIC vs. ARM64 GICv3

Featurex86_64 APIC/x2APICARM64 GICv3
Per-CPU unitLocal APICRedistributor + CPU Interface
System-wide routerI/O APICDistributor
Wire-based interruptsI/O APIC pins → redirection tableSPI (ID 32–1019) via Distributor
Message-based interruptsMSI/MSI-X → direct APIC writeLPI (ID 8192+) via ITS
Inter-processor interruptsICR write → target APICSGI (ID 0–15) via ICC_SGI1R_EL1
Per-CPU privateLINT0/LINT1, timer, thermal, PMUPPI (ID 16–31): timer, PMU
Priority modelTPR (rarely used by Linux)ICC_PMR_EL1 + binary point; used for pseudo-NMI
Register accessMSR (x2APIC) or MMIO (xAPIC)System registers (ICC_*_EL1)
EOI mechanismWrite to the EOI register — MMIO in xAPIC, APIC_BASE_MSR + (APIC_EOI >> 4) in x2APICWrite to ICC_EOIR1_EL1. In EOImode 1 that only drops priority, and a separate ICC_DIR_EL1 write deactivates the interrupt

The evolution from PIC to APIC on x86, and from GICv1/v2 to GICv3/v4 on ARM, follows a consistent theme: each generation moves register access closer to the CPU (from I/O ports to MMIO to MSRs/system registers), increases the number of addressable CPUs and interrupt sources, replaces wire-based signaling with message-based delivery, and pushes configuration complexity into software rather than hardware pin assignments.

Faults, Traps, and Aborts

The fault / trap / abort distinction matters because it tells the kernel what sort of recovery is possible. A fault says, in effect, "the current instruction could not complete, but if software fixes the underlying condition, the CPU may try again." A trap says, "the instruction already completed, and software is being notified afterward." An abort says, "something went wrong badly enough that precise restart information may not exist or may not be trustworthy." Linux maps its handler structure, signal generation, and fixup logic onto those semantics.

  • A page fault on a not-yet-present user page is a fault. Linux can allocate a page, update the page tables, and restart the faulting load or store.
  • A breakpoint created by int3 is a trap. The debuggee should resume at the next instruction after the one-byte breakpoint opcode.
  • A machine check is an abort. The CPU reports an error state, but ordinary instruction restart is usually not the right response.

Concrete examples help. If a process touches an anonymous page that has not been allocated yet, the x86 MMU raises #PF. Linux inspects the fault address, recognizes a valid VMA, allocates memory, installs a PTE, and returns. The faulting instruction then runs again and succeeds. By contrast, when a debugger inserts 0xcc at an address, the processor executes the breakpoint instruction, advances RIP, and then raises #BP. Linux should not back RIP up and re-execute the trap instruction unless a debugger deliberately asks for that behavior.

Programmed Exceptions

Programmed exceptions are deliberate control transfers caused by software. They look superficially like faults or traps, but their purpose is often cooperative rather than erroneous. Historically Unix used int 0x80 as the system-call gate on x86. Modern x86_64 Linux uses syscall for native 64-bit system calls, but int 0x80 still exists for 32-bit compatibility. int3 remains the standard one-byte breakpoint instruction because debuggers can patch a single byte without disturbing surrounding code alignment.

  • int n enters vector n explicitly. Linux still uses this model for some compatibility and virtualization paths.
  • int3 is a special one-byte trap instruction. Its compact encoding makes live code patching convenient for debuggers and kprobes.
  • into was the historical signed-overflow trap on x86. It is largely obsolete on x86_64, but it matters in the architectural taxonomy because it is a software-requested trap conditioned on OF.
  • bound checked an array index against memory-stored bounds and raised #BR on violation. It is also obsolete in mainstream 64-bit code, but Linux still documents the exception class because the vector remains architecturally defined.

Programmed exceptions are important because they remind us that not every exception indicates a bug. Some are debugging interfaces. Some are ABI entry points. Some are architectural compatibility leftovers. And some are now used by security and virtualization features that did not exist when Linux 2.6 was current. Vectors #VE and #CP, for example, belong to virtualization exception delivery and Control-flow Enforcement Technology rather than to the older arithmetic and segmentation world.

Table 4-8. x86_64 exception and architected event vectors used by Linux 7.0.12

Vector Name Type Primary handler Usual signal Typical cause
0 Divide Error (#DE) Fault exc_divide_error() SIGFPE integer divide overflow or divide by zero
1 Debug (#DB) Fault/Trap exc_debug() SIGTRAP single-step, hardware breakpoints, ICEBP, bus-lock detect
2 Nonmaskable Interrupt Interrupt exc_nmi() — watchdogs, perf, some platform failures
3 Breakpoint (#BP) Trap exc_int3() SIGTRAP int3 instruction
4 Overflow (#OF) Trap exc_overflow() SIGSEGV historical into
5 BOUND Range Exceeded (#BR) Fault exc_bounds() SIGSEGV historical bound instruction
6 Invalid Opcode (#UD) Fault exc_invalid_op() SIGILL undefined instruction, bad prefix combination
7 Device Not Available (#NM) Fault exc_device_not_available() — XFD-armed first use of AMX tile state; legacy CR0.TS lazy-FPU trap
8 Double Fault (#DF) Abort exc_double_fault() — fault while delivering another fault
9 Coprocessor Segment Overrun Fault exc_coproc_segment_overrun() SIGFPE historical 80387 condition
10 Invalid TSS (#TS) Fault exc_invalid_tss() SIGSEGV bad task-state segment references
11 Segment Not Present (#NP) Fault exc_segment_not_present() SIGBUS loading an absent segment descriptor
12 Stack Segment Fault (#SS) Fault exc_stack_segment() SIGBUS bad stack segment or stack limit condition
13 General Protection (#GP) Fault exc_general_protection() SIGSEGV protection-rule violation outside #PF
14 Page Fault (#PF) Fault exc_page_fault() SIGSEGV translation or permission fault
15 Spurious Interrupt Interrupt (erratum) exc_spurious_interrupt_bug() — Intel-reserved slot; wired only to absorb a Pentium Pro local-APIC erratum
16 x87 Floating-Point Error (#MF) Fault exc_coprocessor_error() SIGFPE deferred x87 exception
17 Alignment Check (#AC) Fault exc_alignment_check() SIGBUS split-lock detection; or unaligned access with CR0.AM and EFLAGS.AC set
18 Machine Check (#MC) Abort exc_machine_check() — hardware-detected CPU or memory error
19 SIMD Floating-Point Exception (#XF) Fault exc_simd_coprocessor_error() SIGFPE SSE/AVX floating-point exception
20 Virtualization Exception (#VE) Fault exc_virtualization_exception() — Intel TDX / virtualization exitless event
21 Control Protection Exception (#CP) Fault exc_control_protection() SIGSEGV CET shadow-stack or indirect-branch policy failure
29 VMM Communication Exception (#VC) Fault exc_vmm_communication() — AMD SEV-ES/SNP guest action needing hypervisor emulation

The table gives the compact map. The following subsections expand each vector in turn. For each one, the important questions are the same. What event raises it? Is the saved instruction pointer restartable? Can Linux fix the condition internally? If not, which signal or panic path should the kernel use?

Detailed Description of the x86 Exception Vectors

Vector 0: Divide Error (#DE)

Type: Fault

Usual user-visible signal: SIGFPE

#DE is raised when an integer divide instruction has a zero divisor or when the quotient does not fit in the destination width. Because it is a fault, RIP points to the divide instruction itself. Linux handles it through exc_divide_error(), which funnels into the common do_error_trap() pattern and eventually delivers SIGFPE to user tasks unless a notifier or fixup consumes it. Kernel-mode divide errors are almost always programming bugs, so fixup_exception() is tried first and die() follows if no exception-table recovery exists.

Vector 1: Debug (#DB)

Type: Fault/Trap

Usual user-visible signal: SIGTRAP

#DB is unusual because several independent mechanisms can cause it: trap-flag single-step, hardware breakpoints, ICEBP, block-step, bus-lock detection, and some virtualization/debug corner cases. Linux splits the handling between kernel-mode and user-mode paths in exc_debug_kernel() and exc_debug_user(). On x86_64 the kernel-mode path uses an IST stack because recursive debug events are especially dangerous during entry handling. User-mode debug exceptions generally become SIGTRAP, while kernel-mode ones are first offered to notifiers such as kgdb and hardware breakpoint infrastructure.

Vector 2: Nonmaskable Interrupt

Type: Interrupt

Usual user-visible signal: —

The NMI is asynchronous and ignores IF, so it is not maskable by normal local interrupt disable instructions. Linux uses it for watchdogs, performance monitoring, lockup detection, and a few emergency stop/report paths. Because NMI can interrupt almost anything, x86 gives it a dedicated IST stack and Linux enters through irqentry_nmi_enter() / irqentry_nmi_exit() rather than the ordinary hardirq helpers. There is no user signal analogue because NMI is fundamentally a kernel and platform integrity event, not an application-level condition.

Vector 3: Breakpoint (#BP)

Type: Trap

Usual user-visible signal: SIGTRAP

#BP is generated by int3, the classic one-byte breakpoint instruction. Because it is a trap, RIP already points past the breakpoint byte when the handler runs. Linux uses this vector for user debugging, kernel probes, and some live instrumentation facilities. User-mode #BP becomes SIGTRAP unless a debugger or probe framework intercepts it first.

Vector 4: Overflow (#OF)

Type: Trap

Usual user-visible signal: SIGSEGV

#OF is the architectural result of the into instruction when the overflow flag is set. In modern 64-bit application code the instruction is rare, but the vector remains part of the architecture and therefore part of the kernel entry table. Linux still wires it up through exc_overflow() and the generic error-trap path. The event is treated as a software-requested diagnostic trap rather than as an MMU-style recoverable fault.

Vector 5: BOUND Range Exceeded (#BR)

Type: Fault

Usual user-visible signal: SIGSEGV

#BR is raised by the historical bound instruction when an index lies outside programmer-supplied bounds. The instruction is obsolete in long-mode software, but the vector survives and Linux still names the handler exc_bounds(). Because it is a fault, the instruction pointer references the failing bound instruction. In practice this mostly matters for compatibility, completeness, and for understanding why vector 5 exists at all.

Vector 6: Invalid Opcode (#UD)

Type: Fault

Usual user-visible signal: SIGILL

#UD means the decoder could not interpret the instruction stream as a legal instruction for the current mode and feature set. Typical causes include undefined opcodes, forbidden prefix combinations, or using an instruction whose feature bit is absent. Linux also exploits ud2 as an intentional bug trap: BUG(), WARN(), and the static-call and objtool machinery all encode a trap as a two-byte ud2 plus an out-of-line table entry describing what it means. This is why exc_invalid_op() is one of the few DEFINE_IDTENTRY_RAW handlers (arch/x86/kernel/traps.c:480): it calls handle_bug() before irqentry_enter(), so that a WARN() triggered inside the entry code itself cannot recurse through entry and deadlock. Only if the trap is not a recognized kernel bug entry does the handler take the ordinary path. User mode usually receives SIGILL with ILL_ILLOPN; kernel mode first tries exception-table fixups and otherwise treats the event as fatal.

Vector 7: Device Not Available (#NM)

Type: Fault

Usual user-visible signal: —

Historically Linux used #NM for lazy FPU context switching by setting CR0.TS and taking a trap on first floating-point use. That old Linux 2.6 pattern is gone: Linux 7.0.12 manages the FPU eagerly, and the handler now treats a set CR0.TS as a bug, clearing it under a WARN() and carrying on.

But the vector is not a relic. On x86_64 the first thing exc_device_not_available() does is call handle_xfd_event() (arch/x86/kernel/traps.c:1509), and that is now the vector’s main live use. Extended Feature Disable (XFD) lets the kernel arm a trap on individual XSAVE state components, and Linux arms it for the very large AMX tile state. A task that has never used AMX has no space allocated for those tiles; when it executes its first tile instruction the CPU raises #NM and records the offending state bit in MSR_IA32_XFD_ERR. The handler reads and clears that MSR, then calls xfd_enable_feature() to grow the task’s xstate buffer and disarm XFD for it, and the instruction is retried. This is demand paging applied to register state: the cost of the largest architectural state is paid only by the tasks that actually use it.

Two failure paths are worth noting because they are the only way #NM becomes user-visible. If the task has not been granted permission to use the feature through arch_prctl(ARCH_REQ_XCOMP_PERM), the kernel sends SIGILL with ILL_ILLOPC; if the buffer allocation fails, it sends SIGSEGV. An #NM from kernel mode with XFD_ERR set is a bug and warns. An #NM that is neither an XFD event nor a stale CR0.TS ends in die("unexpected #NM exception"), deliberately killing the task rather than looping forever on a fault the kernel cannot clear.

Vector 8: Double Fault (#DF)

Type: Abort

Usual user-visible signal: —

#DF means the CPU encountered a second exception while trying to invoke the first handler and could not deliver the pair in the ordinary way. Linux treats it with extreme caution and assigns it a dedicated IST stack on x86_64. The handler exc_double_fault() often ends in panic because the most common root causes are stack corruption, stack overflow into a guard page, or catastrophic descriptor-state failure. The source comments in arch/x86/kernel/traps.c note that some x86_64 double faults are technically recoverable, but Linux still treats them as emergency situations.

Vector 9: Coprocessor Segment Overrun

Type: Fault

Usual user-visible signal: SIGFPE

This is a historical leftover from 386/387 era hardware. Modern processors do not use it for normal floating-point exception delivery, which now goes through #MF and SIMD-specific mechanisms. Linux still reserves the vector and preserves the handler name because the architectural table defines it. Its continued presence is mostly pedagogical: it shows how long interrupt architecture history lingers in a modern kernel.

Vector 10: Invalid TSS (#TS)

Type: Fault

Usual user-visible signal: SIGSEGV

#TS reports problems involving the Task State Segment or segment descriptors referenced during privilege transitions and legacy task-switch machinery. Long mode no longer uses hardware task switching for ordinary process scheduling, but the TSS still matters for stack pointers and IST entries. Linux handles #TS with the generic error-trap flow and treats kernel occurrences as severe setup bugs. User-mode delivery usually becomes SIGSEGV because the condition is a protection failure, not an arithmetic one.

Vector 11: Segment Not Present (#NP)

Type: Fault

Usual user-visible signal: SIGBUS

#NP arises when software tries to use a segment descriptor marked not present. In 64-bit Linux most segmentation is vestigial, but user compatibility paths, TLS segments, and low-level entry transitions still make the vector architecturally relevant. Linux routes it through exc_segment_not_present() and then into the common trap logic. The usual signal is SIGBUS, reflecting an addressing or object-presence problem rather than an illegal instruction.

Vector 12: Stack Segment Fault (#SS)

Type: Fault

Usual user-visible signal: SIGBUS

#SS reports problems involving the stack segment, stack-limit checks, or stack references that violate x86's stack semantics. On x86_64 its day-to-day visibility is low because the kernel and native 64-bit user processes use a flat address model, but compatibility code still keeps the vector alive. Linux uses exc_stack_segment() and the standard error-trap logic. Kernel-mode #SS usually indicates entry/return damage and is therefore close to fatal.

Vector 13: General Protection (#GP)

Type: Fault

Usual user-visible signal: SIGSEGV

#GP is the architecture's catch-all protection fault for violations that are not page faults. It covers descriptor privilege errors, noncanonical address faults in some contexts, forbidden instructions such as UMIP-protected reads, and many return-path mishaps. Linux therefore gives exc_general_protection() extra logic for user-mode fixups, vDSO exceptions, UMIP emulation, and kernel fault hinting. When recovery fails, user mode receives SIGSEGV and kernel mode proceeds to die_addr().

Vector 14: Page Fault (#PF)

Type: Fault

Usual user-visible signal: SIGSEGV

#PF is the most important x86 exception for normal system operation because every demand page, copy-on-write write fault, and many kernel probing tricks rely on it. Linux handles it in exc_page_fault(), reads CR2 or the FRED event payload, classifies the address, and then dispatches to do_user_addr_fault() or do_kern_addr_fault(). Because valid page faults may sleep while allocating memory or reading storage, the entry code deliberately treats them as a special raw IDT case. No other ordinary exception is as central to virtual memory performance and semantics.

Vector 15: Spurious Interrupt (Intel-reserved)

Type: Interrupt (erratum workaround)

Usual user-visible signal: —

Vector 15 is reserved by the architecture: Intel defines no exception for it, and in a correctly behaving system nothing should ever deliver it. Linux nevertheless installs a handler. As the IDT listing later in this chapter shows, the default trap table populates the slot with INTG(X86_TRAP_SPURIOUS, asm_exc_spurious_interrupt_bug) (arch/x86/kernel/idt.c:95), and the C entry point exc_spurious_interrupt_bug() exists at arch/x86/kernel/traps.c:1486.

The reason is a Pentium Pro erratum. When the APIC subsystem is configured in mixed mode with Virtual Wire mode implemented through the local APIC, the local APIC can generate vector 0x0F on receipt of a spurious interrupt — one withdrawn before the system completes the INTA sequence — instead of the spurious vector programmed into the 8259. An operating system that left vector 15 empty would take an unhandled trap and crash. The kernel comment notes that the workaround could in theory be limited to 32-bit builds, but that the handler is harmless and other CPUs may share the defect, so it is installed unconditionally.

The handler body is therefore empty: it exists purely to absorb and discard the event. Vector 15 is a useful reminder that an architecturally reserved slot and an unused slot are not the same thing. Linux populates every vector it can plausibly receive — including this one, which by the letter of the architecture it should never see — because an IDT hole is not a benign gap but an unhandled-exception panic waiting for defective hardware to trigger it. Vectors 22 through 28, 30, and 31 are the genuinely unpopulated slots. Vector 29 is not among them: AMD claimed it for the VMM Communication Exception, described below.

Vector 16: x87 Floating-Point Error (#MF)

Type: Fault

Usual user-visible signal: SIGFPE

#MF reports pending x87 floating-point exceptions. Unlike early coprocessor-era designs, modern x86 delivers a precise architectural exception once the deferred x87 condition becomes visible. Linux converts user-mode cases into SIGFPE with an appropriate code after inspecting the floating-point status. Kernel-mode cases try exception-table fixups before declaring failure.

Vector 17: Alignment Check (#AC)

Type: Fault

Usual user-visible signal: SIGBUS

#AC was originally raised only for unaligned memory accesses made at CPL3 with CR0.AM set and the AC flag enabled in EFLAGS. That classic use is rare, because most x86 unaligned accesses are perfectly legal and nobody enables alignment checking. On current Intel hardware the vector has a second and far more common cause: split-lock detection. A split lock is an atomic read-modify-write whose operand straddles a cache-line boundary, forcing the processor to take a bus lock and stall every other core on the system for microseconds. When X86_FEATURE_SPLIT_LOCK_DETECT is enabled, the CPU raises #AC on such an access regardless of CR0.AM.

This is why exc_alignment_check() looks the way it does (arch/x86/kernel/traps.c:523). A kernel-mode #AC is not offered to fixup_exception() at all; it goes straight to die("Split lock detected\n", ...), on the reasoning that the kernel has no legitimate reason to issue an unaligned atomic and the misbehaviour must be found rather than tolerated. A user-mode #AC is passed to handle_user_split_lock() (arch/x86/kernel/cpu/bus_lock.c:319) first. That function distinguishes the two causes by inspecting EFLAGS.AC: if the task set the flag itself, this is a genuine alignment check and is left to the normal path; otherwise it is a split lock, and the response depends on the split_lock_detect= boot policy. Under warn the kernel logs the offending instruction pointer, rate-limits the task, and lets it continue; under fatal it declines to handle the event so that the generic path below delivers SIGBUS. Only when the split-lock handler returns false does the classic behaviour apply: do_trap() delivers SIGBUS with BUS_ADRALN.

A closely related mechanism, bus-lock detection, catches the wider class of locked accesses that cross a cache line even on hardware without split-lock #AC, but it reports through #DB rather than #AC (handle_bus_lock() in the same file), which is why vector 1’s cause list includes bus-lock detect.

Vector 18: Machine Check (#MC)

Type: Abort

Usual user-visible signal: —

#MC reports a hardware-detected failure such as cache corruption, memory ECC failure, bus error, or CPU-internal fault. Because the error may invalidate ordinary assumptions about execution reliability, Linux assigns machine check its own IST stack and a highly specialized recovery path. Some machine checks are recoverable enough to kill a process or offline a page, but many are fatal or at least system-integrity threatening. This is one of the clearest examples of an abort rather than a fault. For an uncorrected memory error consumed in user mode, the handler only queues task work; the page is then retired and the task signaled by the memory-failure code described in Chapter 17 (see Memory Failure and HWPoison), which also covers the ARM64 equivalent, the synchronous external abort.

Vector 19: SIMD Floating-Point Exception (#XF)

Type: Fault

Usual user-visible signal: SIGFPE

#XF covers SSE and later SIMD floating-point exceptions. Linux treats it similarly to #MF at the user ABI level, but the internal status extraction differs because the exception state lives in SIMD control/status structures rather than in the old x87 stack alone. As vector extensions expanded from SSE to AVX and beyond, this vector became far more relevant than the old coprocessor-segment-overrun relic. User mode again sees SIGFPE with a suitable si_code.

Vector 20: Virtualization Exception (#VE)

Type: Fault

Usual user-visible signal: —

#VE is a modern addition used by virtualization technologies such as Intel TDX to reflect certain events to a guest without a traditional VM exit. Linux 7.0.12 therefore includes exc_virtualization_exception() in the IDT when the relevant guest support is configured. This vector did not meaningfully exist in the Linux 2.6 mental model. Its appearance is a good example of how the exception architecture has absorbed virtualization-specific semantics.

Vector 21: Control Protection Exception (#CP)

Type: Fault

Usual user-visible signal: SIGSEGV

#CP is the CET-era control-protection exception, and it is really two exceptions sharing a vector. Its handler lives in arch/x86/kernel/cet.c:149 rather than in traps.c, and its first act is to split on privilege level, because user space and the kernel use different halves of CET.

User space uses shadow stacks. Every call pushes the return address onto a hardware-protected shadow stack in addition to the ordinary one; every ret compares the two and raises #CP if they disagree. do_user_cp_fault() reads the offending shadow-stack pointer from MSR_IA32_PL3_SSP before re-enabling interrupts, logs a rate-limited diagnostic, and delivers SIGSEGV with the CET-specific code SEGV_CPERR. A stack-smashing attack that overwrote a return address is exactly what this is designed to catch.

The kernel uses Indirect Branch Tracking instead. With IBT enabled the CPU requires every indirect branch to land on an endbr64 instruction; landing anywhere else raises #CP with the CP_ENDBR error code. do_kernel_cp_fault() prints Missing ENDBR: %pS naming the branch target and then, by default, calls BUG(). The boot parameter ibt=warn downgrades that to a WARN() so the machine keeps running, and ibt=off clears the feature entirely. Any #CP that does not match the expected half for its privilege level — a kernel fault without IBT, or a user fault without shadow-stack support — falls into do_unexpected_cp(), which warns once and returns.

Vector 29: VMM Communication Exception (#VC)

Type: Fault

Usual user-visible signal: —

#VC is AMD’s counterpart to Intel’s #VE, and it exists for the same reason: a confidential-computing guest must sometimes cooperate with a hypervisor it does not trust. Under SEV-ES and SEV-SNP the guest’s registers are encrypted and the hypervisor cannot read them, so the traditional VM exit — where the hypervisor inspects guest state and emulates the instruction — no longer works. Instead the CPU delivers #VC to the guest itself. The guest handler decodes the instruction, decides exactly which values it is willing to disclose, copies only those into the shared Guest-Hypervisor Communication Block, and issues a VMGEXIT. The guest, not the hypervisor, controls what leaves the encrypted context.

#VC is the reason Table 4-8 does not end at vector 21. It is also unusual in its entry requirements. Because a #VC can occur almost anywhere — including inside the entry code that is still setting up a stack — it needs an IST stack, and it must tolerate nesting on that stack, which an ordinary IST entry cannot. Linux therefore gives it a dedicated IST slot (ISTG(X86_TRAP_VC, asm_exc_vmm_communication, IST_INDEX_VC)) and then immediately gets off it: the idtentry_vc stub (arch/x86/entry/entry_64.S:454) switches to the task stack when the fault came from user mode, and otherwise calls vc_switch_off_ist() to move back to the interrupted kernel stack, falling back to a second dedicated stack (VC2) when the interrupted stack cannot be trusted. Freeing the IST slot on entry is what makes a nested #VC survivable. The two halves land in separate C functions built by the bespoke macros DEFINE_IDTENTRY_VC_KERNEL and DEFINE_IDTENTRY_VC_USER (arch/x86/coco/sev/vc-handle.c:957 and :1009). The whole entry is compiled in only when CONFIG_AMD_MEM_ENCRYPT is set; on any other build vector 29 stays empty.

ARM64 Exception Model

ARM64 does not use an IDT. Instead it defines exception levels EL0 through EL3 and a separate vector table for each privileged level that may receive exceptions. Linux normally runs at EL1. User space runs at EL0. When an exception occurs, the CPU consults VBAR_EL1 and chooses one of sixteen vector slots based on two axes: where the exception came from and what broad class it belongs to.

Table 4-9. ARM64 EL1 vector table layout and the C handlers it dispatches to (arch/arm64/kernel/entry-common.c)

Source context Synchronous IRQ FIQ SError
Current EL using SP_EL0 (el1t) el1t_64_sync_handler() el1t_64_irq_handler() el1t_64_fiq_handler() el1t_64_error_handler()
Current EL using SP_ELx (el1h) el1h_64_sync_handler() el1h_64_irq_handler() el1h_64_fiq_handler() el1h_64_error_handler()
Lower EL, AArch64 (el0t, 64-bit) el0t_64_sync_handler() el0t_64_irq_handler() el0t_64_fiq_handler() el0t_64_error_handler()
Lower EL, AArch32 (el0t, 32-bit compat) el0t_32_sync_handler() el0t_32_irq_handler() el0t_32_fiq_handler() el0t_32_error_handler()

The four el1t entries in the first row deserve a note. They cover exceptions taken from EL1 while the CPU is using SP_EL0 as its stack pointer, a configuration Linux never adopts: kernel code always runs on SP_EL1. The row exists only because the 4×4 geometry is architecturally fixed and the slots must be filled. Accordingly, all four are generated by the UNHANDLED() macro (arch/arm64/kernel/entry-common.c:291-294), which expands to a function that calls __panic_unhandled() and dies with “Unhandled exception”. Reaching one of them means the kernel’s stack-pointer invariant has already been violated, so panicking is the only honest response. This is the ARM64 counterpart of the x86 story told under vector 15: an entry that should never fire still gets a handler, because a hole in the table would be far worse than a controlled panic.

The last row is conditional in a different way. The el0t_32_* handlers are real only when CONFIG_COMPAT is set; on a kernel built without 32-bit user support they are generated by the same UNHANDLED() macro as the el1t row (arch/arm64/kernel/entry-common.c:951-956). The slots are still occupied, because the table geometry does not shrink — but an AArch32 process cannot exist to enter them, so reaching one again means something has gone badly wrong.

Each vector slot is 128 bytes wide. Sixteen slots of 128 bytes make the table exactly 2 KB, and VBAR_EL1 requires that alignment — which is why the table is declared with .align 11 in arch/arm64/kernel/entry.S:518. The fixed slot width lets the hardware compute the branch target by adding a small constant offset to VBAR_EL1, with no table lookup and no memory read of a descriptor: the entry code for each class begins at the vector, rather than being pointed to by it. This is the deepest structural difference from x86, where the IDT holds gate descriptors that the CPU must load and validate before it can jump anywhere.

It also follows that there need not be only one table. Because a vector table is just 2 KB of code at an aligned address, Linux can install a different one by writing a different value to VBAR_EL1, and it does so for two mitigations. When KPTI is active (CONFIG_UNMAP_KERNEL_AT_EL0), EL0 entries go through tramp_vectors, a table living in a small region that stays mapped in the user page tables and whose only job is to switch TTBR1 and jump to the real handler. When the Spectre-BHB branch-history mitigation is needed, __bp_harden_el1_vectors supplies variants that run a loop, firmware call, or clearing instruction before dispatching. The x86 equivalent of this idea is the CPU entry area discussed later in this chapter; ARM64 achieves it by swapping the whole table rather than by remapping stacks.

The low-level assembly saves state into a pt_regs frame, records ELR_EL1 and SPSR_EL1, masks or unmasks additional conditions via the DAIF bits, and then branches to C handlers in arch/arm64/kernel/entry-common.c. The synchronous dispatch functions el1h_64_sync_handler() and el0t_64_sync_handler() then switch on ESR_ELx_EC(esr) to decide which kind of abort, debug event, or system instruction trap occurred. ESR_EL1 is the ARM64 answer to the x86 error code, but it carries far more: a six-bit exception class in its top bits plus a class-specific syndrome field describing the access size, direction, and register involved. For aborts, the offending address arrives separately in FAR_EL1, the analogue of x86’s CR2.

  • ESR_ELx_EC_DABT_LOW and ESR_ELx_EC_IABT_LOW route user data and instruction aborts to the page-fault analogue paths.
  • ESR_ELx_EC_SVC64 is the 64-bit system-call class and leads to el0_svc().
  • Debug classes such as breakpoint, single-step, and watchpoint have distinct ESR values and dedicated helper functions.
  • Pseudo-NMI support on ARM64 can treat some high-priority interrupts specially when GIC priority masking is available.

IRQs and Interrupt Controllers

The CPU entry hardware answers only one question: given a vector or exception class, where should execution jump next? Something else must answer the earlier questions. Which device asserted an interrupt? Can several devices share a line? Which CPU should receive it? Should it be edge-triggered or level-triggered? Should the source remain masked until software finishes? Those duties belong to the interrupt controller fabric.

The Cascaded 8259A PIC

The original PC design used the Intel 8259A Programmable Interrupt Controller. One PIC exported eight input lines. IBM needed more, so the AT architecture cascaded a second PIC behind IRQ2 of the first. That gave 15 usable IRQ inputs: IRQ0 through IRQ7 on the master, IRQ8 through IRQ15 on the slave, with the master's IRQ2 reserved as the cascade link.

Table 4-10. 8259A PIC legacy IRQ layout

Logical IRQ Controller Meaning in classic PCs Modern status
IRQ0 master timer usually routed through IO-APIC compatibility
IRQ1 master keyboard legacy / often emulated
IRQ2 master cascade to slave PIC not a normal device line
IRQ8 slave RTC still visible on some systems
IRQ14-15 slave ATA channels mostly legacy

In protected mode Linux must remap the PIC, because its power-on vector base collides with the CPU exception vectors. The remapping is programmed by init_8259A() through the ICW2 initialization word:

/* arch/x86/kernel/i8259.c:364 */
/* ICW2: 8259A-1 IR0-7 mapped to ISA_IRQ_VECTOR(0) */
outb_pic(ISA_IRQ_VECTOR(0), PIC_MASTER_IMR);
...
/* ICW2: 8259A-2 IR0-7 mapped to ISA_IRQ_VECTOR(8) */
outb_pic(ISA_IRQ_VECTOR(8), PIC_SLAVE_IMR);

As established earlier in this chapter, ISA_IRQ_VECTOR(irq) evaluates to 0x30 + irq, not 0x20 + irq. Older texts speak of "IRQ plus 32" when converting a PIC line into an IDT vector, and that arithmetic is no longer correct: the master PIC's base is vector 48 and the slave's is vector 56. The tradition survives because 32 is still where the usable external range begins; what changed is that Linux no longer places the ISA lines at the very bottom of it.

Masking is not the same as disabling. Masking tells the controller not to deliver an already configured interrupt source at the moment. Disabling in Linux usually means a deeper software state change that increments descriptor depth, updates bookkeeping flags, and may call controller hooks such as irq_disable() or irq_mask(). The distinction matters because a masked line might still be configured, counted, and wake-capable, while a fully disabled line is logically unavailable to ordinary delivery.

APIC and x2APIC on x86_64

Modern x86 systems use the Advanced Programmable Interrupt Controller architecture. Each CPU has a local APIC. One or more I/O APICs accept external interrupt inputs and route them as APIC messages. Many PCIe devices bypass I/O APIC pins entirely by sending MSI or MSI-X messages straight to a destination APIC. The result is a distributed interrupt fabric rather than a single central chip.

Figure 4-4 redraws the x86_64 fabric with both delivery paths visible at once: legacy pin-based interrupts travelling through an I/O APIC, and MSI or MSI-X messages written by the device straight to the address of a destination local APIC, with no interrupt controller pin involved at all.

The local APIC handles CPU-local events such as the APIC timer, thermal interrupts, performance-monitoring interrupts, and inter-processor interrupts. The I/O APIC's main job is routing external interrupt inputs. Its redirection table determines the vector, trigger type, polarity, delivery mode, mask state, and destination. Linux programs those entries during early boot and during later affinity or trigger-mode changes.

Table 4-11. I/O APIC redirection table entry fields

Field Meaning Typical Linux use
Vector IDT vector delivered to the target CPU usually a dynamically assigned external IRQ vector above 31
Delivery mode fixed, lowest priority, SMI, NMI, INIT, ExtINT fixed for every device IRQ; Linux never programs lowest-priority (see below)
Destination mode physical or logical APIC destination logical routing common on SMP systems
Delivery status whether message is pending in APIC delivery mostly diagnostic / readback
Polarity active high or active low depends on platform interrupt source configuration
Remote IRR level-trigger bookkeeping in controller relevant for level interrupts until EOI
Trigger mode edge or level PCI lines typically level; some legacy sources edge
Mask blocks delivery when set used by Linux flow handlers and affinity transitions
Destination target APIC ID or logical set programmed from affinity policy

The heading of this section names x2APIC, and the difference is worth stating precisely because it constrains everything above. The original xAPIC exposes the local APIC as a 4 KB memory-mapped page, conventionally at physical address 0xFEE00000, and reserves only eight bits for an APIC ID. x2APIC replaces the MMIO window with a bank of MSRs starting at APIC_BASE_MSR (0x800), one per 16-byte APIC register offset, and widens the APIC ID to 32 bits. Two consequences follow. First, register access becomes a wrmsr rather than an uncached memory write, which is both faster and not subject to MMIO ordering rules; sending an IPI collapses into a single 64-bit write with no polling of the delivery-status bit:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/apic.h:227 */
static inline void native_x2apic_icr_write(u32 low, u32 id)
{
    wrmsrq(APIC_BASE_MSR + (APIC_ICR >> 4), ((__u64) id) << 32 | low);
}

Second, and more consequentially, x2APIC is what allows a machine to have more than 255 logical CPUs. But the wider APIC ID is not by itself enough, because the interrupt sources also have to be able to name the destination. A plain MSI message carries only eight destination bits in the address, so on a system without interrupt remapping the kernel simply refuses to compose a message aimed higher:

/* arch/x86/kernel/apic/apic.c — __irq_msi_compose_msg() */
if (dmar)
    msg->arch_addr_hi.destid_8_31 = cfg->dest_apicid >> 8;
else if (virt_ext_dest_id && cfg->dest_apicid < 0x8000)
    msg->arch_addr_lo.virt_destid_8_14 = cfg->dest_apicid >> 8;
else
    WARN_ON_ONCE(cfg->dest_apicid > 0xFF);

Only the IOMMU can place a destination ID in the high address bits; anything else attempting that would just be writing to memory. This is why enable_IR_x2apic() (arch/x86/kernel/apic/apic.c:1900) calls irq_remapping_prepare() and irq_remapping_enable() before try_to_enable_x2apic(), and why that function refuses to enter x2APIC mode on bare metal when remapping did not come up. The one exception is virtualization: a hypervisor advertising an extended destination ID field (address bits 5 through 11) lets the kernel address 15 bits' worth of APIC IDs without an IOMMU, which is the virt_ext_dest_id branch above and raises the usable limit from 255 to 32767. The practical rule for a reader debugging a large server is therefore that x2APIC, interrupt remapping, and CPU counts above 255 stand or fall together.

Static and Dynamic APIC Routing

A fixed, or static, route sends each interrupt to a predetermined CPU or destination mask. This is easy to reason about. The same CPU tends to touch the same device queues and cache lines. Driver writers can align per-queue vectors with NUMA-local CPUs. The downside is hot spots. If a busy NIC sends all completions to one CPU, that CPU may saturate while neighbors remain idle.

The alternative is dynamic routing, historically via the APIC's lowest-priority delivery mode. In that mode the controller targets one CPU from a destination set according to APIC arbitration rules. Linux abandoned it.
