APIC_DELIVERY_MODE_LOWESTPRIO is still defined in arch/x86/include/asm/apicdef.h, but no code path assigns it: __irq_msi_compose_msg() hardcodes APIC_DELIVERY_MODE_FIXED (arch/x86/kernel/apic/apic.c:2295), and the only non-fixed modes the I/O APIC code touches are SMI and ExtINT, both of which it recognizes in order to avoid disturbing them. Every device interrupt on a modern x86_64 Linux system is delivered in fixed mode to a single, explicitly chosen destination.

Distribution is therefore entirely a software policy: Linux changes affinity masks over time rather than relying on the controller's built-in arbitration, and spreads load across per-queue MSI-X vectors rather than across CPUs within one vector. Software policy is easier to tune, easier to align with scheduler topology, and — unlike hardware arbitration — it can be reasoned about from /proc/interrupts. The section "What Changed Since Linux 2.6" later in this chapter revisits why the hardware mechanism was dropped.

IRQ Affinity and irqbalance

Linux exposes IRQ affinity through /proc/irq/N/smp_affinity and /proc/irq/N/smp_affinity_list. These files describe which CPUs may receive IRQ N. Writing a cpumask there causes the generic IRQ layer to validate the request and, if the underlying chip supports it, call irq_set_affinity() through the chip method table. The core helper path in kernel/irq/manage.c checks whether the interrupt is balanceable, whether it is managed, and whether the requested mask collides with housekeeping or isolated CPU policy.

  • __irq_can_set_affinity() is the underlying test: it requires that the descriptor exists, that irqd_can_balance() holds, and that the chip actually implements an irq_set_affinity method.
  • A write from userspace goes through the stricter irq_can_set_affinity_usr() wrapper, which adds !irqd_affinity_is_managed(). That single extra term is what makes managed interrupts refuse arbitrary userspace masks: the kernel owns their placement for queue-count and CPU-hotplug correctness, so it declines to let /proc break that invariant. In-kernel callers using irq_can_set_affinity() are not subject to the restriction.
  • The irqbalance daemon observes load and periodically redistributes eligible interrupts across CPUs.
  • Drivers can request placement directly by calling irq_set_affinity() or by allocating managed MSI-X vectors whose affinities are computed during setup.

Affinity is central to scalable I/O. The modern pattern is one vector per receive or completion queue, each vector pinned to a CPU or CPU subset that also runs the associated NAPI poll or completion processing. That design reduces cache bouncing and cross-CPU wakeups. It is one reason MSI-X displaced line-based shared interrupts in high-performance devices.

Message Signaled Interrupts: MSI and MSI-X

MSI converts an interrupt into a memory write. Instead of asserting a physical wire, the device writes a data value to a special APIC-targeting address. That write is turned into an interrupt message at the interconnect level. MSI-X generalizes the model by allowing many independently programmable vectors, often one per hardware queue.

Table 4-12. Conceptual MSI address and data fields on x86

Piece Role Comment
Address base (0xFEE... in xAPIC model) selects APIC interrupt message space special memory-mapped destination recognized by platform logic
Destination APIC ID bits choose target CPU or logical destination programmed from affinity policy
Redirection hint / destination mode physical vs logical interpretation used differently by xAPIC/x2APIC and platform routing
Data vector IDT vector number must match a valid Linux-assigned external vector
Delivery mode bits fixed or other APIC delivery mode fixed is overwhelmingly common for device MSI
Level / trigger semantics message attributes, often edge-like MSI is naturally edge-oriented because each write is a distinct event

MSI-X matters more than basic MSI in modern servers. A NIC with 128 receive queues can request a separate vector for each queue. A storage controller can isolate submission and completion paths. A GPU or NVMe device can steer interrupts toward the CPUs that submit work. The generic Linux IRQ layer sees the result as a set of Linux IRQ numbers backed by an MSI irq domain rather than by legacy global system interrupt pins.

ARM64 GICv3

ARM64 servers and phones typically use the ARM Generic Interrupt Controller version 3. Its architecture looks different from APIC but solves analogous problems. The Distributor sees system-wide interrupts. Each CPU has a Redistributor for SGIs and PPIs. The CPU interface is accessed mainly through system registers such as ICC_IAR1_EL1, ICC_EOIR1_EL1, and ICC_PMR_EL1. An optional ITS translates MSI writes into Locality-specific Peripheral Interrupts.

Table 4-13. GICv3 interrupt classes

Class ID range Scope Examples
SGI 0-15 per-CPU software-generated IPIs, reschedule, smp calls
PPI 16-31 per-CPU private peripheral architected timer, PMU
SPI 32-1019 shared peripheral platform device interrupts
EPPI 1056-1119 extended per-CPU private (GICv3.1) additional per-CPU sources beyond the 16 classic PPIs
ESPI 4096-5119 extended shared peripheral (GICv3.1) large SoCs that exhaust the 988 classic SPIs
LPI 8192+ ITS-translated message-based PCIe MSI/MSI-X

The two extended ranges are frequently omitted from summaries of GICv3, but Linux implements both. drivers/irqchip/irq-gic-v3.c classifies every INTID through a get_intid_range() helper whose switch returns EPPI_RANGE for EPPI_BASE_INTID (1056) through 1119 and ESPI_RANGE for ESPI_BASE_INTID (4096) through 5119, and every register-offset calculation in the driver then has to be range-aware. A reader tracing an INTID above 1019 in a GIC dump will not find it in the classic tables at all.

GICv3 adds a priority model that deserves explicit mention. Each interrupt has a numerical priority, and lower numbers mean higher priority. ICC_PMR_EL1 defines the priority mask, and the comparison is strict: an interrupt is signaled only if its priority value is numerically less than the programmed threshold. An interrupt whose priority equals the mask is blocked, not delivered — a detail that is easy to get backwards and that the kernel's own constants depend on. The binary point registers determine how priority is partitioned into preemption groups versus subpriority.

Linux does not use the 256-value space in any general way; it uses exactly three points in it, defined in include/linux/irqchip/arm-gic-v3-prio.h:

/* include/linux/irqchip/arm-gic-v3-prio.h */
#define GICV3_PRIO_UNMASKED    0xe0   /* permits both IRQs and pseudo-NMIs */
#define GICV3_PRIO_IRQ         0xc0   /* priority of a regular interrupt */
#define GICV3_PRIO_NMI         0x80   /* priority of a pseudo-NMI */

The scheme falls out of the strict comparison. Regular interrupts are programmed into the distributor at GICV3_PRIO_IRQ, pseudo-NMIs at GICV3_PRIO_NMI (dist_prio_irq and dist_prio_nmi in drivers/irqchip/irq-gic-v3.c:40). Writing 0xe0 to the PMR admits both, because 0xc0 < 0xe0 and 0x80 < 0xe0. Writing 0xc0 masks regular IRQs while still admitting pseudo-NMIs, because 0xc0 is not less than 0xc0 but 0x80 is. Writing 0x80 masks everything. That is the whole mechanism by which ARM64 obtains a class of interrupts that remain deliverable while ordinary IRQs are "disabled" — there is no separate NMI signal on the GIC, only a priority band. The values are also chosen so they are valid in both the absolute priority space and the non-secure view of it; when the GIC exposes only the non-secure view, gic_prio_init() rewrites them through __gicv3_prio_to_ns() so the same numbers still appear in the PMR. A fourth constant, GICV3_PRIO_PSR_I_SET, is a software-only flag ORed into the saved mask to record that a code section fell back to masking with PSTATE.I rather than by priority.

Inter-Processor Interrupts

An IPI is an interrupt whose source is another CPU. Linux uses IPIs for rescheduling, TLB shootdowns, cross-CPU function calls, stop-machine operations, and several maintenance paths. On x86, software writes the local APIC's ICR or uses x2APIC MSRs. On ARM64, software emits SGIs through ICC_SGI1R_EL1. In either case, the receiver lands in a normal interrupt entry path and then dispatches to an IPI-specific handler.

Table 4-14. Common Linux IPI purposes

Purpose x86_64 vector ARM64 IPI Why it exists
Reschedule RESCHEDULE_VECTOR (0xfd) IPI_RESCHEDULE force remote CPU to notice runnable higher-priority work
Call function (mask) CALL_FUNCTION_VECTOR (0xfc) IPI_CALL_FUNC execute a callback on many target CPUs
Call function (single) CALL_FUNCTION_SINGLE_VECTOR (0xfb) IPI_CALL_FUNC execute a callback on one target CPU
TLB shootdown no dedicated vector — rides the call-function IPI none — hardware broadcast invalidate remote address-translation state
Stop / reboot REBOOT_VECTOR (0xf8) IPI_CPU_STOP, IPI_CPU_STOP_NMI quiesce remote CPUs for restart or crash handling
IRQ work IRQ_WORK_VECTOR (0xf6) IPI_IRQ_WORK defer work from contexts that cannot run it immediately
Timer broadcast — (handled by the local APIC timer) IPI_TIMER wake CPUs whose local timer stops in deep idle

The TLB row is the one that repays a second look, because the two architectures answer the question in completely different places. On x86_64 there is no TLB-shootdown vector at all; older kernels had an INVALIDATE_TLB_VECTOR range and it is gone. native_flush_tlb_multi() (arch/x86/mm/tlb.c:1347) calls on_each_cpu_cond_mask(should_flush_tlb, flush_tlb_func, ...), so a shootdown is just an ordinary call-function IPI carrying flush_tlb_func as its callback — and it inherits everything described above, including the batching and the idle-CPU elision. On ARM64 there is no IPI whatsoever: the TLBI instruction has inner-shareable variants that the interconnect broadcasts to every CPU in the domain, so translation invalidation is a hardware operation with no software notification and no entry into any handler. This is why NR_IPI on ARM64 lists rescheduling, function calls, CPU stop, timer, and irq_work — but nothing for memory management.

The interesting modern detail is how smp_call_function() is implemented. Linux defines struct __call_single_data in include/linux/smp.h with a lockless list node, a callback pointer, and an opaque information pointer. Per-CPU call_single_queue lists live in kernel/smp.c. When one CPU wants another to run a callback, it enqueues a call_single_data_t on the target CPU's lockless list and sends the call-function IPI if the list was previously empty. The receiving CPU drains the list in generic_smp_call_function_single_interrupt() by calling __flush_smp_call_function_queue(true).

/* include/linux/smp.h */
struct __call_single_data {
    struct __call_single_node node;
    smp_call_func_t func;
    void *info;
};

/* kernel/smp.c */
void __smp_call_single_queue(int cpu, struct llist_node *node)
{
    if (llist_add(node, &per_cpu(call_single_queue, cpu)))
        send_call_function_single_ipi(cpu);
}

void generic_smp_call_function_single_interrupt(void)
{
    __flush_smp_call_function_queue(true);
}

This design is efficient for three reasons. First, the enqueue path is mostly lockless, using llist_add(). Second, multiple callbacks can be batched behind one IPI if several senders queue work before the target drains the list — llist_add() returns true only when the list was previously empty, so the IPI is sent by the first enqueuer and every later one rides along for free. Third, the IPI may be skipped entirely. send_call_function_single_ipi() consults call_function_single_prep_ipi(), which is implemented in the scheduler:

/* kernel/sched/core.c:3777 */
bool call_function_single_prep_ipi(int cpu)
{
    if (set_nr_if_polling(cpu_rq(cpu)->idle)) {
        trace_sched_wake_idle_without_ipi(cpu);
        return false;
    }

    return true;
}

If the target CPU is idle in a polling idle loop, setting its TIF_NEED_RESCHED flag is sufficient: the CPU is already spinning on that word and will notice the queued work without any interrupt at all. The hardware notification is elided and only a memory write remains. The same general pattern appears throughout the kernel: minimal hardware notification, heavier software batching, and an escape hatch that removes the notification entirely when the target is already looking.

The Interrupt Descriptor Table

A system table called the Interrupt Descriptor Table (IDT) associates each interrupt or exception vector with the address of the corresponding interrupt or exception handler. The IDT must be properly initialized before the kernel enables interrupts.

On x86, the IDT is a system table similar in concept to the GDT and LDTs examined in Chapter 2: a fixed-size array of descriptors at an address specified by a dedicated CPU register. However, while the GDT and LDT contain segment descriptors, the IDT contains gate descriptors — the format is different, but the table-lookup mechanism is analogous. Each entry corresponds to an interrupt or exception vector and consists of a gate descriptor. In 32-bit protected mode, each descriptor is 8 bytes wide, allowing a maximum of 256 × 8 = 2,048 bytes for the full table. In 64-bit long mode (x86_64), each descriptor is 16 bytes wide, doubling the table size to 256 × 16 = 4,096 bytes.

The IDTR CPU register allows the IDT to be located anywhere in memory: it specifies both the IDT base linear address and its limit (maximum length). It must be initialized before enabling interrupts by using the LIDT assembly language instruction. On x86_64, the IDTR holds a 10-byte value: 2 bytes for the limit and 8 bytes for the 64-bit base address.

Gate Descriptor Types

The x86 architecture defines three types of gate descriptors that may appear in the IDT. Each type serves a different purpose and has a slightly different format. A Type field near the middle of the descriptor identifies which type is being used.

The exact width of that field is worth pinning down, because the architecture manuals and the Linux source count it differently. Intel documents a 4-bit Type field at bits 43:40, immediately above which sits the S (descriptor type) bit at bit 44, cleared to zero to mark the entry as a system descriptor rather than a code or data segment. Linux does not model the S bit separately. Because every IDT entry is by definition a system descriptor, the kernel folds the always-zero S bit into the type and declares a single 5-bit field spanning bits 44:40. The constants come out the same either way — GATE_INTERRUPT is 0xE whether you read it as a 4-bit type or as a 5-bit field whose top bit is zero — but the bit numbering in Table 4-16 follows the kernel's layout, so dpl lands at 46:45 rather than at the 45:44 you would get by counting Intel's four-bit Type and then adding one for S. This is the kind of off-by-one that silently produces a descriptor the CPU rejects with a general-protection fault, so it is worth reading the struct rather than trusting a diagram.

Task Gate (Type = 0x5)

A task gate includes the TSS selector of the process that must replace the current one when the interrupt or exception occurs. The CPU performs a hardware task switch, loading an entirely new execution context from the TSS. In the original 32-bit Linux 2.6, the double-fault exception (#DF) was handled through a task gate. Linux on x86_64 does not use task gates at all — the hardware task-switch mechanism is not supported in 64-bit long mode. Instead, Linux uses the Interrupt Stack Table (IST) mechanism for the same purpose: providing a known-good stack for critical exceptions.

Figure 4-5 shows the 32-bit task gate descriptor for reference. No IDT entry in Linux 7.0.12 on x86_64 uses this format; it appears here only as a reference for reading 32-bit x86 code and older documentation.

Interrupt Gate (Type = 0xE)

An interrupt gate includes the Segment Selector and the offset (handler address) inside the segment of an interrupt or exception handler. While transferring control to the handler, the processor clears the IF flag, thus disabling further maskable interrupts. This is the gate type that Linux uses for virtually all IDT entries on x86_64.

Figure 4-6 shows the 32-bit interrupt gate layout. It is worth studying because the 16-byte x86_64 descriptor that Linux actually programs is a direct extension of it.

Trap Gate (Type = 0xF)

A trap gate is similar to an interrupt gate, except that while transferring control to the handler, the processor does not modify the IF flag. If interrupts were enabled before the trap, they remain enabled during the handler. If they were disabled, they remain disabled.

Figure 4-7 shows the trap gate layout. Compare it with the interrupt gate in Figure 4-6: the two are bit-for-bit identical apart from the value in the Type field, which is the single bit of information the processor uses to decide whether to clear IF.

Table 4-15. IDT gate type comparison

Gate typeType fieldIF flag on entryHandler addressStack switchLinux 2.6 usageLinux 7.0.12 x86_64 usage
Task gate0x5N/A (full task switch)Via TSS selectorFull context switch via hardware#DF onlyNot available in long mode
Interrupt gate0xECleared (interrupts disabled)Segment:OffsetIST or current kernel stackMost interruptsAll IDT entries (GATE_INTERRUPT)
Trap gate0xFUnchangedSegment:OffsetIST or current kernel stackMost exceptionsNot used (Linux uses interrupt gates for all entries)

As we saw earlier, Linux 7.0.12 uses interrupt gates to handle both interrupts and exceptions. The reason is explained in detail in the “Why Linux Uses Interrupt Gates” subsection below.

x86_64 Long-Mode Gate Descriptor (16 bytes)

In 64-bit long mode, the gate descriptor is expanded from 8 to 16 bytes to accommodate 64-bit handler addresses and the IST field. The format is the same for both interrupt gates and trap gates — only the Type field value differs.

Linux populates the IDT in phases during boot because some handlers are needed very early, before the per-CPU TSS and IST stacks are fully available, while others can be installed only after the entry environment is complete.

Gate Descriptor Format

Figure 4-8 shows the descriptor format Linux actually programs on x86_64. It is 16 bytes rather than 8, the handler offset is split across three separate fields for backward compatibility, and the small IST field selects one of seven dedicated stacks — the mechanism that replaced task gates.

Table 4-16. Fields of the x86_64 IDT gate descriptor

Field Bits Meaning Linux 7.0.12 usage
offset_low 15:0 low address bits part of handler entry address
segment 31:16 code segment selector usually __KERNEL_CS
ist 34:32 IST slot index + 1 encoding nonzero for NMI, #DF, #DB, #MC, sometimes #VC
zero 39:35 must be zero the zero : 5 member of struct idt_bits
type 44:40 gate type (Intel's 4-bit Type plus the always-zero S bit) GATE_INTERRUPT (0xE) for every Linux IDT entry
dpl 46:45 descriptor privilege level 3 for user-invocable traps like breakpoint or int80, 0 otherwise
p 47 present set for installed entries
offset_middle 63:48 middle address bits part of handler address
offset_high 95:64 high address bits part of handler address
reserved 127:96 must be zero Linux clears it in idt_init_desc() 
/* arch/x86/include/asm/desc_defs.h */
struct idt_bits {
    u16 ist : 3, zero : 5, type : 5, dpl : 2, p : 1;
} __attribute__((packed));

struct gate_struct {
    u16 offset_low;
    u16 segment;
    struct idt_bits bits;
    u16 offset_middle;
#ifdef CONFIG_X86_64
    u32 offset_high;
    u32 reserved;
#endif
} __attribute__((packed));

Linux never fills this structure in by hand. Every IDT entry in arch/x86/kernel/idt.c is written with one of four macros layered on a common G() constructor, and because those macros appear in all of the tables quoted later in this section it is worth reading them once:

/* arch/x86/kernel/idt.c */
#define DPL0        0x0
#define DPL3        0x3
#define DEFAULT_STACK   0

#define G(_vector, _addr, _ist, _type, _dpl, _segment)  \
    {                                                   \
        .vector     = _vector,                          \
        .bits.ist   = _ist,                             \
        .bits.type  = _type,                            \
        .bits.dpl   = _dpl,                             \
        .bits.p     = 1,                                \
        .addr       = _addr,                            \
        .segment    = _segment,                         \
    }

/* Interrupt gate */
#define INTG(_vector, _addr)    \
    G(_vector, _addr, DEFAULT_STACK, GATE_INTERRUPT, DPL0, __KERNEL_CS)

/* System interrupt gate */
#define SYSG(_vector, _addr)    \
    G(_vector, _addr, DEFAULT_STACK, GATE_INTERRUPT, DPL3, __KERNEL_CS)

/*
 * Interrupt gate with interrupt stack. The _ist index is the index in
 * the tss.ist[] array, but for the descriptor it needs to start at 1.
 */
#define ISTG(_vector, _addr, _ist)  \
    G(_vector, _addr, _ist + 1, GATE_INTERRUPT, DPL0, __KERNEL_CS)

/* Task gate */
#define TSKG(_vector, _gdt)     \
    G(_vector, NULL, DEFAULT_STACK, GATE_TASK, DPL0, _gdt << 3)

Three observations follow directly from reading these definitions, and together they answer most of the questions a reader has when scanning the IDT tables.

  • All three usable macros produce GATE_INTERRUPT. There is no TRAPG. The claim made earlier — that Linux programs every entry as an interrupt gate — is not a summary of observed behaviour but a property of the only tools available for building an entry.
  • INTG and SYSG differ in exactly one field: DPL0 versus DPL3. The "SYS" prefix does not mean "system vector"; it means the gate is reachable by an explicit instruction from user mode. Only the handful of vectors a program is allowed to raise deliberately — int3, into, and the legacy int $0x80 system-call entry — use it. Every other gate is DPL0, so a user-mode INT n aimed at, say, vector 14 produces a general-protection fault rather than a spoofed page fault.
  • ISTG silently adds one to the IST index. The comment above it explains why: the C code indexes tss.ist[] from zero, but the descriptor encoding reserves zero to mean "do not switch stacks", so slot n in the array is n + 1 in the descriptor. Reading a raw IDT dump therefore requires subtracting one before comparing against IST_INDEX_NMI and friends.
  • TSKG is dead code on x86_64. It survives only for the 32-bit build; long mode has no hardware task switch, as noted above.

Why Linux Uses Interrupt Gates

The older x86 architecture distinguishes trap gates from interrupt gates. A trap gate leaves IF unchanged. An interrupt gate clears IF on entry. Linux 7.0.12 programs its ordinary IDT entries as interrupt gates, even for exceptions that are conceptually traps, because the kernel wants explicit control over the moment at which maskable interrupts become re-enabled.

That design avoids a class of reentrancy problems. The low-level entry stub needs time to build a stable pt_regs frame, tell RCU that the CPU is no longer quiescent, establish tracing and lockdep state, and sometimes switch to a safer stack. If another maskable interrupt arrived before those invariants held, the nested path would have to reason about half-finished entry work. By using interrupt gates and reenabling interrupts later in well-audited C helpers such as cond_local_irq_enable(regs), Linux keeps the difficult part of entry deterministic.

IDT Initialization Phases

arch/x86/kernel/idt.c defines four static tables: early_idts[], def_idts[], apic_idts[], and early_pf_idts[]. The names are descriptive. Early IDTs are enough to survive the very first phases of boot. Default IDTs install the architectural exception handlers before CPU-local setup is complete. APIC IDTs add the SMP and local-APIC vectors once that machinery is ready. The fourth exists because page-fault handling has to become available at a very specific moment, described below. A fifth table, ia32_idt[], appears under CONFIG_IA32_EMULATION to install the int $0x80 gate.

/* arch/x86/kernel/idt.c */
static const __initconst struct idt_data early_idts[] = {
    INTG(X86_TRAP_DB, asm_exc_debug),
    SYSG(X86_TRAP_BP, asm_exc_int3),
#ifdef CONFIG_INTEL_TDX_GUEST
    INTG(X86_TRAP_VE, asm_exc_virtualization_exception),
#endif
};

static const __initconst struct idt_data def_idts[] = {
    INTG(X86_TRAP_DE, asm_exc_divide_error),
    ISTG(X86_TRAP_NMI, asm_exc_nmi, IST_INDEX_NMI),
    INTG(X86_TRAP_GP, asm_exc_general_protection),
    ISTG(X86_TRAP_DF, asm_exc_double_fault, IST_INDEX_DF),
    ISTG(X86_TRAP_DB, asm_exc_debug, IST_INDEX_DB),
    SYSG(X86_TRAP_OF, asm_exc_overflow),
};

static const __initconst struct idt_data apic_idts[] = {
#ifdef CONFIG_SMP
    INTG(RESCHEDULE_VECTOR, asm_sysvec_reschedule_ipi),
    INTG(CALL_FUNCTION_VECTOR, asm_sysvec_call_function),
    INTG(CALL_FUNCTION_SINGLE_VECTOR, asm_sysvec_call_function_single),
#endif
    INTG(LOCAL_TIMER_VECTOR, asm_sysvec_apic_timer_interrupt),
    INTG(IRQ_WORK_VECTOR, asm_sysvec_irq_work),
    INTG(SPURIOUS_APIC_VECTOR, asm_sysvec_spurious_apic_interrupt),
};

Table 4-17. Major phases of x86 IDT setup

Phase Function What gets installed Why the phase exists
Bootstrap catch-all idt_setup_early_handler() all 32 exception vectors pointed at early_idt_handler_array[] gives the decompressed kernel some handler before anything real exists, so an early fault prints a message instead of triple-faulting
Earliest trap setup idt_setup_early_traps() minimum handlers such as debug and breakpoint needed before full CPU-local stack/TSS setup
Early page-fault setup idt_setup_early_pf() temporary #PF handler on x86_64 memory initialization itself may fault before final IST environment exists
Default trap setup idt_setup_traps() architectural exception handlers establishes complete baseline exception coverage
CPU entry area setup setup_cpu_entry_areas() per-CPU entry stack, TSS, IST mappings required before final IST-capable entries are safe
Optional system vectors sysvec_install() → idt_install_sysvec() conditionally compiled vectors at or above FIRST_SYSTEM_VECTOR lets optional features claim a vector; must run before the APIC gate phase, and enforces that with a WARN_ON(idt_setup_done)
APIC / IRQ gate setup idt_setup_apic_and_irq_gates() device IRQ and SMP vectors, then every remaining vector depends on APIC and per-CPU interrupt-stack environment; closes the table and marks it read-only

Filling the Remaining Vectors

Adding up every named entry in the tables above accounts for only a few dozen of the 256 slots. A reader who stops there is left with an obvious question: what happens if the hardware delivers vector 87? The answer is that the last initialization phase fills every slot that nobody claimed, and it does so from two arrays of generated assembly stubs rather than from a table of C function pointers.

/* arch/x86/kernel/idt.c — idt_setup_apic_and_irq_gates() */
int i = FIRST_EXTERNAL_VECTOR;

idt_setup_from_table(idt_table, apic_idts, ARRAY_SIZE(apic_idts), true);

for_each_clear_bit_from(i, system_vectors, FIRST_SYSTEM_VECTOR) {
    entry = irq_entries_start + IDT_ALIGN * (i - FIRST_EXTERNAL_VECTOR);
    set_intr_gate(i, entry);
}

for_each_clear_bit_from(i, system_vectors, NR_VECTORS) {
    entry = spurious_entries_start + IDT_ALIGN * (i - FIRST_SYSTEM_VECTOR);
    set_intr_gate(i, entry);
}

The system_vectors bitmap records which vectors have already been spoken for, so for_each_clear_bit_from() walks precisely the gaps. Everything in the device range gets a stub from irq_entries_start; everything left over in the system range gets one from spurious_entries_start. Because the stubs are laid out at a fixed stride, the address of the stub for vector i is pure arithmetic — no table lookup is needed.

The stubs themselves are generated by an assembler .rept loop in arch/x86/include/asm/idtentry.h:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/idtentry.h */
#define IDT_ALIGN   (8 * (1 + HAS_KERNEL_IBT))

    .align IDT_ALIGN
SYM_CODE_START(irq_entries_start)
    vector=FIRST_EXTERNAL_VECTOR
    .rept NR_EXTERNAL_VECTORS
    UNWIND_HINT_IRET_REGS
0 :
    ENDBR
    .byte   0x6a, vector
    jmp     asm_common_interrupt
    /* Ensure that the above is IDT_ALIGN bytes max */
    .fill 0b + IDT_ALIGN - ., 1, 0xcc
    vector = vector+1
    .endr
SYM_CODE_END(irq_entries_start)

Each stub is three things and nothing more: an ENDBR landing pad so indirect-branch tracking accepts the jump, a one-byte push of the vector number, and a jump to the single shared entry point. Several details in those few lines repay attention.

  • The push is hand-assembled as .byte 0x6a, vector rather than written as pushq $vector. Opcode 0x6a is push-immediate-byte, which keeps every stub the same length regardless of whether the vector number happens to fit in a small encoding. Uniform length is what makes the address arithmetic above possible.
  • That push sign-extends. A vector of 0x80 or above arrives on the stack as a negative 64-bit value, so the C entry point has to mask off the bits above bit 7 before treating it as a vector number. The kernel accepts this rather than spending an extra byte per stub.
  • IDT_ALIGN is 8, or 16 when the kernel is built with indirect-branch tracking, because the ENDBR instruction costs four more bytes than the stub can otherwise spare.
  • The padding byte is 0xcc, the encoding of int3. Execution that somehow lands in the slack between stubs traps immediately instead of sliding forward into the next one.

So the IDT is not really a table of 256 distinct handlers. It is a table of a few dozen specific handlers plus roughly two hundred nearly identical trampolines whose only job is to recover the vector number the hardware did not bother to pass in a register, and then converge on asm_common_interrupt. This is the x86 answer to a problem ARM64 solves differently: ARM64's vector table has only sixteen entries and recovers the specific cause from ESR_EL1 or the GIC, whereas x86 encodes the cause in which stub ran.

Descriptor Construction Helpers

The helper idt_init_desc() converts the abstract struct idt_data description into a packed gate_desc. write_idt_entry() then copies that descriptor into the chosen slot. The implementation is intentionally boring, which is exactly what low-level descriptor code should be. It copies the offset fragments, selector, bitfields, and clears the reserved word.

/* arch/x86/include/asm/desc.h */
static inline void idt_init_desc(gate_desc *gate, const struct idt_data *d)
{
    unsigned long addr = (unsigned long)d->addr;

    gate->offset_low    = (u16)addr;
    gate->segment       = (u16)d->segment;
    gate->bits          = d->bits;
    gate->offset_middle = (u16)(addr >> 16);
#ifdef CONFIG_X86_64
    gate->offset_high   = (u32)(addr >> 32);
    gate->reserved      = 0;
#endif
}

static inline void native_write_idt_entry(gate_desc *idt, int entry,
                                          const gate_desc *gate)
{
    memcpy(&idt[entry], gate, sizeof(*gate));
}

The CPU Entry Area and KPTI Safety

Since Linux 4.15, x86 has used the per-CPU cpu_entry_area to make entry-time objects reliably mapped and protected even under Kernel Page Table Isolation. The IDT, GDT, TSS, entry stack, and IST stacks live in or are aliased through this per-CPU region. The code in arch/x86/include/asm/cpu_entry_area.h emphasizes that these are virtual aliases of backing storage rather than a naively allocated per-CPU struct. That design lets Linux keep the entry structures mapped in both the user and kernel page-table variants needed for KPTI transitions.

/* arch/x86/include/asm/cpu_entry_area.h */
struct cpu_entry_area {
    char gdt[PAGE_SIZE];
    struct entry_stack_page entry_stack_page;
    struct tss_struct tss;
#ifdef CONFIG_X86_64
    struct cea_exception_stacks estacks;
#endif
    struct debug_store cpu_debug_store;
    struct debug_store_buffers cpu_debug_buffers;
};

The practical consequence is important. Entry code cannot depend on arbitrary kernel virtual addresses being mapped while coming from user mode under KPTI. It can, however, depend on the CPU entry area. That is why the IDT table in idt.c is declared __page_aligned_bss and why the entry stacks are laid out with guard pages in the CPU entry area mapping. The hardening story and the entry-performance story meet in the same data structure.

The IDT's relationship with the entry area goes one step further than mere mapping, and the reason is stated plainly in the source. Once every gate has been installed, idt_setup_apic_and_irq_gates() does not simply leave the table where the linker put it:

/* arch/x86/kernel/idt.c */
static gate_desc idt_table[IDT_ENTRIES] __page_aligned_bss;

static void __init idt_map_in_cea(void)
{
    /*
     * Set the IDT descriptor to a fixed read-only location in the cpu
     * entry area, so that the "sidt" instruction will not leak the
     * location of the kernel, and to defend the IDT against arbitrary
     * memory write vulnerabilities.
     */
    cea_set_pte(CPU_ENTRY_AREA_RO_IDT_VADDR, __pa_symbol(idt_table),
                PAGE_KERNEL_RO);
    idt_descr.address = CPU_ENTRY_AREA_RO_IDT;
}

/* ... at the end of idt_setup_apic_and_irq_gates(): */
idt_map_in_cea();
load_idt(&idt_descr);

/* Make the IDT table read only */
set_memory_ro((unsigned long)&idt_table, 1);

Two distinct defences are being erected here, and it is worth separating them.

The first is an information leak. SIDT is an unprivileged instruction: any user-mode program can execute it and read back the IDT base address. If that address were the ordinary kernel-image address of idt_table, SIDT would hand every process the kernel's load address and defeat KASLR in one instruction. By re-pointing IDTR at CPU_ENTRY_AREA_RO_IDT, a fixed virtual address that is the same on every boot, the kernel makes the leak worthless. The same reasoning is why the GDT lives in the entry area — SGDT is equally unprivileged.

The second is write protection. An attacker who obtains an arbitrary kernel write and can reach the IDT does not need any further primitive: overwriting one gate's offset field redirects an exception straight into attacker-chosen code, at ring 0, with the CPU having already done the privilege transition. The set_memory_ro() call makes the backing page read-only in the kernel's own mapping, and the entry-area alias is created with PAGE_KERNEL_RO, so neither view of the page is writable after boot. This is also why the IDT must be page aligned: set_memory_ro() operates on whole pages, so an IDT sharing a page with mutable data could not be protected without freezing that data too. The 4,096-byte size of the x86_64 IDT is exactly one page, which makes the arithmetic come out perfectly.

The idt_setup_done flag mentioned earlier now makes sense as well. It is not bookkeeping for its own sake — after this point the table is genuinely immutable, so an attempt to install a late vector would fault rather than quietly fail, and the WARN_ON() in idt_install_sysvec() exists to catch that programming error at the source.

Interrupt Stack Table Usage

The Interrupt Stack Table is part of the x86 TSS. Each nonzero IST field in an IDT gate tells the CPU to load a preconfigured stack pointer before transferring control. Linux uses this for events where continuing on the current stack is too risky: NMI, double fault, debug, machine check, and, when configured, VMM communication exceptions. The cpu_entry_area.h definitions show that Linux allocates dedicated EXCEPTION_STKSZ-sized stacks plus guard pages for each IST slot.

  • IST_INDEX_DF protects double-fault entry from stack-corruption cascades.
  • IST_INDEX_NMI isolates NMIs from whatever context they interrupt, including other entry code.
  • IST_INDEX_DB reduces recursion risk for debug exceptions and hardware breakpoints.
  • IST_INDEX_MCE keeps machine-check handling off potentially corrupted ordinary stacks.

Hardware stack switching is simple in concept. If the gate's IST field is nonzero, the CPU loads the corresponding pointer from the TSS and then pushes the interrupt frame on that stack. Linux later returns to the interrupted context by unwinding the frame and executing iretq. The subtlety lies not in the switch itself but in proving that every nested and exceptional path has enough stack space, correct guard pages, and consistent unwind metadata.

ARM64: The Exception Vector Table (IDT Equivalent)

ARM64 does not use an IDT or gate descriptors. Instead, it uses an exception vector table — a fixed-layout table of branch instructions pointed to by the VBAR_EL1 system register. The table contains 16 entries organized as a 4 × 4 matrix: four exception sources crossed with four exception types.

Each vector entry is exactly 128 bytes (32 instructions) wide. The hardware computes the target address by adding a fixed offset to the base address in VBAR_EL1:

Figure 4-9 shows the resulting table. Because every slot is a fixed 128 bytes, the hardware needs no lookup: it computes the target address arithmetically from VBAR_EL1 and the exception’s row and column.

Table 4-18. ARM64 vector table vs. x86_64 IDT comparison

Featurex86_64 IDTARM64 vector table
Location registerIDTR (LIDT instruction)VBAR_EL1 (MSR instruction)
Number of entries256 (one per vector number)16 (4 sources × 4 types)
Entry size16 bytes (gate descriptor)128 bytes (executable code)
Total size4,096 bytes2,048 bytes
ContentData structure (address + metadata)Executable instructions (branch stubs)
Handler addressEncoded in gate descriptor offset fieldsInline code jumps to full handler
Gate type / IF controlType field (0xE clears IF, 0xF leaves IF); Linux uses 0xE everywhereNo equivalent field — hardware masks all four DAIF bits on every entry
Stack switchingIST field selects TSS stack slotSP_EL1 selected automatically; no IST equivalent
Privilege checkDPL field for user-invocable gatesException level transition is implicit
Vector identificationVector number selects IDT entry directlySynchronous: ESR_EL1.EC; IRQ: read ICC_IAR1_EL1

The key architectural difference is that x86_64 encodes handler metadata in data descriptors (the IDT entries), while ARM64 places executable code directly in the vector table. Each 128-byte slot is large enough for a minimal assembly stub that saves registers, identifies the exception type, and branches to the appropriate C handler. The full ARM64 vector table is defined at SYM_CODE_START(vectors) in arch/arm64/kernel/entry.S, with each slot using the kernel_ventry macro to generate the entry stub.

It is worth asking what those thirty-two instructions actually do, because a naive answer — "branch to the handler" — would leave most of the slot empty. The macro spends its budget on two jobs that must happen before any C code, or indeed any register save, can run:

/* arch/arm64/kernel/entry.S */
    .macro kernel_ventry, el:req, ht:req, regsize:req, label:req
    .align 7                                /* force 128-byte alignment */
    ...
    sub sp, sp, #PT_REGS_SIZE
    /*
     * Test whether the SP has overflowed, without corrupting a GPR.
     * Task and IRQ stacks are aligned so that SP & (1 << THREAD_SHIFT)
     * should always be zero.
     */
    add sp, sp, x0          // sp' = sp + x0
    sub x0, sp, x0          // x0' = sp' - x0 = sp
    tbnz    x0, #THREAD_SHIFT, 0f
    sub x0, sp, x0          // x0'' = x0
    sub sp, sp, x0          // sp'' = sp
    b   el\el\ht\()_\regsize\()_\label

The first job is the .align 7 directive, which is what actually enforces the 128-byte spacing the architecture requires; the assembler pads each stub out to the boundary with the next stub.

The second is stack-overflow detection, and the three-instruction dance above rewards a moment's study. The problem is that the check needs a scratch register, but nothing has been saved yet — clobbering any general-purpose register would destroy interrupted state. The macro solves this with the classic addition-based swap: add sp, sp, x0 followed by sub x0, sp, x0 leaves the old SP in x0 and a mangled value in SP, with no third register involved. Because task and IRQ stacks are aligned to THREAD_SIZE, a healthy stack pointer always has bit THREAD_SHIFT clear, so a single tbnz decides the question. Two more subtractions unwind the swap and the stub falls through to its handler. On the failure path, execution has already left the boundaries of the current stack, so the kernel switches to a dedicated per-CPU overflow_stack and heads for __bad_stack — which is how a kernel stack overflow on ARM64 becomes a clean panic with a backtrace rather than silent corruption of whatever lay below the stack. This has no x86_64 counterpart in the entry stubs; on x86_64 the equivalent protection comes from unmapped guard pages plus the double-fault IST entry described earlier.

On ARM64, the hardware does not clear or set interrupt masks based on a gate descriptor. It does something simpler and, from Linux's point of view, more convenient: taking any exception to EL1 sets all four DAIF bits. Debug (D), SError (A), IRQ (I), and FIQ (F) are all masked on entry, regardless of which of the sixteen vectors was taken and regardless of what was masked before.

This is not a per-exception-type rule, and it is easy to assume otherwise — that an IRQ entry masks only IRQs, an FIQ entry only FIQs, and so on. The kernel source settles the question. DAIF_MASK in arch/arm64/include/asm/daifflags.h is defined as PSR_D_BIT | PSR_A_BIT | PSR_I_BIT | PSR_F_BIT, all four together, and the helper that undoes the hardware's work is documented as restoring "the DAIF bits that were modified by taking an exception":

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/daifflags.h */
#define DAIF_PROCCTX        0
#define DAIF_PROCCTX_NOIRQ  (PSR_I_BIT | PSR_F_BIT)
#define DAIF_ERRCTX         (PSR_A_BIT | PSR_I_BIT | PSR_F_BIT)
#define DAIF_MASK           (PSR_D_BIT | PSR_A_BIT | PSR_I_BIT | PSR_F_BIT)

/*
 * Called by synchronous exception handlers to restore the DAIF bits that were
 * modified by taking an exception.
 */
static inline void local_daif_inherit(struct pt_regs *regs)
{
    unsigned long flags = regs->pstate & DAIF_MASK;
    ...
    write_sysreg(flags, daif);
}

The two helpers correspond to the two situations a handler can be in. A handler that interrupted user mode knows the previous state was "everything enabled", so it calls local_daif_restore(DAIF_PROCCTX) — and DAIF_PROCCTX is simply 0, meaning nothing masked. That constant being zero is itself the proof: if a synchronous exception from EL0 had left IRQ and FIQ untouched, there would be nothing for the I and F bits of this call to do. A handler that interrupted kernel mode cannot make that assumption, because the interrupted code may deliberately have had interrupts off, so it calls local_daif_inherit(regs) to put back exactly what SPSR_EL1 recorded.

The comparison with x86_64 is therefore closer than the two architectures' vocabularies suggest. Linux on x86_64 chooses interrupt gates over trap gates so that every entry begins with interrupts masked; ARM64 gives the kernel that guarantee unconditionally, with no descriptor field to get wrong. In both cases the entry stub runs with maskable interrupts off until well-audited C code decides to re-enable them — cond_local_irq_enable(regs) on one side, local_daif_restore() or local_daif_inherit() on the other. The remaining ARM64-specific subtlety is that when pseudo-NMIs are configured, "masking IRQs" is implemented by writing ICC_PMR_EL1 rather than by setting PSTATE.I, which is why local_daif_inherit() restores regs->pmr alongside the DAIF bits and why the constant GICV3_PRIO_PSR_I_SET exists to record which of the two mechanisms was in force.

A Note on FRED

Everything described in this section — gate descriptors, the IST, the read-only alias in the CPU entry area — describes x86_64's classic event-delivery mechanism, and that mechanism now has a successor. FRED (Flexible Return and Event Delivery) is an Intel architectural extension that replaces IDT-based delivery outright, and because the rest of this chapter refers to it in passing, it is worth introducing here.

The motivation is that the IDT path accumulated a great deal of historical baggage. A classic entry may or may not switch stacks, may or may not have a usable GS base, and leaves the handler to work out which by inspecting the saved CS; IRET, for its part, has the notorious property of unblocking NMIs at the wrong moment, which is the root cause of the NMI-nesting machinery covered later. FRED addresses this by having the CPU establish a complete, well-defined supervisor context on entry: the stack level is chosen by the hardware from an explicit table rather than by the descriptor's IST index, GS is switched by the CPU rather than by a SWAPGS the entry stub has to reason about, and the event type and vector arrive in a structured stack frame instead of being implied by which gate was taken. Return is performed by two new instructions, ERETS (return to supervisor) and ERETU (return to user), which restore the corresponding state without IRET's side effects.

In Linux 7.0.12 the two worlds coexist. The FRED entry path lives in arch/x86/entry/entry_64_fred.S and arch/x86/entry/entry_fred.c, guarded by CONFIG_X86_FRED and enabled at runtime only when the CPU advertises the feature; the sysvec_install() macro seen earlier is one of the seams between them, dispatching to fred_install_sysvec() or idt_install_sysvec() depending on which mechanism is active. The handlers themselves are shared — the DEFINE_IDTENTRY* macros generate bodies usable from either path — so the divergence is confined to entry and exit. This chapter continues to describe the IDT path, which remains the one in use on the overwhelming majority of deployed hardware, and notes FRED's differences where they matter.

Hardware Handling of Interrupts and Exceptions

We now describe how the CPU control unit handles interrupts and exceptions at the hardware level. We assume that the kernel has been initialized and that the CPU is operating in its normal execution mode — long mode on x86_64 or EL1 (kernel) / EL0 (user) on ARM64. Understanding this hardware choreography is essential because every software handler, every C function that deals with an interrupt or exception, is built on top of the guarantees provided by the hardware entry sequence. The CPU performs a surprisingly large amount of work in microcode or hardwired logic before any software instruction of the handler runs.

The General Model

After the control unit executes an instruction, and before it fetches the next one, it checks whether an interrupt or exception occurred during (or as a result of) the instruction just executed. If nothing happened, it proceeds to the next instruction normally. If an event did occur, the control unit suspends the normal instruction fetch and begins a deterministic hardware sequence.

The precise steps differ between x86_64 and ARM64, but every architecture must solve the same set of problems:

  1. Identify the event — Determine which interrupt or exception fired. On x86_64 this means reading a vector number (0–255) from the exception logic or interrupt controller. On ARM64 this means classifying the exception type (Synchronous, IRQ, FIQ, or SError) and combining it with the originating exception level and stack pointer selection to compute an offset into the vector table.
  2. Look up the handler — Use the event identity to index into a table that maps events to handler addresses. On x86_64 this table is the IDT (Interrupt Descriptor Table), indexed by vector number. On ARM64 this table is the Exception Vector Table pointed to by VBAR_EL1, organized as a 4 × 4 grid (exception type × source exception level).
  3. Check privilege and access rights — Ensure the event source is authorized. On x86_64 the CPU compares the CPL with the gate DPL and target segment DPL, preventing user code from using software interrupts to reach kernel-only gates. On ARM64 privilege checking is implicit in the exception level transitions: EL0 → EL1 is always allowed for any exception, and the hardware does not need a per-entry DPL because there are no software-invoked exception vectors equivalent to INT n.
  4. Switch stacks if necessary — If the interrupted context was running at a lower privilege, the handler must not use the interrupted context’s stack (which could be corrupted or user-controlled). On x86_64 the CPU loads a fresh RSP from the TSS (RSP0) or from an IST slot. On ARM64 the CPU selects SP_EL1 as the stack pointer when entering EL1, leaving the user-mode SP_EL0 untouched.
  5. Save enough state for later resumption — The hardware must save at least the program counter, processor status flags, and (on x86_64) the code segment and stack pointer of the interrupted context. On x86_64, the CPU pushes SS, RSP, RFLAGS, CS, RIP, and an optional error code onto the kernel stack. On ARM64, the CPU saves the return address in ELR_EL1 and the processor state in SPSR_EL1 — both are special-purpose registers, not stack locations.
  6. Transfer control to the handler — Jump to the handler’s first instruction. On x86_64, the CPU loads CS:RIP from the IDT gate descriptor. On ARM64, the CPU branches to the computed vector table offset relative to VBAR_EL1.

The entire sequence happens atomically from the software’s point of view — no instruction of the handler can execute until all six conceptual steps are complete. This guarantee is critical: it means the handler can rely on having a valid stack, knowing its privilege level, and finding the interrupted state already saved.

Why This Matters for Software

The kernel’s entry code depends on the hardware invariants above. For example:

  • The stack switch guarantee means the kernel never needs to check “am I on a valid stack?” at the very first instruction of the handler — the hardware already ensured it.
  • The state-saving guarantee means the handler can immediately use the stack (x86_64) or system registers (ARM64) to inspect the interrupted context.
  • The privilege check guarantee means the kernel does not need to add its own CPL validation at the start of every handler.

If any of these invariants were violated, the handler would face a chicken-and-egg problem: it would need to perform setup work (switch stacks, save state) before it could safely perform that same setup work. The hardware breaks this bootstrapping cycle.

Faults, Traps, and Aborts: When Is the Saved PC Meaningful?

The saved program counter (RIP on x86_64, ELR_EL1 on ARM64) has different semantics depending on the exception class:

  • Faults — The saved PC points to the instruction that caused the fault. The intent is that the handler can fix the condition (e.g., demand-page a missing page) and restart the faulting instruction. If the handler cannot fix it, it delivers a signal or terminates the process.
  • Traps — The saved PC points to the instruction after the trapping one. Traps are reported after the instruction completes (e.g., INT3 breakpoint, single-step). Restarting from the saved PC continues execution normally.
  • Aborts — The saved PC may not be meaningful at all. Aborts indicate severe, often unrecoverable conditions (machine check, double fault). The hardware saves a “best effort” PC, but the handler typically panics rather than attempting to resume.

The extra row for BRK is not pedantry, and it is a genuine trap for anyone carrying x86 intuitions across to ARM64. The two architectures' software-breakpoint instructions look like counterparts — both are one-instruction traps planted by debuggers, BUG(), and kprobes — but they differ in exactly the way that matters. x86's INT3 is a true trap: the CPU saves the address after it, which is why the earlier discussion noted that a handler treating that address as fault-style would loop forever. ARM64's BRK does the opposite: ELR_EL1 holds the address of the BRK, so a handler that simply returns re-executes it and loops. Every kernel BRK handler that intends to continue therefore steps over the instruction by hand:

/* arch/arm64/kernel/traps.c: bug_brk_handler() */
case BUG_TRAP_TYPE_WARN:
    break;
...
/* If thread survives, skip over the BUG instruction and continue: */
arm64_skip_faulting_instruction(regs, AARCH64_INSN_SIZE);
return DBG_HOOK_HANDLED;

This is why a WARN_ON() on ARM64 resumes correctly: the handler advanced ELR_EL1 by four bytes on its way out. The same call appears in the CFI, kprobe, and kernel-text-patching BRK handlers.

This distinction matters because the kernel’s exception handlers must know whether to restart the faulting instruction (faults) or continue from the next one (traps). A page-fault handler that treated the saved RIP as a trap-style “next instruction” address would skip the faulting instruction entirely, leading to silent data corruption. Conversely, a breakpoint handler that treated the saved RIP as a fault-style “faulting instruction” address would re-execute INT3 in an infinite loop.

Table 4-19. Exception classes and saved program counter semantics

ClassSaved PC points toResume behaviorx86_64 examplesARM64 examples
FaultFaulting instructionRe-execute after handler fixes condition#PF, #GP, #NM, #SSData Abort, Instruction Abort, Alignment Fault
TrapNext instructionContinue normallyINT3, #DB (single-step), INTOSVC, Software Step
Fault-like breakpointThe breakpoint instruction itselfHandler must advance the PC explicitly—BRK
AbortImprecise / best effortTypically panic#DF (Double Fault), #MC (Machine Check)SError (asynchronous external abort)

Interrupt Acknowledgment and Vector Delivery

For external hardware interrupts (as opposed to exceptions caused by the CPU itself), there is an additional step that logically precedes the entry sequence: the CPU must acknowledge the interrupt and receive a vector number from the interrupt controller.

On x86_64, the Local APIC delivers the vector number to the CPU core. The CPU reads the highest-priority pending vector from the APIC’s interrupt request register and uses it to index the IDT. The APIC also sets the corresponding bit in the In-Service Register (ISR), blocking further interrupts at the same or lower priority until the handler sends an End-of-Interrupt (EOI) signal. If the CPU has RFLAGS.IF cleared (interrupts masked), the APIC holds the interrupt pending until IF is set again.

On ARM64, the GICv3 CPU interface delivers the interrupt via a read of the ICC_IAR1_EL1 (Interrupt Acknowledge Register) system register — but this read is performed by software, not by hardware during the entry sequence. The hardware only sees a generic “IRQ” or “FIQ” signal asserted by the GIC. The vector table entry for IRQ then runs the software handler, which reads ICC_IAR1_EL1 to learn the interrupt ID and simultaneously acknowledge it. This is a fundamental architectural difference: on x86_64 the vector is known before the first handler instruction runs; on ARM64 the interrupt ID is discovered by the handler itself.

Table 4-20. Interrupt acknowledgment: x86_64 vs ARM64

Aspectx86_64 (APIC)ARM64 (GICv3)
When vector/ID is knownBefore first handler instructionAfter software reads ICC_IAR1_EL1
Who acknowledgesHardware (auto-ACK via IDT lookup)Software (register read)
Priority blockingISR bit set in APIC automaticallyRunning priority raised on ACK; readable (not writable) via ICC_RPR_EL1
EOI mechanismWrite to the APIC EOI register: MMIO offset 0xB0 under xAPIC, MSR 0x80B under x2APICWrite to ICC_EOIR1_EL1
Masking during handlerRFLAGS.IF = 0 (interrupt gate)All four DAIF bits masked by the hardware on entry

With this general model in mind, we now describe the precise hardware entry sequence for each architecture.

x86_64 Hardware Entry Sequence

When x86_64 detects an interrupt or exception, the control unit performs the following steps:

  1. Determines the vector i (0 ≤ i ≤ 255) associated with the interrupt or exception. For exceptions, the vector is architecturally defined (e.g., vector 14 for #PF). For external interrupts, the vector comes from the interrupt controller (Local APIC delivers the vector via the system bus or MSR interface).
  2. Reads the ith entry of the IDT referred to by the IDTR register. The entry must contain an interrupt gate or trap gate descriptor (task gates are not available in long mode).
  3. Validates the gate descriptor. The processor checks that the P (present) bit is set. If it is clear, the CPU raises #NP (Segment Not Present, vector 11) — not #GP, which is the response to a descriptor of the wrong type (a call gate, or a task gate in long mode) or to a privilege violation. The error code identifies the offending vector and has the IDT bit set, so the handler can tell an IDT-sourced #NP from one caused by an ordinary segment load. This is the architectural reason vector 11 remains meaningful on x86_64 even though segmentation is otherwise vestigial, as noted in its description earlier in this chapter.
  4. Performs privilege checks. The processor compares the Current Privilege Level (CPL), stored in the two least significant bits of the CS register, with the gate’s DPL:
    • For hardware-generated interrupts and exceptions: CPL may be any value; the check is only that the target segment DPL ≤ CPL.
    • For software-generated interrupts (INT n, INT3, INTO): the gate DPL must be ≥ CPL, otherwise the CPU raises #GP. This prevents user-mode code from invoking kernel-only gates. This is why Linux sets DPL=3 for the breakpoint gate (INT3) and the legacy int $0x80 system-call gate, but DPL=0 for all other entries.
  5. Checks whether a privilege-level change is occurring — that is, if CPL differs from the target code segment’s DPL. If so, the CPU must switch to the stack associated with the new privilege level. (Note that it is the stack switch that is conditional, not the saving of SS:RSP — see step 7.)
    • Reads the TR register to locate the TSS of the running task.
    • Loads SS and RSP with the stack segment and stack pointer for the new privilege level (RSP0 in the TSS for transitions to ring 0).
    • On the new stack, saves the previous values of SS and RSP.
    • If the gate has a nonzero IST field (1–7), the CPU ignores the TSS ring-0 stack and instead loads RSP from the corresponding IST slot. This provides a known-good stack even when the kernel stack may be corrupted (used for NMI, #DF, #DB, #MC).
  6. If a fault has occurred, loads CS and RIP with the address of the faulting instruction so that it can be re-executed after the handler repairs the condition. For traps, RIP points to the instruction after the trapping one.
  7. Saves the interrupted context on the (new) stack by pushing, in order: SS, RSP, RFLAGS, CS, RIP, and error code (if the exception architecturally defines one — e.g., #GP, #PF, #DF, #TS, #NP, #SS, #AC, #CP).

    The unconditional push of SS and RSP is a 64-bit-mode change that is easy to miss, because 32-bit protected mode pushed them only when the privilege level actually changed. In long mode the five-slot frame is always the same shape, whether the interrupt arrived from ring 3, from ring 0, or on an IST stack. The kernel relies on this: struct pt_regs declares sp and ss unconditionally, entry code in arch/x86/entry/entry_64.S notes that "in 64-bit mode SS:RSP on the exception stack is always valid", and the unwinder can therefore find the interrupted stack pointer without first deciding which ring it came from. The CPU also aligns RSP down to a 16-byte boundary before pushing, so the frame is always suitably aligned for the ABI.

  8. If the gate is an interrupt gate (Type = 0xE), clears the IF flag in RFLAGS, disabling further maskable interrupts. If it is a trap gate (Type = 0xF), IF is left unchanged. Linux uses interrupt gates for all IDT entries, so maskable interrupts are always disabled on entry.
  9. Loads CS and RIP with the Segment Selector and Offset fields from the gate descriptor. These define the address of the first instruction of the interrupt or exception handler. Execution begins there.

Table 4-21. Summary of x86_64 hardware entry steps

StepActionKey register/field
1Determine vector iInterrupt controller or CPU exception logic
2Read IDT[i]IDTR base + i × 16
3Validate descriptorGate P bit (clear → #NP)
4Check privilegeCPL vs gate DPL; CPL vs segment DPL
5Switch stack if neededTSS RSP0 or IST[1–7]
6Adjust RIP for fault/trapFault: faulting instruction; Trap: next instruction
7Push interrupted contextSS, RSP, RFLAGS, CS, RIP, [error code]
8Clear IF if interrupt gateRFLAGS.IF ← 0
9Load handler addressGate offset → RIP; gate selector → CS

The last step is equivalent to a jump to the interrupt or exception handler. The instruction processed by the control unit after this sequence is the first instruction of the selected handler.

Software Completion of the Entry Frame

After the hardware pushes the architectural frame, the software entry stub completes the picture.

Figure 4-10 shows the completed frame. The colour coding matters: the blue registers were pushed by the processor before the first instruction of the handler ran, while the green ones were pushed by the entry stub afterwards. Only once both halves are present does the frame become a valid struct pt_regs.

One slot deserves separate comment, because the diagram would otherwise mislead. The architectural error code and the kernel's orig_ax field are not two slots but one. Where the hardware pushed an error code, that word is orig_ax; where it did not, the entry stub pushes something itself so that the frame is the same shape either way. Which value it pushes depends on the entry type: -1 for an exception with no error code, the bitwise complement of the vector number for a device interrupt, and the system-call number for a syscall entry. The kernel then reads the error code out and immediately overwrites the slot:

/* arch/x86/entry/entry_64.S: idtentry_body */
.if \has_error_code == 1
    movq    ORIG_RAX(%rsp), %rsi    /* get error code into 2nd argument */
    movq    $-1, ORIG_RAX(%rsp)     /* no syscall to restart */
.endif

The -1 written back is not tidiness. On the way out of the kernel, the signal and restart machinery inspects orig_ax to decide whether the interrupted operation was a system call that should be restarted; leaving a stale error code there would make an exception look like a syscall. Storing -1 is how the entry path says "there is nothing to restart".

The software entry stub then pushes the remaining general-purpose registers until the stack layout matches struct pt_regs. That step is Linux-specific, not hardware-defined. It is what makes later C code architecture-neutral within x86. Every trap handler receives a struct pt_regs * and can reason about the interrupted state through fields such as ip, sp, flags, and orig_ax.

/* arch/x86/include/asm/ptrace.h: x86_64 excerpt */
struct pt_regs {
    unsigned long r15, r14, r13, r12, bp, bx;
    unsigned long r11, r10, r9, r8;
    unsigned long ax, cx, dx, si, di;
    unsigned long orig_ax;
    unsigned long ip;
    union { u16 cs; u64 csx; };
    unsigned long flags;
    unsigned long sp;
    union { u16 ss; u64 ssx; };
};

Table 4-22. Key x86_64 pt_regs fields relevant to interrupt entry

Field Who provides it Meaning
r15..di software stub saved general registers for later inspection or restoration
orig_ax software convention syscall number, error code, or vector depending on entry type
ip hardware saved instruction pointer (RIP)
cs hardware saved code-segment selector
flags hardware saved RFLAGS
sp hardware (always, in 64-bit mode) saved stack pointer for return
ss hardware (always, in 64-bit mode) saved stack segment selector

Privilege Checks and TSS Stack Switching

The TSS no longer performs hardware task switching for ordinary process context switches. Nevertheless it remains vital on x86_64 because it stores the ring-0 stack pointer and the IST pointers. When an event arrives from user mode, the processor loads the privileged stack from the TSS before it pushes the return frame. This guarantees that entry does not trust a user-controlled stack pointer. That single mechanism is one of the oldest but still most important security properties of x86 privilege transitions.

The IST mechanism extends the same idea to kernel-originated emergencies. A double fault taken while the ordinary kernel stack is corrupted would be impossible to handle if the CPU kept using that stack. By redirecting #DF to a known-good IST stack, Linux gets one last chance to print diagnostics and panic in an orderly way. The same logic applies to NMI and machine check.

Returning with IRETQ

After the interrupt or exception is processed, the corresponding handler must relinquish control to the interrupted process by executing the IRETQ instruction. This forces the control unit to perform the reverse of the entry sequence:

  1. Pops RIP, CS, and RFLAGS from the stack, restoring the interrupted instruction pointer, code segment, and processor flags. If a hardware error code was pushed during entry, software must pop it before executing IRETQ — the hardware does not remove it automatically.
  2. Pops RSP and SS from the stack. In 64-bit mode this happens unconditionally, mirroring the unconditional push on entry; the 32-bit behaviour of popping them only on a privilege change does not apply.
  3. Checks whether a privilege-level change is occurring by comparing the CPL of the handler with the RPL in the restored CS, and completes the transition accordingly.
  4. If the return was to a less privileged level, nulls any of DS, ES, FS, and GS that hold selectors for non-conforming segments whose DPL is lower than the new CPL. This prevents user-mode code from retaining kernel segment register values. Returns that stay at the same privilege level skip this step entirely.

Table 4-23. x86_64 IRETQ sequence

StepActionCondition
1Pop RIP, CS, RFLAGSAlways
2Pop RSP, SSAlways in 64-bit mode (unlike 32-bit protected mode)
3Check privilege changeCompare handler CPL with restored CS RPL
4Null stale segment registersOnly on return to less privilege; segments with DPL < new CPL are nulled

Architecturally, IRETQ is the exact inverse of the entry frame push sequence — which is precisely why both halves are unconditional in long mode. Practically, Linux surrounds it with additional software work: speculation-mitigation barriers (IBRS/STIBP management), SWAPGS discipline (to restore the user-mode GS base), tracing hooks, and pending-thread-flag processing.

The important mental model is that IRETQ itself does not decide whether rescheduling, signal delivery, uprobe notifications, or livepatch state updates are pending. Linux decides those in irqentry_exit() and the generic exit-to-user helpers before the final assembly return executes. The hardware return instruction is only the final commit step.

Interrupt, Trap, and System Gates in Linux 7.0.12

Linux uses its own terminology and breakdown when classifying the gate descriptors it installs in the IDT. The four gate-construction macros were introduced earlier, in Gate Descriptor Format; Table 4-24 summarizes what each one produces.

Table 4-24. Linux gate classification in kernel 7.0.12

Linux nameMacroIntel gate typeDPLISTUsage
Interrupt gateINTG(vector, addr)Interrupt gate (0xE)0default (none)All standard exception and interrupt handlers
System interrupt gateSYSG(vector, addr)Interrupt gate (0xE)3default (none)The three user-invocable entries: INT3 breakpoint (vector 3), INTO overflow (vector 4), and the legacy int $0x80 system call (vector 128)
IST interrupt gateISTG(vector, addr, ist)Interrupt gate (0xE)0IST slot 1–7Critical exceptions needing a known-good stack: NMI, #DF, #DB, #MC, #VC
Task gateTSKG(vector, gdt)Task gate (0x5)0N/ANot used on x86_64 (long mode does not support hardware task switching)

Note the key difference from Linux 2.6: the old kernel used both interrupt gates and trap gates, with separate set_trap_gate() and set_intr_gate() helpers. Linux 7.0.12 uses interrupt gates exclusively: every macro that can actually install an entry on x86_64 — INTG, SYSG, and ISTG — hardcodes GATE_INTERRUPT. TSKG is the exception that proves the rule: it is the only macro that produces a different gate type, and it is unreachable on x86_64 because long mode has no hardware task switch. Within the three usable macros, only two fields vary: the DPL (DPL0 for kernel-only gates, DPL3 for the three user-invocable ones) and the IST index (which selects whether a dedicated stack is used).

This simplification means Linux no longer relies on the hardware IF-preservation behavior of trap gates. Instead, entry code explicitly manages when to re-enable interrupts using cond_local_irq_enable(regs) after the entry invariants are established.

ARM64 Hardware Entry Sequence

ARM64 uses a different vocabulary but a similar conceptual flow. When an exception occurs (synchronous, IRQ, FIQ, or SError), the processor performs these hardware steps:

  1. Saves the return address in ELR_EL1 (Exception Link Register). For asynchronous exceptions (IRQ, FIQ, SError), this is the address of the first instruction that was not executed because of the interrupt. For synchronous exceptions the architecture defines a preferred exception return address per exception class, and it is not uniform: aborts and BRK report the address of the instruction that generated the exception, whereas SVC and software step report the address of the following one. This is the ARM64 spelling of the fault/trap distinction in Table 4-19, and the reason kernel handlers that mean to continue past a BRK must call arm64_skip_faulting_instruction().
  2. Saves the interrupted processor state in SPSR_EL1 (Saved Program Status Register). This captures the PSTATE fields: condition flags (NZCV), interrupt masks (DAIF), execution state (AArch64/AArch32), exception level, stack pointer selection, and other control bits.
  3. Updates PSTATE to the exception-level context:
    • Sets the exception level to EL1 (if coming from EL0)
    • Selects SP_EL1 as the stack pointer
    • Masks all four DAIF bits — debug, SError, IRQ, and FIQ — regardless of which exception was taken. This is the ARM64 counterpart of Linux's decision to use x86 interrupt gates everywhere, except that the architecture makes it unconditional rather than leaving it to a descriptor field.
    • Clears single-step and other debug state
  4. For synchronous exceptions and SErrors, records the cause in ESR_EL1 (Exception Syndrome Register). IRQ and FIQ leave it alone, because there is nothing to describe — the interrupt ID comes from the GIC instead. For SError the syndrome carries the RAS classification the kernel uses in do_serror() to decide between a recoverable notification and a panic. The EC (Exception Class) field identifies the specific type: data abort, instruction abort, SVC, breakpoint, watchpoint, FP trap, BTI violation, PAC failure, etc. For data and instruction aborts, the faulting address is recorded in FAR_EL1 (Fault Address Register).
  5. Computes the vector offset from VBAR_EL1 based on the source (current EL vs. lower EL, SP_EL0 vs. SP_ELx) and type (sync, IRQ, FIQ, SError), then branches to that address.

Table 4-25. ARM64 hardware entry — registers saved/set by the processor

RegisterDirectionContent
ELR_EL1SavedReturn address (faulting/interrupted PC)
SPSR_EL1SavedInterrupted PSTATE (flags, DAIF, EL, SP selection)
ESR_EL1Set (synchronous and SError; not IRQ/FIQ)Exception syndrome: EC class + ISS details
FAR_EL1Set (aborts only)Faulting virtual address
PSTATEUpdatedNew EL, SP, DAIF masks for exception context

Unlike x86_64, which pushes a frame of 5–6 values onto the stack, ARM64 saves the return state entirely in system registers. The hardware does not push anything onto the stack — that is the job of the software entry prologue.

The assembly prologue (kernel_entry macro in arch/arm64/kernel/entry.S) then:

  • Saves all 31 general-purpose registers (x0–x30) plus SP onto the kernel stack to form a struct pt_regs frame
  • Reads ELR_EL1 and SPSR_EL1 from system registers and stores them in the frame
  • For EL0-originated entries, moves the user stack pointer from SP_EL0 into the frame
  • Loads the current task pointer, disables single-step state as needed
  • When pseudo-NMI support is enabled, saves and adjusts ICC_PMR_EL1 so that priority masking behaves correctly during entry and exit

ARM64 Returning with ERET

The ARM64 equivalent of IRETQ is the ERET (Exception Return) instruction:

  1. Restores PSTATE from SPSR_EL1, including the exception level, DAIF mask bits, and condition flags. Because entry masked all four DAIF bits, this restore is what re-enables interrupts for the interrupted context — the same role IRETQ's reload of RFLAGS.IF plays on x86_64.
  2. Branches to the address in ELR_EL1, resuming execution at the interrupted point.
  3. If returning to EL0, the processor switches to SP_EL0 as the stack pointer.

Before executing ERET, Linux’s exit code restores general-purpose registers from the pt_regs frame on the stack, writes the saved PC back to ELR_EL1 and the saved PSTATE back to SPSR_EL1, and performs any necessary TIF flag checks, signal delivery, and rescheduling decisions.

Table 4-26. x86_64 vs ARM64 hardware entry/return comparison

Aspectx86_64ARM64
State saved by hardwareSS, RSP, RFLAGS, CS, RIP pushed to stack — all five unconditionally in 64-bit modeELR_EL1, SPSR_EL1 written to system registers
Error/syndrome infoError code pushed for some exceptionsESR_EL1 (sync), FAR_EL1 (aborts)
Stack switchTSS RSP0 or IST slotSP_EL1 used automatically at EL1
IF/interrupt maskingInterrupt gate clears RFLAGS.IF (Linux uses interrupt gates for every entry)Hardware masks all four DAIF bits on every entry
GP register saveSoftware pushes to form pt_regsSoftware pushes to form pt_regs
Return instructionIRETQ (pops from stack)ERET (reads ELR_EL1 + SPSR_EL1)
Privilege transitionRing 3 → Ring 0 (CPL change)EL0 → EL1 (exception level change)

Exception Handling

Most exceptions raised by the CPU are interpreted by Linux as error conditions. When one occurs, the kernel sends a signal to the process that caused the exception, notifying it of an anomalous condition. If, for instance, a process performs a division by zero, the CPU raises a “Divide error” exception (#DE, vector 0), and the corresponding exception handler sends a SIGFPE signal to the current process. The process then takes the necessary steps to recover (if it has installed a signal handler for SIGFPE) or, if no handler is installed, aborts.

There are important cases, however, where Linux deliberately exploits CPU exceptions to manage hardware resources more efficiently rather than treating them as errors:

  • Lazy FPU/SIMD state management — On older kernels the “Device not available” exception (#NM, vector 7) was used together with the TS flag in CR0 to defer loading the FPU/SSE/AVX registers until the process actually executed a floating-point instruction. In kernel 7.0.12 the eager FPU model is the default on x86_64 (the FPU state is always saved and restored at context switch), but #NM is still handled for edge cases such as late initialization and XFD (Extended Feature Disable) faults when a task first uses AMX tiles.
  • Demand paging — The “Page Fault” exception (#PF, vector 14 on x86; Data Abort / Instruction Abort on ARM64) is the engine behind demand paging, copy-on-write, memory-mapped files, and lazy allocation. A page fault does not necessarily indicate an error — it may simply mean the kernel has not yet allocated a physical page frame for a valid virtual address. The page fault handler is by far the most complex exception handler in the kernel (see the section “Page Fault Handling” later in this chapter, and Chapter 9 for full details).
  • Breakpoints and single-stepping — The “Debug” exception (#DB, vector 1) and “Breakpoint” exception (INT3, vector 3) are used by debuggers (ptrace, kprobes, kgdb, uprobes) to set software and hardware breakpoints. These are not errors but deliberate control-flow intercepts.

The Three-Step Exception Handler Structure

Exception handlers in Linux follow a standard structure, whether on x86_64 or ARM64:

  1. Save register state (assembly prologue) — The hardware pushes a partial frame (x86_64: SS, RSP, RFLAGS, CS, RIP, error code; ARM64: ELR_EL1, SPSR_EL1), and the software entry stub completes it by saving all general-purpose registers into a struct pt_regs on the kernel stack. On x86_64, the error_entry / idtentry macros handle this. On ARM64, the kernel_entry macro in entry.S saves x0–x30, sp, pc, and pstate.
  2. Handle the exception in C — The assembly stub calls the high-level C handler (e.g., exc_page_fault(), exc_divide_error(), do_mem_abort()). This handler determines whether the exception is recoverable, sends a signal if it came from user space, attempts exception-table fixup if it came from kernel space, or panics if the kernel itself is misbehaving.
  3. Return from the handler — The handler returns through the generic exit path (irqentry_exit() on x86_64, ret_to_user / ret_to_kernel on ARM64), which checks for pending work (rescheduling, signals, uprobe notifications) before executing the final hardware return instruction (IRETQ on x86_64, ERET on ARM64).

IDT Initialization: idt_setup_traps()

To handle exceptions, the IDT must be properly initialized before the kernel enables interrupts. In Linux 7.0.12, the function idt_setup_traps() in arch/x86/kernel/idt.c loads the default exception entries into the IDT. The table def_idts[] maps each exception vector to its assembly entry point using the gate macros described earlier:

/* arch/x86/kernel/idt.c */
static const __initconst struct idt_data def_idts[] = {
    INTG(X86_TRAP_DE,    asm_exc_divide_error),
    ISTG(X86_TRAP_NMI,   asm_exc_nmi, IST_INDEX_NMI),
    INTG(X86_TRAP_BR,    asm_exc_bounds),
    INTG(X86_TRAP_UD,    asm_exc_invalid_op),
    INTG(X86_TRAP_NM,    asm_exc_device_not_available),
    INTG(X86_TRAP_OLD_MF,asm_exc_coproc_segment_overrun),
    INTG(X86_TRAP_TS,    asm_exc_invalid_tss),
    INTG(X86_TRAP_NP,    asm_exc_segment_not_present),
    INTG(X86_TRAP_SS,    asm_exc_stack_segment),
    INTG(X86_TRAP_GP,    asm_exc_general_protection),
    INTG(X86_TRAP_SPURIOUS, asm_exc_spurious_interrupt_bug),
    INTG(X86_TRAP_MF,    asm_exc_coprocessor_error),
    INTG(X86_TRAP_AC,    asm_exc_alignment_check),
    INTG(X86_TRAP_XF,    asm_exc_simd_coprocessor_error),

#ifdef CONFIG_X86_32
    TSKG(X86_TRAP_DF,    GDT_ENTRY_DOUBLEFAULT_TSS),
#else
    ISTG(X86_TRAP_DF,    asm_exc_double_fault, IST_INDEX_DF),
#endif
    ISTG(X86_TRAP_DB,    asm_exc_debug, IST_INDEX_DB),

#ifdef CONFIG_X86_MCE
    ISTG(X86_TRAP_MC,    asm_exc_machine_check, IST_INDEX_MCE),
#endif

#ifdef CONFIG_X86_CET
    INTG(X86_TRAP_CP,    asm_exc_control_protection),
#endif

#ifdef CONFIG_AMD_MEM_ENCRYPT
    ISTG(X86_TRAP_VC,    asm_exc_vmm_communication, IST_INDEX_VC),
#endif

    SYSG(X86_TRAP_OF,    asm_exc_overflow),
};

The conditional compilation is worth preserving in your mental model of the table, because it means the contents of the IDT are not the same on every machine or in every build. Vectors 18 (#MC), 21 (#CP), and 29 (#VC) are present only when machine-check reporting, Intel CET, or AMD memory encryption support is configured in; on a kernel built without them the corresponding IDT slots stay at whatever idt_setup_early_handler() put there. The TSKG arm of the #DF conditional is the last survivor of the task-gate mechanism that 32-bit Linux used for double faults: on x86_64 it is compiled out entirely, and the double fault is handled on an IST stack instead.
