Compare this with the Linux 2.6 initialization which used set_trap_gate(), set_intr_gate(), set_system_gate(), and set_task_gate() as separate helper functions. In kernel 7.0.12, the unified INTG/SYSG/ISTG macros are data-driven: the table is iterated by idt_setup_traps() which calls idt_init_desc() for each entry. This is cleaner and easier to audit.

Note the key differences from Linux 2.6:

  • All gates are interrupt gates (GATE_INTERRUPT), not a mix of trap and interrupt gates. The IF flag is always cleared on entry; handlers re-enable interrupts explicitly when safe.
  • IST stacks are used for NMI, #DF, #DB, #MC, and #VC — events that may arrive on a corrupted stack.
  • SYSG (DPL=3) marks the three vectors that user code is allowed to raise deliberately with an INT instruction: #OF (vector 4, from def_idts[] above), INT3 (vector 3, installed earlier from early_idts[] by idt_setup_early_traps()), and the legacy int $0x80 system-call vector (installed from ia32_idt[]). Every other gate has DPL 0, so an INT n from ring 3 aimed at it raises #GP rather than entering the handler.
  • New exception vectors not present in 2.6: #CP (Control Protection, vector 21 — for CET shadow-stack and indirect-branch-tracking violations), #VC (VMM Communication, vector 29 — for AMD SEV-ES guests), and #VE (Virtualization Exception, vector 20 — for Intel TDX guests, installed from early_idts[]). All three are confidential-computing or control-flow-integrity features that simply had no analogue two decades ago.

Table 4-27. x86_64 exception vectors, handlers, and signals in Linux 7.0.12
