ARM64 Exception Initialization

ARM64 does not have a per-vector IDT. Instead, the exception vector table is a fixed-format 2 KB region (16 entries × 128 bytes each) whose base address is loaded into VBAR_EL1 during early boot. Each 128-byte slot contains a small sequence of instructions (typically a branch to the real handler), not a descriptor. The layout is:

vectors (from arch/arm64/kernel/entry.S):
  Offset 0x000: Synchronous, from EL1t (SP_EL0, rare)
  Offset 0x080: IRQ,          from EL1t
  Offset 0x100: FIQ,          from EL1t
  Offset 0x180: SError,       from EL1t
  Offset 0x200: Synchronous, from EL1h (SP_EL1, normal kernel)
  Offset 0x280: IRQ,          from EL1h
  Offset 0x300: FIQ,          from EL1h
  Offset 0x380: SError,       from EL1h
  Offset 0x400: Synchronous, from EL0 (64-bit user)
  Offset 0x480: IRQ,          from EL0 (64-bit user)
  Offset 0x500: FIQ,          from EL0 (64-bit user)
  Offset 0x580: SError,       from EL0 (64-bit user)
  Offset 0x600: Synchronous, from EL0 (32-bit user / compat)
  Offset 0x680: IRQ,          from EL0 (32-bit user / compat)
  Offset 0x700: FIQ,          from EL0 (32-bit user / compat)
  Offset 0x780: SError,       from EL0 (32-bit user / compat)

For synchronous exceptions, the dispatcher reads ESR_EL1 (Exception Syndrome Register) to determine the specific exception class. The EC (Exception Class) field provides a 6-bit code identifying the cause:

Table 4-28. Key ARM64 exception classes (ESR_EL1.EC values)

EC valueException classTypical handlerSignal
0x15SVC instruction (AArch64)el0_svc() → do_el0_svc()—
0x20Instruction Abort (lower EL)el0_ia() → do_mem_abort()SIGSEGV
0x21Instruction Abort (same EL)el1_abort() → do_mem_abort()—
0x24Data Abort (lower EL)el0_da() → do_mem_abort()SIGSEGV/SIGBUS
0x25Data Abort (same EL)el1_abort() → do_mem_abort()—
0x26SP Alignment Faultel0_sp()SIGBUS
0x22PC Alignment Faultel0_pc() / el1_pc()SIGBUS
0x00Unknownel0_undef() / el1_undef()SIGILL
0x07FP/Advanced SIMD access trapel0_fpsimd_acc()—
0x19SVE access trapel0_sve_acc()—
0x1DSME access trapel0_sme_acc()—
0x1CPointer-authentication failure (FPAC)el0_fpac() / el1_fpac()SIGILL
0x0DBranch Target Identification (BTI)el0_bti() / el1_bti()SIGILL
0x18Trapped MSR/MRS/system instructionel0_sys()SIGILL
0x27Memory Copy/Set (MOPS) exceptionel0_mops() / el1_mops()—
0x2DGuarded Control Stack (GCS)el0_gcs() / el1_gcs()SIGSEGV (SEGV_CPERR)
0x30Hardware breakpoint (lower EL)el0_breakpt()SIGTRAP
0x32Software Step (lower EL)el0_softstp()SIGTRAP
0x34Watchpoint (lower EL)el0_watchpt()SIGTRAP
0x3CBRK instruction (AArch64)el0_brk64() / el1_brk64()SIGTRAP
anything elseUnrecognised classel0_inv() / __panic_unhandled()SIGILL (EL0 only)

Two details of this table repay attention. First, the FP-family classes are genuinely distinct: 0x07 is raised when the task touches the base FP/ASIMD register file with CPACR_EL1.FPEN trapping, 0x19 when it touches the SVE registers, and 0x1D when it touches the SME state. Linux uses these three traps as the trigger for lazy FP-state allocation — the handler allocates the (large) SVE or SME save area on first use and then re-executes the trapping instruction, which is the ARM64 counterpart of the #NM-driven lazy-FPU switching that Linux 2.6 used on i386. Second, there is no single el0_dbg() catch-all: since the debug rework each debug exception class has its own noinstr handler, because breakpoints, single-step, watchpoints and BRK need different kprobe, uprobe and ptrace interactions.

Classes with no case at all — illegal execution state (0x0E), for instance — fall through to the switch statement’s default arm, and the two exception levels are treated very differently there. From EL0 the kernel calls el0_inv(), which reports a bad exception class and sends SIGILL; a malformed user program must not be able to bring the machine down. From EL1 the same situation calls __panic_unhandled(), because an exception class the kernel does not recognise, taken while the kernel itself was running, means the kernel’s model of the CPU is wrong and there is nothing safe left to do. This is the ARM64 equivalent of an x86 vector whose IDT gate was never filled in, and it also mirrors the SEGV_CPERR pairing above: note that GCS violations on ARM64 and #CP shadow-stack violations on x86_64 deliberately report the same si_code to user space.

Unlike x86, where each exception vector can have different gate types and privilege settings, ARM64 uses a uniform dispatch model: the hardware always branches to the same vector table entry for all synchronous exceptions from a given source level, and software (the el1h_64_sync_handler() or el0t_64_sync_handler() C function) performs the switch over the EC field.

The User-Mode vs. Kernel-Mode Decision

Every exception handler must first determine whether the exception occurred in user mode or kernel mode, because the consequences are very different:

  • User-mode exception — The handler typically sends a signal to the faulting process. The signal may be SIGFPE (arithmetic error), SIGILL (illegal instruction), SIGSEGV (memory access violation), SIGBUS (bus error or alignment fault), or SIGTRAP (debug trap). The process can catch the signal with a user-space handler, or it will be terminated with a core dump.
  • Kernel-mode exception — The handler must determine whether the fault was expected (an anticipated failure in user-copy code, MSR probing, etc.) or unexpected (a genuine kernel bug). For expected faults, the kernel uses exception tables (see fixup_exception() later in this section) to redirect execution to a recovery path. For unexpected faults, the handler calls die() on x86_64 or die_kernel_fault() on ARM64, which prints a register dump (the “kernel oops”), logs a stack trace, and either terminates the faulting task or panics the system if the fault occurred in an unrecoverable context such as an interrupt handler.

On x86_64, the check is performed by user_mode(regs), which tests bit 0 and 1 of the saved CS register (CPL 3 = user, CPL 0 = kernel). On ARM64 the equivalent macro tests the saved PSTATE that was copied out of SPSR_EL1 into the frame — ((regs->pstate & PSR_MODE_MASK) == PSR_MODE_EL0t) — so it is true only when the interrupted context was running at EL0 with SP_EL0 selected. Both macros read the saved frame rather than any live register, which is what makes them safe to call at any point in the handler, including after the handler has itself been preempted.

Saving the Registers for the Exception Handler

Before any C handler can run, the assembly entry code must save the complete register state of the interrupted context onto the kernel stack. This is the first step of the three-step handler structure described above, and it is worth examining in detail because the register-save layout directly determines the struct pt_regs that every C handler receives as its argument.

x86_64: The idtentry and error_entry Path

In Linux 2.6, each exception handler started with a hand-written assembly fragment that pushed a dummy error code (if the hardware did not provide one), pushed the address of the C handler, and jumped to a shared error_code label. Linux 7.0.12 replaces this with structured macros, but the conceptual flow is the same.

Each exception entry point is generated by the idtentry assembly macro in arch/x86/entry/entry_64.S. The macro performs these steps:

  1. Land the indirect branch and clear AC — The very first instruction is ENDBR64, the CET indirect-branch-tracking landing pad without which an indirect jump to this address would raise #CP. It is followed by ASM_CLAC, which clears EFLAGS.AC so that a stray kernel access to user memory faults instead of silently succeeding under SMAP. Both are pure security scaffolding that had no counterpart in 2.6.
  2. Clear the direction flag (CLD) — Ensures autoincrement semantics for string instructions, matching the C ABI.
  3. Pad the error code slot — If the exception does not push a hardware error code, the macro pushes $-1 into the orig_ax slot of pt_regs. This serves double duty: it pads the stack to a uniform layout and marks the entry as “not a system call” (system calls store the syscall number in orig_ax). This is the modern equivalent of the old pushl $0 padding.
  4. Call error_entry() — This function performs the bulk of the register save. Internally it invokes the PUSH_AND_CLEAR_REGS macro: 
    /* arch/x86/entry/calling.h — PUSH_REGS (called by PUSH_AND_CLEAR_REGS) */
pushq   %rdi            /* pt_regs->di */
pushq   %rsi            /* pt_regs->si */
pushq   %rdx            /* pt_regs->dx */
pushq   %rcx            /* pt_regs->cx */
pushq   %rax            /* pt_regs->ax */
pushq   %r8             /* pt_regs->r8 */
pushq   %r9             /* pt_regs->r9 */
pushq   %r10            /* pt_regs->r10 */
pushq   %r11            /* pt_regs->r11 */
pushq   %rbx            /* pt_regs->bx */
pushq   %rbp            /* pt_regs->bp */
pushq   %r12            /* pt_regs->r12 */
pushq   %r13            /* pt_regs->r13 */
pushq   %r14            /* pt_regs->r14 */
pushq   %r15            /* pt_regs->r15 */

    After pushing all 15 general-purpose registers, CLEAR_REGS zeroes most of them again. It leaves %rdi and %rax alone — those two are about to carry live values into the C handler — and it zeroes %rsi, %rdx, %rcx, %r8–%r11 plus, when clear_callee is set, %rbx, %rbp and %r12–%r15. The purpose is stated verbatim in calling.h:

    /* arch/x86/entry/calling.h */
/*
 * Sanitize registers of values that a speculation attack might
 * otherwise want to exploit. The lower registers are likely clobbered
 * well before they could be put to use in a speculative execution
 * gadget.
 */

    In other words this is not primarily an architectural information-leak fix but a Spectre-era hardening measure: a register still holding a user-controlled value is raw material for a speculative-execution gadget somewhere deep inside the handler, so entry code destroys that material before the handler can run. Linux 2.6 did no register clearing at all, because the attack class had not been discovered.

  5. Handle SWAPGS and CR3 — If the exception came from user mode (bit 0–1 of the saved CS are nonzero), error_entry() executes SWAPGS to switch from the user GS base to the kernel GS base, switches CR3 from the user page table to the kernel page table (KPTI), applies IBRS/retpoline mitigations, and then calls sync_regs() to copy the register frame from the IST or entry stack onto the real task stack. The function returns a pointer to the pt_regs on the correct stack, which idtentry_body immediately installs as the new %rsp.
  6. Annotate the frame for the unwinders — Two different annotations follow, one for each unwinder the kernel can be built with. ENCODE_FRAME_POINTER stores a tagged %rsp in %rbp so that the frame-pointer unwinder can recognise an exception boundary; it compiles to nothing unless CONFIG_UNWINDER_FRAME_POINTER is set. UNWIND_HINT_REGS emits an entry in the .discard.unwind_hints section that tells objtool — and hence the ORC unwinder, which is the default — that a full pt_regs now sits at %rsp. Neither annotation generates run-time work in the common configuration; they exist so that a stack trace taken inside the handler can walk back through the exception.
  7. Set up C arguments — The idtentry_body macro loads %rsp (the pt_regs pointer) into %rdi (first C argument), and if the exception has an error code, copies it from ORIG_RAX(%rsp) into %rsi (second C argument) and overwrites orig_ax with $-1.
  8. Call the C handler — call \cfunc invokes the C handler (e.g., exc_page_fault, exc_divide_error).
  9. Jump to error_return — After the C handler returns, control flows to error_return, which eventually calls irqentry_exit() and the architecture-specific return path.

After all these steps, the kernel stack contains a complete struct pt_regs frame:

Figure 4-11 shows that layout in memory order, which is also the order in which the fields appear in the structure definition.

Table 4-29. Comparison of register-save sequences: Linux 2.6 vs 7.0.12 (x86)

AspectLinux 2.6 (i386)Linux 7.0.12 (x86_64)
Entry macroHand-written handler_name: + jmp error_codeidtentry macro + idtentry_body
Register saveSAVE_ALL (saves eax–edi, segment regs)PUSH_AND_CLEAR_REGS (saves 15 GP regs)
Register clearNot doneZeroes all GP regs after save (security)
Error code handlingPush $0 padding + C handler addrPush $-1 as orig_ax marker
Segment registersLoads kernel __USER_DS into ds/esNot needed (flat 64-bit model)
Direction flagcld in error_codecld in idtentry macro
SWAPGSNot applicable (32-bit)swapgs if from user mode
CR3 switchNot applicable (no KPTI)SWITCH_TO_KERNEL_CR3 (KPTI)
Argument passingeax = pt_regs, edx = error code (regparm)rdi = pt_regs, rsi = error code (SysV ABI)
Stack switchVia sync_regs from entry to task stackVia sync_regs from IST/entry to task stack
Frame resultstruct pt_regs on kernel stackstruct pt_regs on kernel stack

ARM64: The kernel_entry Macro

ARM64 uses the kernel_entry macro in arch/arm64/kernel/entry.S to save registers. Unlike x86_64, which pushes registers one at a time, ARM64 uses STP (Store Pair) instructions to save two 64-bit registers at once, which is both faster and more compact:

/* arch/arm64/kernel/entry.S — kernel_entry macro (simplified) */
    stp     x0,  x1,  [sp, #16 * 0]
    stp     x2,  x3,  [sp, #16 * 1]
    stp     x4,  x5,  [sp, #16 * 2]
    stp     x6,  x7,  [sp, #16 * 3]
    stp     x8,  x9,  [sp, #16 * 4]
    stp     x10, x11, [sp, #16 * 5]
    stp     x12, x13, [sp, #16 * 6]
    stp     x14, x15, [sp, #16 * 7]
    stp     x16, x17, [sp, #16 * 8]
    stp     x18, x19, [sp, #16 * 9]
    stp     x20, x21, [sp, #16 * 10]
    stp     x22, x23, [sp, #16 * 11]
    stp     x24, x25, [sp, #16 * 12]
    stp     x26, x27, [sp, #16 * 13]
    stp     x28, x29, [sp, #16 * 14]

The stack space was pre-allocated by the kernel_ventry trampoline, which decrements SP by PT_REGS_SIZE before branching to the actual entry code. PT_REGS_SIZE is emitted by arch/arm64/kernel/asm-offsets.c as sizeof(struct pt_regs), which in 7.0.12 is 320 bytes: 31 × 8 for regs[], three more doublewords for sp, pc and pstate, then orig_x0, syscallno, pmr, sdei_ttbr1 and a 24-byte stackframe metadata record. Because the whole frame is carved out in one sub, every field has a fixed compile-time offset (S_LR, S_SP, S_PC, …) that the assembly can address directly — there is no equivalent of x86’s incremental push sequence, and no moment during entry at which the frame is only half-formed.

After saving the 30 general-purpose registers (x0–x29), the macro performs additional steps depending on whether the exception came from EL0 (user) or EL1 (kernel):

From EL0 (user mode):

  1. Clear GP registers — clear_gp_regs zeroes x0–x29 to prevent information leaks from user to kernel context.
  2. Save the user SP and reload current — Reads SP_EL0 (the user stack pointer) into x21 for later storage in the frame, loads the per-CPU __entry_task pointer into tsk, and then writes that task pointer back into SP_EL0 with msr sp_el0, tsk. This one instruction is how current works on ARM64: while the kernel is running, SP_EL0 is not a stack pointer at all but the task_struct pointer, which get_current() reads with a single mrs. It plays the role that the GS base and SWAPGS play on x86_64, and like SWAPGS it must happen exactly once per user→kernel transition.
  3. Disable single-step — Clears MDSCR_EL1.SS if the task’s TIF_SINGLESTEP is not set.
  4. MTE check — Checks for asynchronous tag check faults (Memory Tagging Extension).
  5. PAC key management — Installs the kernel’s Pointer Authentication keys if needed.
  6. SSBD mitigation — apply_ssbd 1 applies Speculative Store Bypass Disable.
  7. MTE GCR — mte_set_kernel_gcr installs the kernel’s tag-generation mask so that kernel allocations do not inherit the user task’s Memory Tagging configuration.
  8. Shadow Call Stack — scs_load_current loads the shadow call stack pointer for the current task.

Two further points are easy to miss. The macro’s very first instruction, before any register is saved, is alternative_insn nop, SET_PSTATE_DIT(1), ARM64_HAS_DIT: on CPUs that implement it, the kernel turns on Data Independent Timing for the whole of kernel execution, so that instruction timing cannot be used as a side channel against kernel data. And several of these steps are followed by an ISB under an alternative_if, because system-register writes such as the PAC-key install and the MTE mask are not self-synchronising — the source carries an explicit comment warning that any non-self-synchronising system-register update required for kernel entry must be placed before that barrier.

From EL1 (kernel mode):

  1. Computes the pre-exception SP value (add x21, sp, #PT_REGS_SIZE) and saves it in the frame — the kernel was already using SP_EL1, so unwinding the frame size recovers the interrupted stack pointer exactly.
  2. Gets the current task pointer with get_current_task, which simply reads SP_EL0; none of the EL0 work above is needed, because SP_EL0 already holds the task pointer and the PAC, MTE, SSBD and shadow-call-stack state is already the kernel’s.
  3. No SWAPGS equivalent needed (ARM64 has per-EL stack pointers and no GS base concept).

The final pt_regs layout on ARM64:

Figure 4-12 shows the ARM64 layout in memory order. The contrast with Figure 4-11 is instructive: ARM64 needs no SWAPGS equivalent and no segment registers, but it must save the general-purpose register file explicitly because the exception entry itself saves almost nothing.

Table 4-30. Register-save comparison: x86_64 vs ARM64

Aspectx86_64ARM64
Save instructionIndividual pushq (15 instructions)STP pairs (15 instructions, 2 regs each)
Pre-allocationNo (push decrements RSP)Yes (kernel_ventry subtracts PT_REGS_SIZE)
PC/PSTATE saveHardware pushes RIP/CS/RFLAGS to stackSoftware reads ELR_EL1/SPSR_EL1 into pt_regs
Register clearingCLEAR_REGS zeroes after saveclear_gp_regs zeroes after save (EL0 only)
Segment registersNot saved (flat model)Not applicable
Frame size168 bytes (21 × 8)320 bytes (all regs + metadata + frame record)
GS/task switchSWAPGS + CR3 switch (KPTI)msr sp_el0, tsk (task pointer), PAC key install
Field addressingOffsets implied by push orderFixed S_* offsets from asm-offsets.c

Entering and Leaving Exception Handlers

After the hardware entry sequence and assembly register-save prologue described above, control reaches the C-level exception handler. But the handler cannot simply start executing arbitrary kernel code. The kernel’s internal state must first be brought into a consistent condition — RCU must be told the CPU is no longer quiescent, lockdep must track that hardware interrupts are off, tracing hooks must be updated, and KMSAN (if enabled) must unpoison the register frame. This bookkeeping is handled by irqentry_enter() on x86_64 (and by arm64_enter_from_user_mode() / inline equivalents on ARM64) and is the conceptual “front door” of every exception handler.

The Entry Path: irqentry_enter()

On x86_64, most exception handlers are wrapped by the DEFINE_IDTENTRY macro family (see the next section), which calls irqentry_enter(regs) before the actual handler body. The function lives at kernel/entry/common.c:106-171 and is shared across architectures — it is the generic front door for every interrupt and exception path that uses the normal wrappers. It performs different work depending on whether the interrupted context was user mode or kernel mode:

Case 1 — From user mode (user_mode(regs) is true):

irqentry_enter() calls irqentry_enter_from_user_mode(regs), which in turn calls:

  1. enter_from_user_mode(regs) — The critical state-transition function:
    • Context tracking (ct_user_exit()) — Tells RCU that the CPU has left user space and is now executing kernel code. Essential for RCU grace-period management and NO_HZ_FULL tick accounting.
    • Lockdep (lockdep_hardirqs_off()) — Records that hardware interrupts are currently disabled.
    • Tracing (trace_hardirqs_off_finish()) — Completes the irq-off trace event.
    • KMSAN (kmsan_unpoison_entry_regs()) — Marks the saved registers as initialized for the memory sanitizer.
  2. rseq_note_user_irq_entry() — Records that a restartable sequence in user space was interrupted.

The returned irqentry_state_t has exit_rcu = false because the user→kernel RCU transition is handled by the simpler irqentry_exit_to_user_mode() path on exit.

Case 2 — From kernel mode, idle task or RCU extended quiescent state:

If the interrupted task is the idle task or the CPU was in an extended quiescent state (EQS), the function unconditionally calls ct_irq_enter() to revive RCU. The returned state has exit_rcu = true, telling the exit path that it must perform ct_irq_exit() on the way out.

The source comment explains why this must be unconditional: interrupts can nest (softirq processing re-enables interrupts), and a nested tick interrupt must not falsely believe it is the first interrupt leaving idle.

Case 3 — From kernel mode, RCU already watching:

The simplest case. The function just performs lockdep bookkeeping, KMSAN unpoisoning, and rcu_irq_enter_check_tick() to restart the tick if needed in NO_HZ mode.

/* kernel/entry/common.c — irqentry_enter() simplified flow */
irqentry_state_t irqentry_enter(struct pt_regs *regs)
{
    irqentry_state_t ret = { .exit_rcu = false };

    if (user_mode(regs)) {
        irqentry_enter_from_user_mode(regs);  /* Case 1 */
        return ret;
    }

    if (is_idle_task(current) || arch_in_rcu_eqs()) {
        lockdep_hardirqs_off(CALLER_ADDR0);
        ct_irq_enter();                       /* Case 2 */
        instrumentation_begin();
        kmsan_unpoison_entry_regs(regs);
        trace_hardirqs_off_finish();
        instrumentation_end();
        ret.exit_rcu = true;
        return ret;
    }

    lockdep_hardirqs_off(CALLER_ADDR0);       /* Case 3 */
    instrumentation_begin();
    kmsan_unpoison_entry_regs(regs);
    rcu_irq_enter_check_tick();
    trace_hardirqs_off_finish();
    instrumentation_end();
    return ret;
}

On ARM64, the equivalent for user-mode exceptions is arm64_enter_from_user_mode(regs), which calls the same generic enter_from_user_mode(regs) helper and then mte_disable_tco_entry(current). For kernel-mode exceptions, ARM64 handlers call enter_from_kernel_mode(regs), a thin noinstr wrapper in arch/arm64/kernel/entry-common.c that calls the shared irqentry_enter() and then adds two architecture-specific steps: mte_check_tfsr_entry(), which drains any pending asynchronous Memory Tagging Extension fault recorded in TFSR_EL1 before the kernel can confuse it with one of its own, and mte_disable_tco_entry(), which turns tag checking back on for kernel accesses. The symmetric exit_to_kernel_mode() calls mte_check_tfsr_exit() before irqentry_exit(). Both wrappers are marked noinstr, and the comments above them state the rule plainly: before enter_from_kernel_mode() has run it is not safe to call regular kernel code, instrumentable code, or anything that might itself fault.

Notice how little of this is actual work. irqentry_enter() allocates nothing, takes no locks, and makes no decisions about the exception itself. Its entire job is to make the rest of the handler legal — to move RCU, lockdep, tracing, and the sanitizers into a state in which ordinary instrumentable kernel code may run. That is the modern form of the classic rule that entry code must first preserve the kernel’s internal accounting before any real handling can begin; what has changed is not the principle but the number of subsystems that now need to be accounted for.

The Handler Body

Once irqentry_enter() returns, the instrumentation_begin() / instrumentation_end() guards open a region where tracing, kprobes, and other instrumentation are safe to use. Inside this region, the actual C handler (e.g., __exc_divide_error(), __exc_page_fault()) runs. The handler may:

  • Attempt to fix up a kernel-mode fault via fixup_exception()
  • Send a signal to the user-mode process via force_sig_fault()
  • Invoke die() / die_kernel_fault() for unrecoverable kernel bugs
  • Handle demand paging, copy-on-write, or other VM operations (page faults)
  • Notify kprobes, kgdb, perf, or other event consumers

The Exit Path: irqentry_exit()

When the C handler returns, irqentry_exit(regs, state) performs the symmetric teardown. Again, the behavior differs based on the interrupted context:

Returning to user mode (user_mode(regs) is true):

Calls irqentry_exit_to_user_mode(regs), which runs the full exit-to-user work loop. The important structural point is that this is a loop, not a checklist: __exit_to_user_mode_loop() in kernel/entry/common.c re-enables interrupts to do its work, then disables them again and re-reads the thread flags, because anything it just did — scheduling in particular — may have set new ones. It only falls out when no EXIT_TO_USER_MODE_WORK_LOOP flag remains set with interrupts off.

  1. Assert interrupts are disabled, then call tick_nohz_user_enter_prepare() to flush any pending rcuog wakeup before the last need_resched() check.
  2. Read the thread-info flags once. If none of EXIT_TO_USER_MODE_WORK is set — the overwhelmingly common case — skip the loop entirely.
  3. Inside the loop, with interrupts re-enabled: if TIF_NEED_RESCHED or TIF_NEED_RESCHED_LAZY is set, first offer the task an rseq slice extension (rseq_grant_slice_extension(), which lets a task that is inside a registered critical section briefly defer preemption); only if that is declined does it call schedule().
  4. TIF_UPROBE → uprobe_notify_resume().
  5. TIF_PATCH_PENDING → klp_update_patch_state(), the livepatch consistency-model transition point.
  6. TIF_SIGPENDING or TIF_NOTIFY_SIGNAL → arch_do_signal_or_restart(), which delivers signals and applies syscall-restart semantics.
  7. TIF_NOTIFY_RESUME → resume_user_mode_work(), the catch-all for task-work, memory-cgroup accounting, and similar deferred jobs.
  8. arch_exit_to_user_mode_work() for any architecture-specific TIF work; then interrupts off, tick_nohz_user_enter_prepare() again to catch deferred wakeups queued by the work above, and re-read the flags.
  9. After the loop: arch_exit_to_user_mode_prepare(), the rseq fixup appropriate to this entry type (rseq_irqentry_exit_to_user_mode()), and __exit_to_user_mode_validate(), which asserts through kmap_assert_nomap() and lockdep_sys_exit() that no kmap is still held and no lock is still taken.
  10. Finally exit_to_user_mode(): unwind_reset_info(), trace_hardirqs_on_prepare(), lockdep_hardirqs_on_prepare(), ct_user_enter() to tell RCU the CPU is leaving the kernel, and arch_exit_to_user_mode() for last-minute speculation mitigations.
  11. The assembly epilogue then does the machine-specific part: on x86_64 switching CR3 back to the user page table (KPTI) and executing SWAPGS; on ARM64 handling ASID, PAC keys, MTE, and SSBD state.

Two of these steps are recent arrivals and worth flagging. TIF_NEED_RESCHED_LAZY comes from the PREEMPT_LAZY work: it marks a reschedule that is desirable but not urgent, so the kernel can honor it at a cheap boundary such as this one instead of preempting immediately. And the rseq slice-extension check in front of schedule() did not exist in earlier kernels at all — it is a cooperative hint from userspace that it is in the middle of a short critical section.

Returning to kernel mode:

If the saved flags indicate interrupts will be re-enabled on return, irqentry_exit() may call irqentry_exit_cond_resched(). On preemptible kernels this invokes preempt_schedule_irq() if need_resched() is true and preempt_count() is zero. Only after that possible reschedule does it report hardirqs-on to tracing and lockdep, so that the bookkeeping reflects the state the CPU will actually return to rather than the state it was in when the handler finished. If the entry path set exit_rcu = true, the exit path performs the more careful ct_irq_exit() sequence to restore the extended quiescent state. The asymmetry is deliberate: the kernel-mode exit is the one that has to reconstruct a state it did not create, so it is the one that consults the state token returned by the entry path.

Table 4-31. Exception handler entry/exit flow summary

PhaseUser-mode exceptionKernel-mode exception
Entryirqentry_enter_from_user_mode() → context tracking, lockdep, tracing, KMSAN, rseqlockdep_hardirqs_off() + ct_irq_enter() (if idle/EQS) or rcu_irq_enter_check_tick()
HandlerC handler runs inside instrumentation_begin()/end() guardsSame
Exitirqentry_exit_to_user_mode() → schedule, signals, uprobe, rseq, context tracking, KPTI/SWAPGSirqentry_exit_cond_resched() (if preemptible) + ct_irq_exit() (if idle/EQS)

The IDTENTRY Macro Family

Modern x86 entry code uses macros to generate consistent wrappers. The most important are DEFINE_IDTENTRY, DEFINE_IDTENTRY_ERRORCODE, DEFINE_IDTENTRY_RAW, and DEFINE_IDTENTRY_IRQ. They are not just syntactic sugar. They encode which handlers receive the generic irqentry_enter() / irqentry_exit() treatment automatically and which handlers must do some part of the bookkeeping manually. Page fault is the most famous raw case because valid page faults can sleep and therefore need slightly different discipline.

/* arch/x86/include/asm/idtentry.h */
#define DEFINE_IDTENTRY(func)                                   \
static __always_inline void __##func(struct pt_regs *regs);     \
__visible noinstr void func(struct pt_regs *regs)               \
{                                                               \
    irqentry_state_t state = irqentry_enter(regs);              \
    instrumentation_begin();                                    \
    __##func(regs);                                             \
    instrumentation_end();                                      \
    irqentry_exit(regs, state);                                 \
}                                                               \
static __always_inline void __##func(struct pt_regs *regs)

#define DEFINE_IDTENTRY_ERRORCODE(func)                         \
static __always_inline void __##func(struct pt_regs *regs,      \
                                     unsigned long error_code); \
__visible noinstr void func(struct pt_regs *regs,               \
                            unsigned long error_code)           \
{                                                               \
    irqentry_state_t state = irqentry_enter(regs);              \
    instrumentation_begin();                                    \
    __##func(regs, error_code);                                 \
    instrumentation_end();                                      \
    irqentry_exit(regs, state);                                 \
}                                                               \
static __always_inline void __##func(struct pt_regs *regs,      \
                                     unsigned long error_code)

#define DEFINE_IDTENTRY_RAW(func)                               \
__visible noinstr void func(struct pt_regs *regs)

#define DEFINE_IDTENTRY_IRQ(func)                               \
static void __##func(struct pt_regs *regs, u32 vector);         \
__visible noinstr void func(struct pt_regs *regs,               \
                            unsigned long error_code)           \
{                                                               \
    irqentry_state_t state = irqentry_enter(regs);              \
    u32 vector = (u32)(u8)error_code;                           \
    kvm_set_cpu_l1tf_flush_l1d();                               \
    instrumentation_begin();                                    \
    run_irq_on_irqstack_cond(__##func, regs, vector);           \
    instrumentation_end();                                      \
    irqentry_exit(regs, state);                                 \
}                                                               \
static noinline void __##func(struct pt_regs *regs, u32 vector)

The macro family teaches an important lesson. Entry code is not merely about getting from assembly into C. It is about constraining which helper sequence is legal for a given event class. A raw handler is an explicit warning to the reader: this path cannot use the generic wrapper blindly. Conversely, a plain DEFINE_IDTENTRY() says that the normal entry/exit bookends are correct and sufficient.

Register Saving and pt_regs

The assembly entry stubs save the machine state into the architecture's canonical register frame. On x86_64, struct pt_regs is the interface between low-level entry and high-level C handlers. It deliberately places the IRET frame at the tail of the struct so that return code can treat the lower portion of the stack as the architectural return frame while C code sees a complete snapshot. orig_ax is overloaded: for syscalls it records the original system-call number, for exceptions it often carries the hardware error code, and for device interrupts it can be used to preserve the vector number pushed by the entry stub.

This layout choice enables a great deal of code reuse. Signal code, ptrace, tracepoints, exception fixups, and architecture-neutral helpers all speak through pt_regs. The price is that entry code must preserve the exact ABI of the frame. That is why changes to pt_regs are rare, heavily reviewed, and intimately tied to the assembly prologue.

Common Error-Trap Pattern: do_error_trap()

Many x86 exceptions differ only in name, signal, and error-code semantics. Linux factors the common logic into do_error_trap(). The helper first warns if RCU was not made active properly. It then calls notify_die() so kprobes, kgdb, perf, or other notifiers may intercept the event. If nobody claims it, the helper temporarily enables local interrupts when appropriate, calls do_trap(), and then restores the previous interrupt state.

/* arch/x86/kernel/traps.c */
static void do_error_trap(struct pt_regs *regs, long error_code, char *str,
    unsigned long trapnr, int signr, int sicode, void __user *addr)
{
    RCU_LOCKDEP_WARN(!rcu_is_watching(), "entry code didn't wake RCU");

    if (notify_die(DIE_TRAP, str, regs, error_code, trapnr, signr) !=
            NOTIFY_STOP) {
        cond_local_irq_enable(regs);
        do_trap(trapnr, signr, str, regs, error_code, sicode, addr);
        cond_local_irq_disable(regs);
    }
}

do_trap() immediately delegates the “can this be handled without a signal?” question to do_trap_no_signal(), which distinguishes three cases:

  • vm86 mode (32-bit builds only) — Traps 0, 1, 3, 4 and 5 are forwarded to the vm86 monitor with handle_vm86_trap(). The v8086_mode() test compiles to a constant false on x86_64, so this arm vanishes entirely on the architecture this book targets.
  • Kernel mode — Linux first tries fixup_exception(). If a fixup exists, the kernel rewrites the saved instruction pointer and resumes, as if the fault were part of a planned probe or user-copy boundary. If no fixup exists, the event is a genuine kernel bug: tsk->thread.error_code and trap_nr are recorded and die() is invoked.
  • User mode — Even here a fixup is tried first, via fixup_vdso_exception(). The vDSO contains kernel-supplied code running with user privileges, and a fault inside it (for instance in the RDTSCP-based clock_gettime() fast path on a machine where the instruction is trapped) should be corrected rather than reported. Only if that fails does the task proceed toward signal generation.

If do_trap_no_signal() declines to handle the event, do_trap() calls show_signal() — which is what produces the familiar rate-limited “comm[pid] trap str ip:… sp:… error:…” line in dmesg when show_unhandled_signals is enabled — and then delivers the signal with force_sig() or, when the exception has a meaningful faulting address, force_sig_fault(). Note the deliberate asymmetry commented in the source: error_code and trap_nr are stored for user-mode faults and for kernel faults that end in die(), but not for kernel faults that were fixed up, so that a successful copy_from_user() failure does not overwrite the diagnostic state of a genuine fault that is still queued for delivery.

Page Fault Handling

exc_page_fault() is the centerpiece of x86 exception handling. It is defined with DEFINE_IDTENTRY_RAW_ERRORCODE in arch/x86/mm/fault.c, which already tells us that the generic wrappers are not quite enough. The handler reads the fault address from CR2 or from FRED event data. It gives KVM's asynchronous-page-fault mechanism a chance to consume the event. Then it explicitly calls irqentry_enter(regs), runs handle_page_fault(), and finishes with irqentry_exit(regs, state).

/* arch/x86/mm/fault.c */
DEFINE_IDTENTRY_RAW_ERRORCODE(exc_page_fault)
{
    irqentry_state_t state;
    unsigned long address;

    address = cpu_feature_enabled(X86_FEATURE_FRED)
                ? fred_event_data(regs) : read_cr2();

    if (kvm_handle_async_pf(regs, (u32)address))
        return;

    state = irqentry_enter(regs);

    instrumentation_begin();
    handle_page_fault(regs, error_code, address);
    instrumentation_end();

    irqentry_exit(regs, state);
}

handle_page_fault() first emits tracepoints. It then checks special mechanisms such as KMMIO. The decisive branch is whether the fault address lies in kernel-controlled address space. Kernel-space faults go to do_kern_addr_fault(). User-space faults go to do_user_addr_fault(). Both paths may eventually lead to page-table installation, VMA access checks, copy-on-write, major faults that require I/O, or signal generation.

The remarkable point is that a page fault is both ordinary and dangerous. It is ordinary because every demand-paged process relies on it constantly. It is dangerous because a kernel page fault can happen while holding locks or on paths where sleeping is illegal. That is why exception tables and carefully delimited faultable regions are so central to kernel robustness.

General Protection Handling

exc_general_protection() is the second most educational exception handler because #GP is so broad. The implementation in arch/x86/kernel/traps.c works through a ladder of increasingly pessimistic possibilities.

The very first test, taken before interrupts are even re-enabled, is try_fixup_enqcmd_gp(). A user-mode ENQCMD instruction faults if IA32_PASID has not yet been populated for the task, and since that MSR is managed through XSAVE it can only be written while the FPU registers are live — which is exactly the situation on entry, and no longer true once cond_local_irq_enable() has run. This is a good illustration of why the ordering inside an exception handler is not arbitrary.

Then come the remaining user-mode emulation cases: UMIP fixups (fixup_umip_exception(), which emulates SGDT, SIDT, SLDT, STR and SMSW for legacy programs on a machine where UMIP makes them fault), vm86 compatibility, IOPL emulation, and vDSO faults. Only after all of those does the handler give up on the user process and call gp_user_force_sig_segv().

For kernel-mode faults it tries gp_try_fixup_and_notify() — the exception-table and notifier path — and, failing that, decodes the faulting instruction with get_kernel_gp_address() to attach a diagnostic hint before calling die_addr(). The hints in 7.0.12 are:

  • GP_NON_CANONICAL — “probably for non-canonical address”, the classic 64-bit wild-pointer signature.
  • GP_CANONICAL — “maybe for address”, when the operand was canonical and the cause is less obvious.
  • GP_LASS_VIOLATION — “probably LASS violation for address”, raised when Linear Address Space Separation catches the kernel touching a user address outside a uaccess region. This hint did not exist before the LASS support landed, and it is a good example of a new CPU feature routing its failures into #GP.
  • GP_NULL_POINTER — “kernel NULL pointer dereference”.

Note the interaction with KASAN at the end of the function: gp_addr is deliberately zeroed unless the hint was GP_NON_CANONICAL, so that KASAN’s report only tries to describe an address it can actually reason about.

This handler shows modern Linux entry style at its best. It preserves the architectural meaning of #GP while layering in user ABI considerations, virtualization details, instrumentation hooks, and diagnostics that did not exist in the 2.6 era. #GP is no longer just a dull protection exception. It is a catchment basin for many newer CPU features whose failures do not belong to #PF.

Debug Exceptions and Breakpoints

Before looking at #DB itself, it is worth returning to a curiosity in the idtentry macro that was skipped over earlier, because it exists entirely for the sake of INT3. When the vector being generated is X86_TRAP_BP and the breakpoint was taken from kernel space, the stub opens a six-doubleword hole in the stack before doing anything else:

/* arch/x86/entry/entry_64.S — inside the idtentry macro */
    .if \vector == X86_TRAP_BP
        /*
         * If coming from kernel space, create a 6-word gap to allow the
         * int3 handler to emulate a call instruction.
         */
        testb   $3, CS-ORIG_RAX(%rsp)
        jnz     .Lfrom_usermode_no_gap_\@
        .rept   6
        pushq   5*8(%rsp)
        .endr
        UNWIND_HINT_IRET_REGS offset=8
.Lfrom_usermode_no_gap_\@:
    .endif

The reason is live kernel patching in the broadest sense. ftrace, static calls, jump labels and kprobes all rewrite kernel instructions while other CPUs may be executing them, and the standard text_poke_bp() protocol does this by first replacing the target instruction’s opening byte with 0xCC (INT3). Any CPU that arrives mid-patch traps into exc_int3(), which then has to emulate the instruction that is being installed. If that instruction is a CALL, emulation means pushing a return address — but there is no room to push anything, because the interrupted frame sits immediately below. Copying the frame six words further down creates that room in advance, and only for kernel-mode INT3, since a user-mode breakpoint is never an in-flight text patch. The same conditional also passes signal=0 to UNWIND_HINT_IRET_ENTRY, because #BP is a trap: the saved RIP already points past the INT3, so the unwinder must not treat it as an instruction boundary that is about to be re-executed.

The debug path proper is complicated because recursive debug exceptions are easy to create and hard to reason about. exc_debug_kernel() immediately saves and disables local debug state via local_db_save() so that handler execution itself does not re-trigger the same hardware breakpoint. It then enters through the NMI-style bookkeeping helpers because the x86_64 debug vector uses an IST stack. After notifiers have had a chance to consume the event, the code checks for impossible leftover DR_STEP conditions and sanitizes TF if necessary.

The user path exc_debug_user() behaves differently. It deliberately does not clear DR7 up front because the exit-to-user path may schedule, fault, or otherwise run code that tolerates nested debug exceptions only through the kernel-mode emergency path. It stores a virtual DR6 view for ptrace, clears TIF_BLOCKSTEP to match the hardware BTF semantics, optionally enables local IRQs once the critical debug state is saved, and finally sends SIGTRAP when the event corresponds to single-step or breakpoint conditions.

Why Double Fault Needs IST

The x86 comments around exc_double_fault() are worth reading carefully. A double fault is often a symptom of stack trouble while delivering another exception. If Linux tried to enter the handler on the same damaged stack, it might triple-fault and reset the CPU before any diagnostics could be printed. The IST stack gives the handler a clean foothold from which it can inspect CR2, detect guard-page overflows, and panic intelligibly.

Linux also contains special recovery for one famous corner case: faults on the espfix64 return stack that can be transformed into an artificial #GP frame. That oddity is a reminder that entry code is full of historical and architectural scar tissue. The handler is not merely about screaming "double fault" and dying. It is about preserving as much debuggability as the machine still permits.

Signal Delivery from Exceptions

When an exception should be reported to user space, Linux builds a kernel_siginfo and queues a signal. The helper force_sig_fault() in kernel/signal.c is the common path for faults with an address payload. It fills si_signo, si_code, and si_addr, then forwards the request to force_sig_info_to_task() for the current task. The exact signal depends on the exception: SIGSEGV, SIGBUS, SIGILL, SIGFPE, or SIGTRAP are the most common.

/* kernel/signal.c */
int force_sig_fault_to_task(int sig, int code, void __user *addr,
                            struct task_struct *t)
{
    struct kernel_siginfo info;

    clear_siginfo(&info);
    info.si_signo = sig;
    info.si_errno = 0;
    info.si_code  = code;
    info.si_addr  = addr;
    return force_sig_info_to_task(&info, t, HANDLER_CURRENT);
}

fixup_exception() and Exception Tables

Kernel-mode faults are not always bugs. Sometimes they are planned probes across unsafe memory boundaries. The classic example is user-copy code: the kernel may attempt a load from a user pointer, expecting that the access could fault. Instead of crashing the kernel, Linux uses exception tables to redirect control to a fixup location that records failure and resumes execution.

The table itself lives in the __ex_table section, and on x86 each entry is remarkably small:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/extable.h */
struct exception_table_entry {
    int insn, fixup, data;
};

All three fields are 32-bit relative displacements rather than pointers, which keeps the table compact and, more importantly, position-independent, so that KASLR relocation and module loading do not have to rewrite it. insn locates the faulting instruction, fixup the recovery label, and data packs a fixup type together with a register number and an immediate. The type is what makes the mechanism general: rather than forcing every recovery path to be hand-written assembly, the fault handler consults a small interpreter. arch/x86/mm/extable.c implements roughly two dozen types, among them:

  • EX_TYPE_DEFAULT and EX_TYPE_FAULT — jump to the fixup label, the latter also depositing the trap number in a register.
  • EX_TYPE_UACCESS — the user-copy case, which additionally warns if the faulting address was not actually a user address.
  • EX_TYPE_IMM_REG and its aliases EX_TYPE_EFAULT_REG, EX_TYPE_ZERO_REG, EX_TYPE_ONE_REG — set a nominated register to a constant, so a C wrapper can simply return -EFAULT.
  • EX_TYPE_RDMSR_SAFE / EX_TYPE_WRMSR_SAFE — synthesize an -EIO return, which is how rdmsrl_safe() can probe a model-specific register that may not exist.
  • EX_TYPE_ZEROPAD — re-executes a word-sized load as a zero-padded partial load, used by load_unaligned_zeropad() in the dentry-name comparison fast path.
  • EX_TYPE_POP_REG, EX_TYPE_UCOPY_LEN, EX_TYPE_CLEAR_FS, EX_TYPE_FPU_RESTORE, EX_TYPE_BPF, EX_TYPE_ERETU — specialised recoveries for the stack, string copies, segment loads, XRSTOR, the BPF interpreter and FRED’s ERETU.

Two further properties matter. First, fixup_exception() is consulted by several handlers, not just #PF: #GP, #UD, #AC and the machine-check path all route through it, which is why the trap number is part of the interface. Second, the _MCE_SAFE variants exist so that a recoverable memory error encountered inside a user-copy can be turned into a short copy rather than a machine-check panic.

This mechanism is one of the most elegant fault-recovery tools in the kernel, because it lets ordinary-looking inline assembly or C wrappers operate over potentially faulting accesses without scattering explicit page-fault branches everywhere. ARM64 uses exactly the same idea, with its own ex_handler types declared through the _ASM_EXTABLE-style macros in arch/arm64/include/asm/asm-extable.h.

ARM64 Exception Dispatch

ARM64 makes the dispatch logic highly explicit. el1h_64_sync_handler() reads esr_el1, extracts the exception class with ESR_ELx_EC(esr), and switches over it. Data and instruction aborts route to el1_abort(). PC alignment issues route to el1_pc(). Undefined instruction classes route to el1_undef(). Breakpoints, watchpoints, single-step traps, branch target identification faults, and guarded-control-stack faults each have dedicated helpers.

User-originated synchronous exceptions follow el0t_64_sync_handler(). That dispatcher recognizes SVC64, data abort, instruction abort, FP/SIMD access, SVE/SME access, system instruction traps, stack-pointer alignment, PC alignment, breakpoints, watchpoints, BRK64, and newer FPAC or guarded-control-stack events. The structure is pedagogically helpful: on ARM64 the architectural ESR class is the primary software dispatch key in a way that resembles, but is not identical to, x86 vector dispatch.

Interrupt Handling

As we explained earlier, most exceptions are handled simply by sending a Unix signal to the process that caused the exception. The action to be taken is thus deferred until the process receives the signal; as a result, the kernel is able to process the exception quickly.

This approach does not hold for interrupts, because they frequently arrive long after the process to which they are related (for instance, a process that requested a data transfer) has been suspended and a completely unrelated process is running. So it would make no sense to send a Unix signal to the current process.

Interrupt handling depends on the type of interrupt. For our purposes, we distinguish three main classes of interrupts:

I/O interrupts — An I/O device requires attention; the corresponding interrupt handler must query the device to determine the proper course of action. These are by far the most common type of interrupt. They are covered in detail in the next section, “I/O Interrupt Handling.”

Timer interrupts — A timer, either the Local APIC timer on x86_64 or an ARM Generic Timer, has issued an interrupt; this kind of interrupt tells the kernel that a fixed-time interval has elapsed. Timer interrupts are the heartbeat of the kernel. They drive process scheduling, timekeeping, timeout detection, and delayed work. We discuss the peculiar characteristics of timer interrupts in the section “Timer Interrupt Handling” later in this chapter and in Chapter 6.

Interprocessor interrupts (IPIs) — A CPU issued an interrupt to another CPU of a multiprocessor system. IPIs are used for reschedule requests, remote function calls, TLB shootdowns, and CPU stop/crash operations. We cover such interrupts in the section “Interprocessor Interrupt Handling” later in this chapter.

Actions Performed by Interrupt Handlers

Not all actions to be performed when an interrupt occurs have the same urgency. In fact, the interrupt handler itself is not a suitable place for all kinds of actions. Long noncritical operations should be deferred, because while an interrupt handler is running, the signals on the corresponding IRQ line may be temporarily ignored.

The classic statement of the fundamental constraint is that the process on behalf of which an interrupt handler is executing must always stay in the TASK_RUNNING state, or a system freeze can occur. That is still the right intuition, but it is worth restating in modern terms, because the mechanism has changed. A hardirq handler does not run as a process at all; it runs in interrupt context, borrowing whichever task happened to be current on this CPU and (on both x86_64 and ARM64) usually running on a dedicated per-CPU interrupt stack rather than that task’s kernel stack. There is no task_struct that the scheduler could meaningfully put to sleep and later wake, because the thing that would need waking is not a schedulable entity. Blocking in interrupt context therefore does not merely stall one process — it stalls the CPU, with the interrupt still in flight and the controller still waiting for its acknowledgment. This is why in_task() is false inside a handler, why might_sleep() fires there, and why interrupt handlers cannot perform any blocking procedure such as an I/O disk operation or a memory allocation with GFP_KERNEL: they must use GFP_ATOMIC, they may take only spinlocks (never mutexes or semaphores), and they may never call copy_to_user(), which can fault and sleep.

Linux divides the actions to be performed following an interrupt into three classes:

  1. Critical — Actions such as acknowledging the interrupt to the interrupt controller, reprogramming the controller or device, or updating data structures accessed by both the device and the processor. These can be executed quickly and are critical because they must be performed as soon as possible. Critical actions are executed within the hardirq handler immediately.
  2. Noncritical — Actions such as updating data structures that are accessed only by the processor (for instance, reading the scan code after a keyboard key has been pressed). These actions can also finish quickly, so they too are executed by the interrupt handler immediately.
  3. Noncritical deferrable — Actions such as copying a buffer’s contents into the address space of a process (for instance, sending the contents of a network packet to the receiving socket’s buffer). These actions may be delayed for a long time without affecting kernel operation; the interested process will just wait for the data. Deferrable actions are performed by softirqs, tasklets, or workqueues (see “Softirqs and Tasklets”).

One detail of this classification has changed since Linux 2.6, and it is worth being explicit about because the old rule is still widely repeated. In 2.6, whether a handler ran with local interrupts enabled or disabled was a per-handler choice, expressed by the IRQF_DISABLED (formerly SA_INTERRUPT) flag: “critical” actions ran with interrupts off, “noncritical” ones with interrupts on. That flag no longer exists. In 7.0.12 every hardirq handler runs with local interrupts disabled on the CPU that took the interrupt, without exception, and the generic layer enforces it — __handle_irq_event_percpu() checks after each handler returns and both warns and re-disables if the handler left interrupts on:

/* kernel/irq/handle.c */
if (WARN_ONCE(!irqs_disabled(), "irq %u handler %pS enabled interrupts\n",
              irq, action->handler))
        local_irq_disable();

The reason for the change was that the two-mode scheme bought very little and cost a great deal: it made every driver reason about reentrancy on its own, it made lock-ordering analysis nearly impossible, and it produced hard-to-reproduce bugs when a “noncritical” handler was interrupted mid-update. Uniformly disabling interrupts is simpler to reason about and, once handlers became short by convention, cheap enough. So the modern distinction between critical and noncritical is not about the interrupt-enable state at all — both run identically — but purely about urgency and cost: how soon the work must happen, and how much of the interrupt-off window it is fair to consume.

This three-level classification is what drives the split between hardirq handlers (top halves) and softirqs/tasklets/workqueues (bottom halves), one of the most fundamental architectural patterns in the Linux interrupt subsystem.

Table 4-32. Three classes of interrupt actions

ClassWhen executedLocal interruptsCan sleep?Allowed allocationExamples
CriticalImmediately in hardirqDisabledNoGFP_ATOMICACK interrupt controller, update shared hardware state
NoncriticalImmediately in hardirqDisabled (same as critical since IRQF_DISABLED was removed)NoGFP_ATOMICRead device registers, update kernel-only data structures
Noncritical deferrableLater, in a softirq, tasklet, workqueue, or threaded IRQEnabledSoftirq/tasklet: No; workqueue and threaded IRQ: YesSoftirq/tasklet: GFP_ATOMIC; workqueue/threaded: GFP_KERNELCopy buffers to user space, process network packets

The I/O Interrupt Path: From Hardware Signal to Handler Return

Regardless of the kind of circuit that caused the interrupt, all I/O interrupt handlers in Linux 7.0.12 perform the same fundamental sequence of actions. The old kernel (2.6) described four basic steps; the modern kernel refines these into a seven-step pipeline that separates the architecture-specific entry from the generic IRQ layer. The separation is the important structural change: steps 1, 2 and 7 are the only ones that know anything about the machine, and everything between them is architecture-independent code in kernel/irq/ that an x86_64 APIC and an ARM64 GIC reach by exactly the same route.

  1. Save registers and switch context — The CPU hardware pushes the interrupted context onto the kernel stack (IRET frame on x86_64; exception frame on ARM64). The architecture entry code then saves the remaining general-purpose registers, forming a complete struct pt_regs frame. On x86_64, PUSH_AND_CLEAR_REGS pushes 15 GP registers; SWAPGS and KPTI CR3 switch occur if coming from user mode. On ARM64, kernel_entry saves 31 GP registers plus system registers via STP pairs.
  2. Look up the IRQ descriptor — The entry code translates the hardware vector (x86_64) or the value read from IAR (ARM64 GICv3) into a struct irq_desc. On x86_64, common_interrupt() — itself a DEFINE_IDTENTRY_IRQ, so it gets the standard irqentry_enter()/irqentry_exit() treatment for free — calls call_irq_handler(), which reads __this_cpu_read(vector_irq[vector]). Note that vector_irq[] is a per-CPU array of struct irq_desc *, not of IRQ numbers: the vector-to-descriptor mapping is resolved in one load, with no intervening lookup. If that entry is NULL or an ERR_PTR, the code does not immediately give up. It retakes vector_lock and calls reevaluate_vector(), because a free_irq() on one CPU racing with a request_irq() on another can legitimately leave the slot marked VECTOR_SHUTDOWN at the instant a genuine interrupt for that vector arrives; the comment above the function draws out the interleaving in full. Only if the second look also fails does common_interrupt() treat the interrupt as spurious and issue a bare apic_eoi() so the APIC is not left blocked.

    On ARM64, handle_arch_irq (typically gic_handle_irq()) reads ICC_IAR1_EL1 to get the hardware interrupt ID, then hands it to generic_handle_domain_irq(), which walks the irq_domain hierarchy to reach the descriptor. Reading the IAR is itself the acknowledgment on a GIC — the act of reading it moves the interrupt to active state — which is why an ISB follows: the IAR may report an interrupt that was signalled after the CPU took the exception, and for CPU-coupled devices such as the PMU the handler must not observe stale system-register state.

  3. Switch to the interrupt stack — Before any driver code runs, both architectures move off the interrupted task’s kernel stack onto a dedicated per-CPU interrupt stack, so that a deep interrupt does not overflow a thread stack that may already be nearly full. On x86_64 this is run_irq_on_irqstack_cond(), invoked from inside the DEFINE_IDTENTRY_IRQ macro itself. On ARM64 it is do_interrupt_handler() in entry-common.c, which calls call_on_irq_stack().

    Both are conditional, but the conditions are not identical, and the difference is instructive. ARM64 asks if (on_thread_stack()) — switch unless we are already on the interrupt stack — so a nested interrupt continues on the stack the outer one already selected rather than resetting to its base and clobbering the outer frame. x86_64 skips the switch in two cases: if (user_mode(regs) || __this_cpu_read(hardirq_stack_inuse)). The second is the nesting case ARM64 also handles. The first is an optimization the comment states outright — on entry from user mode the task’s kernel stack is empty, so there is nothing to protect and the switch would buy nothing. Note also how carefully the flag is maintained: hardirq_stack_inuse is set before and cleared after the switch, with interrupts disabled at both points, so there is no window in which a nested interrupt could see a stale value and switch onto a stack that is already in use.

    This step is also where the softirq accounting lives. The same macro wraps the handler in irq_enter_rcu() and irq_exit_rcu(), and it is irq_exit_rcu() — on the way out of the outermost hardirq, when preempt_count()’s hardirq field drops to zero — that runs any softirqs the handlers raised. Because the calls sit inside the switched region, those softirqs run on the interrupt stack too, not on the interrupted task’s stack. On ARM64 the same pairing appears explicitly in __el1_irq() and its EL0 counterpart, around do_interrupt_handler().

  4. Invoke the flow handler — The generic IRQ layer calls the per-line flow handler stored in desc->handle_irq (e.g., handle_fasteoi_irq(), handle_edge_irq(), handle_level_irq()). The flow handler is the piece that encodes what kind of signal the line carries — edge or level, ack-then-handle or handle-then-EOI — and it is chosen once, when the irqchip driver maps the line, so that the hot path never has to test for it. It manages masking, acknowledging, and end-of-interrupt (EOI) signaling. handle_fasteoi_irq(), the variant used for APIC and GIC lines, shows the shape: take desc->lock, bail out early (still EOI-ing, via cond_eoi_irq()) if the line is suspended or has no usable action, mask the line if IRQS_ONESHOT is set so a threaded handler can run without a retrigger, bump kstat_incr_irqs_this_cpu(), call handle_irq_event(), then cond_unmask_eoi_irq(). Its final act is a subtle one worth noting: if an affinity change raced with delivery and the interrupt was marked IRQS_PENDING while in progress, it calls check_irq_resend() so the interrupt is not silently lost.
  5. Execute the ISR chain — The flow handler calls handle_irq_event(), which iterates every struct irqaction on the shared-IRQ chain via __handle_irq_event_percpu(). handle_irq_event() performs a small but important dance around the iteration: it clears IRQS_PENDING, sets IRQD_IRQ_INPROGRESS, and then drops desc->lock for the duration of the driver handlers, reacquiring it afterwards. Driver code therefore never runs under the descriptor lock — which is what makes it safe for a handler to call back into the IRQ layer — while IRQD_IRQ_INPROGRESS stands in for the lock to keep a second CPU from entering the same descriptor concurrently. Each handler returns IRQ_NONE, IRQ_HANDLED, or IRQ_WAKE_THREAD, and the results are OR-ed together, which is how the spurious-interrupt detector later recognizes a line on which every registered handler disclaimed the interrupt. If a handler returns IRQ_WAKE_THREAD, __irq_wake_thread() wakes the per-action IRQ thread for deferred processing — and if the driver returned that value without ever supplying a thread_fn, the kernel catches the mistake with warn_no_thread() rather than silently dropping the work. Two diagnostics wrap the loop. trace_irq_handler_entry/trace_irq_handler_exit bracket each handler, and if the CPU booted with irqhandler.duration_warn_us= a static branch enables irqhandler_duration_check(), which times every handler with local_clock() and emits a rate-limited warning naming the offending function when it runs long. That parameter is new in the 7.x series and is the most direct tool the kernel offers for finding the “who is holding interrupts off” class of latency bug.
  6. Send end-of-interrupt (EOI) — After all ISRs return, the flow handler signals EOI to the interrupt controller through chip->irq_eoi(). On x86_64 this writes the APIC EOI register, telling the controller the interrupt has been serviced and it may deliver the next pending interrupt.

    GICv3 is more interesting, because it has two EOI modes and the book’s description depends on which one is in use. The GIC separates two distinct notions that the APIC merges: priority drop, which lowers the running priority so further interrupts can preempt, and deactivation, which releases the interrupt so the same line may fire again.

    • EOImode 0 (the plain case) — a single write to ICC_EOIR1_EL1 does both, and it happens after the handlers, in gic_eoi_irq(). This matches the classic description.
    • EOImode 1 (selected when the GIC supports split deactivation, which KVM requires so it can hand an interrupt to a guest) — the priority drop is issued early, in gic_complete_ack(), before the handler runs; the deactivation is deferred to gic_eoimode1_eoi_irq() afterwards, which writes ICC_DIR_EL1. That path also declines to deactivate at all for an LPI or for an interrupt marked as forwarded to a vCPU, since in those cases the deactivation is someone else’s responsibility.

    Which mode is active is a static branch (supports_deactivate_key), so the cost of supporting both is close to zero at runtime. The practical consequence for a reader tracing the code is that on a virtualization-capable ARM64 machine you will find a write to ICC_EOIR1_EL1 on the entry side, and should not mistake it for the end-of-interrupt.

  7. Restore context and return — The architecture exit code calls irqentry_exit(), which checks for pending work exactly as described in “Entering and Leaving Exception Handlers” above: the full exit-to-user work loop if the interrupt came from user space, or at most an irqentry_exit_cond_resched() if it came from the kernel. Note the ordering relative to step 3: softirqs have already run, inside irq_exit_rcu() and on the interrupt stack, by the time control reaches here. The registers are restored from the pt_regs frame, and the CPU executes IRETQ (x86_64) or ERET (ARM64) to resume the interrupted context.

Table 4-33. I/O interrupt path comparison: Linux 2.6 vs 7.0.12

StepLinux 2.6.11Linux 7.0.12
1. Save registersSAVE_ALL macro, single stackPUSH_AND_CLEAR_REGS (x86_64) / kernel_entry (ARM64), KPTI trampoline
2. Find handlerdo_IRQ() indexes the global irq_desc[irq] arraycommon_interrupt() reads per-CPU vector_irq[], which holds irq_desc pointers directly; ARM64 reads GIC IAR + irq_domain
3. Stack switchx86 only, optional (CONFIG_4KSTACKS); ARM had no IRQ stackUnconditional on both, via run_irq_on_irqstack_cond() / call_on_irq_stack()
4. ACK controllerDirect PIC/APIC ack in do_IRQ()Flow handler calls chip->irq_ack() or defers to EOI
5. Run ISR chain__do_IRQ() → handle_IRQ_event()handle_fasteoi_irq() → handle_irq_event() → __handle_irq_event_percpu()
6. EOIImplicit or ack() at entryExplicit chip->irq_eoi() after ISR chain returns; GICv3 EOImode 1 splits priority drop from deactivation
7. Returnjmp ret_from_intrirqentry_exit() → work checks → IRETQ / ERET
Interrupt state in handlerPer-handler choice via SA_INTERRUPT / IRQF_DISABLEDAlways disabled; enforced by WARN_ONCE in __handle_irq_event_percpu()
Threaded handlersNot availableIRQ_WAKE_THREAD + request_threaded_irq(); mandatory under PREEMPT_RT

Figure 4-13 shows the complete path from hardware signal to handler return in Linux 7.0.12, through the modern generic IRQ layer. Note the coloring: only the blue boxes at the top and the purple one at the bottom contain architecture-specific code. Everything in green is kernel/irq/, shared verbatim between x86_64 and ARM64.

One aspect of the ARM64 path deserves a note here because it has no x86_64 equivalent and it is easy to miss when reading gic_handle_irq(). On a GIC configured for pseudo-NMIs, the same vector serves both ordinary interrupts and NMI-priority ones, so the handler must work out which it has before it does anything else. It does so indirectly: gic_handle_irq() first asks whether interrupts were enabled in the interrupted context. If they were disabled, the only thing that could have got through is an NMI, so it takes the __gic_handle_irq_from_irqsoff() path, which the entry code has already wrapped in NMI entry. If they were enabled, the interrupt could be either, so __gic_handle_irq_from_irqson() reads the IAR and then consults ICC_RPR_EL1 — the running priority, which the acknowledgment just set — to see whether it landed in the NMI band, wrapping the handler in nmi_enter()/nmi_exit() if so. Only after the NMI case is dealt with does it re-mask ordinary IRQs through ICC_PMR_EL1 and unmask DAIF.I, in that order, so that there is no window in which a normal IRQ could be taken while the CPU still believes it is in NMI context.

Several descriptors are needed to represent both the state of the IRQ lines and the functions to be executed when an interrupt occurs. The key data structures — struct irq_desc, struct irqaction, struct irq_data, and struct irq_chip — are discussed in the “I/O Interrupt Handling” section that follows.

I/O Interrupt Handling

The exception handlers above are architecture entry points. The I/O interrupt subsystem begins after the CPU has already decided that a device-generated interrupt is in flight. Linux’s generic IRQ layer abstracts controllers, routing, flow styles, affinity, statistics, threading, spurious detection, and dynamic allocation behind a common set of data structures. Drivers register handlers through this layer rather than programming hardware vectors directly.

Interrupt handler flexibility is achieved in two distinct ways:

IRQ sharing — Multiple devices may share a single IRQ line. When the interrupt fires, the kernel walks a linked list of interrupt service routines (ISRs), calling each one in turn. Each ISR checks whether its own device actually needs attention and, if so, performs the required work; otherwise it returns IRQ_NONE immediately. Sharing is the norm on modern systems: PCI and PCIe devices routinely share interrupt lines when MSI/MSI-X is unavailable or deliberately disabled.

IRQ dynamic allocation — An IRQ line is associated with a device driver at the last possible moment, typically when the device is first opened or when the driver is loaded. When the driver no longer needs the line (e.g., the device file is closed, the module is unloaded, or the filesystem is unmounted), it releases the IRQ. This allows a single IRQ number to be time-shared by several devices that are never active simultaneously — even devices that do not support IRQ sharing.

Interrupt Vectors

Before examining the data structures, it is important to understand how the 256 IDT vectors (on x86_64) and the GICv3 interrupt ID space (on ARM64) are partitioned. The vector/interrupt-ID layout determines which hardware events reach which handler code, and it constrains where device interrupts can be placed.

x86_64 Vector Layout

On x86_64, the IDT has 256 entries (vectors 0–255). Linux partitions them as shown in the following table, defined in arch/x86/include/asm/irq_vectors.h:

Table 4-34. x86_64 IDT vector layout in Linux 7.0.12

Vector rangeHex rangeUse
0–310x00–0x1FCPU exceptions and NMI (hardcoded by the processor)
32–1270x20–0x7FDevice interrupts (external IRQs)
1280x80Legacy int 0x80 syscall interface (IA-32 compat)
129–2340x81–0xEADevice interrupts (external IRQs, continued)
235 (0xEB)POSTED_MSI_NOTIFICATION_VECTORPosted MSI notification for host-kernel MSI delivery
236 (0xEC)LOCAL_TIMER_VECTORLocal APIC timer interrupt
237 (0xED)HYPERV_STIMER0_VECTORHyper-V synthetic timer (if CONFIG_HYPERV)
238 (0xEE)HYPERV_REENLIGHTENMENT_VECTORHyper-V re-enlightenment (if CONFIG_HYPERV)
239 (0xEF)MANAGED_IRQ_SHUTDOWN_VECTORManaged IRQ shutdown during CPU offline
240–242 (0xF0–0xF2)POSTED_INTR_*_VECTORKVM posted interrupt vectors (nested, wakeup, notification)
243 (0xF3)HYPERVISOR_CALLBACK_VECTORHypervisor callback (Xen, etc.)
244 (0xF4)DEFERRED_ERROR_VECTORDeferred machine-check error
245 (0xF5)PERF_GUEST_MEDIATED_PMI_VECTORMediated PMU performance monitoring interrupt
246 (0xF6)IRQ_WORK_VECTORIRQ work (deferred work from NMI-safe contexts)
247 (0xF7)X86_PLATFORM_IPI_VECTORPlatform-specific IPI (UV, Xen, etc.)
248 (0xF8)REBOOT_VECTORReboot IPI (stop CPU during reboot)
249 (0xF9)THRESHOLD_APIC_VECTORAMD machine-check threshold
250 (0xFA)THERMAL_APIC_VECTORThermal monitoring interrupt
251 (0xFB)CALL_FUNCTION_SINGLE_VECTORIPI: execute function on one remote CPU
252 (0xFC)CALL_FUNCTION_VECTORIPI: execute function on all remote CPUs
253 (0xFD)RESCHEDULE_VECTORIPI: reschedule (wake target CPU’s scheduler)
254 (0xFE)ERROR_APIC_VECTORLocal APIC error interrupt
255 (0xFF)SPURIOUS_APIC_VECTORLocal APIC spurious interrupt

Key constants from irq_vectors.h:

#define FIRST_EXTERNAL_VECTOR     0x20    /* Vectors 0x00–0x1F are exceptions */
#define IA32_SYSCALL_VECTOR       0x80    /* Legacy int 0x80 syscall         */
#define FIRST_SYSTEM_VECTOR       0xEB    /* POSTED_MSI_NOTIFICATION_VECTOR  */
#define NR_VECTORS                256
#define NR_EXTERNAL_VECTORS       (FIRST_SYSTEM_VECTOR - FIRST_EXTERNAL_VECTOR)  /* 203 */
#define NR_SYSTEM_VECTORS         (NR_VECTORS - FIRST_SYSTEM_VECTOR)             /* 21  */

Vectors 32 through FIRST_SYSTEM_VECTOR - 1 (0x20–0xEA, 203 vectors) form the external interrupt space available for I/O APIC pin-based interrupts and MSI/MSI-X delivery. The APIC dynamically assigns vectors from this pool when irq_domain mapping requests a hardware vector via irq_matrix_alloc(). The high vectors (0xEB–0xFF, 21 vectors) are system vectors reserved for APIC-internal, IPI, and platform-specific uses — they are never assigned to device interrupts.

Two details of that pool deserve mention because they explain numbers a reader will see in /proc/interrupts. First, the sixteen legacy ISA IRQs are not assigned dynamically; they occupy a fixed window computed by a macro:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/irq_vectors.h
 * Vectors 0x30-0x3f are used for ISA interrupts:
 * round up to the next 16-vector boundary.
 */
#define ISA_IRQ_VECTOR(irq)   (((FIRST_EXTERNAL_VECTOR + 16) & ~15) + irq)

With FIRST_EXTERNAL_VECTOR at 0x20, this rounds to 0x30, so ISA IRQ n lands on vector 0x30 + n — which is why the timer on IRQ 0 shows up at vector 0x30 and the keyboard on IRQ 1 at 0x31 in the table below. Second, vectors are allocated at 16-vector granularity by the APIC vector matrix rather than one at a time, so a system with few devices leaves large gaps in the numbering.

Each CPU has its own per-CPU vector_irq[] array that maps vector numbers to irq_desc pointers. This per-CPU mapping is what allows different CPUs to use the same vector number for different devices when IRQ affinity changes move interrupts between processors.

ARM64 GICv3 Interrupt ID Space

ARM64 does not use an IDT. Instead, the GICv3 Generic Interrupt Controller assigns each interrupt source a hardware INTID (interrupt identifier). The INTID space is partitioned as follows:

Table 4-35. ARM64 GICv3 INTID ranges

INTID rangeTypeScopeDescription
0–15SGI (Software Generated Interrupt)Per-CPUUsed for IPIs between CPUs
16–31PPI (Private Peripheral Interrupt)Per-CPUPer-CPU timers, PMU, watchdog, etc.
32–1019SPI (Shared Peripheral Interrupt)GlobalShared device interrupts (UART, USB, PCIe, etc.)
1020–1023Special—Reserved (1023 = spurious)
1024–1055——Reserved
1056–1119EPPI (Extended PPI)Per-CPUExtended PPIs (GICv3.1+)
4096–5119ESPI (Extended SPI)GlobalExtended SPIs (GICv3.1+)
8192–16777215LPI (Locality-specific Peripheral Interrupt)GlobalMSI/MSI-X (ITS-routed, GICv3+); the upper bound depends on GICD_TYPER.IDbits and is capped at 224−1 by __get_intid_range()

Key differences from x86_64:

  • No vector table indirection for device IRQs — The CPU reads ICC_IAR1_EL1 to get the INTID directly; there is no per-CPU vector-to-descriptor mapping array like x86_64’s vector_irq[].
  • SGIs replace dedicated IPI vectors — Instead of fixed IDT vectors for each IPI type, ARM64 uses SGI INTIDs 0–15 and distinguishes IPI purpose by a software-defined message number.
  • LPIs for MSI — ARM64 uses the GICv3 ITS (Interrupt Translation Service) to map MSI writes from PCIe devices to LPI INTIDs. This is analogous to x86_64’s MSI vector allocation but uses a completely different hardware mechanism.
  • Flat namespace — All interrupt sources share a single INTID namespace routed by the GIC Distributor and Redistributors, rather than being split across IDT + APIC + I/O APIC layers.

How Devices Get Their Vectors/INTIDs

In the Linux 2.6 era, there were three ways to select an IRQ line for an IRQ-configurable device:

  • Hardware jumpers — On very old ISA cards, physical jumper pins on the board selected the IRQ line (e.g., moving a jumper to select IRQ 5 or IRQ 7).
  • Vendor utility programs — Some devices shipped with DOS/BIOS-level utilities that probed for available IRQ lines and programmed the device’s IRQ register.
  • Hardware negotiation at boot (PCI) — The PCI standard introduced automatic negotiation. During system startup, the BIOS/firmware enumerates all PCI devices, examines which interrupt pins they declare, and assigns IRQ lines to minimize conflicts. The kernel then reads the assigned values from PCI configuration space (register 0x3C Interrupt Line, 0x3D Interrupt Pin) using functions like pci_read_config_byte().

In Linux 7.0.12, the first two methods are obsolete. Modern systems use three firmware-driven and software-driven mechanisms:

  1. Firmware/device-tree assignment — On ARM64 (and ACPI-based x86_64 systems), interrupt assignments are declared in firmware data: the device tree (interrupts property specifying SPI/PPI number, trigger type, and priority) or ACPI tables (MADT for GIC/APIC topology, DSDT/SSDT _CRS resources for per-device IRQs). The kernel’s irq_domain layer maps these hardware INTIDs or GSIs (Global System Interrupts) to Linux IRQ numbers during device probing. Platform drivers call platform_get_irq() to retrieve the Linux IRQ number resolved from the device tree or ACPI: 
    irq = platform_get_irq(pdev, 0);   /* Get first IRQ from DT/ACPI */
if (irq < 0)
    return irq;
ret = request_irq(irq, my_handler, 0, "mydev", priv);
  2. PCI legacy interrupt pins — PCI and PCIe devices declare their interrupt pin (INTA–INTD) in configuration register 0x3D. The BIOS/firmware maps each PCI interrupt pin to a system interrupt (I/O APIC input on x86_64, SPI on ARM64 GICv3) and writes the result to configuration register 0x3C. The kernel reads this mapping during PCI enumeration, creates an irq_domain mapping, and stores the resulting Linux IRQ number in pdev->irq. On x86_64, ACPI’s _PRT (PCI Routing Table) methods or the MP table translate PCI pins to I/O APIC entries; on ARM64, the device tree interrupt-map property does the equivalent.
  3. MSI/MSI-X auto-allocation — Modern PCIe devices bypass physical interrupt lines entirely. Instead, the device writes a small message (containing a vector number and a destination address) directly to a special memory-mapped address. Drivers request MSI or MSI-X vectors via pci_alloc_irq_vectors(): 
    /* Request up to 8 MSI-X vectors, fall back to 1 MSI */
nr = pci_alloc_irq_vectors(pdev, 1, 8, PCI_IRQ_MSIX | PCI_IRQ_MSI);
if (nr < 0)
    return nr;

/* Get the Linux IRQ number for each allocated vector */
for (i = 0; i < nr; i++) {
    irq = pci_irq_vector(pdev, i);
    request_irq(irq, my_queue_handler, 0, "mydev-q", &queues[i]);
}
    On x86_64, this allocates IDT vectors from the external interrupt pool (vectors 0x20–0xEA) via irq_matrix_alloc(). On ARM64, the GICv3 ITS (Interrupt Translation Service) allocates LPI INTIDs. MSI-X can provide up to 2048 individual interrupt vectors per device, enabling per-queue interrupts for high-performance NICs, NVMe controllers, and GPU compute engines.

Table 4-36. Example IRQ assignments on a modern x86_64 server (from /proc/interrupts)
