The kernel must discover which I/O device corresponds to which interrupt number before enabling interrupts. This correspondence is established while initializing each device driver (see Chapter 13).

Core IRQ Data Structures

As always when discussing complicated operations involving state transitions, it helps to understand first where key data is stored. Several descriptors are needed to represent both the state of IRQ lines and the functions to be executed when an interrupt occurs. The modern Linux generic IRQ layer (genirq) uses four principal data structures that form a layered abstraction:

  • struct irq_desc — The per-IRQ-line descriptor. Every Linux IRQ number has exactly one irq_desc. It holds the flow handler, the action chain, state flags, locking, and statistics. This is the successor of the old irq_desc_t from Linux 2.6.
  • struct irqaction — A registered interrupt handler. Each device driver that calls request_irq() or request_threaded_irq() creates one irqaction. Multiple irqaction structures can be chained together on a shared IRQ line via the next pointer.
  • struct irq_data — The controller-facing view of an IRQ. It holds the hardware IRQ number (hwirq), the irq_chip pointer, the irq_domain, and chip-private data. This layer isolates drivers from controller internals.
  • struct irq_chip — The interrupt controller abstraction (the “PIC object”). It contains function pointers for masking, unmasking, acknowledging, EOI-signaling, affinity setting, and other hardware operations. This is the successor of the old hw_interrupt_type / hw_irq_controller from Linux 2.6.

Figure 4-14 shows how these structures relate to each other. Follow the pointers outward from irq_desc: one way leads to the controller that owns the line, the other to the list of drivers that asked to be told about it.

struct irq_desc — The IRQ Line Descriptor

Every interrupt vector has its own irq_desc descriptor. In Linux 7.0.12 with CONFIG_SPARSE_IRQ (the default on x86_64 and ARM64), descriptors are allocated dynamically from a maple tree keyed by the Linux IRQ number, rather than the old fixed-size irq_desc[NR_IRQS] array. (Two different trees appear in this chapter and it is easy to conflate them: the descriptor table sparse_irqs is a maple tree, looked up by mtree_load(), whereas an irq_domain's reverse map for hwirqs that do not fit its linear table is a radix_tree_root. They answer different questions — “which irq_desc is Linux IRQ n?” versus “which Linux IRQ is this controller's line h?”)

/* include/linux/irqdesc.h */
struct irq_desc {
    struct irq_common_data irq_common_data;
    struct irq_data        irq_data;
    struct irqstat __percpu *kstat_irqs;
    irq_flow_handler_t     handle_irq;
    struct irqaction       *action;
    unsigned int           status_use_accessors;
    unsigned int           core_internal_state__do_not_mess_with_it;
    unsigned int           depth;
    unsigned int           wake_depth;
    unsigned int           tot_count;
    unsigned int           irq_count;
    unsigned long          last_unhandled;
    unsigned int           irqs_unhandled;
    atomic_t               threads_handled;
    int                    threads_handled_last;
    raw_spinlock_t         lock;
    unsigned long          threads_oneshot;
    atomic_t               threads_active;
    wait_queue_head_t      wait_for_threads;
    struct mutex           request_mutex;
    int                    parent_irq;
    struct module          *owner;
    const char             *name;
};

Table 4-39. Important fields of struct irq_desc

FieldPurpose
irq_common_dataGeneric per-IRQ metadata shared with chips and domains
irq_dataController-facing view containing hwirq number, chip pointer, domain, and chip-specific data
kstat_irqsPer-CPU interrupt counters for statistics and /proc/interrupts
handle_irqSelected flow handler such as handle_level_irq() or handle_fasteoi_irq()
actionHead of the linked list of registered irqaction handlers
status_use_accessorsStatus flags (use irq_settings_*() accessors, never access directly)
depthNested disable depth; nonzero means logically disabled
wake_depthNested wake-enable accounting for suspend wakeup semantics
tot_countTotal interrupt occurrences observed
irq_countCounter used in broken/spurious interrupt detection
last_unhandledjiffies timestamp for aging unhandled-interrupt detection
irqs_unhandledNumber of unhandled occurrences in the spurious detection heuristic
threads_handledThreaded-IRQ success accounting
threads_handled_lastPrevious threaded-IRQ count, used by spurious detection
lockRaw spinlock protecting descriptor state
threads_oneshotBitmask of oneshot threaded handlers currently active
threads_activeNumber of IRQ threads currently running for this descriptor
wait_for_threadsWait queue used by synchronize_irq() to block until threads complete
request_mutexSerializes request_irq() / free_irq() and reconfiguration
parent_irqHierarchical-domain parent IRQ when applicable
ownerModule owning the descriptor’s chip/domain relationship
nameHuman-readable name shown in procfs/debug output

The depth field and enable/disable nesting

The depth field and the disable status of irq_desc work together to implement nested enable/disable semantics. Every time disable_irq() or disable_irq_nosync() is invoked, the depth field is incremented; if depth was 0, the function masks the IRQ line at the controller. Conversely, each invocation of enable_irq() decrements depth; when depth returns to 0, the function unmasks the IRQ line:

/* kernel/irq/manage.c — simplified */
static void __disable_irq(struct irq_desc *desc)
{
    if (!desc->depth++)        /* First disable? Mask the line */
        irq_disable(desc);
}

static void __enable_irq(struct irq_desc *desc)
{
    switch (desc->depth) {
    case 1:
        desc->depth--;         /* Reached 0 → unmask the line */
        irq_enable(desc);
        break;
    default:
        desc->depth--;         /* Still nested → just decrement */
    }
}

The key difference between disable_irq() and disable_irq_nosync(): disable_irq() calls synchronize_irq() after disabling, which waits for any currently-executing handler instances on other CPUs to complete. disable_irq_nosync() returns immediately — suitable for use from within interrupt context where waiting would deadlock.

Spurious interrupt detection

An interrupt is unexpected if no ISR in the action chain claims it (all return IRQ_NONE). note_interrupt() in kernel/irq/spurious.c monitors the return values after each interrupt and updates the counters in the descriptor. The kernel stores the total count and the unhandled count in irq_count and irqs_unhandled. When the 100,000th interrupt is observed (controlled by desc->irq_count), the kernel checks: if irqs_unhandled exceeds 99,900 (i.e., fewer than 100 out of 100,000 interrupts were handled), the line is disabled and the kernel prints:

irq N: nobody cared (try booting with the "irqpoll" option)

This conservative heuristic avoids disabling lines that are merely occasionally spurious — only persistent storms trigger shutdown. A false positive would silence a live device, so the threshold is intentionally generous. The threaded-IRQ case defers the check by one hardware interrupt because the actual handler result arrives only after the IRQ thread completes, which is why threads_handled and threads_handled_last exist. When the heuristics do conclude that an IRQ is broken or stuck, Linux disables it and prints diagnostics showing the descriptor and the registered handlers, giving the administrator enough information to identify the offending device.

Initialization. All irq_desc descriptors are set up early during boot by early_irq_init() (kernel/irq/irqdesc.c). With CONFIG_SPARSE_IRQ (the default on x86_64 and ARM64), the function pre-allocates a set of descriptors (the count is architecture-dependent, returned by arch_probe_nr_irqs()) and inserts them into a maple-tree keyed by Linux IRQ number. Each descriptor is dynamically allocated by alloc_desc(), which calls kzalloc_node() followed by init_desc(). Without CONFIG_SPARSE_IRQ, a fixed array irq_desc[NR_IRQS] is used instead, statically initialized with handle_bad_irq and depth = 1.

The core defaults are established by desc_set_defaults():

/* kernel/irq/irqdesc.c — desc_set_defaults() */
desc->irq_data.common   = &desc->irq_common_data;
desc->irq_data.irq       = irq;
desc->irq_data.chip       = &no_irq_chip;     /* dummy until a real chip is wired */
desc->irq_data.chip_data  = NULL;
desc->handle_irq          = handle_bad_irq;    /* panics if called before setup */
desc->depth               = 1;                 /* born disabled */
desc->irq_count           = 0;
desc->irqs_unhandled      = 0;
irqd_set(&desc->irq_data, IRQD_IRQ_DISABLED);
irqd_set(&desc->irq_data, IRQD_IRQ_MASKED);

The descriptor starts life in a “born disabled” state (depth = 1, IRQD_IRQ_DISABLED | IRQD_IRQ_MASKED) with handle_bad_irq as the flow handler. Later, when the interrupt controller driver (e.g., GICv3, IO-APIC) initializes an IRQ domain and maps hardware interrupts, it calls irq_set_chip_and_handler() to install the real irq_chip and flow handler (e.g., handle_fasteoi_irq). When a device driver calls request_irq(), __setup_irq() decrements depth to 0 and enables the line.

struct irqaction — The Interrupt Service Routine Descriptor

As described earlier, multiple devices can share a single IRQ line. Therefore, the kernel maintains irqaction descriptors, each of which refers to a specific hardware device and a specific interrupt service routine. When multiple devices share an IRQ, their irqaction descriptors are linked in a singly-linked list via the next pointer, forming the shared-IRQ action chain.

/* include/linux/interrupt.h */
struct irqaction {
    irq_handler_t          handler;
    union {
        void               *dev_id;
        void __percpu      *percpu_dev_id;
    };
    const struct cpumask   *affinity;
    struct irqaction       *next;
    irq_handler_t          thread_fn;
    struct task_struct     *thread;
    struct irqaction       *secondary;
    unsigned int           irq;
    unsigned int           flags;
    unsigned long          thread_flags;
    unsigned long          thread_mask;
    const char             *name;
    struct proc_dir_entry  *dir;
};

Table 4-40. Fields of struct irqaction

FieldPurpose
handlerPrimary hardirq handler invoked from __handle_irq_event_percpu()
dev_idDriver cookie — uniquely identifies this device for handler dispatch and free_irq() matching
percpu_dev_idPer-CPU alternative to dev_id for per-CPU interrupts
affinityPer-action affinity hint when relevant
nextNext action in a shared-IRQ chain (NULL if sole handler or end of list)
thread_fnThreaded follow-up handler — runs in a dedicated kernel thread when primary returns IRQ_WAKE_THREAD
threadPointer to the kernel thread executing thread_fn (created at registration time)
secondarySecondary action used by forced-threading setups
irqLinux IRQ number associated with this action
flagsRegistration flags — see table below
thread_flagsRuntime bits such as IRQTF_RUNTHREAD, IRQTF_AFFINITY, IRQTF_READY
thread_maskBit assigned within desc->threads_oneshot for oneshot synchronization
nameName shown in /proc/interrupts (e.g., “eth0”, “nvme0q0”)
dirProcfs directory entry for this action

Table 4-41. Registration flags for struct irqaction

FlagValueDescription
IRQF_SHARED0x00000080The IRQ line may be shared with other devices
IRQF_PROBE_SHARED0x00000100Caller expects sharing mismatches. This does not make a mismatch legal — __setup_irq() still fails with -EBUSY; the flag only suppresses the "Flags mismatch irq…" error message and the CONFIG_DEBUG_SHIRQ stack dump
__IRQF_TIMER0x00000200Raw timer-interrupt bit. Drivers use the composite IRQF_TIMER = __IRQF_TIMER | IRQF_NO_SUSPEND | IRQF_NO_THREAD, so marking a handler as a timer also exempts it from suspend and from force-threading
IRQF_PERCPU0x00000400Interrupt is per-CPU (no affinity balancing)
IRQF_NOBALANCING0x00000800Exclude from IRQ balancing
IRQF_IRQPOLL0x00001000Used for IRQ polling/recovery
IRQF_ONESHOT0x00002000Keep line masked until threaded handler completes
IRQF_NO_SUSPEND0x00004000Do not disable during suspend
IRQF_FORCE_RESUME0x00008000Force re-enable on resume
IRQF_NO_THREAD0x00010000Never force-thread this handler
IRQF_EARLY_RESUME0x00020000Resume early during syscore phase
IRQF_COND_SUSPEND0x00040000Conditionally disable for shared lines during suspend
IRQF_NO_AUTOEN0x00080000Do not auto-enable at registration time
IRQF_NO_DEBUG0x00100000Exclude from note_interrupt() runaway detection; intended for IPI and similar handlers, and only meaningful together with IRQF_PERCPU
IRQF_COND_ONESHOT0x00200000“Agree to IRQF_ONESHOT if the line already uses it.” Lets a driver join a shared oneshot line without unconditionally demanding oneshot semantics of its own
IRQF_TRIGGER_NONE … IRQF_TRIGGER_LOW0x0–0x8Trigger type: none, rising, falling, high, low. These occupy the low four bits (IRQF_TRIGGER_MASK) and must match across all sharers of a line

Initialization. An irqaction is allocated and populated by request_threaded_irq() (kernel/irq/manage.c), the primary API for driver IRQ registration:

/* kernel/irq/manage.c — request_threaded_irq() */
action = kzalloc_obj(struct irqaction);

action->handler   = handler;       /* primary hardirq handler */
action->thread_fn = thread_fn;     /* threaded follow-up (may be NULL) */
action->flags     = irqflags;      /* IRQF_SHARED, IRQF_ONESHOT, etc. */
action->name      = devname;       /* shown in /proc/interrupts */
action->dev_id    = dev_id;        /* cookie for shared-IRQ disambiguation */

retval = __setup_irq(irq, desc, action);

If handler is NULL but thread_fn is set, the kernel substitutes irq_default_primary_handler (which simply returns IRQ_WAKE_THREAD). The __setup_irq() function then:

  1. Validates sharing rules (both old and new action must have IRQF_SHARED, trigger types must match).
  2. If thread_fn is not NULL and the IRQ is not nested-threaded, creates a dedicated kernel thread via setup_irq_thread() — the thread name appears as irq/%d-%s in ps output.
  3. Links the action into the tail of desc->action chain.
  4. If this is the first action on the line, calls irq_startup() which unmasks the interrupt and decrements depth to 0.

struct irq_data — The Controller-Facing View

The irq_data structure sits inside irq_desc and provides the interface between the generic IRQ layer and the hardware controller. All irq_chip callbacks receive an irq_data pointer rather than a raw IRQ number, which allows hierarchical interrupt controllers to stack — each level has its own irq_data with its own chip, domain, and hwirq.

/* include/linux/irq.h */
struct irq_data {
    u32                     mask;
    unsigned int            irq;
    irq_hw_number_t         hwirq;
    struct irq_common_data  *common;
    struct irq_chip         *chip;
    struct irq_domain       *domain;
#ifdef CONFIG_IRQ_DOMAIN_HIERARCHY
    struct irq_data         *parent_data;
#endif
    void                    *chip_data;
};

Table 4-42. Fields of struct irq_data

FieldPurpose
maskChip-local bit mask or selector for the interrupt source
irqLinux virtual IRQ number
hwirqHardware IRQ number inside the owning domain (e.g., GIC INTID, I/O APIC pin)
commonShared state and flags manipulated through irqd_* accessors
chipController operations for this IRQ (pointer to irq_chip)
domainIRQ domain translating between hwirq and Linux IRQ
parent_dataParent-level irq_data in hierarchical domains (e.g., GIC → ITS → MSI)
chip_dataController-private pointer (e.g., GIC distributor base address)

The separation of irq (software namespace) from hwirq (hardware namespace) is fundamental. A Linux IRQ number is a software-assigned integer with no inherent hardware meaning. The irq_domain maps between the two: hwirq is the number meaningful to the controller (e.g., GIC INTID 45), while irq is what drivers and /proc/interrupts display.

Initialization. Because irq_data is embedded directly inside irq_desc, its initial values are set by desc_set_defaults() during boot — irq is set to the Linux IRQ number, chip is set to &no_irq_chip (a dummy), and chip_data is NULL.

The real hardware binding happens in two phases:

  1. Chip and handler wiring — When an interrupt controller driver initializes, it calls irq_set_chip_and_handler() for each IRQ line it owns. This invokes irq_set_chip() (kernel/irq/chip.c) which stores the irq_chip pointer, and __irq_set_handler() which installs the appropriate flow handler (e.g., handle_fasteoi_irq).

  2. Domain mapping — The controller’s irq_domain_ops->map() callback is invoked by irq_domain_associate_locked() (kernel/irq/irqdomain.c), which sets the hardware-side fields:

    /* kernel/irq/irqdomain.c — irq_domain_associate_locked() */
irq_data->hwirq  = hwirq;       /* controller-local ID (e.g., GIC INTID) */
irq_data->domain  = domain;      /* owning irq_domain */
domain->ops->map(domain, virq, hwirq);  /* controller-specific setup */

    The map() callback typically calls irq_domain_set_info() which in turn sets the chip, handler, chip_data, and handler_data all at once. For hierarchical domains, each level in the hierarchy gets its own irq_data with its own chip and hwirq, linked via parent_data.

struct irq_chip — The Interrupt Controller Abstraction

To handle different interrupt controllers (8259A PIC, I/O APIC, Local APIC, GICv3, GICv4, ITS, etc.) in a uniform way, Linux uses the irq_chip abstraction — the successor to the old hw_interrupt_type / hw_irq_controller structure. Each controller driver fills in a static irq_chip instance with function pointers for its hardware-specific operations. The advantage of this object-oriented approach is that flow handlers and drivers need not be aware of which controller is installed — each interrupt source is transparently wired to the appropriate chip.

For concreteness, here are two real irq_chip instances from Linux 7.0.12:

x86_64 Local APIC controller (arch/x86/kernel/apic/vector.c):

static struct irq_chip lapic_controller = {
    .name                    = "APIC",
    .irq_ack                 = apic_ack_edge,
    .irq_set_affinity        = apic_set_affinity,
    .irq_compose_msi_msg     = x86_vector_msi_compose_msg,
    .irq_force_complete_move = apic_force_complete_move,
    .irq_retrigger           = apic_retrigger_irq,
};

ARM64 GICv3 controller (drivers/irqchip/irq-gic-v3.c):

static struct irq_chip gic_chip = {
    .name                  = "GICv3",
    .irq_mask              = gic_mask_irq,
    .irq_unmask            = gic_unmask_irq,
    .irq_eoi               = gic_eoi_irq,
    .irq_set_type          = gic_set_type,
    .irq_set_affinity      = gic_set_affinity,
    .irq_retrigger         = gic_retrigger,
    .irq_get_irqchip_state = gic_irq_get_irqchip_state,
    .irq_set_irqchip_state = gic_irq_set_irqchip_state,
    .irq_nmi_setup         = gic_irq_nmi_setup,
    .irq_nmi_teardown      = gic_irq_nmi_teardown,
    .ipi_send_mask         = gic_ipi_send_mask,
    .flags                 = IRQCHIP_SET_TYPE_MASKED |
                             IRQCHIP_SKIP_SET_WAKE |
                             IRQCHIP_MASK_ON_SUSPEND,
};

Notice how the APIC chip provides irq_ack (edge-style acknowledge at entry) but no irq_eoi (EOI is handled separately by the flow handler), while the GICv3 chip provides irq_eoi (fasteoi-style) but no irq_ack. This reflects the fundamental difference in how these controllers work — the flow handler selected for each IRQ line (e.g., handle_edge_irq vs. handle_fasteoi_irq) calls the appropriate chip methods.

The GICv3 driver is worth a second look, because gic_chip is not the structure most systems actually use. The driver defines two chips and selects between them with a static key:

/* drivers/irqchip/irq-gic-v3.c */
static DEFINE_STATIC_KEY_TRUE(supports_deactivate_key);

static struct irq_chip gic_eoimode1_chip = {
    .name                  = "GICv3",
    .irq_mask              = gic_eoimode1_mask_irq,
    .irq_unmask            = gic_unmask_irq,
    .irq_eoi               = gic_eoimode1_eoi_irq,
    .irq_set_vcpu_affinity = gic_irq_set_vcpu_affinity,
    /* … remaining members identical to gic_chip … */
};

static int gic_irq_domain_map(struct irq_domain *d, unsigned int irq,
                              irq_hw_number_t hw)
{
    struct irq_chip *chip = &gic_chip;

    if (static_branch_likely(&supports_deactivate_key))
        chip = &gic_eoimode1_chip;
    ...
}

supports_deactivate_key is declared with DEFINE_STATIC_KEY_TRUE, so EOImode 1 is the default; gic_init_bases() turns the key off only when the kernel was not entered at EL2 (!is_hyp_mode_available()), because the split exists mainly so that KVM can leave the deactivation of a forwarded interrupt to the guest, and it reports the choice with the boot message GIC: Using split EOI/Deactivate mode. The practical consequences are worth spelling out because they change the meaning of “EOI” described earlier in this chapter:

  • Acknowledgment is split in two. Under EOImode 1, gic_complete_ack() writes ICC_EOIR1_EL1 immediately after reading the IAR — that write performs only the priority drop. The interrupt remains active in the distributor until gic_eoimode1_eoi_irq() writes ICC_DIR_EL1 from chip->irq_eoi(). Under EOImode 0 there is a single ICC_EOIR1_EL1 write, issued by gic_eoi_irq() at the end.
  • Masking differs. gic_eoimode1_mask_irq() both masks the line and drops the priority, because a line that is masked while still active would otherwise block lower-priority interrupts.
  • KVM passthrough becomes possible. Only the EOImode 1 chip supplies irq_set_vcpu_affinity. Deferred deactivation is precisely what lets a hypervisor hand an active physical interrupt to a guest and let the guest perform the deactivation.
  • LPIs and forwarded interrupts are excluded. gic_eoimode1_eoi_irq() skips the ICC_DIR_EL1 write for hwirq >= 8192 (LPIs are never “active” in the distributor sense) and for interrupts marked IRQD_FORWARDED_TO_VCPU.

gic_irq_domain_map() also decides the flow handler from the INTID range: SGIs, PPIs, and EPPIs get irq_set_percpu_devid() plus handle_percpu_devid_irq; SPIs, ESPIs, and LPIs get handle_fasteoi_irq. SPIs and ESPIs additionally get irq_set_probe() and irqd_set_single_target(). Finally, every line gets irqd_set_handle_enforce_irqctx(), which forbids software injection of the interrupt from non-interrupt context — the source comment explains why: a software retrigger would “mess up the ACK/EOI ordering” that the two-phase EOImode 1 protocol depends on.

Table 4-43. Key methods of struct irq_chip

MethodWhen calledPurpose
irq_startup / irq_shutdownFirst request_irq() / last free_irq()Initialize/tear down the hardware source
irq_enable / irq_disableenable_irq() / disable_irq()Logically enable or disable delivery
irq_ackFlow handler (edge-type)Acknowledge an edge-triggered interrupt at the controller
irq_mask / irq_unmaskFlow handler, disable_irq()Mask or unmask the source without acknowledging
irq_mask_ackFlow handlerCombined mask-and-ack fast path
irq_eoiFlow handler (fasteoi-type)Signal end-of-interrupt to the controller
irq_set_affinityirq_set_affinity_hint(), irqbalanceProgram hardware routing toward a CPU mask
irq_set_typeirq_set_irq_type()Change trigger type (edge/level, rising/falling)
irq_set_wakeenable_irq_wake()Mark source as suspend wakeup capable
irq_retriggerSoftware-triggered replayRe-fire a pending or lost interrupt
irq_compose_msi_msgMSI setupBuild the MSI message (address + data) for this IRQ
irq_set_vcpu_affinityKVM passthroughRoute interrupt toward a guest vCPU
ipi_send_single / ipi_send_masksmp_cross_call()Send controller-native IPIs
irq_nmi_setup / irq_nmi_teardownenable_nmi()Prepare NMI delivery for eligible lines

Initialization. Unlike the other three structures, irq_chip instances are not dynamically allocated — they are declared as static const (or static) structures in controller drivers and live for the lifetime of the kernel. Each controller driver fills in a compile-time-constant irq_chip with function pointers for its hardware operations.

The chip is then wired to individual IRQ lines through two mechanisms:

  1. Explicit wiring — irq_set_chip_and_handler_name() (kernel/irq/chip.c) calls irq_set_chip() to store the chip pointer in irq_data.chip, then __irq_set_handler() to install the matching flow handler.
  2. Domain mapping — Controller irq_domain_ops->map() callbacks typically call irq_domain_set_info(), which sets chip, handler, and chip_data in one call. For example, the GICv3 gic_irq_domain_map() (drivers/irqchip/irq-gic-v3.c) wires gic_chip with handle_fasteoi_irq for SPIs and handle_percpu_devid_irq for PPIs.

On x86_64, the wiring happens during APIC and IO-APIC initialization — lapic_controller is installed as the chip for MSI and IPI vectors. On ARM64, gic_of_init() → gic_init_bases() creates the IRQ domain, and the map() callback installs gic_chip per-IRQ as devices are probed or device-tree entries are parsed.

Table 4-44. IRQ data structure comparison: Linux 2.6 vs 7.0.12

AspectLinux 2.6.11Linux 7.0.12
IRQ descriptorirq_desc_t — fixed array irq_desc[NR_IRQS]struct irq_desc — dynamically allocated, indexed by a maple tree
PIC abstractionhw_interrupt_type / hw_irq_controller with 7 methodsstruct irq_chip with 25+ methods
Controller datahandler pointer in irq_desc_tstruct irq_data with chip, domain, hwirq, parent_data
IRQ handlerstruct irqaction with SA_* flagsstruct irqaction with IRQF_* flags + thread_fn
Flow handling__do_IRQ() — single monolithic functionPer-line flow handlers: handle_fasteoi_irq(), handle_edge_irq(), etc.
HierarchyNone — flat controller modelirq_domain hierarchy: MSI → ITS → GIC, or pin → I/O APIC → APIC
Per-CPU statsirq_cpustat_t with __softirq_pending, NMI count, etc.Per-CPU kstat_irqs in irq_desc + softirq state in struct irq_stat
Thread supportNoneBuilt-in: thread_fn, per-action IRQ threads, IRQF_ONESHOT
Lockingspin_lock in irq_desc_traw_spinlock_t lock + struct mutex request_mutex

The following diagram shows the three-phase initialization sequence that brings the IRQ subsystem from raw boot to fully operational. Phase 1 (early boot) creates descriptors with safe defaults. Phase 2 (controller init) wires real hardware chips and flow handlers. Phase 3 (driver probe) registers handlers and enables interrupt delivery.

Figure 4-15 draws all three phases as one call flow, so it is easy to see which structures exist after each phase and which fields are still holding placeholder values.

IRQ Status Flags

In Linux 2.6, each irq_desc_t had a single status field carrying all state as IRQ_* flags (IRQ_INPROGRESS, IRQ_DISABLED, IRQ_PENDING, IRQ_REPLAY, IRQ_AUTODETECT, IRQ_WAITING, IRQ_LEVEL, IRQ_MASKED, and IRQ_PER_CPU).

Linux 7.0.12 splits that single field into three separate state stores, each with its own namespace, access rules, and locking semantics:

  1. status_use_accessors (in struct irq_desc) — High-level settings that are configured once at setup time and rarely change afterwards. These describe the character of the IRQ line (per-cpu, level, no-probe, nested thread, etc.). The field is accessed exclusively through irq_settings_*() helper functions defined in kernel/irq/settings.h. Direct access is intentionally discouraged — the after-definition macros redefine the old IRQ_* symbols to GOT_YOU_MORON to catch any code that tries to use them raw:

    /* kernel/irq/settings.h — after enum definition */
#define IRQ_PER_CPU         GOT_YOU_MORON
#define IRQ_NO_BALANCING    GOT_YOU_MORON
#define IRQ_LEVEL           GOT_YOU_MORON
...

  2. core_internal_state__do_not_mess_with_it (in struct irq_desc, aliased as istate via a macro in kernel/irq/internals.h) — Runtime internal state managed by the core IRQ subsystem. These IRQS_* flags track transient conditions like “currently being polled”, “pending resend”, “suspended for PM”, or “spurious-disabled”. The aggressive naming and the macro alias are deliberate: only code inside kernel/irq/ should ever touch this field.

    /* kernel/irq/internals.h */
#define istate core_internal_state__do_not_mess_with_it

  3. state_use_accessors (in struct irq_common_data, embedded in struct irq_data) — Hardware-facing state visible to irq_chip drivers and irq-domain code. These IRQD_* flags track conditions like “disabled at the controller”, “masked in hardware”, “handler in progress”, “level-triggered”, “wakeup armed”, “affinity managed”, etc. The irqd_*() inline accessors in include/linux/irq.h enforce read/write discipline.

This three-way split serves two purposes: it prevents out-of-tree drivers from manipulating internal state (only IRQD_* flags are visible to chip code), and it separates rarely-changed configuration from rapidly-toggled runtime bits so that locking can be more targeted.

Settings Flags (status_use_accessors)

These flags describe the permanent character of an IRQ line. They are set during irq_set_chip_and_handler() or by platform code and seldom change at runtime.

Table 4-45. IRQ settings flags (stored in irq_desc.status_use_accessors)

FlagBitMeaning
IRQ_TYPE_SENSE_MASK0–3Requested trigger type, sharing the same word as the settings bits. IRQ_TYPE_DEFAULT is defined as the full mask, meaning “whatever firmware already configured”
IRQ_LEVEL8Interrupt is level-triggered (affects masking/unmasking strategy)
IRQ_PER_CPU9Interrupt is per-CPU; no affinity, no balancing, no cross-CPU masking
IRQ_NOPROBE10Do not include this IRQ in legacy auto-probing sweeps
IRQ_NOREQUEST11Cannot be requested by request_irq() (reserved for internal use)
IRQ_NOAUTOEN12Do not auto-enable the IRQ when the first handler is installed
IRQ_NO_BALANCING13Exempt from irqbalance or sysfs affinity changes
IRQ_NESTED_THREAD15Handler runs in the thread of the parent IRQ (for cascaded controllers)
IRQ_NOTHREAD16Cannot be force-threaded even under threadirqs boot parameter
IRQ_PER_CPU_DEVID17Per-CPU with a per-CPU device-id (used by request_percpu_irq())
IRQ_IS_POLLED18Used only in polled mode (no real interrupt delivery)
IRQ_DISABLE_UNLAZY19Always call irq_disable() immediately; do not defer (lazy disable)
IRQ_HIDDEN20Not shown in /proc/interrupts
IRQ_NO_DEBUG21Suppress spurious interrupt detection for this line

Not every one of these bits is equally writable at runtime. irq_settings_clr_and_set() masks both its arguments with _IRQF_MODIFY_MASK, and that mask deliberately omits IRQ_NO_DEBUG, so a line’s exemption from spurious-interrupt tracking cannot be toggled through irq_modify_status() after the fact. The GOT_YOU_MORON redefinitions shown above are what make this enforceable: because the plain IRQ_* names become unusable inside kernel/irq/, every read and write has to route through an accessor that applies the mask.

Internal State Flags (istate)

These flags are managed exclusively by kernel/irq/*.c and must never be accessed by drivers or chip code. They track transient runtime conditions.

Table 4-46. Internal state flags (stored in irq_desc.istate / core_internal_state__do_not_mess_with_it)

FlagValueMeaning
IRQS_AUTODETECT0x0001Descriptor participates in legacy auto-probing
IRQS_SPURIOUS_DISABLED0x0002Kernel disabled this IRQ after detecting >99,900/100,000 unhandled
IRQS_POLL_INPROGRESS0x0008Polling for recovery or legacy probing is in progress
IRQS_ONESHOT0x0020Threaded oneshot semantics: keep masked until thread completion
IRQS_REPLAY0x0040Interrupt should be replayed (re-triggered) after re-enable
IRQS_WAITING0x0080Legacy auto-detection waiting for first interrupt on this line
IRQS_PENDING0x0200Another occurrence arrived while current one was busy; needs resend
IRQS_SUSPENDED0x0800Descriptor is suspended for system power management
IRQS_TIMINGS0x1000Timing statistics collection is active

IRQS_SYSFS0x4000Sysfs entries have been created for this descriptor

These flags live in irq_data.common.state_use_accessors and are visible to irq-chip drivers through irqd_*() accessors. They track the current hardware-facing state of the interrupt line.

Mapping Old Flags to New

Readers familiar with the 2.6 kernel will notice that every old IRQ_* flag has a direct successor, but the successor may now live in a different state store and use a different name:

Table 4-48. Linux 2.6 IRQ_* flags → Linux 7.0.12 equivalents

Linux 2.6 FlagLinux 7.0.12 EquivalentState StoreNotes
IRQ_INPROGRESSIRQD_IRQ_INPROGRESSirq_dataMoved to chip-visible state
IRQ_DISABLEDIRQD_IRQ_DISABLEDirq_dataPlus depth in irq_desc for nesting count
IRQ_PENDINGIRQS_PENDINGistateInternal only; not visible to drivers
IRQ_REPLAYIRQS_REPLAYistateSame semantics, different namespace
IRQ_AUTODETECTIRQS_AUTODETECTistateLegacy probing; rarely used on modern hardware
IRQ_WAITINGIRQS_WAITINGistateLegacy probing companion
IRQ_LEVELIRQD_LEVEL + IRQ_LEVEL settingbothMirrored in irq_data for fast chip access
IRQ_MASKEDIRQD_IRQ_MASKEDirq_dataNow actively tracked; was unused in 2.6/x86
IRQ_PER_CPUIRQD_PER_CPU + IRQ_PER_CPU settingbothMirrored for both settings and chip access
(none)IRQS_SPURIOUS_DISABLEDistateNew: automatic spurious IRQ shutdown
(none)IRQS_NMIistateNew: NMI line support
(none)IRQD_FORWARDED_TO_VCPUirq_dataNew: KVM/virtualization pass-through
(none)IRQD_AFFINITY_MANAGEDirq_dataNew: automatic affinity management
(none)IRQD_CAN_RESERVEirq_dataNew: MSI reservation mode

The depth field in irq_desc continues to work as in Linux 2.6: disable_irq() increments it, enable_irq() decrements it, and the actual hardware disable/enable only happens at the 0↔1 transition. However, the IRQ_DISABLED flag itself no longer exists as a simple bit in irq_desc.status — it is now IRQD_IRQ_DISABLED in irq_data, reflecting its role as a chip-visible state that the flow handler and controller need to inspect.

IRQ Domains

The Linux IRQ number seen by drivers is not the same thing as the controller's hardware interrupt number. That distinction became unavoidable once SoCs, MSI hierarchies, and nested controllers became common. An irq domain is the translation object between the two spaces. Every mapping records a Linux IRQ number, a hardware number meaningful only within the domain, and often a parent-child relationship to another domain above or below it.

/* include/linux/irqdomain.h */
struct irq_domain_ops {
    int  (*match)(struct irq_domain *d, struct device_node *node,
                  enum irq_domain_bus_token bus_token);
    int  (*select)(struct irq_domain *d, struct irq_fwspec *fwspec,
                   enum irq_domain_bus_token bus_token);
    int  (*map)(struct irq_domain *d, unsigned int virq, irq_hw_number_t hw);
    void (*unmap)(struct irq_domain *d, unsigned int virq);
    int  (*xlate)(struct irq_domain *d, struct device_node *node,
                  const u32 *intspec, unsigned int intsize,
                  unsigned long *out_hwirq, unsigned int *out_type);
#ifdef CONFIG_IRQ_DOMAIN_HIERARCHY
    int  (*alloc)(struct irq_domain *d, unsigned int virq,
                  unsigned int nr_irqs, void *arg);
    void (*free)(struct irq_domain *d, unsigned int virq,
                 unsigned int nr_irqs);
    int  (*activate)(struct irq_domain *d, struct irq_data *irqd, bool reserve);
    void (*deactivate)(struct irq_domain *d, struct irq_data *irq_data);
    int  (*translate)(struct irq_domain *d, struct irq_fwspec *fwspec,
                      unsigned long *out_hwirq, unsigned int *out_type);
#endif
};

Table 4-49. Fields of struct irq_domain

Field Purpose
name human-readable domain name used in debug and diagnostics
ops translation/allocation callbacks
host_data controller-private pointer
flags domain capabilities such as hierarchy or MSI semantics
mapcount current number of active mappings
mutex serialization for mapping operations
root root domain or root-like owner for locking
fwnode firmware node that names the interrupt namespace
bus_token refines which interrupt bus this firmware node describes
gc generic-chip helper linkage when used
dev / pm_dev device association and PM anchor
parent parent domain in hierarchical setups
msi_parent_ops MSI-parent integration hooks

hwirq_max largest hardware number considered valid
revmap_size size of the linear reverse-map array
revmap_tree radix-tree reverse map for sparse hardware numbers
revmap[] linear hwirq-to-irq_data reverse map

irq_create_mapping() is the basic public helper. Its inline wrapper in include/linux/irqdomain.h simply calls irq_create_mapping_affinity(domain, hwirq, NULL). The real work happens in kernel/irq/irqdomain.c. Linux first allocates a virtual IRQ descriptor range, then associates the chosen virq with the domain and hardware number, and finally installs the domain-specific irq_data state. Linear reverse maps are fast for dense small hwirq spaces, while the radix tree handles sparse spaces efficiently.

/* include/linux/irqdomain.h */
static inline unsigned int irq_create_mapping(struct irq_domain *domain,
                                              irq_hw_number_t hwirq)
{
    return irq_create_mapping_affinity(domain, hwirq, NULL);
}

/* kernel/irq/irqdomain.c */
virq = irq_domain_alloc_descs(-1, 1, hwirq, of_node_to_nid(of_node), affinity);
if (virq <= 0)
    return 0;
if (irq_domain_associate_locked(domain, virq, hwirq)) {
    irq_free_desc(virq);
    return 0;
}

IRQ Sharing

In Linux 7.0.12, IRQ sharing is coordinated through the IRQF_SHARED flag and the linked list of struct irqaction descriptors hanging off desc->action. When a driver calls request_threaded_irq() (or the convenience wrapper request_irq()), the kernel checks the sharing rules before inserting the new action into the chain:

  1. Both old and new must agree — If an action chain already exists for this IRQ number, __setup_irq() verifies that both the existing action and the new one have IRQF_SHARED set. If either side does not, the request fails with -EBUSY.
  2. Trigger type must match — All sharers on a line must use the same trigger mode (level-high, edge-rising, etc.). Mismatched trigger types also produce -EBUSY.
  3. Per-CPU consistency — All actions must agree on IRQF_PERCPU. Mixing per-CPU and non-per-CPU handlers on the same line is not allowed.
  4. ONESHOT compatibility — If the existing chain uses IRQF_ONESHOT (keep the line masked until the threaded handler completes), new sharers must also use IRQF_ONESHOT or IRQF_COND_ONESHOT. Otherwise, a sharer could re-enable the line before another sharer’s thread finishes, causing spurious re-entry.
  5. dev_id is mandatory — Shared IRQs must supply a non-NULL dev_id cookie so that free_irq() can identify which action to remove from the chain.

When the interrupt fires, the flow handler (e.g., handle_fasteoi_irq()) calls handle_irq_event(), which iterates the action chain in __handle_irq_event_percpu():

/* kernel/irq/handle.c — simplified */
irqreturn_t __handle_irq_event_percpu(struct irq_desc *desc)
{
    irqreturn_t retval = IRQ_NONE;
    struct irqaction *action;

    for_each_action_of_desc(desc, action) {
        irqreturn_t res;

        res = action->handler(irq, action->dev_id);

        switch (res) {
        case IRQ_WAKE_THREAD:
            __irq_wake_thread(desc, action);
            break;
        default:
            break;
        }

        retval |= res;
    }

    return retval;
}

Every handler in the chain is called, regardless of whether earlier handlers already claimed the interrupt. Each handler must check its own device to determine whether it actually raised the interrupt. The return value convention is:

  • IRQ_NONE — “This interrupt was not for my device.” The kernel keeps trying other handlers.
  • IRQ_HANDLED — “I serviced the interrupt.” OR’d into the aggregate return.
  • IRQ_WAKE_THREAD — “I need my threaded handler to run.” The kernel wakes the per-action IRQ thread.

If all handlers in the chain return IRQ_NONE, the spurious-interrupt detection logic in note_interrupt() increments a counter. After enough consecutive unhandled interrupts (typically 99,900 out of 100,000), the kernel disables the line and logs a warning — the familiar “irq N: nobody cared” message.

Table 4-50. IRQ sharing rules enforced by __setup_irq()

RuleCheckFailure
Both sides must agree on sharingold->flags & new->flags & IRQF_SHARED-EBUSY
Trigger type must matcholdtype == (new->flags & IRQF_TRIGGER_MASK)-EBUSY
ONESHOT consistencyold->flags ^ new->flags & IRQF_ONESHOT-EBUSY
Per-CPU consistencyold->flags & IRQF_PERCPU == new->flags & IRQF_PERCPU-EBUSY
Shared IRQs need dev_idirqflags & IRQF_SHARED && !dev_id-EINVAL
No IRQF_NO_AUTOEN with sharedirqflags & IRQF_SHARED && irqflags & IRQF_NO_AUTOEN-EINVAL
NMI lines cannot be sharedirq_is_nmi(desc) && !per_cpu_devid-EINVAL
Per-CPU sharers need disjoint affinities!valid_percpu_irqaction(old, new)-EINVAL
IRQF_COND_SUSPEND requires IRQF_SHARED!(irqflags & IRQF_SHARED) && (irqflags & IRQF_COND_SUSPEND)-EINVAL
IRQF_COND_SUSPEND excludes IRQF_NO_SUSPEND(irqflags & IRQF_NO_SUSPEND) && (irqflags & IRQF_COND_SUSPEND)-EINVAL

Two subtleties are easy to miss when reading that list.

The first is that the trigger type is not simply compared — it may be inherited. __setup_irq() asks irqd_trigger_type_was_set() whether anyone has ever configured this line. If not, the first requester’s IRQF_TRIGGER_* bits become the line’s trigger type and are recorded with irqd_set_trigger_type(); only subsequent requesters are checked against it. A driver that passes no trigger bits at all therefore does not force edge-rising — it accepts whatever firmware or the device tree already programmed, which is exactly what the IRQF_TRIGGER_NONE comment in include/linux/interrupt.h recommends.

The second is that the oneshot check is asymmetric. The ordinary rule is an exclusive-or — (old->flags ^ new->flags) & IRQF_ONESHOT is a mismatch in either direction — but a requester that sets IRQF_COND_ONESHOT is silently upgraded (new->flags |= IRQF_ONESHOT) when the existing chain already uses oneshot. This exists so that a driver which does not itself need oneshot semantics can still join a line owned by a threaded handler that does, instead of failing with -EBUSY for reasons entirely outside its control. Note that the upgrade is one-directional: an existing non-oneshot chain is never converted to oneshot to accommodate a newcomer.

IRQ Distribution in Multiprocessor Systems

Linux follows the Symmetric Multiprocessing (SMP) model: the kernel should not be biased toward any single CPU. As a consequence, it tries to distribute I/O interrupt load across all available processors so that no single CPU becomes a bottleneck for interrupt servicing. How this distribution is achieved has changed dramatically between Linux 2.6 and 7.0.12.

The Old Approach: Hardware Lowest-Priority Delivery (Linux 2.6)

In the 2.6 kernel, the multi-APIC system was configured to use the lowest-priority delivery mode for I/O interrupts. During bootstrap, setup_IO_APIC_irqs() filled the I/O APIC’s Interrupt Redirection Table so that each IRQ was routed according to lowest-priority arbitration among all CPUs. Each local APIC’s Task Priority Register (TPR) was initialized to the same value, and the arbitration priority registers were automatically rotated after each interrupt, producing a rough round-robin distribution.

When this hardware mechanism failed (as it did on certain Pentium 4 SMP motherboards), Linux ran a kernel thread called kirqd that periodically executed do_irq_balance() to correct the distribution by reprogramming I/O APIC Redirection Table entries.

The Modern Approach: Software-Managed Affinity (Linux 7.0.12)

Modern Linux no longer relies on hardware lowest-priority arbitration. Instead, each IRQ line has an explicit CPU affinity mask that determines which CPUs may receive it. The distribution strategy has moved entirely into software:

  1. Default affinity at startup. When a new irq_desc is created, its affinity is initialized from irq_default_affinity (all online CPUs by default). When a handler is first installed via __setup_irq(), the function irq_setup_affinity() (kernel/irq/manage.c) computes the initial CPU target:

    /* kernel/irq/manage.c — irq_setup_affinity() simplified */
int irq_setup_affinity(struct irq_desc *desc)
{
    struct cpumask *set = irq_default_affinity;

    if (!__irq_can_set_affinity(desc))   /* skip PER_CPU, NO_BALANCE */
        return 0;

    /* Honour managed or user-set affinity if targets are online */
    if (irqd_affinity_is_managed(&desc->irq_data) ||
        irqd_has_set(&desc->irq_data, IRQD_AFFINITY_SET)) {
        if (cpumask_intersects(desc->irq_common_data.affinity,
                               cpu_online_mask))
            set = desc->irq_common_data.affinity;
    }

    cpumask_and(&mask, cpu_online_mask, set);
    /* Prefer NUMA-local CPUs when possible */
    if (node != NUMA_NO_NODE)
        cpumask_and(&mask, &mask, cpumask_of_node(node));

    return irq_do_set_affinity(&desc->irq_data, &mask, false);
}

  2. The irqbalance daemon. The old in-kernel kirqd thread has been replaced by the userspace irqbalance daemon, which observes interrupt load via /proc/interrupts and periodically writes new CPU masks to /proc/irq/N/smp_affinity. The advantages are significant: the policy can be more sophisticated (considering NUMA topology, power domains, cache sharing), and it runs in userspace where changes to the balancing algorithm do not require kernel updates.

  3. MSI-X per-queue spreading. Multi-queue devices (NVMe, high-speed NICs) use MSI-X to allocate one interrupt vector per hardware queue. irq_create_affinity_masks() (kernel/irq/affinity.c) computes an optimal spread of vectors across CPUs using group_cpus_evenly(), ensuring each queue’s interrupt lands on the CPU most likely to process its data. This eliminates the need for dynamic rebalancing — the affinity is computed once at device probe time and remains stable.

    /* kernel/irq/affinity.c — irq_create_affinity_masks() simplified */
struct irq_affinity_desc *
irq_create_affinity_masks(unsigned int nvecs, struct irq_affinity *affd)
{
    /* Compute how many vectors need affinity assignment */
    affvecs = nvecs - affd->pre_vectors - affd->post_vectors;

    /* Spread vectors evenly across online CPUs */
    result = group_cpus_evenly(this_vecs, &nr_masks);

    /* Each vector gets a cpumask targeting specific CPUs */
    for (j = 0; j < nr_masks; j++)
        cpumask_copy(&masks[curvec + j].mask, &result[j]);
    ...
}

  4. Managed affinity. When drivers allocate MSI-X vectors with PCI_IRQ_AFFINITY, the kernel marks those IRQ lines as managed (IRQD_AFFINITY_MANAGED). Managed IRQs have their affinity controlled exclusively by the kernel: the irqbalance daemon and /proc/irq/N/smp_affinity writes are ignored. When a target CPU goes offline, the kernel automatically migrates the interrupt to another online CPU in the allowed set; if no CPUs remain, the line is shut down (IRQD_MANAGED_SHUTDOWN) until a target comes back online.

  5. Housekeeping CPUs. Systems configured with nohz_full= or isolcpus=managed_irq designate certain CPUs as housekeeping CPUs. Managed IRQs are automatically steered away from isolated CPUs and toward housekeeping CPUs, ensuring that latency-sensitive real-time workloads on isolated cores are not disturbed by interrupt processing.

The /proc/irq Interface

The per-IRQ proc interface remains the primary userspace control mechanism:

FileFormatPurpose
/proc/irq/N/smp_affinityhex bitmaskRead/write the CPU affinity mask for IRQ N
/proc/irq/N/smp_affinity_listCPU list (e.g., 0-3,8)Human-friendly version of the above
/proc/irq/N/nodeNUMA node IDPreferred NUMA node for this IRQ
/proc/irq/N/effective_affinityhex bitmaskThe actual CPUs that can receive this IRQ (after kernel filtering)
/proc/irq/N/effective_affinity_listCPU listHuman-friendly version of effective affinity
/proc/irq/default_smp_affinityhex bitmaskDefault affinity for newly created IRQs

Writing to smp_affinity causes the kernel to:

  1. Parse the cpumask and intersect it with cpu_online_mask.
  2. Check irq_can_set_affinity_usr() — returns false for per-CPU, non-balanceable, or managed IRQs. (The plain irq_can_set_affinity() used by in-kernel callers does not reject managed IRQs.)
  3. Call irq_set_affinity() → irq_do_set_affinity() → chip->irq_set_affinity().
  4. The chip method reprograms the hardware routing (e.g., writes a new MSI address/data pair or updates the I/O APIC Redirection Table entry).

Table 4-51. IRQ distribution: Linux 2.6 vs 7.0.12

AspectLinux 2.6.11Linux 7.0.12
Primary mechanismHardware lowest-priority delivery via APICSoftware-managed affinity masks per IRQ
BalancingIn-kernel kirqd thread running do_irq_balance()Userspace irqbalance daemon via /proc/irq/N/smp_affinity
MSI distributionNot widely usedMSI-X per-queue spreading via irq_create_affinity_masks()
Affinity interface/proc/irq/N/smp_affinity + set_ioapic_affinity_irq()/proc/irq/N/smp_affinity + irq_set_affinity() + irq_do_set_affinity()
Managed interruptsNot availableIRQD_AFFINITY_MANAGED — kernel-exclusive control, auto-migration on CPU hotplug
NUMA awarenessNoneirq_setup_affinity() prefers NUMA-local CPUs
Housekeeping isolationNot availablenohz_full= / isolcpus=managed_irq steers managed IRQs to housekeeping CPUs
Focus processorPentium III feature, dropped in P4Not used

The Complete Dispatch Path

In Linux 2.6, all I/O interrupts funneled through two functions: do_IRQ(), which set up the execution environment (IRQ stack switching, preempt counting), and __do_IRQ(), a monolithic dispatcher that handled acknowledgment, flag manipulation, ISR chaining, and end-of-interrupt in a single large function. Both functions have been removed entirely from the modern kernel. Their work has been decomposed into a layered pipeline where each layer has a single responsibility:

Figure 4-16 traces that pipeline end to end for both architectures. The two paths differ only in the leftmost boxes, where the hardware-specific acknowledgment happens; from generic_handle_irq() onward the code is shared.

Table 4-52. Mapping of Linux 2.6 functions to Linux 7.0.12 equivalents

Linux 2.6 functionPurposeLinux 7.0.12 replacementLocation
do_IRQ()Entry point: IRQ stack switch, preempt count, call dispatchercommon_interrupt() (x86), gic_handle_irq() (ARM64) + DEFINE_IDTENTRY_IRQ macroarch/x86/kernel/irq.c, drivers/irqchip/irq-gic-v3.c
__do_IRQ()Monolithic dispatch: ack, flags, ISR chain, endPer-line flow handlers: handle_fasteoi_irq(), handle_edge_irq(), handle_level_irq(), etc.kernel/irq/chip.c
handle_IRQ_event()Iterate ISR action chainhandle_irq_event() → __handle_irq_event_percpu()kernel/irq/handle.c
ret_from_intr()Assembly return pathirqentry_exit() via IDT entry frameworkarch/x86/include/asm/idtentry.h

The key insight is that __do_IRQ() tried to be one function for every controller type — 8259A, IO-APIC, MSI. That led to a tangle of conditionals. The modern design replaces it with a small family of flow handlers, each tailored to one interrupt delivery model, producing code that is both simpler per-handler and more correct overall.

The common_interrupt() Function (x86_64)

On x86_64, common_interrupt() in arch/x86/kernel/irq.c is the architectural front door for all normal device IRQs. It corresponds to what do_IRQ() was in Linux 2.6, but the heavy lifting — IRQ stack switching, preempt count manipulation, and RCU notification — is handled by the DEFINE_IDTENTRY_IRQ macro framework before common_interrupt() is even called.

The DEFINE_IDTENTRY_IRQ(common_interrupt) macro generates a wrapper that performs the following actions before entering the function body:

  1. Calls irqentry_enter() — establishes a legal kernel context: it makes RCU watch this CPU if it was idle or in a userspace-extended quiescent state, updates lockdep and context tracking, and enters hardirq context.
  2. Passes the vector number — unlike 2.6, where do_IRQ() recovered the IRQ number from the negated value stashed in regs->orig_eax by the per-vector stub, the modern IDT framework declares the vector as an ordinary int parameter of the generated function.

Note what the wrapper does not do: it does not switch stacks. Device interrupts do not use the IST mechanism — IST slots are reserved for the handful of exceptions that must run on a known-good stack regardless of what the interrupted context was doing (#DF, #MC, NMI, #DB, #VC). An ordinary device interrupt therefore begins executing on whatever stack the interrupted code was using. The switch to the per-CPU hardirq stack happens later and in C, inside run_irq_on_irqstack_cond(), which handle_irq() reaches only after the descriptor has been resolved. That ordering is deliberate: the entry code stays as short and as noinstr-clean as possible, and the stack switch is skipped entirely when it would be pointless or unsafe — see Interrupt Handling earlier in this chapter for the exact conditions.

The function body is remarkably small:

/* arch/x86/kernel/irq.c */
DEFINE_IDTENTRY_IRQ(common_interrupt)
{
    struct pt_regs *old_regs = set_irq_regs(regs);

    /* entry code tells RCU that we're not quiescent.  Check it. */
    RCU_LOCKDEP_WARN(!rcu_is_watching(), "IRQ failed to wake up RCU");

    if (unlikely(!call_irq_handler(vector, regs)))
        apic_eoi();

    set_irq_regs(old_regs);
}

The function executes the following actions:

  1. Saves the previous irq_regs pointer — set_irq_regs() stores the current regs in a per-CPU variable and returns the previous value, exactly as 2.6’s do_IRQ() did. Nested interrupt handlers can later retrieve the interrupted context.
  2. Validates RCU state — a debug assertion ensures the RCU subsystem is watching, because interrupt handlers may read RCU-protected data.
  3. Calls call_irq_handler() — this replaces the old __do_IRQ() call. If no valid descriptor is found (e.g., a stale vector after free_irq()), it returns false and common_interrupt() sends a bare APIC EOI to prevent the interrupt from being stuck.
  4. Restores irq_regs — after the handler completes, the previous value is restored.

When common_interrupt() returns, the DEFINE_IDTENTRY_IRQ wrapper calls irqentry_exit(), which decrements the preempt count, checks whether deferred work (softirqs) needs to run, and potentially reschedules — mirroring the old irq_exit() + ret_from_intr() path.

The call_irq_handler() Function

call_irq_handler() bridges the architecture-specific vector number to the generic IRQ layer. In Linux 2.6, do_IRQ() simply indexed irq_desc[irq] to find the descriptor. In 7.0.12, each CPU maintains a per-CPU vector_irq[] array that maps local APIC vector numbers to irq_desc pointers.

/* arch/x86/kernel/irq.c */
static __always_inline bool call_irq_handler(int vector, struct pt_regs *regs)
{
    struct irq_desc *desc = __this_cpu_read(vector_irq[vector]);

    if (likely(!IS_ERR_OR_NULL(desc))) {
        handle_irq(desc, regs);
        return true;
    }

    /* Reevaluate with vector_lock held to prevent a race
     * against request_irq() setting up the vector */
    lock_vector_lock();
    desc = reevaluate_vector(vector);
    unlock_vector_lock();

    if (!desc)
        return false;

    handle_irq(desc, regs);
    return true;
}

The function performs the following steps:

  1. Reads the descriptor — __this_cpu_read(vector_irq[vector]) looks up the per-CPU mapping. Because vector assignment is per-CPU (a consequence of APIC vector management), the same vector number can point to different IRQ descriptors on different CPUs.
  2. Fast path — if the pointer is a valid irq_desc, the function calls handle_irq(desc, regs), which on x86_64 becomes generic_handle_irq_desc(desc) — a simple inline that invokes desc->handle_irq(desc).
  3. Race resolution — if the descriptor is VECTOR_SHUTDOWN or VECTOR_UNUSED, a race may have occurred between free_irq() and interrupt delivery. The function acquires the vector_lock, re-reads the mapping, and either dispatches the interrupt or returns false. The comment in the source provides a detailed timeline of the race scenario.

On ARM64, the equivalent is simpler: gic_handle_irq() reads the hardware INTID from ICC_IAR1_EL1 and calls generic_handle_domain_irq(gic_data.domain, irqnr), which translates the hardware IRQ number to a Linux IRQ number via the irq_domain and then invokes the flow handler.

The handle_irq()/generic_handle_irq_desc() Function

On x86_64, handle_irq() is a thin wrapper:

/* arch/x86/kernel/irq.c */
static __always_inline void handle_irq(struct irq_desc *desc,
                                       struct pt_regs *regs)
{
    if (IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_X86_64))
        generic_handle_irq_desc(desc);
    else
        __handle_irq(desc, regs);
}

generic_handle_irq_desc() simply calls desc->handle_irq(desc). This single indirect call is the pivot point of the entire generic IRQ subsystem. The function pointer desc->handle_irq was installed when the interrupt controller driver set up the IRQ line (e.g., irq_set_chip_and_handler(irq, &gic_chip, handle_fasteoi_irq)). From this point on, execution is entirely architecture-independent — the same flow handler code runs on x86_64, ARM64, RISC-V, or any other architecture.

This separation is the heart of the generic IRQ design: architecture code chooses the descriptor, generic code handles policy, and chip-specific code is reached only through standardized flow-handler callbacks.

Flow Handlers (Replacing __do_IRQ())

In Linux 2.6, the monolithic __do_IRQ() function handled all interrupt types with a single set of flags and conditionals:

/* Linux 2.6 — __do_IRQ() (simplified) */
spin_lock(&(irq_desc[irq].lock));
irq_desc[irq].handler->ack(irq);
irq_desc[irq].status &= ~(IRQ_REPLAY | IRQ_WAITING);
irq_desc[irq].status |= IRQ_PENDING;
if (!(irq_desc[irq].status & (IRQ_DISABLED | IRQ_INPROGRESS))
         && irq_desc[irq].action) {
    irq_desc[irq].status |= IRQ_INPROGRESS;
    do {
         irq_desc[irq].status &= ~IRQ_PENDING;
         spin_unlock(&(irq_desc[irq].lock));
         handle_IRQ_event(irq, regs, irq_desc[irq].action);
         spin_lock(&(irq_desc[irq].lock));
    } while (irq_desc[irq].status & IRQ_PENDING);
    irq_desc[irq].status &= ~IRQ_INPROGRESS;
}
irq_desc[irq].handler->end(irq);
spin_unlock(&(irq_desc[irq].lock));

This monolithic approach had several problems:

  • Level-triggered and edge-triggered interrupts need fundamentally different ack/mask choreography, but __do_IRQ() tried to handle both with the same flags.
  • The IRQ_PENDING loop was designed for edge retriggering but added unnecessary overhead for level or MSI interrupts.
  • Adding new interrupt styles (MSI, per-CPU, oneshot/threaded) meant more conditionals in an already complex function.

Linux 7.0.12 replaces __do_IRQ() with a family of flow handlers, each tailored to one interrupt delivery model. A flow handler embodies the controller semantics for one style of interrupt line. Linux chooses a flow handler when the IRQ is set up via irq_set_chip_and_handler(), and stores the function pointer in desc->handle_irq.

The three most important flow handlers are handle_level_irq(), handle_edge_irq(), and handle_fasteoi_irq().

The handle_level_irq() Function

handle_level_irq() services level-triggered interrupt lines. A level interrupt remains electrically asserted as long as the device condition is active. Without masking, the controller would repeatedly redeliver the same interrupt while Linux is handling it. Therefore, the flow handler must mask the source before running any ISR.

/* kernel/irq/chip.c */
void handle_level_irq(struct irq_desc *desc)
{
    guard(raw_spinlock)(&desc->lock);
    mask_ack_irq(desc);           /* mask + ack at controller */
    if (!irq_can_handle(desc))    /* check disabled, in-progress */
        return;
    kstat_incr_irqs_this_cpu(desc);
    handle_irq_event(desc);       /* run ISR chain */
    cond_unmask_irq(desc);        /* re-enable when safe */
}

The function executes the following steps:

  1. Acquires desc->lock — using the guard() macro, which automatically releases the lock when the function returns (a C cleanup attribute pattern new to modern kernels).
  2. Masks and acknowledges — mask_ack_irq() calls chip->irq_mask() then chip->irq_ack() (or chip->irq_mask_ack() if available). This prevents redelivery during handler execution.
  3. Checks eligibility — irq_can_handle() is the conjunction of two helpers. irq_can_handle_pm() rejects the interrupt if it is already in progress or is an armed wakeup source, and also handles the polling and affinity-race cases. irq_can_handle_actions() then clears IRQS_REPLAY and IRQS_WAITING and rejects the interrupt if no action is registered or the line is disabled, setting IRQS_PENDING in that case so it can be replayed later. Together these replace the manual IRQ_DISABLED | IRQ_INPROGRESS flag tests in __do_IRQ().
  4. Updates statistics — increments per-CPU kstat_irqs.
  5. Runs the ISR chain — handle_irq_event() (described below) iterates all irqaction handlers.
  6. Conditionally unmasks — cond_unmask_irq() re-enables the source unless the IRQ is oneshot threaded (in which case the thread will unmask when it finishes).

The handle_edge_irq() Function

Edge-triggered interrupts present the opposite challenge: an edge is a momentary event latched by the controller. If a second edge arrives while the first is being handled, it must not be lost. Linux acknowledges the edge to clear the latch, then loops if more edges arrived during ISR execution.

/* kernel/irq/chip.c */
void handle_edge_irq(struct irq_desc *desc)
{
    guard(raw_spinlock)(&desc->lock);

    if (!irq_can_handle(desc)) {
        desc->istate |= IRQS_PENDING;
        mask_ack_irq(desc);
        return;
    }

    kstat_incr_irqs_this_cpu(desc);

    /* Start handling the irq */
    desc->irq_data.chip->irq_ack(&desc->irq_data);

    do {
        if (unlikely(!desc->action)) {
            mask_irq(desc);
            return;
        }

        /*
         * When another irq arrived while we were handling
         * one, we could have masked the irq.
         * Reenable it, if it was not disabled in meantime.
         */
        if (unlikely(desc->istate & IRQS_PENDING)) {
            if (!irqd_irq_disabled(&desc->irq_data) &&
                irqd_irq_masked(&desc->irq_data))
                unmask_irq(desc);
        }

        handle_irq_event(desc);

    } while ((desc->istate & IRQS_PENDING) &&
             !irqd_irq_disabled(&desc->irq_data));
}

The key differences from handle_level_irq():

  • Ack without mask — because edges are latched, the controller can record a new edge even while the current one is being handled, so masking would lose the new edge.
  • Pending loop — if IRQS_PENDING is set after handle_irq_event() returns, another edge arrived during ISR execution. The handler loops and services it. This is the direct descendant of __do_IRQ()’s while (IRQ_PENDING) loop, but now it only runs for edge-triggered lines where it is actually needed.
  • Mask on pending when disabled — if no action is registered or the IRQ is disabled, the handler masks the source and sets IRQS_PENDING so the interrupt can be retriggered later.
  • Nothing clears IRQS_PENDING in the loop body — that is done by handle_irq_event() itself, which clears the flag and sets IRQD_IRQ_INPROGRESS before dropping desc->lock to run the ISR chain. Any edge that arrives while the lock is dropped therefore re-sets IRQS_PENDING, and the while condition observes it. The flag is thus a race-free record of “an edge arrived after we last began handling one.”

The handle_fasteoi_irq() Function

handle_fasteoi_irq() is the most common flow handler on modern systems because both the IO-APIC (in EOI mode) and the ARM GICv2/v3 use transparent end-of-interrupt signaling. The controller handles most state tracking internally; Linux mostly needs to run the action chain and then issue EOI.

/* kernel/irq/chip.c */
void handle_fasteoi_irq(struct irq_desc *desc)
{
    struct irq_chip *chip = desc->irq_data.chip;
    guard(raw_spinlock)(&desc->lock);

    if (!irq_can_handle_pm(desc)) {
        if (irqd_needs_resend_when_in_progress(&desc->irq_data))
            desc->istate |= IRQS_PENDING;
        cond_eoi_irq(chip, &desc->irq_data);
        return;
    }
    if (!irq_can_handle_actions(desc)) {
        mask_irq(desc);
        cond_eoi_irq(chip, &desc->irq_data);
        return;
    }

    kstat_incr_irqs_this_cpu(desc);
    if (desc->istate & IRQS_ONESHOT)
        mask_irq(desc);

    handle_irq_event(desc);

    cond_unmask_eoi_irq(desc, chip);

    /* When the affinity race above happens, resend the interrupt. */
    if (unlikely(desc->istate & IRQS_PENDING))
        check_irq_resend(desc, false);
}

The function performs the following steps:

  1. Acquires desc->lock and checks PM/suspend state.
  2. Oneshot masking — if the IRQ uses IRQF_ONESHOT (common with threaded interrupts), it masks the source before running the ISR to prevent redelivery until the thread completes.
  3. Runs the ISR chain via handle_irq_event().
  4. Conditional EOI + unmask — cond_unmask_eoi_irq() issues chip->irq_eoi() (writing to APIC EOI register or GIC ICC_EOIR1_EL1) and unmasks if needed.
  5. Resends if an interrupt was lost to an affinity race — if IRQS_PENDING is still set on return, check_irq_resend() replays the interrupt.

That last step is the counterpart of the first. When an affinity change races with interrupt handling, the next interrupt can arrive on the new target CPU while the original CPU is still inside handle_irq_event(). The new CPU finds IRQD_IRQ_INPROGRESS set, so irq_can_handle_pm() refuses; rather than dropping the interrupt it records IRQS_PENDING — but only when the chip asked for this by setting irqd_needs_resend_when_in_progress() — and issues a conditional EOI so the controller is not left waiting. The original CPU then observes IRQS_PENDING on its way out and calls check_irq_resend() to replay the lost interrupt. Note also that the “no action registered or line disabled” case masks the line and still issues cond_eoi_irq(): a fasteoi controller must always be told the interrupt is finished, even when Linux has nothing to run.

The simplicity of handle_fasteoi_irq() compared to the old __do_IRQ() demonstrates the benefit of per-type flow handlers: the function does not need to check whether the controller is edge or level triggered, or whether acknowledgment happens at entry or exit.

Other Flow Handlers

Table 4-53. Comparison of generic IRQ flow handlers

Flow handlerTypical hardwareEntry actionExit actionKey concern
handle_level_irq()level-triggered line controllersmask + ackconditional unmaskline remains asserted until device is quiescent
handle_edge_irq()edge-latched controllersack edgeloop/retrigger if pendingdo not lose a second edge while first is handled
handle_fasteoi_irq()APIC/GIC transparent EOI styleminimal masking, maybe oneshot maskEOI and optional unmaskcontroller expects explicit end-of-interrupt
handle_percpu_devid_irq()per-CPU devices (timer, PMU)ack (if the chip has one)EOI (if the chip has one)takes no lock at all, and selects a single action by CPU affinity instead of walking a chain
handle_simple_irq()software-demuxed or chainednonenoneparent handler already did ack/mask
handle_untracked_irq()untracked (no spurious detection)nonenoneidentical to handle_simple_irq() except that it calls __handle_irq_event_percpu() directly, bypassing note_interrupt() and the entropy pool

handle_percpu_devid_irq() deserves a closer look, because it is the handler behind the ARM Generic Timer, the PMU, and every SGI-delivered IPI, and it breaks several of the rules the other flow handlers follow. It takes desc->lock nowhere: a per-CPU interrupt is by construction never concurrent with itself, so there is nothing to serialize against. For the same reason it uses __kstat_incr_irqs_this_cpu() rather than kstat_incr_irqs_this_cpu(), deliberately skipping the update of the shared desc->tot_count that would reintroduce cross-CPU traffic on the hottest interrupt in the system. It does not call handle_irq_event() and therefore never sets IRQD_IRQ_INPROGRESS, runs note_interrupt(), or feeds the entropy pool.

Most distinctively, it does not iterate the action chain. Instead it scans for the first action whose action->affinity includes the current CPU and invokes only that one, passing raw_cpu_ptr(action->percpu_dev_id) rather than dev_id. This is what allows a single Linux IRQ number, such as the one behind an IPI, to carry genuinely different handlers on different CPUs. If no action matches, the handler treats the interrupt as spurious: it disables the line on this CPU with irq_percpu_disable() and prints a one-shot "Spurious percpu IRQ" message, since the ordinary spurious-detection machinery is not running for this line.

The handle_irq_event() Function

handle_irq_event() in kernel/irq/handle.c is the successor to Linux 2.6’s handle_IRQ_event(). It manages the transition between the locked flow-handler context and the unlocked ISR execution context.

/* kernel/irq/handle.c */
irqreturn_t handle_irq_event(struct irq_desc *desc)
{
    irqreturn_t ret;

    desc->istate &= ~IRQS_PENDING;
    irqd_set(&desc->irq_data, IRQD_IRQ_INPROGRESS);
    raw_spin_unlock(&desc->lock);

    ret = handle_irq_event_percpu(desc);

    raw_spin_lock(&desc->lock);
    irqd_clear(&desc->irq_data, IRQD_IRQ_INPROGRESS);
    return ret;
}

The function performs the following steps:

  1. Clears IRQS_PENDING — the interrupt is now being serviced. In __do_IRQ(), the equivalent was irq_desc[irq].status &= ~IRQ_PENDING.
  2. Sets IRQD_IRQ_INPROGRESS — this flag tells other CPUs that this interrupt line is currently being handled. It replaces the old IRQ_INPROGRESS flag. If another CPU receives the same interrupt, the flow handler will see IRQD_IRQ_INPROGRESS set and will either set IRQS_PENDING and return (for edge) or simply return (for level, since the controller is masked).
  3. Releases the spin lock — ISR handlers run without holding desc->lock, exactly as in 2.6 where spin_unlock preceded handle_IRQ_event(). This allows the device driver’s handler to use irq_set_*() functions and allows other CPUs to manipulate the descriptor.
  4. Calls handle_irq_event_percpu() — this wrapper calls __handle_irq_event_percpu() (the actual ISR loop), then feeds entropy to the random pool via add_interrupt_randomness() and calls note_interrupt() for spurious interrupt detection.
  5. Reacquires the lock and clears IRQD_IRQ_INPROGRESS — the flow handler resumes with the lock held and can check whether new edges arrived (IRQS_PENDING).

The __handle_irq_event_percpu() Function

__handle_irq_event_percpu() in kernel/irq/handle.c is the innermost loop that actually invokes device driver handlers. It replaces the loop in the old handle_IRQ_event() function.

/* kernel/irq/handle.c */
irqreturn_t __handle_irq_event_percpu(struct irq_desc *desc)
{
    irqreturn_t retval = IRQ_NONE;
    unsigned int irq = desc->irq_data.irq;
    struct irqaction *action;

    for_each_action_of_desc(desc, action) {
        irqreturn_t res;

        /* Check force-threading for lockdep */
        if (irq_settings_can_thread(desc) &&
            !(action->flags & (IRQF_NO_THREAD | IRQF_PERCPU | IRQF_ONESHOT)))
            lockdep_hardirq_threaded();

        trace_irq_handler_entry(irq, action);

        if (static_branch_unlikely(&irqhandler_duration_check_enabled)) {
            u64 ts_start = local_clock();
            res = action->handler(irq, action->dev_id);
            irqhandler_duration_check(ts_start, irq, action);
        } else {
            res = action->handler(irq, action->dev_id);
        }

        trace_irq_handler_exit(irq, action, res);

        if (WARN_ONCE(!irqs_disabled(),
                "irq %u handler %pS enabled interrupts\n",
                irq, action->handler))
            local_irq_disable();

        switch (res) {
        case IRQ_WAKE_THREAD:
            if (unlikely(!action->thread_fn)) {
                warn_no_thread(irq, action);
                break;
            }
            __irq_wake_thread(desc, action);
            break;
        default:
            break;
        }

        retval |= res;
    }
    return retval;
}

For each action in the shared-IRQ chain, the function:

  1. Emits tracepoints — trace_irq_handler_entry() and trace_irq_handler_exit() allow ftrace and perf to measure per-handler latency.
  2. Optionally checks handler duration — when irqhandler_duration_check_enabled is set, the function timestamps the call and warns if the handler runs too long (a debugging aid for detecting handlers that should be threaded).
  3. Calls the primary handler — action->handler(irq, action->dev_id) is the device driver’s ISR function, registered via request_irq() or request_threaded_irq().
  4. Validates interrupt state — if the handler accidentally re-enabled interrupts (a bug), the function warns once and re-disables them to maintain the invariant that hardirq handlers run with local interrupts disabled.
  5. Interprets the return value:
    • IRQ_HANDLED — the handler claimed the interrupt; ORed into retval.
    • IRQ_NONE — the handler did not recognize the interrupt; contributes nothing. The accumulated retval is later checked by note_interrupt() for spurious interrupt detection.
    • IRQ_WAKE_THREAD — the handler requests that its threaded continuation be scheduled (see below).

Interrupt Service Routines

An interrupt service routine (ISR) is the device-specific function that the kernel calls when an interrupt occurs. Every ISR is registered via request_irq() or request_threaded_irq() and stored in an irqaction descriptor. When __handle_irq_event_percpu() iterates the action chain, it calls action->handler(irq, action->dev_id) for each registered ISR.

ISR Function Signature

In Linux 7.0.12, all interrupt service routines share a single prototype:

/* include/linux/interrupt.h */
typedef irqreturn_t (*irq_handler_t)(int, void *);

The ISR receives exactly two parameters:

ParameterTypePurpose
irqintThe Linux IRQ number. Allows a single ISR to handle multiple IRQ lines (rare in practice).
dev_idvoid *The device identifier passed to request_irq(). For shared IRQs, this is typically a pointer to the driver’s private data structure, allowing the ISR to identify which device raised the interrupt.

Table 4-54. ISR parameter changes from Linux 2.6 to 7.0.12

AspectLinux 2.6Linux 7.0.12
Prototypeirqreturn_t handler(int irq, void *dev_id, struct pt_regs *regs)irqreturn_t handler(int irq, void *dev_id)
regs parameterPointer to saved CPU registers on the Kernel Mode stackRemoved — drivers that need register access (very rare) use get_irq_regs() instead
Local interruptsControlled by SA_INTERRUPT flag; ISR could run with interrupts enabledAlways disabled — all hardirq handlers run with local interrupts disabled; WARN_ONCE fires if a handler re-enables them
Return typeint (0 or 1)irqreturn_t enum (IRQ_NONE, IRQ_HANDLED, IRQ_WAKE_THREAD)
Sharing flagSA_SHIRQIRQF_SHARED
Thread supportNot availablethread_fn parameter in request_threaded_irq()

ISR Return Values

Every ISR must return an irqreturn_t value:

/* include/linux/irqreturn.h */
enum irqreturn {
    IRQ_NONE        = (0 << 0),  /* interrupt was not from this device */
    IRQ_HANDLED     = (1 << 0),  /* interrupt was handled by this device */
    IRQ_WAKE_THREAD = (1 << 1),  /* request to wake handler thread */
};
  • IRQ_NONE — The ISR inspected its device and determined the interrupt was not from it. On a shared IRQ line, the next ISR in the chain will be called. If all ISRs return IRQ_NONE, the kernel counts this as an unhandled interrupt. After 99,900 unhandled interrupts out of 100,000 total on a given line, note_interrupt() disables the line and prints "irq %d: nobody cared".
  • IRQ_HANDLED — The ISR recognized and serviced the interrupt. The OR-accumulation in __handle_irq_event_percpu() ensures that even if one handler on a shared line returns IRQ_HANDLED, the overall result is considered handled.
  • IRQ_WAKE_THREAD — The ISR performed minimal work (typically just acknowledging the device) and requests that the kernel schedule its threaded handler (thread_fn) to do the rest in process context. The convenience macro IRQ_RETVAL(x) converts a boolean to IRQ_HANDLED or IRQ_NONE.

ISR Constraints

An ISR runs in hardirq context, which imposes strict constraints:

  1. Cannot sleep — no mutex_lock(), kmalloc(GFP_KERNEL), copy_from_user(), or any function that might block.
  2. Cannot schedule — no schedule(), wait_event(), or msleep().
  3. Must be fast — long-running work should be deferred to a threaded handler (thread_fn), softirq, or workqueue. The kernel optionally monitors handler duration and warns if an ISR runs too long.
  4. Local interrupts are disabled — the ISR must not call local_irq_enable(). If it does, __handle_irq_event_percpu() will WARN_ONCE and re-disable them.
  5. Must handle shared IRQs correctly — if registered with IRQF_SHARED, the ISR must check its own device before claiming the interrupt. Reading a device status register is the standard approach.

Example: A Minimal ISR

/* A typical modern ISR for a shared PCI device */
static irqreturn_t my_device_isr(int irq, void *dev_id)
{
    struct my_dev *priv = dev_id;
    u32 status;

    /* Read device status register to check if this device raised the interrupt */
    status = readl(priv->regs + STATUS_REG);
    if (!(status & IRQ_PENDING_BIT))
        return IRQ_NONE;       /* not our interrupt */

    /* Acknowledge the interrupt at the device */
    writel(status, priv->regs + STATUS_REG);

    /* Minimal urgent work in hardirq context */
    priv->event_count++;

    return IRQ_WAKE_THREAD;    /* defer heavy processing to thread */
}

/* The threaded handler — runs in process context, can sleep */
static irqreturn_t my_device_thread(int irq, void *dev_id)
{
    struct my_dev *priv = dev_id;

    /* Heavy processing: DMA completion, data copy, userspace notification */
    process_dma_buffers(priv);
    wake_up_interruptible(&priv->wait_queue);

    return IRQ_HANDLED;
}

/* Registration */
ret = request_threaded_irq(pdev->irq, my_device_isr, my_device_thread,
                           IRQF_SHARED, "mydevice", priv);

The structure of an ISR depends on the characteristics of the device handled. Specific examples of ISRs for block devices, network interfaces, and character devices are discussed in subsequent chapters.

Reviving a Lost Interrupt

The old kernel’s “reviving a lost interrupt” scenario — where an IRQ is disabled on one CPU while another CPU is about to handle a pending occurrence — still exists in 7.0.12 but is handled differently.

In Linux 2.6, enable_irq() checked IRQ_PENDING | IRQ_REPLAY and called hw_resend_irq() to generate a self-interrupt via the local APIC. In Linux 7.0.12, the mechanism is generalized in check_irq_resend() (kernel/irq/resend.c):

/* kernel/irq/resend.c */
int check_irq_resend(struct irq_desc *desc, bool inject)
{
    /* Level interrupts are resent by hardware when still active */
    if (irq_settings_is_level(desc)) {
        desc->istate &= ~IRQS_PENDING;
        return -EINVAL;
    }

    if (desc->istate & IRQS_REPLAY)
        return -EBUSY;

    if (!(desc->istate & IRQS_PENDING) && !inject)
        return 0;

    desc->istate &= ~IRQS_PENDING;

    if (!try_retrigger(desc))       /* try chip->irq_retrigger() */
        err = irq_sw_resend(desc);  /* fallback: tasklet resend */

    if (!err)
        desc->istate |= IRQS_REPLAY;
    return err;
}

The key differences from the 2.6 approach:

  • Level interrupts are skipped — they are reasserted by hardware when still active, so software resend is unnecessary.
