  • Hardware retrigger first — try_retrigger() calls chip->irq_retrigger(), which on x86 uses APIC self-IPI and on GICv3 sets the pending bit in ISPENDR.
  • Software fallback — if the chip does not support hardware retriggering, irq_sw_resend() adds the descriptor to a per-CPU irq_resend_list and schedules a tasklet that calls desc->handle_irq(desc) to replay the interrupt in software. This is more general than 2.6’s local-APIC-only approach.
  • IRQS_REPLAY prevents double resend — exactly as in 2.6, this flag ensures at most one resend is in flight.

The software fallback is more constrained than it first appears, and the constraint matters on ARM64. irq_sw_resend() begins by refusing outright if irqd_is_handle_enforce_irqctx() is true:

/* kernel/irq/resend.c */
static int irq_sw_resend(struct irq_desc *desc)
{
    /* Validate whether this interrupt can be safely injected from
     * non interrupt context */
    if (irqd_is_handle_enforce_irqctx(&desc->irq_data))
        return -EINVAL;
    ...
}

As noted earlier, gic_irq_domain_map() sets that flag on every line it maps, precisely to prevent a replay from disturbing the ACK/EOI ordering. The practical consequence is that on a GICv3 system the software resend path is dead code: every retrigger must go through try_retrigger() and land on gic_retrigger(), which sets the pending bit in GICD_ISPENDR. The tasklet path exists for simpler controllers — typically GPIO expanders and other chips that offer no retrigger register at all.

irq_sw_resend() also handles nested threaded interrupts specially: a line whose handler runs inside the parent’s IRQ thread cannot be triggered directly, so the function walks up to desc->parent_irq and replays the parent instead, giving up if there is none. Both paths append the descriptor to the global irq_resend_list hlist and schedule resend_tasklet, which calls desc->handle_irq(desc) for each queued descriptor.

Threaded Interrupts

Motivation: Why Threaded Interrupts Were Introduced

In Linux 2.6, all interrupt handling ran in hardirq context — the CPU executed the ISR with local interrupts disabled and preemption forbidden. This model was simple but created several increasingly serious problems as hardware evolved:

  1. Latency — A long-running ISR delays all other interrupts on that CPU. On a busy network interface processing thousands of packets per second, a slow ISR could cause jitter in unrelated real-time or audio workloads.
  2. Lock contention — ISRs cannot use sleeping locks (mutex, semaphore). Drivers that shared data with process-context code had to use spin_lock_irqsave() everywhere, increasing lock hold times and disabling interrupts in process context.
  3. Complexity — Drivers needed to split work into a “top half” (hardirq) and “bottom half” (tasklet or workqueue), manually managing state between the two halves.
  4. Priority inversion — All hardirqs have the same effective priority (above all kernel threads). A low-priority device could starve a high-priority real-time thread.
  5. PREEMPT_RT compatibility — The PREEMPT_RT patchset (merged into mainline) needs to convert most interrupt handlers into schedulable threads to achieve deterministic latency. The threaded IRQ infrastructure makes this transparent.

Benefits of Threaded Interrupts

BenefitExplanation
Reduced hardirq latencyThe primary handler runs for microseconds (just ack + wake); heavy work runs in thread context where it can be preempted.
Sleeping allowedThe thread function can use mutex_lock(), kmalloc(GFP_KERNEL), i2c_transfer(), spi_sync(), and other blocking APIs.
Priority schedulingIRQ threads run at SCHED_FIFO priority by default, but can be reprioritized via chrt or cgroup control to match system requirements.
Simpler driver codeNo need to manually split work into top/bottom halves; request_threaded_irq() provides the split naturally.
Better shared-IRQ behaviorIRQF_ONESHOT keeps the line masked until the thread finishes, preventing interrupt storms on level-triggered shared lines.
PREEMPT_RT readyWhen threadirqs is on the kernel command line, even non-threaded handlers are force-threaded transparently via irq_setup_forced_threading().
Clean synchronizationsynchronize_irq() waits for both hardirq and thread completion, making free_irq() and suspend paths race-free without driver effort.

How Threaded Interrupts Work

A driver may register both a primary hardirq handler and a thread_fn, or it may supply only a thread function and let Linux install irq_default_primary_handler (which simply returns IRQ_WAKE_THREAD). The primary handler runs in hardirq context, performs the truly urgent work (typically just acknowledging the device), and returns IRQ_WAKE_THREAD when the threaded continuation should run. The continuation executes in a dedicated kernel thread (irq/%d-%s) associated with the irqaction, running in process context where it can sleep, use mutexes, and perform I/O.

The thread itself is created by __setup_irq() via setup_irq_thread():

/* kernel/irq/manage.c — irq_thread() main loop (simplified) */
static int irq_thread(void *data)
{
    struct irqaction *action = data;
    struct irq_desc *desc = irq_to_desc(action->irq);

    sched_set_fifo(current);     /* SCHED_FIFO priority */

    while (!irq_wait_for_interrupt(desc, action)) {
        irqreturn_t action_ret;

        action_ret = handler_fn(desc, action);
        if (action_ret == IRQ_WAKE_THREAD)
            irq_wake_secondary(desc, action);

        wake_threads_waitq(desc);  /* notify synchronize_irq() */
    }
    return 0;
}

The thread sleeps in irq_wait_for_interrupt() until IRQTF_RUNTHREAD is set by __irq_wake_thread(). When woken, it calls the thread handler function, which eventually calls irq_finalize_oneshot() to unmask the IRQ line.

The __irq_wake_thread() Protocol

__irq_wake_thread() is called from __handle_irq_event_percpu() when a primary handler returns IRQ_WAKE_THREAD:

/* kernel/irq/handle.c */
void __irq_wake_thread(struct irq_desc *desc, struct irqaction *action)
{
    if (action->thread->flags & PF_EXITING)
        return;
    if (test_and_set_bit(IRQTF_RUNTHREAD, &action->thread_flags))
        return;
    desc->threads_oneshot |= action->thread_mask;
    atomic_inc(&desc->threads_active);
    wake_up_state(action->thread, TASK_INTERRUPTIBLE);
}

The function performs the following steps:

  1. Checks thread health — if the IRQ thread is exiting (PF_EXITING), there is no point waking it.
  2. Sets IRQTF_RUNTHREAD — test_and_set_bit() ensures idempotency; if the flag is already set (thread hasn’t consumed the previous wakeup), the function returns immediately.
  3. Records oneshot state — threads_oneshot is a bitmask tracking which actions have threads in flight. Oneshot IRQs remain masked until all threads finish.
  4. Increments threads_active — used by synchronize_irq() to wait for thread completion.
  5. Wakes the thread — the dedicated irq/%d-%s kernel thread transitions from TASK_INTERRUPTIBLE to runnable.

The Oneshot Mechanism

When IRQF_ONESHOT is set (required for thread-only handlers on shared lines), the flow handler masks the IRQ line after the primary handler completes. The line stays masked while the thread runs. When the thread finishes, irq_finalize_oneshot() unmasks the line:

/* kernel/irq/manage.c */
static void irq_finalize_oneshot(struct irq_desc *desc,
                                 struct irqaction *action)
{
    if (!(desc->istate & IRQS_ONESHOT))
        return;

    /* Wait if hardirq handler is still running on another CPU */
    while (unlikely(irqd_irq_inprogress(&desc->irq_data)))
        cpu_relax();

    desc->threads_oneshot &= ~action->thread_mask;
    if (!desc->threads_oneshot && !irqd_irq_disabled(&desc->irq_data))
        unmask_threaded_irq(desc);
}

This prevents an interrupt storm on level-triggered lines: without oneshot, the device would immediately re-assert the line after EOI, before the thread has had a chance to clear the device condition.

Force-Threading (threadirqs Boot Parameter)

When the kernel boots with threadirqs on the command line (or when running a PREEMPT_RT kernel), irq_setup_forced_threading() converts conventional hardirq handlers into threaded handlers transparently:

/* kernel/irq/manage.c */
static int irq_setup_forced_threading(struct irqaction *new)
{
    if (!force_irqthreads())
        return 0;
    if (new->flags & (IRQF_NO_THREAD | IRQF_PERCPU | IRQF_ONESHOT))
        return 0;

    new->flags |= IRQF_ONESHOT;
    set_bit(IRQTF_FORCED_THREAD, &new->thread_flags);
    new->thread_fn = new->handler;
    new->handler = irq_default_primary_handler;
    return 0;
}

The original handler is moved to thread_fn, and irq_default_primary_handler (which just returns IRQ_WAKE_THREAD) takes its place. Handlers marked IRQF_NO_THREAD (e.g., timer interrupts, IPI handlers) are exempt and always run in hardirq context, as are per-CPU handlers and handlers that already requested IRQF_ONESHOT.

The listing above is simplified in one respect that explains an otherwise mysterious field of struct irqaction. Consider a driver that already supplies both a primary handler and a thread_fn. Force-threading cannot simply overwrite thread_fn with the primary handler — that would discard the driver’s own threaded work. The real function therefore allocates a secondary action:

/* kernel/irq/manage.c — irq_setup_forced_threading(), full form */
if (new->handler == irq_default_primary_handler)
    return 0;                     /* already thread-only; nothing to do */

new->flags |= IRQF_ONESHOT;

if (new->handler && new->thread_fn) {
    new->secondary = kzalloc_obj(struct irqaction);
    if (!new->secondary)
        return -ENOMEM;
    new->secondary->handler   = irq_forced_secondary_handler;
    new->secondary->thread_fn = new->thread_fn;   /* driver's own thread work */
    new->secondary->dev_id    = new->dev_id;
    new->secondary->irq       = new->irq;
    new->secondary->name      = new->name;
}

set_bit(IRQTF_FORCED_THREAD, &new->thread_flags);
new->thread_fn = new->handler;                   /* ex-primary becomes threaded */
new->handler   = irq_default_primary_handler;

The result is a two-stage chain. The (now threaded) former primary handler runs first; when it returns IRQ_WAKE_THREAD, irq_thread() calls irq_wake_secondary(), which wakes the secondary action’s thread to run the driver’s original thread_fn. The two threads are given different priorities — irq_thread() calls sched_set_fifo_secondary() instead of sched_set_fifo() when it detects irq_forced_secondary_handler — so the ordering intended by the driver is preserved under SCHED_FIFO. This is the sole purpose of the secondary field listed in Table 4-40, and it is why forced threading is transparent to drivers that were never written with it in mind.

Using Threaded Interrupts in Driver Code

There are three common patterns for using threaded interrupts:

Pattern 1: Thread-only handler (simplest, for slow buses like I2C/SPI)

/* The driver only provides thread_fn; the kernel installs
 * irq_default_primary_handler automatically.
 * IRQF_ONESHOT is required to keep the line masked until thread finishes. */
ret = request_threaded_irq(client->irq,
                           NULL,              /* no primary handler */
                           my_threaded_handler,
                           IRQF_ONESHOT | IRQF_TRIGGER_LOW,
                           "my-i2c-device", priv);

This is ideal for I2C/SPI devices where even the “acknowledge” requires bus transactions that must sleep.

Pattern 2: Primary + thread (most common for fast buses)

/* Primary handler acks the device in hardirq context;
 * thread does the heavy processing */
ret = request_threaded_irq(pdev->irq,
                           my_primary_handler,  /* hardirq: ack device */
                           my_thread_handler,   /* thread: process data */
                           IRQF_SHARED | IRQF_ONESHOT,
                           "my-pci-device", priv);

static irqreturn_t my_primary_handler(int irq, void *dev_id)
{
    struct my_dev *priv = dev_id;
    u32 status = readl(priv->regs + IRQ_STATUS);

    if (!(status & MY_IRQ_BITS))
        return IRQ_NONE;

    /* Disable device interrupt source to prevent storm */
    writel(0, priv->regs + IRQ_ENABLE);
    priv->irq_status = status;

    return IRQ_WAKE_THREAD;
}

static irqreturn_t my_thread_handler(int irq, void *dev_id)
{
    struct my_dev *priv = dev_id;

    /* Can sleep here: process buffers, do DMA cleanup, etc. */
    handle_rx_buffers(priv);
    handle_tx_complete(priv);

    /* Re-enable device interrupts */
    writel(MY_IRQ_BITS, priv->regs + IRQ_ENABLE);

    return IRQ_HANDLED;
}

Pattern 3: Using devm_request_threaded_irq() (managed, auto-cleanup)

/* Device-managed version — automatically freed when device is removed */
ret = devm_request_threaded_irq(&pdev->dev, pdev->irq,
                                my_primary_handler,
                                my_thread_handler,
                                IRQF_SHARED | IRQF_ONESHOT,
                                dev_name(&pdev->dev), priv);
if (ret)
    return dev_err_probe(&pdev->dev, ret, "Failed to request IRQ\n");

Table 4-55. When to use each threaded interrupt pattern

PatternUse whenKey flagExample devices
Thread-only (handler=NULL)Device ack requires sleeping (I2C/SPI)IRQF_ONESHOTtouchscreens, sensors, PMICs
Primary + threadFast ack possible, heavy processing neededIRQF_ONESHOTNVMe, network cards, GPU
Plain hardirq (no thread)Ultra-low latency, minimal work—timer, IPI, simple GPIO edge

Synchronization with synchronize_irq()

When a driver frees an IRQ, suspends a device, or reconfigures hardware, it must ensure that no interrupt handler — neither hardirq nor threaded — is still running. Without this guarantee, the handler could access freed memory, touch powered-down registers, or corrupt data structures being torn down.

In Linux 2.6, this was a simpler problem: all handlers ran in hardirq context, so the kernel only needed to wait until IRQ_INPROGRESS cleared. With threaded interrupts, the kernel must also wait for threads that may be sleeping on a mutex, performing I2C transactions, or doing other blocking work. The synchronize_irq() family handles both cases.

The Three Synchronization Functions
/* kernel/irq/manage.c */

/* Phase 1: Wait for hardirq handlers on all CPUs */
static void __synchronize_hardirq(struct irq_desc *desc, bool sync_chip)
{
    bool inprogress;

    do {
        /* Busy-wait until IRQD_IRQ_INPROGRESS clears */
        while (irqd_irq_inprogress(&desc->irq_data))
            cpu_relax();

        /* Double-check under lock */
        guard(raw_spinlock_irqsave)(&desc->lock);
        inprogress = irqd_irq_inprogress(&desc->irq_data);

        /* Optionally check hardware-level in-flight state */
        if (!inprogress && sync_chip)
            __irq_get_irqchip_state(irqd, IRQCHIP_STATE_ACTIVE,
                                    &inprogress);
    } while (inprogress);
}

/* Phase 2: Wait for threaded handlers */
static void __synchronize_irq(struct irq_desc *desc)
{
    synchronize_irqwork(desc);          /* flush pending irq_work */
    __synchronize_hardirq(desc, true);  /* phase 1 + chip check */

    /* Wait until all IRQ threads finish */
    wait_event(desc->wait_for_threads,
               !atomic_read(&desc->threads_active));
}

void synchronize_irq(unsigned int irq)  /* full sync */
{
    struct irq_desc *desc = irq_to_desc(irq);
    if (desc)
        __synchronize_irq(desc);
}

The synchronization proceeds in three phases:

  1. Busy-wait for hardirq completion — __synchronize_hardirq() spins on IRQD_IRQ_INPROGRESS until it clears. Because handle_irq_event() sets IRQD_IRQ_INPROGRESS before releasing desc->lock and clears it after reacquiring the lock, this spin ensures the hardirq handler has returned. The double-check under lock prevents a race where the flag is cleared and immediately re-set by a new interrupt.
  2. Check hardware-level state — When sync_chip is true, the function also queries the interrupt controller via __irq_get_irqchip_state(IRQCHIP_STATE_ACTIVE). This catches the window where an interrupt has been accepted by the CPU (pending in the APIC or GIC) but handle_irq_event() hasn’t been called yet. Not all controllers support this check.
  3. Sleep-wait for thread completion — wait_event() blocks until desc->threads_active reaches zero. Each IRQ thread decrements this counter in wake_threads_waitq() after finishing its handler function. This phase makes synchronize_irq() a sleeping function — it cannot be called from hardirq or atomic context.
The Non-Sleeping Variant: synchronize_hardirq()

synchronize_hardirq() only does phase 1 (busy-wait for hardirq) without waiting for threads:

bool synchronize_hardirq(unsigned int irq)
{
    struct irq_desc *desc = irq_to_desc(irq);
    if (desc) {
        __synchronize_hardirq(desc, false);
        return !atomic_read(&desc->threads_active);
    }
    return true;
}

It returns false if threaded handlers are still active, letting the caller decide what to do. This variant can be called from atomic context and is used by disable_hardirq().

How threads_active Drives the Waitqueue

The threads_active counter connects __irq_wake_thread() to synchronize_irq():

__irq_wake_thread()                         irq_thread() loop body
  atomic_inc(&desc->threads_active)           action_ret = handler_fn(desc, action)
  wake_up_state(action->thread, ...)          wake_threads_waitq(desc)
                                                 ↓
                                              atomic_dec_and_test(&desc->threads_active)
                                              if (zero) wake_up(&desc->wait_for_threads)
                                                 ↓
                                              synchronize_irq() unblocks

If a thread exits abnormally (PF_EXITING), the irq_thread_dtor() callback — installed via task_work_add() — also decrements threads_active and calls irq_finalize_oneshot() to prevent a permanently masked IRQ line.

Who Calls synchronize_irq()?

Table 4-56. Key callers of synchronize_irq and synchronize_hardirq

CallerFunction usedWhy
free_irq() / __free_irq()__synchronize_irq()Ensure no handler accesses the irqaction after it is freed; prevent use-after-free
disable_irq()synchronize_irq()Ensure the handler has finished before the caller modifies shared state; can sleep
disable_hardirq()synchronize_hardirq()Same, but usable from atomic context; does not wait for threads
Device suspend (pm_runtime_suspend)synchronize_irq()Ensure no handler touches hardware after power is removed
Driver reconfigurationsynchronize_irq()Ensure handlers see consistent state after ring buffer resize, DMA reconfig, etc.
Deadlock Avoidance

A common mistake is calling synchronize_irq() while holding a resource that the IRQ handler also needs:

/* DEADLOCK: handler holds my_lock, synchronize_irq waits for handler */
spin_lock(&my_lock);
synchronize_irq(irq);   /* waits for handler that wants my_lock */
spin_unlock(&my_lock);

/* CORRECT: disable first, then take the lock */
disable_irq(irq);       /* internally calls synchronize_irq() */
spin_lock(&my_lock);
/* ... safe to modify shared state ... */
spin_unlock(&my_lock);
enable_irq(irq);

The __free_irq() implementation demonstrates the correct ordering: it removes the action from the descriptor chain under desc->lock, releases the lock, drops the bus lock (for I2C/SPI chip controllers), and only then calls __synchronize_irq(). This ensures the threaded handler cannot deadlock on the bus lock during irq_finalize_oneshot().

The abstraction boundary is clean: callers that need a fully quiescent interrupt line do not need to know whether the driver used plain hardirq handling, threaded handling, or a mixture — synchronize_irq() handles all cases transparently.

Dynamic Allocation and Freeing of IRQ Lines

In the old Linux 2.6 kernel a handful of IRQ lines were permanently wired to specific devices — the timer on IRQ 0, the keyboard on IRQ 1 — while the remaining lines were handed out dynamically as drivers appeared. The principle is unchanged in Linux 7.0.12, but the mechanism is considerably more flexible: irq_domain decouples the Linux IRQ number from the hardware line, MSI gives each device its own private interrupts rather than a shared pin, and the descriptor array itself has become a dynamically grown maple tree.

In Linux 2.6, request_irq() and free_irq() were the way drivers dynamically claim and release IRQ lines, with setup_irq() doing the internal work of inserting irqaction descriptors into the per-IRQ chain. The concept remains the same in Linux 7.0.12, but the implementation has evolved significantly: setup_irq() has been replaced by __setup_irq() (no longer exported), request_irq() is now a simple wrapper around request_threaded_irq(), device-managed variants (devm_request_irq()) automate cleanup, and the sparse IRQ subsystem allows the IRQ namespace to grow dynamically far beyond the old fixed-size irq_desc[] array.

The request_threaded_irq() Function — Allocating an IRQ Line

request_threaded_irq() is the primary function drivers use to claim an interrupt line. The older request_irq() is now just an inline wrapper that passes NULL for thread_fn:

/* include/linux/interrupt.h */
static inline int __must_check
request_irq(unsigned int irq, irq_handler_t handler, unsigned long flags,
            const char *name, void *dev)
{
    return request_threaded_irq(irq, handler, NULL, flags, name, dev);
}

The full request_threaded_irq() function in kernel/irq/manage.c performs the following steps:

/* kernel/irq/manage.c */
int request_threaded_irq(unsigned int irq, irq_handler_t handler,
                         irq_handler_t thread_fn, unsigned long irqflags,
                         const char *devname, void *dev_id)
{
    struct irqaction *action;
    struct irq_desc *desc;
    int retval;

    if (irq == IRQ_NOTCONNECTED)
        return -ENOTCONN;

    /* Sanity checks for shared IRQs */
    if (((irqflags & IRQF_SHARED) && !dev_id) ||
        ((irqflags & IRQF_SHARED) && (irqflags & IRQF_NO_AUTOEN)))
        return -EINVAL;

    desc = irq_to_desc(irq);
    if (!desc)
        return -EINVAL;

    if (!irq_settings_can_request(desc))
        return -EINVAL;

    /* If no primary handler, install default that just wakes thread */
    if (!handler) {
        if (!thread_fn)
            return -EINVAL;
        handler = irq_default_primary_handler;
    }

    /* Allocate and initialize the irqaction descriptor */
    action = kzalloc_obj(struct irqaction);
    if (!action)
        return -ENOMEM;

    action->handler = handler;
    action->thread_fn = thread_fn;
    action->flags = irqflags;
    action->name = devname;
    action->dev_id = dev_id;

    retval = __setup_irq(irq, desc, action);
    if (retval) {
        kfree(action->secondary);
        kfree(action);
    }
    return retval;
}

The function performs these actions:

  1. Validates parameters — checks that IRQ_NOTCONNECTED is not passed, that shared IRQs have a non-NULL dev_id, and that conflicting flags are not combined.
  2. Looks up the descriptor — irq_to_desc() finds the irq_desc for the given IRQ number.
  3. Checks requestability — irq_settings_can_request() verifies the IRQ is not reserved for internal use.
  4. Installs default primary handler if needed — if the driver passes handler=NULL, irq_default_primary_handler is installed.
  5. Allocates and fills irqaction — the descriptor is kzalloced and populated.
  6. Calls __setup_irq() — this internal function does the heavy lifting.

Table 4-57. IRQF flags for request_threaded_irq()

FlagPurpose
IRQF_SHAREDMultiple devices share this IRQ line; each handler must check if its device raised the interrupt
IRQF_ONESHOTKeep the line masked until the threaded handler finishes; required for thread-only handlers on shared lines
IRQF_NO_THREADNever force-thread this handler (timer, IPI, NMI handlers)
IRQF_TRIGGER_*Specify edge/level trigger type (rising, falling, high, low)
IRQF_NO_AUTOENDo not automatically enable the IRQ after registration
IRQF_NO_SUSPENDDo not disable this IRQ during system suspend
IRQF_COND_SUSPENDConditionally disable during suspend (only with IRQF_SHARED)
IRQF_PERCPUPer-CPU interrupt; no locking needed in flow handler

The __setup_irq() Function — Internal Setup

__setup_irq() in kernel/irq/manage.c is the successor to Linux 2.6’s setup_irq(). Unlike its predecessor, it is not exported to modules — drivers always go through request_threaded_irq(). The function performs the following key actions:

  1. Sets up force-threading — if the threadirqs boot parameter is active, converts the handler to a threaded handler transparently.
  2. Creates IRQ threads — if thread_fn is non-NULL, creates a dedicated kernel thread named irq/%d-%s.
  3. Acquires desc->request_mutex — serializes against concurrent request_irq() and free_irq() calls.
  4. Validates sharing — checks that both existing and new handlers have IRQF_SHARED, compatible trigger types, and consistent IRQF_ONESHOT usage.
  5. Appends the action — adds the new irqaction to the end of the linked list at desc->action.
  6. Enables the IRQ — if this is the first handler on the line, calls irq_startup() to unmask the interrupt at the controller.

The free_irq() Function — Freeing an IRQ Line

When a device driver finishes using an IRQ line, it calls free_irq() to remove its handler. The dev_id parameter identifies which handler to remove on shared IRQ lines.

free_irq() calls __free_irq(), which performs the following steps:

  1. Acquires desc->request_mutex and desc->lock.
  2. Walks the action chain — iterates desc->action to find the irqaction whose dev_id matches.
  3. Unlinks the action — removes the irqaction from the linked list.
  4. Shuts down the IRQ — if this was the last handler, calls irq_shutdown() to mask the interrupt.
  5. Releases locks and drops the bus lock (for I2C/SPI controllers).
  6. Calls __synchronize_irq() — waits for any in-flight hardirq or threaded handler to complete.
  7. Stops the IRQ thread — kthread_stop_put() terminates the thread and releases the reference.
  8. Frees memory — the irqaction is freed after synchronization completes.

The devm_request_threaded_irq() Function — Device-Managed IRQs

Modern drivers increasingly use the device-managed variant, which ties the IRQ lifetime to the device lifetime:

int devm_request_threaded_irq(struct device *dev, unsigned int irq,
                              irq_handler_t handler, irq_handler_t thread_fn,
                              unsigned long irqflags,
                              const char *devname, void *dev_id);

When the device is removed (via the driver model’s remove callback or at module unload), the IRQ is automatically freed. This eliminates an entire class of resource-leak bugs and simplifies error-handling paths in probe() functions. devm_free_irq() can be used for explicit early release if needed.

Example: a simplified PCI driver lifecycle

/* During probe — allocate IRQ */
ret = request_irq(pdev->irq, my_isr, IRQF_SHARED, "mydev", priv);
if (ret)
    return ret;

/* ... device is operational, my_isr() handles interrupts ... */

/* During remove — release IRQ */
free_irq(pdev->irq, priv);

Because the driver passes IRQF_SHARED and a unique priv pointer as dev_id, multiple drivers can share the same PCI interrupt line. When free_irq() is called, the kernel uses the priv pointer to identify which action to remove from the shared chain.

Table 4-58. IRQ allocation API comparison: Linux 2.6 vs 7.0.12

AspectLinux 2.6.11Linux 7.0.12
Registrationrequest_irq() / setup_irq()request_threaded_irq() / request_irq()
Threaded handlersNot availableBuilt-in via thread_fn parameter
Sharing flagSA_SHIRQIRQF_SHARED
Interrupt-disabled flagSA_INTERRUPTRemoved (hardirq context is always IRQs-disabled)
Dynamic descriptor allocFixed irq_desc[NR_IRQS] arrayMaple tree, irq_alloc_descs() on demand
Managed cleanupManual free_irq() onlydevm_request_irq() with automatic devres cleanup
Serializationspin_lock in setup_irq()desc->request_mutex + desc->lock in __setup_irq()
Chip PM integrationNoneirq_chip_pm_get() / irq_chip_pm_put()

Sparse IRQ: Dynamic Descriptor Allocation

In Linux 2.6, IRQ descriptors lived in a fixed-size array:

/* Linux 2.6 — kernel/irq/handle.c */
struct irq_desc irq_desc[NR_IRQS];

NR_IRQS was a compile-time constant, typically 224 on x86. This worked when systems had a handful of PCI devices, but modern servers with many NVMe SSDs, network adapters, and SR-IOV virtual functions can easily require thousands of MSI/MSI-X vectors.

Linux 7.0.12 uses sparse IRQ (CONFIG_SPARSE_IRQ, enabled by default on x86_64 and ARM64) to allocate descriptors on demand using a maple tree — a cache-efficient B-tree data structure:

/* kernel/irq/irqdesc.c */
static struct maple_tree sparse_irqs = MTREE_INIT_EXT(sparse_irqs,
                                      MT_FLAGS_ALLOC_RANGE | ...);

static void irq_insert_desc(unsigned int irq, struct irq_desc *desc)
{
    mtree_insert(&sparse_irqs, irq, desc, GFP_KERNEL);
}

static void delete_irq_desc(unsigned int irq)
{
    mtree_erase(&sparse_irqs, irq);
}

Descriptor lookup via irq_to_desc() performs a maple tree search:

struct irq_desc *irq_to_desc(unsigned int irq)
{
    return mtree_load(&sparse_irqs, irq);
}
The __irq_alloc_descs() Function — Allocating Descriptor Ranges
/* kernel/irq/irqdesc.c */
int __irq_alloc_descs(int irq, unsigned int from, unsigned int cnt,
                      int node, struct module *owner,
                      const struct irq_affinity_desc *affinity)
{
    int start;

    if (irq >= 0) {
        from = irq;          /* specific IRQ requested */
    } else {
        from = arch_dynirq_lower_bound(from);  /* arch can set floor */
    }

    guard(mutex)(&sparse_irq_lock);

    start = irq_find_free_area(from, cnt);     /* find contiguous range */
    if (irq >= 0 && start != irq)
        return -EEXIST;

    if (start + cnt > nr_irqs) {
        if (!irq_expand_nr_irqs(start + cnt))  /* grow namespace */
            return -ENOMEM;
    }
    return alloc_descs(start, cnt, node, affinity, owner);
}

The function:

  1. Determines the search floor — arch_dynirq_lower_bound() lets the architecture reserve a low range. On x86 (arch/x86/kernel/apic/io_apic.c) it returns ioapic_dynirq_base, the first IRQ number above every GSI claimed by a registered I/O APIC, so that GSI-numbered pin interrupts keep their identity mapping. It falls back to gsi_top when called before setup_IO_APIC(), and to the caller’s from argument on device-tree systems where both are zero.
  2. Finds a free range — irq_find_free_area() searches the maple tree for a contiguous block of cnt unused IRQ numbers.
  3. Expands the namespace — if the allocation would exceed nr_irqs, irq_expand_nr_irqs() tries to grow it. The ceiling is MAX_SPARSE_IRQS, which kernel/irq/internals.h defines as INT_MAX when CONFIG_SPARSE_IRQ is set (and as NR_IRQS otherwise). In practice the binding limit is the number of vectors the interrupt controllers can actually deliver, not the Linux IRQ namespace.
  4. Allocates descriptors — alloc_descs() calls alloc_desc() for each IRQ, which kzalloc_node()s the irq_desc, calls init_desc(), inserts it into the maple tree, and creates sysfs/debugfs entries.
The irq_free_descs() Function — Freeing Descriptors

free_desc() removes the sysfs and debugfs entries, deletes the descriptor from the maple tree, and frees it via call_rcu() to ensure no concurrent irq_to_desc() lookups are in progress.

Table 4-59. Comparison of IRQ descriptor management: Linux 2.6 vs. 7.0.12

AspectLinux 2.6Linux 7.0.12
Descriptor storageFixed array irq_desc[NR_IRQS]Maple tree (sparse_irqs), on-demand allocation
Maximum IRQsNR_IRQS (compile-time, typically 224)MAX_SPARSE_IRQS = INT_MAX; nr_irqs itself grows on demand
Allocation APIImplicit (descriptors always exist)irq_alloc_descs() / __irq_alloc_descs()
Freeing APINot needed (fixed array)irq_free_descs() with RCU-deferred free
MSI vector supportLimited (NR_IRQS ceiling)Thousands of MSI-X vectors per device
Memory efficiencyAll descriptors always allocatedOnly allocated descriptors consume memory
NUMA awarenessNokzalloc_node() places descriptors near the requesting CPU
LookupDirect array indexmtree_load() maple tree lookup (O(log n))
How Sparse IRQ Works with IRQ Domains

Sparse IRQ and IRQ domains (irq_domain) work together in a two-level allocation scheme:

  1. IRQ domain allocation — When an interrupt controller driver registers hardware IRQs, it calls irq_domain_alloc_irqs(), which internally calls __irq_alloc_descs() to allocate Linux IRQ numbers and then calls the domain’s alloc callback to set up the hardware mapping.
  2. MSI domain allocation — For MSI/MSI-X, msi_domain_alloc_irqs() allocates potentially hundreds of vectors at once. Each gets a sparse IRQ descriptor, a domain mapping, and a per-CPU vector assignment.
  3. Driver request — When a driver calls request_threaded_irq(), the descriptor already exists and is fully wired to the hardware through the domain hierarchy.
Device probe
    |
    v
pci_alloc_irq_vectors(dev, 1, 64, PCI_IRQ_MSIX)
    |
    v
msi_domain_alloc_irqs()
    |
    v
__irq_alloc_descs(cnt=64)          <-- 64 sparse descriptors
    |                                   allocated in maple tree
    v
irq_domain_alloc_irqs()            <-- each mapped to hw vector
    |
    v
request_threaded_irq(dev->irq)     <-- driver claims the IRQ

This matters for MSI-heavy systems. A large PCIe topology with many multi-queue NVMe devices may consume hundreds or thousands of Linux IRQ numbers. Allocating descriptors lazily avoids wasting memory on unused slots while still allowing the namespace to scale when devices request large vector sets.

Timer Interrupt Handling

Timer interrupts are the kernel’s heartbeat. Unlike I/O interrupts, which arrive at unpredictable times driven by external hardware, timer interrupts fire at regular intervals — or, in tickless (NO_HZ) configurations, on demand. Timer interrupts drive process scheduling (timeslice expiration), timekeeping (updating jiffies and the wall clock), timeout detection, delayed work processing, and RCU grace-period management.

It helps to keep two layers separate from the outset, because the chapter’s two architectures differ only in the lower one. The lower layer is a piece of per-CPU hardware — the Local APIC timer on x86_64, the Generic Timer on ARM64 — wrapped in a struct clock_event_device whose set_next_event() and set_state_*() methods know how to program that hardware. The upper layer is the architecture-independent tick machinery in kernel/time/, which never touches a timer register directly. The two meet at exactly one field: clock_event_device->event_handler. Every architecture’s low-level timer interrupt handler ends by calling that function pointer, and from that call onward the code is shared. This is the same layering seen for device interrupts in the previous section, where desc->handle_irq() played the equivalent role.

A second idea is easy to miss and explains much of the code: on an SMP system the timer interrupt is per-CPU, but most of the work it triggers is not. Every CPU must account its own current task, run its own expired timers, and check whether its own task should be preempted. Only one CPU may advance jiffies and the wall clock, or time would run at N times its proper rate on an N-CPU machine. The kernel resolves this with a single global variable, tick_do_timer_cpu (kernel/time/tick-common.c), naming the CPU currently responsible for global timekeeping. Every tick handler in the kernel is built around this split, and the sections below return to it repeatedly.

x86_64: Local APIC Timer

On x86_64, each CPU has a Local APIC timer that generates a per-CPU timer interrupt. The timer is programmed either to fire repeatedly at a fixed rate, or in one-shot mode, where each interrupt must explicitly program the next expiry. One-shot mode is what makes both high-resolution timers and tickless operation possible, and it is the mode nearly every modern system actually runs in.

The fixed rate, when one is used, is CONFIG_HZ. Its Kconfig default is 250 (kernel/Kconfig.hz offers 100, 250, 300, and 1000), but the defaults differ between the two architectures this book follows: arch/x86/configs/x86_64_defconfig selects CONFIG_HZ_1000, while arch/arm64/configs/defconfig sets no value and therefore inherits 250. Distribution kernels vary as well, so no single number should be assumed. What HZ still fixes unconditionally is the resolution of jiffies and the value of TICK_NSEC, even on a system whose hardware timer is running in one-shot mode and firing at irregular intervals.

The interrupt is delivered through LOCAL_TIMER_VECTOR, defined as 0xec in arch/x86/include/asm/irq_vectors.h. That places it among the system vectors above FIRST_SYSTEM_VECTOR (0xEB), not in the device-interrupt range described earlier in this chapter: the Local APIC timer is a CPU-local facility, so it needs no irq_desc, no IRQ domain, and no flow handler. It is wired straight into the IDT at boot and dispatched by the DEFINE_IDTENTRY_SYSVEC mechanism rather than through common_interrupt().

The handler is sysvec_apic_timer_interrupt(), defined in arch/x86/kernel/apic/apic.c:

/* arch/x86/kernel/apic/apic.c */
DEFINE_IDTENTRY_SYSVEC(sysvec_apic_timer_interrupt)
{
    struct pt_regs *old_regs = set_irq_regs(regs);

    apic_eoi();                       /* Acknowledge the APIC */
    trace_local_timer_entry(LOCAL_TIMER_VECTOR);
    local_apic_timer_interrupt();     /* Call the clock event handler */
    trace_local_timer_exit(LOCAL_TIMER_VECTOR);

    set_irq_regs(old_regs);
}

Two details of this handler are worth pausing on. The first is that apic_eoi() is issued before any work is done, not after. The Local APIC timer is edge-like in effect and strictly per-CPU, so there is no shared line to keep masked and no re-entrancy hazard of the kind that forced the careful ack/EOI ordering in the flow handlers of the previous section; acknowledging immediately simply shortens the window in which a further timer interrupt would be held off. The second is that the handler is bracketed by set_irq_regs()/set_irq_regs(old_regs). This publishes the interrupted register frame in a per-CPU variable so that code far below — profiling, and the tick’s own decision about whether the interrupted context was user or kernel — can retrieve it through get_irq_regs() without it being threaded through every intermediate call.

local_apic_timer_interrupt() is deliberately almost empty. It fetches this CPU’s struct clock_event_device and calls its event_handler:

/* arch/x86/kernel/apic/apic.c */
static void local_apic_timer_interrupt(void)
{
    struct clock_event_device *evt = this_cpu_ptr(&lapic_events);

    /*
     * Normally we should not be here till LAPIC has been initialized but
     * in some cases like kdump, its possible that there is a pending LAPIC
     * timer interrupt from previous kernel's context and is delivered in
     * new kernel the moment interrupts are enabled.
     *
     * Interrupts are enabled early and LAPIC is setup much later, hence
     * its possible that when we get here evt->event_handler is NULL.
     * Check for event_handler being NULL and discard the interrupt as
     * spurious.
     */
    if (!evt->event_handler) {
        pr_warn("Spurious LAPIC timer interrupt on cpu %d\n",
                smp_processor_id());
        /* Switch it off */
        lapic_timer_shutdown(evt);
        return;
    }

    /*
     * the NMI deadlock-detector uses this.
     */
    inc_irq_stat(apic_timer_irqs);

    evt->event_handler(evt);
}

The NULL check is not defensive clutter; it is the documented kexec and kdump case, in which a timer the previous kernel armed fires inside the new one before that kernel has set up its own Local APIC. There is no handler to run, so the timer is shut down and the interrupt discarded as spurious.

Which function event_handler points to is decided entirely by the generic layer, and it is the point at which the architecture code stops mattering. There are three possibilities, summarised in Table 4-60.

Table 4-60. Values of clock_event_device->event_handler

HandlerDefined inInstalled whenBehaviour
tick_handle_periodic()kernel/time/tick-common.cHZ_PERIODIC, or before the switch to one-shot mode completesDevice free-runs at HZ; the handler also catches up if ticks were missed
hrtimer_interrupt()kernel/time/hrtimer.cHigh-resolution mode, installed by tick_init_highres() via tick_switch_to_oneshot()Expires the hrtimer queue; the tick itself becomes one hrtimer among many
tick_nohz_lowres_handler()kernel/time/tick-sched.cTickless without HIGH_RES_TIMERSReprograms the device by hand each tick, because in low-resolution mode hrtimers are themselves driven by the tick
clockevents_handle_noop()kernel/time/clockevents.cTransiently, while a device is being replaced or a CPU is going downDiscards the event; exists so the pointer is never NULL

All three real handlers converge on update_process_times(), and it is there that the per-CPU work of a tick is actually defined:

/* kernel/time/timer.c */
void update_process_times(int user_tick)
{
    struct task_struct *p = current;

    /* Note: this timer irq context must be accounted for as well. */
    account_process_tick(p, user_tick);
    run_local_timers();
    rcu_sched_clock_irq(user_tick);
#ifdef CONFIG_IRQ_WORK
    if (in_hardirq())
        irq_work_tick();
#endif
    sched_tick();
    if (IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_POSIX_TIMERS))
        run_posix_cpu_timers();
}

Table 4-61 describes each step. The single user_tick argument — derived by the caller from user_mode(get_irq_regs()), which is why set_irq_regs() mattered above — is what tells the accounting code whether this CPU was executing user or kernel code at the instant the timer fired.

Table 4-61. The work performed by update_process_times(), in order

#CallPurpose
1account_process_tick()Charges the whole tick to the current task’s user, system, or idle time. Coarse by construction: the tick is billed to whoever happened to be running when it fired.
2run_local_timers()Raises TIMER_SOFTIRQ if any timer_list timer on this CPU has expired. Note that it only raises the softirq — the timers themselves run later, outside interrupt context.
3rcu_sched_clock_irq()Reports a quiescent state and lets RCU notice stalled grace periods. Without this, a CPU spinning in a long kernel loop would never be seen to pass through a quiescent state.
4irq_work_tick()Runs deferred irq_work callbacks — work queued from NMI or other contexts too restricted to run it directly. Guarded by in_hardirq(), since the tick can also be delivered from a timer softirq.
5sched_tick()The scheduler tick proper: updates runtime accounting, advances the current task’s slice, may set TIF_NEED_RESCHED, and triggers load balancing.
6run_posix_cpu_timers()Expires per-process and per-thread CPU-time timers (CLOCK_PROCESS_CPUTIME_ID, ITIMER_PROF, RLIMIT_CPU).

Linux 2.6 called step 5 scheduler_tick(), and that name survived in the tree for roughly two decades. It was renamed to sched_tick() in Linux 6.12 as part of a broader cleanup of the scheduler’s tick entry points. Older texts, and a great deal of writing still on the web, use the previous name.

The Periodic Tick and the Timekeeping CPU

Of the three handlers, tick_handle_periodic() is the simplest and shows the global/per-CPU split most plainly. Its core is tick_periodic():

/* kernel/time/tick-common.c */
static void tick_periodic(int cpu)
{
    if (READ_ONCE(tick_do_timer_cpu) == cpu) {
        raw_spin_lock(&jiffies_lock);
        write_seqcount_begin(&jiffies_seq);

        /* Keep track of the next tick event */
        tick_next_period = ktime_add_ns(tick_next_period, TICK_NSEC);

        do_timer(1);                    /* jiffies_64 += 1 */
        write_seqcount_end(&jiffies_seq);
        raw_spin_unlock(&jiffies_lock);
        update_wall_time();
    }

    update_process_times(user_mode(get_irq_regs()));
    profile_tick(CPU_PROFILING);
}

The shape of this function is the split. The guarded block — incrementing jiffies_64 through do_timer() and folding the elapsed time into the wall clock through update_wall_time() — runs on exactly one CPU, the one named by tick_do_timer_cpu. Everything after it runs on every CPU. Readers of jiffies and of the wall clock are protected by jiffies_seq, a seqcount, so they can read a consistent pair of values without taking jiffies_lock at all.

tick_do_timer_cpu is not fixed at boot. It holds one of:

  • a CPU number — that CPU is currently responsible for global timekeeping;
  • TICK_DO_TIMER_NONE — nobody currently owns it, which is the normal state when every CPU has gone idle and the tick has been stopped. The next CPU to wake and notice the value claims the duty for itself;
  • TICK_DO_TIMER_BOOT — the initial value, meaning the duty has not yet been handed to a real CPU.

The hand-off matters because the timekeeping CPU cannot go tickless in the way the others can: whoever holds the duty must keep taking ticks, or jiffies would stop advancing for the whole system. Under NO_HZ_FULL this becomes a structural constraint rather than an implementation detail, as the next-but-one section describes. The related variable tick_do_timer_boot_cpu exists for exactly that reason: on a kernel booted with nohz_full=, the boot CPU holds the duty temporarily until a CPU that is not in the nohz_full set comes online and can take it permanently.

ARM64: Generic Timer

ARM64 uses the ARM Generic Timer, an architected part of the CPU rather than a separate interrupt-controller function. Every core sees a single always-running counter, readable as CNTVCT_EL0, plus several independent comparators. A timer fires when its 64-bit comparator value is reached, so programming a timer means writing an absolute count, not a countdown.

Which comparator Linux uses depends on the exception level it booted at and on firmware, and each is delivered on its own PPI. Because these are PPIs, every CPU sees its own instance of the same INTID — the mechanism described under IRQs and Interrupt Controllers earlier in this chapter — and the driver registers the handler with request_percpu_irq() rather than request_irq().

Table 4-62. ARM Generic Timer comparators used by Linux

Timerarch_timer_ppi_nrConventional INTIDControl / comparatorUsed when
VirtualARCH_TIMER_VIRT_PPI27CNTV_CTL_EL0 / CNTV_CVAL_EL0The default. Kernel at EL1, counting virtual time (physical counter minus the hypervisor’s offset)
Physical, non-secureARCH_TIMER_PHYS_NONSECURE_PPI30CNTP_CTL_EL0 / CNTP_CVAL_EL0Kernel booted at EL2, or firmware that does not provide a usable virtual timer
Physical, secureARCH_TIMER_PHYS_SECURE_PPI29CNTP_CTL_EL0 / CNTP_CVAL_EL0Secure-world firmware; rarely Linux’s
HypervisorARCH_TIMER_HYP_PPI26CNTHP_CTL_EL2 / CNTHP_CVAL_EL2KVM, for its own use at EL2

The choice is recorded in a single variable, whose initialiser states the default plainly:

/* drivers/clocksource/arm_arch_timer.c */
static enum arch_timer_ppi_nr arch_timer_uses_ppi __ro_after_init = ARCH_TIMER_VIRT_PPI;

The INTIDs above are the values the ARM architecture recommends and that essentially all hardware follows, but Linux never hardcodes them: it takes them from the interrupts property of the arm,armv8-timer device-tree node, or from the ACPI GTDT.

The handler registered for that PPI is arch_timer_handler_virt() or arch_timer_handler_phys() (drivers/clocksource/arm_arch_timer.c); both are one-line wrappers that record which register accessor to use and call the shared timer_handler():

/* drivers/clocksource/arm_arch_timer.c */
static __always_inline irqreturn_t timer_handler(const int access,
                                                 struct clock_event_device *evt)
{
    unsigned long ctrl;

    ctrl = arch_timer_reg_read_cp15(access, ARCH_TIMER_REG_CTRL);
    if (ctrl & ARCH_TIMER_CTRL_IT_STAT) {
        ctrl |= ARCH_TIMER_CTRL_IT_MASK;
        arch_timer_reg_write_cp15(access, ARCH_TIMER_REG_CTRL, ctrl);
        evt->event_handler(evt);
        return IRQ_HANDLED;
    }

    return IRQ_NONE;
}

Three things in this short function are worth drawing out, because each has a counterpart on x86_64 that looks quite different.

First, the IT_STAT test is a genuine hardware status check, and the IRQ_NONE return is real. IT_STAT is set only while the comparator condition actually holds, so a handler entered without it is a spurious interrupt. Returning IRQ_NONE feeds the shared-line accounting described under I/O Interrupt Handling — this is an ordinary irqaction handler on an ordinary IRQ line, unlike the x86_64 path, which bypasses the IRQ layer entirely.

Second, setting IT_MASK masks the timer’s interrupt output; it does not stop the timer or clear a pending condition. The Generic Timer has no write-to-acknowledge register. Its output is level-driven: the comparator condition remains true from the moment it is reached, so the only way to stop the interrupt from re-asserting immediately is to mask it, and the only way to unmask it safely is to program a new comparator value. That is precisely what the eventual set_next_event() does, which is why masking here is sufficient rather than merely a stopgap.

Third, and most importantly, the last thing the function does is call evt->event_handler(evt). That is the same field the x86_64 path reaches through local_apic_timer_interrupt(). From this line onward, Table 4-60, Table 4-61, tick_periodic(), tick_do_timer_cpu, and everything in the following section apply unchanged on both architectures.

When the Local Timer Stops: Tick Broadcast

Both architectures have so far been described as though the per-CPU timer always keeps running. On a great deal of real hardware it does not. Saving power means putting an idle core into a deep C-state, and in the deeper states the clock feeding the Local APIC timer is itself gated off. A CPU that programmed its timer to wake it in ten milliseconds and then entered such a state would simply never wake up.

The kernel is told about this by a feature flag on the clock_event_device, whose definition in include/linux/clockchips.h is candid about the situation:

/*
 * x86(64) specific (mis)features:
 *
 * - Clockevent source stops in C3 State and needs broadcast support.
 * - Local APIC timer is used as a dummy device.
 */
# define CLOCK_EVT_FEAT_C3STOP		0x000008

On x86_64 the Local APIC timer declares CLOCK_EVT_FEAT_C3STOP in its static lapic_clockevent initialiser, and setup_APIC_timer() clears it again on any CPU whose CPUID reports X86_FEATURE_ARAT — Always-Running APIC Timer, the hardware guarantee that the timer keeps counting through the deep states. The same call raises the device’s rating from 100 to 150, making it preferable to a per-CPU HPET channel. On ARM64 the equivalent decision is not a CPU feature but a firmware description: arch_timer_c3stop is set from the absence of the always-on property in the device-tree node, or from acpi_gtdt_c3stop(), and the flag is added to the clock event device only if it is true.

When a CPU whose timer will stop enters idle, it hands its wakeup over to a shared timer that does keep running — the broadcast device, typically the HPET on x86_64. That device is programmed for the earliest expiry among all the CPUs currently parked in this way, and when it fires, the CPUs whose deadlines have passed must be woken. Waking them is an interrupt sent from one CPU to others, which is to say an IPI, and this is where the timer subsystem meets the mechanism described in the next section.

The dispatch happens in tick_do_broadcast() (kernel/time/tick-broadcast.c), which calls td->evtdev->broadcast(mask). The two architectures supply that callback differently, and the difference is instructive:

  • x86_64 sets it explicitly in the lapic_clockevent initialiser, as .broadcast = lapic_timer_broadcast. That function is a single line: __apic_send_IPI_mask(mask, LOCAL_TIMER_VECTOR). It reuses the ordinary Local APIC timer vector, so the woken CPU takes what looks to it like a perfectly normal timer interrupt.
  • ARM64 sets nothing. tick_device_setup_broadcast_func() notices the NULL pointer and substitutes the architecture’s own tick_broadcast(), which arch/arm64/kernel/smp.c defines as smp_cross_call(mask, IPI_TIMER) — the SGI listed as IPI_TIMER in Table 4-65. If neither is available the kernel installs err_broadcast(), which does nothing but print “Failed to broadcast timer tick. Some CPUs may be unresponsive.”

On the receiving side the two paths converge again, exactly as they did for the ordinary tick:

/* kernel/time/tick-broadcast.c */
int tick_receive_broadcast(void)
{
    struct tick_device *td = this_cpu_ptr(&tick_cpu_device);
    struct clock_event_device *evt = td->evtdev;

    if (!evt)
        return -ENODEV;

    if (!evt->event_handler)
        return -EINVAL;

    evt->event_handler(evt);
    return 0;
}

The last line is evt->event_handler(evt) once more. Whether a tick originates in a Local APIC timer, an ARM Generic Timer comparator, or an HPET-driven IPI sent to a sleeping core, it reaches the generic layer through the same single function pointer, and everything described in Table 4-61 then follows unchanged.

Tickless Operation (NO_HZ)

Everything above has assumed that a tick arrives, and the only question was what it does. The remaining question is whether it arrives at all. In modern kernels the timer interrupt does not necessarily fire at a fixed rate, and on an idle or carefully isolated CPU it may not fire for seconds at a time. The motivation is not merely power: a tick that fires 250 or 1000 times a second on a CPU with nothing to do prevents the processor from entering its deeper C-states, and on a CPU dedicated to one latency-sensitive task it is a periodic source of jitter and cache pollution.

The three modes are a Kconfig choice in kernel/time/Kconfig, so exactly one is compiled in.

Table 4-63. Timer tick modes in Linux 7.0.12

ModeConfig optionBehaviorUse case
PeriodicHZ_PERIODICFixed-rate tick at HZ frequency on every CPUSimple, predictable
Idle ticklessNO_HZ_IDLE (default)Stop ticks when CPU is idle; restart on wakeupPower saving
Full ticklessNO_HZ_FULLCan also stop the tick on a CPU that is running a task, provided only one is runnable — but only for CPUs listed in nohz_full=Low-latency, real-time, HPC

In NO_HZ_IDLE mode, which is what arch/arm64/configs/defconfig and essentially every distribution kernel select, the tick is not simply switched off when a CPU goes idle. It is reprogrammed to the next event that CPU actually needs to service: the earliest of its pending timer_list timers and hrtimers. If nothing is pending, the CPU can sleep until an interrupt arrives from elsewhere. The idle CPU therefore stops taking periodic interrupts without stopping being able to run timers on time. On waking, tick_nohz_restart_sched_tick() accounts for the idle period in one step, folding all the jiffies that elapsed while the CPU slept into the timekeeping state at once rather than replaying them.

NO_HZ_FULL is more often misunderstood, so it is worth being precise about what enabling it does and does not do. Compiling it in changes nothing by itself. The Kconfig help text is explicit: By default, without passing the nohz_full parameter, this behaves just like NO_HZ_IDLE. A CPU only becomes a full-dynticks CPU if it is named in the nohz_full= boot parameter, which is why distributions can ship CONFIG_NO_HZ_FULL=y without imposing any cost on ordinary users.

Even then the tick stops only under a specific condition — when exactly one task is runnable on that CPU. The reason is straightforward: the scheduler tick exists largely to arbitrate between competing runnable tasks, and with a single runnable task there is nothing to arbitrate. Add a second and the tick must return.

Two consequences follow from the fact that a CPU can now be running user code for long periods with no tick, and they explain the list of options NO_HZ_FULL selects.

  • Somebody must still keep time. The Kconfig comment says it directly — We need at least one periodic CPU for timekeeping — which is why NO_HZ_FULL depends on SMP. A full-dynticks CPU can never hold tick_do_timer_cpu, so at least one housekeeping CPU outside the nohz_full= set must keep ticking on behalf of the whole system.
  • Accounting can no longer be sampled. Charging a whole tick to whatever was running, as account_process_tick() does, is meaningless when ticks may be seconds apart. NO_HZ_FULL therefore selects VIRT_CPU_ACCOUNTING_GEN, which timestamps every user↔kernel transition and computes exact times instead of sampling them. It also selects RCU_NOCB_CPU, so RCU callbacks are offloaded to kernel threads on other CPUs rather than being processed from the (now absent) tick, plus IRQ_WORK and CPU_ISOLATION.

RCU needs one further mechanism. A quiescent state is normally reported from rcu_sched_clock_irq() on each tick, so a CPU that has stopped ticking would appear to RCU to be permanently unresponsive and would stall every grace period in the system. The answer is context tracking: the kernel records the transition to user space as entry into an extended quiescent state (EQS). Because a CPU executing user code cannot possibly hold an RCU read-side critical section, RCU may treat it as quiescent for the entire time it stays there, without interrupting it to ask. This is what CONTEXT_TRACKING_USER and the HAVE_CONTEXT_TRACKING_USER dependency provide, and it is the reason the cost of NO_HZ_FULL shows up as slightly more expensive syscalls and exceptions: each boundary crossing must now be instrumented.

Interprocessor Interrupt Handling

Interprocessor interrupts (IPIs) allow a CPU to send interrupt signals to any other CPU in the system. Every mechanism described so far in this chapter has been about hardware interrupting software. An IPI inverts that: software is the source, and the interrupt controller is used purely as a delivery service between cores. The kernel needs this because a great deal of its work is inherently remote — a task woken on one CPU may belong on another, a page table modified here may be cached in a TLB there, and a CPU spinning with interrupts disabled can be reached in no other way.

It is worth fixing the three layers involved before looking at either architecture, because the two differ in exactly one of them. At the top, generic kernel code calls an architecture-neutral function such as smp_send_reschedule() or smp_call_function_many(). In the middle, each architecture implements the send side and a matching receive side. At the bottom, the interrupt controller physically delivers the signal. The interesting difference is in the middle layer: on x86_64 an IPI arrives on a dedicated system vector and bypasses the IRQ subsystem entirely, whereas on ARM64 it arrives as an ordinary per-CPU IRQ with a real irq_desc, an irqaction, and an entry in /proc/interrupts.

On x86_64, an IPI is delivered by writing the Local APIC’s Interrupt Command Register (ICR) — not through an IRQ line, but as a message on the interconnect naming the destination APIC ID and the vector to raise. On ARM64, IPIs are delivered as Software Generated Interrupts (SGIs), INTIDs 0–15, generated by writing the GICv3 system register ICC_SGI1R_EL1. Both are described in detail below.

x86_64 IPI Vectors

On multiprocessor x86_64 systems, Linux uses several dedicated IDT vectors for IPIs. They are allocated downwards from the top of the vector space, in the region arch/x86/include/asm/irq_vectors.h introduces with the comment “Special IRQ vectors used by the SMP architecture, 0xf0-0xff”. Every one of them sits above FIRST_SYSTEM_VECTOR, which means — exactly as for the Local APIC timer discussed in the previous section — that none of them has an irq_desc, an IRQ domain mapping, or a flow handler. They are wired straight into the IDT at boot, and the higher the vector number, the higher its delivery priority. Table 4-64 lists the ones used as interprocessor interrupts; the same region also holds non-IPI system vectors such as ERROR_APIC_VECTOR (0xFE), SPURIOUS_APIC_VECTOR (0xFF), and LOCAL_TIMER_VECTOR (0xEC).

Table 4-64. x86_64 IPI vectors in Linux 7.0.12

VectorNameHandlerPurpose
0xFDRESCHEDULE_VECTORsysvec_reschedule_ipi()Wake a CPU to reschedule
0xFCCALL_FUNCTION_VECTORsysvec_call_function()Execute function on remote CPUs
0xFBCALL_FUNCTION_SINGLE_VECTORsysvec_call_function_single()Execute function on one remote CPU
0xF8REBOOT_VECTORsysvec_reboot()Stop CPU during reboot
0xF7X86_PLATFORM_IPI_VECTORsysvec_x86_platform_ipi()Generic vector reserved for platform-specific use
0xF6IRQ_WORK_VECTORsysvec_irq_work()Deferred work from NMI-safe contexts

The reschedule IPI is by far the most frequent, and it is also the most instructive, because it shows that the purpose of an IPI is often not to run code on the target at all — it is merely to force the target back through the kernel’s exit path. The decision is made in __resched_curr() (kernel/sched/core.c), reached from resched_curr() whenever the scheduler concludes that the task currently running on some CPU should give way. That function sets TIF_NEED_RESCHED on the target’s current task and, if the target is a different CPU, calls smp_send_reschedule(). The target may be spinning in a long kernel loop or sitting in a low-power idle state; either way it will not notice the flag until something makes it re-examine its state.

Two branches of that function are worth noting, because both illuminate what the IPI is for. When the target is the current CPU, no interrupt is sent at all: the code sets the flag and calls set_preempt_need_resched() directly, since it can simply update its own preempt_count. And when the target is a different CPU, the IPI is sent only if set_nr_and_not_polling() reports that the target was not polling. An idle CPU waiting in a polling loop is already watching the need-resched flag in memory, so setting it is enough; sending an interrupt as well would be pure overhead. That path is traced as trace_sched_wake_idle_without_ipi, and it is why the reschedule count in /proc/interrupts is often far lower than the number of wakeups a system performs.

/* arch/x86/kernel/smp.c */
DEFINE_IDTENTRY_SYSVEC_SIMPLE(sysvec_reschedule_ipi)
{
    apic_eoi();
    trace_reschedule_entry(RESCHEDULE_VECTOR);
    inc_irq_stat(irq_resched_count);
    scheduler_ipi();
    trace_reschedule_exit(RESCHEDULE_VECTOR);
}

Note first the _SIMPLE suffix, which selects a lighter entry macro than the one used by the other system vectors in Table 4-64. Ordinary DEFINE_IDTENTRY_SYSVEC routes the body through run_sysvec_on_irqstack_cond(), switching to the per-CPU interrupt stack when the entry came from kernel mode. The _SIMPLE variant, as its comment in arch/x86/include/asm/idtentry.h puts it, “runs the function on the interrupted stack. No switch to IRQ stack and only the minimal __irq_enter/exit() handling”, and is reserved for “‘empty’ vectors like reschedule IPI and KVM posted interrupt vectors”. The reschedule IPI qualifies because its handler does almost nothing, and skipping the stack switch matters for something sent this often.

The handler is therefore almost empty, and scheduler_ipi() — a one-line inline function in include/linux/sched.h — is emptier still:

/* include/linux/sched.h */
static __always_inline void scheduler_ipi(void)
{
    /*
     * Fold TIF_NEED_RESCHED into the preempt_count; anybody setting
     * TIF_NEED_RESCHED remotely (for the first time) will also send
     * this IPI.
     */
    preempt_fold_need_resched();
}

The comment repays careful reading. TIF_NEED_RESCHED lives in the thread-info flags, but the fast path that decides whether preemption is allowed inspects only preempt_count, where a dedicated bit mirrors the flag. The sender set the flag on a remote CPU and cannot safely update that CPU’s preempt_count, so the fold has to happen locally — and running it is the entire purpose of the interrupt. Everything else follows from machinery already described in this chapter: the interrupt returns through irqentry_exit(), which sees the need-resched state and calls the scheduler. The IPI does not reschedule anything; it merely guarantees that the target reaches a point where rescheduling is considered.

The send side is correspondingly thin. native_smp_send_reschedule() (arch/x86/kernel/apic/ipi.c) checks that the target is online and calls __apic_send_IPI(cpu, RESCHEDULE_VECTOR), under a comment noting that it “goes straight through and wastes no time serializing anything” because the worst consequence of losing the race is a missed reschedule, which the next tick will catch.

ARM64 IPI Types

ARM64 uses GICv3 Software Generated Interrupts (SGIs, INTID 0–15) for IPIs. Unlike the x86_64 system vectors, these are ordinary interrupts as far as the rest of the kernel is concerned: set_smp_ipi_range_percpu() claims a contiguous block of Linux IRQ numbers starting at ipi_irq_base, registers a single handler across them with request_percpu_irq(irq, ipi_handler, "IPI", &irq_stat), and marks each one IRQ_HIDDEN so that it is accounted separately rather than listed among the device interrupts in /proc/interrupts. An IPI on ARM64 therefore has a real irq_desc and travels the flow-handler path described earlier in this chapter, whereas on x86_64 it bypasses the IRQ layer entirely.

The message types are an enumeration in arch/arm64/include/asm/smp.h, and its shape carries information:

/* arch/arm64/include/asm/smp.h */
enum ipi_msg_type {
	IPI_RESCHEDULE,
	IPI_CALL_FUNC,
	IPI_CPU_STOP,
	IPI_CPU_STOP_NMI,
	IPI_TIMER,
	IPI_IRQ_WORK,
	NR_IPI,
	/*
	 * Any enum >= NR_IPI and < MAX_IPI is special and not tracable
	 * with trace_ipi_*
	 */
	IPI_CPU_BACKTRACE = NR_IPI,
	IPI_KGDB_ROUNDUP,
	MAX_IPI
};

The split at NR_IPI divides the ordinary IPIs, which carry tracepoints and a human-readable name in ipi_types[], from the two diagnostic ones. Those last two are excluded from tracing precisely because of when they are used: taking a backtrace of a wedged CPU or rounding CPUs up into KGDB happens when the system is already misbehaving, quite possibly inside the tracing code itself. Table 4-65 lists all eight.

Table 4-65. ARM64 IPI types in Linux 7.0.12

IPINamePurpose
IPI_RESCHEDULERescheduling interruptsTrigger rescheduling on target CPU
IPI_CALL_FUNCFunction call interruptssmp_call_function() remote execution
IPI_CPU_STOPCPU stop interruptsStop CPU during shutdown/kexec
IPI_CPU_STOP_NMICPU stop NMIsNMI-based CPU stop for crash/panic
IPI_TIMERTimer broadcast interruptsTimer events when local timer unavailable
IPI_IRQ_WORKIRQ work interruptsDeferred work from NMI-safe contexts
IPI_CPU_BACKTRACECPU backtrace interruptsDump a wedged CPU’s stack; not traceable
IPI_KGDB_ROUNDUPKGDB roundup interruptsHalt other CPUs for the debugger; not traceable

Three of these can be promoted to pseudo-NMIs. ipi_should_be_nmi() returns true for IPI_CPU_STOP_NMI, IPI_CPU_BACKTRACE, and IPI_KGDB_ROUNDUP, but only when system_uses_irq_prio_masking() holds — that is, when the GIC priority-masking scheme described under IRQs and Interrupt Controllers is in use. When it is, ipi_setup() registers those three with prepare_percpu_nmi() and enable_percpu_nmi() instead of enable_percpu_irq(), giving them a priority that remains deliverable while ordinary interrupts are masked. This is what makes them useful at all: a CPU stuck in a region with interrupts disabled is exactly the CPU whose backtrace is wanted, and an ordinary IPI would never reach it. Where priority masking is unavailable they degrade silently to ordinary IPIs, which is why IPI_CPU_STOP and IPI_CPU_STOP_NMI both exist and share a handler.

Every one of those IRQs is registered with the same handler, ipi_handler(), which recovers the message type from the IRQ number and returns IRQ_HANDLED unconditionally — an IPI cannot be spurious, because it was sent deliberately:

/* arch/arm64/kernel/smp.c */
static irqreturn_t ipi_handler(int irq, void *data)
{
	unsigned int ipi = (irq - ipi_irq_base) % nr_ipi;

	do_handle_IPI(ipi);
	return IRQ_HANDLED;
}

The dispatch itself is a switch over the message type. It is worth reading in full, because several of its arms are guarded and the guards say something about the design:

/* arch/arm64/kernel/smp.c */
static void do_handle_IPI(int ipinr)
{
	unsigned int cpu = smp_processor_id();

	if ((unsigned)ipinr < NR_IPI)
		trace_ipi_entry(ipi_types[ipinr]);

	switch (ipinr) {
	case IPI_RESCHEDULE:
		scheduler_ipi();
		break;

	case IPI_CALL_FUNC:
		generic_smp_call_function_interrupt();
		break;

	case IPI_CPU_STOP:
	case IPI_CPU_STOP_NMI:
		if (IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_KEXEC_CORE) && crash_stop) {
			ipi_cpu_crash_stop(cpu, get_irq_regs());
			unreachable();
		} else {
			local_cpu_stop(cpu);
		}
		break;

#ifdef CONFIG_GENERIC_CLOCKEVENTS_BROADCAST
	case IPI_TIMER:
		tick_receive_broadcast();
		break;
#endif

#ifdef CONFIG_IRQ_WORK
	case IPI_IRQ_WORK:
		irq_work_run();
		break;
#endif

	case IPI_CPU_BACKTRACE:
		/*
		 * NOTE: in some cases this _won't_ be NMI context. See the
		 * comment in arch_trigger_cpumask_backtrace().
		 */
		nmi_cpu_backtrace(get_irq_regs());
		break;

	case IPI_KGDB_ROUNDUP:
		kgdb_nmicallback(cpu, get_irq_regs());
		break;

	default:
		pr_crit("CPU%u: Unknown IPI message 0x%x\n", cpu, ipinr);
		break;
	}

	if ((unsigned)ipinr < NR_IPI)
		trace_ipi_exit(ipi_types[ipinr]);
}

The IPI_RESCHEDULE arm calls the very same scheduler_ipi() examined above, so the reschedule story told for x86_64 applies here unchanged. IPI_TIMER calls tick_receive_broadcast(), closing the loop with the tick-broadcast mechanism of the previous section: this is the arm that runs on a CPU whose Generic Timer was gated off in a deep idle state. The stop arms distinguish an orderly shutdown from a crash — when crash_stop is set the CPU saves its register state for the kdump kernel through ipi_cpu_crash_stop() and never returns, which is why the compiler is told unreachable(). Both diagnostic arms retrieve the interrupted register frame with get_irq_regs(), the same per-CPU stash the timer interrupt used in the previous section; without it, a backtrace would show only the IPI handler rather than the code that was actually stuck.

Sending IPIs

Generic kernel code never names a vector or an SGI. It calls an architecture-neutral function — smp_send_reschedule() to nudge one CPU, or one of the smp_call_function() family to run a function elsewhere — and the architecture supplies the implementation. Table 4-66 shows where each of those lands.

Table 4-66. IPI sending functions

Generic entry pointx86_64 implementationARM64 implementation
smp_send_reschedule(cpu)native_smp_send_reschedule() → __apic_send_IPI(cpu, RESCHEDULE_VECTOR)arch_smp_send_reschedule() → smp_cross_call(cpumask_of(cpu), IPI_RESCHEDULE)
smp_call_function_single()native_send_call_func_single_ipi() → __apic_send_IPI(cpu, CALL_FUNCTION_SINGLE_VECTOR)smp_cross_call(cpumask_of(cpu), IPI_CALL_FUNC)
smp_call_function_many()native_send_call_func_ipi() → __apic_send_IPI_mask(mask, CALL_FUNCTION_VECTOR)smp_cross_call(mask, IPI_CALL_FUNC)
Send to all, or all but self__apic_send_IPI_all(), __apic_send_IPI_allbutself()smp_cross_call(cpu_online_mask, ipi), with self cleared from the mask

The __apic_ functions are not direct calls through a function-pointer table. Each is a thin wrapper around a static call — __apic_send_IPI_mask() expands to static_call(apic_call_send_IPI_mask)(...) — which is patched at boot to jump directly to whichever APIC driver was selected (xAPIC flat, x2APIC cluster, and so on). The indirection is therefore resolved once at boot rather than on every send, which matters on a path this hot. Older kernels implement these as ordinary indirect calls of the form apic->send_IPI_mask(...).

native_send_call_func_ipi() adds one further optimization worth knowing about, because it is visible in traces. When the static key apic_use_ipi_shorthand is enabled and the target mask happens to cover every online CPU, it abandons the mask and issues a single broadcast — __apic_send_IPI_all() or __apic_send_IPI_allbutself() — using the APIC’s shorthand destination encoding. One ICR write then reaches every CPU, instead of one write per target.

On the ARM64 side, smp_cross_call() does not write ICC_SGI1R_EL1 itself, despite what its name and older kernels might suggest. Because IPIs are modelled as ordinary interrupts, the send travels down through the irqchip layer, and the register write happens in the GIC driver at the bottom:

  1. smp_cross_call() emits the trace_ipi_raise tracepoint and calls arm64_send_ipi().
  2. arm64_send_ipi() calls the generic __ipi_send_mask() (or __ipi_send_single() per CPU, when each CPU has its own descriptor) from kernel/irq/ipi.c.
  3. That invokes the irqchip callback chip->ipi_send_mask, which for GICv3 is gic_ipi_send_mask().
  4. gic_ipi_send_mask() issues a dsb(ishst) so that memory written before the IPI is visible to the target, groups the destination CPUs by cluster with gic_compute_target_list(), and calls gic_send_sgi() once per cluster.
  5. gic_send_sgi() assembles the affinity fields, the SGI ID, and the 16-bit target list into one value and writes it to ICC_SGI1R_EL1.

The barrier in step 4 is not incidental. An IPI almost always refers to data the sender prepared first — the function pointer and arguments for a remote call, or a flag the target is expected to observe — and nothing in the interrupt controller guarantees that those stores are visible before the interrupt arrives. The dsb(ishst) supplies that ordering for the whole inner-shareable domain. The target-list encoding in step 5 also explains the clustering: a single ICC_SGI1R_EL1 write can name at most sixteen CPUs, all within one affinity-1 cluster, so a broadcast across a large machine becomes several writes.

TLB Invalidation via IPI

One of the most performance-critical uses of IPIs is TLB invalidation (also called “TLB shootdown”). When a kernel operation modifies page tables (e.g., munmap(), mprotect(), page migration), all CPUs that may have cached the old translations must be notified to invalidate their TLB entries.

On x86_64, Linux uses flush_tlb_multi(), which resolves to native_flush_tlb_multi() (arch/x86/mm/tlb.c:1347) on bare metal and to a paravirt hook under a hypervisor. The older flush_tlb_others() name that appears in pre-5.9 sources no longer exists. There is no dedicated shootdown vector either: the function calls on_each_cpu_cond_mask(should_flush_tlb, flush_tlb_func, ...), so the request travels as an ordinary call-function IPI whose callback is flush_tlb_func(). Each target then selectively invalidates the affected address range. The should_flush_tlb() predicate is where the lazy-TLB optimization lives: a CPU running a kernel thread, and therefore not using the affected mm for translations, is filtered out of the mask before any IPI is sent, because it will reload TLB entries when it next switches to a user process.

On ARM64, TLB invalidation can be performed via broadcast TLBI instructions (e.g., TLBI VALE1IS — invalidate by VA, last level, EL1, inner shareable). These are handled by the hardware coherency mechanism and do not require explicit IPIs, making TLB invalidation significantly cheaper on ARM64 than on x86_64.

Softirqs and Tasklets

We mentioned earlier in the section “Interrupt Handling” that several tasks among those executed by the kernel are not critical: they can be deferred for a long period of time, if necessary. The interrupt service routines of an interrupt handler are serialized, and often there should be no occurrence of the same interrupt until the corresponding handler has terminated. Conversely, deferrable tasks can execute with all interrupts enabled. Taking them out of the interrupt handler helps keep kernel response time small. This is a critically important property for many time-critical applications that expect their interrupt requests to be serviced in microseconds.

Linux 7.0.12 answers this challenge by providing three kinds of deferred-work mechanisms:

  1. Deferrable functions — softirqs and tasklets, which run in interrupt context (with interrupts enabled but preemption disabled) and cannot sleep.
  2. Workqueues — which run in process context in dedicated kernel threads and can sleep.
  3. Threaded interrupts — which move interrupt processing into schedulable kernel threads (covered earlier in this chapter).

Softirqs vs. Tasklets vs. Workqueues: A Conceptual Overview

Softirqs and tasklets are strictly correlated, because tasklets are implemented on top of softirqs. As a matter of fact, the term “softirq” in the kernel source code often denotes both kinds of deferrable functions. Another widely used term is interrupt context: it specifies that the kernel is currently executing either a hardirq handler or a deferrable function (softirq/tasklet).

The key differences between softirqs and tasklets:

  • Softirqs are statically allocated (defined at compile time in the softirq_vec[] array), while tasklets can be allocated and initialized at runtime (for instance, when loading a kernel module).
  • Softirqs can run concurrently on several CPUs, even if they are of the same type. Thus, softirq functions are reentrant and must explicitly protect their data structures with spin locks or per-CPU data.
  • Tasklets of the same type are always serialized: the same type of tasklet cannot be executed by two CPUs at the same time. However, tasklets of different types can run concurrently on different CPUs. This serialization simplifies the life of device driver developers, because the tasklet function need not be reentrant.

Generally speaking, four kinds of operations can be performed on deferrable functions:

OperationDescription
InitializationDefines a new deferrable function. For softirqs this is done at compile time or during kernel boot via open_softirq(). For tasklets this can be done at any time via tasklet_init() or DECLARE_TASKLET().
Activation (raising)Marks a deferrable function as “pending” — to be run the next time the kernel schedules a round of softirq execution. For softirqs: raise_softirq(). For tasklets: tasklet_schedule() or tasklet_hi_schedule().
Masking (disabling)Selectively disables deferrable functions so they will not be executed even if activated. local_bh_disable() increments the softirq counter in preempt_count, preventing softirq execution on the local CPU.
ExecutionExecutes all pending deferrable functions. This happens at well-defined points: on return from hardirq handlers (irq_exit_rcu()), when re-enabling BH (local_bh_enable()), and in the ksoftirqd kernel thread.

Activation and execution are bound together: a deferrable function that has been activated by a given CPU is executed on the same CPU. This rule exploits CPU hardware cache locality — the activating code path and the softirq function typically access the same data structures. However, if a CPU is overwhelmed with pending softirqs, the ksoftirqd per-CPU thread takes over to prevent soft-lockup warnings.

The preempt_count Field and Interrupt Context

A critical field used to track both kernel preemption and nesting of kernel control paths is the preempt_count field stored in the thread_info (or task_struct on architectures using CONFIG_THREAD_INFO_IN_TASK). This field encodes four distinct counters:

Table 4-67. Subfields of the preempt_count field

BitsMaskFieldDescription
