0–7PREEMPT_MASK (0x000000ff)Preemption counterHow many times kernel preemption has been explicitly disabled; zero means preemptible
8–15SOFTIRQ_MASK (0x0000ff00)Softirq counterNonzero when softirqs are disabled (local_bh_disable()). Bit 8 alone is set when actually serving a softirq
16–19HARDIRQ_MASK (0x000f0000)Hardirq counterIncremented on each nested hardirq entry (irq_enter_rcu()); max nesting depth 15
20–23NMI_MASK (0x00f00000)NMI counterNonzero when executing in NMI context

Table 4-68. preempt_count layout changes from Linux 2.6 to 7.0.12

AspectLinux 2.6Linux 7.0.12
Total bits29 (bits 0–28)24 (bits 0–23)
Preemption counterbits 0–7 (8 bits)bits 0–7 (8 bits)
Softirq counterbits 8–15 (8 bits)bits 8–15 (8 bits)
Hardirq counterbits 16–27 (12 bits, max 4096)bits 16–19 (4 bits, max 15)
NMI counterNot presentbits 20–23 (4 bits) — added for proper NMI nesting tracking
PREEMPT_ACTIVE flagbit 28Removed — replaced by PREEMPT_NEED_RESCHED in the TIF flags

The kernel provides several helper macros to query the current context. Note the header’s own division of them into a supported group and a deprecated group:

/* include/linux/preempt.h */
#define nmi_count()          (preempt_count() & NMI_MASK)
#define hardirq_count()      (preempt_count() & HARDIRQ_MASK)
#ifdef CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT
# define softirq_count()     (current->softirq_disable_cnt & SOFTIRQ_MASK)
# define irq_count()         ((preempt_count() & (NMI_MASK | HARDIRQ_MASK)) | softirq_count())
#else
# define softirq_count()     (preempt_count() & SOFTIRQ_MASK)
# define irq_count()         (preempt_count() & (NMI_MASK | HARDIRQ_MASK | SOFTIRQ_MASK))
#endif

/*
 * Macros to retrieve the current execution context:
 *
 * in_nmi()             - We're in NMI context
 * in_hardirq()         - We're in hard IRQ context
 * in_serving_softirq() - We're in softirq context
 * in_task()            - We're in task context
 */
#define in_nmi()             (nmi_count())
#define in_hardirq()         (hardirq_count())
#define in_serving_softirq() (softirq_count() & SOFTIRQ_OFFSET)
#ifdef CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT
# define in_task()           (!((preempt_count() & (NMI_MASK | HARDIRQ_MASK)) | in_serving_softirq()))
#else
# define in_task()           (!(preempt_count() & (NMI_MASK | HARDIRQ_MASK | SOFTIRQ_OFFSET)))
#endif

/*
 * The following macros are deprecated and should not be used in new code:
 * in_softirq()   - We have BH disabled, or are processing softirqs
 * in_interrupt() - We're in NMI,IRQ,SoftIRQ context or have BH disabled
 */
#define in_softirq()         (softirq_count())
#define in_interrupt()       (irq_count())

The four macros in the first group each answer exactly one question, and together they partition the possible contexts. The two in the second group do not, and that is precisely why the kernel deprecates them. in_softirq() is true both when a softirq is genuinely executing and when some unrelated code merely called local_bh_disable(); in_interrupt() lumps NMI, hardirq, softirq, and BH-disabled together into a single bit of information. Code that tests them therefore cannot tell “I am inside an interrupt handler” from “somebody, somewhere up my call stack, took a spin_lock_bh()”. New code is expected to ask the specific question — in_hardirq(), in_serving_softirq(), or in_task() — rather than the vague one.

The CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT variants deserve a note, because they reveal something structural. On a real-time kernel softirqs execute in an ordinary preemptible thread, so a task that has disabled bottom halves can be scheduled out while another task runs on the same CPU. A per-CPU counter would then describe the wrong task. RT therefore moves the softirq disable count out of preempt_count and into current->softirq_disable_cnt, making it a per-task property. The hardirq and NMI counts stay in preempt_count on both configurations, because those contexts genuinely cannot be preempted or migrated.

These macros are used throughout the kernel to make decisions about locking, memory allocation, and scheduling — though usually less directly than one might expect. The allocator, for instance, does not inspect the current context to decide whether it may sleep; the caller declares its intent through the GFP flags, and might_alloc() merely asserts that the declaration is honest:

/* include/linux/sched/mm.h */
static inline void might_alloc(gfp_t gfp_mask)
{
    fs_reclaim_acquire(gfp_mask);
    fs_reclaim_release(gfp_mask);

    if (current->flags & PF_MEMALLOC)
        return;

    might_sleep_if(gfpflags_allow_blocking(gfp_mask));
}

Likewise mutex_lock() opens with a bare might_sleep(). Neither call panics: might_sleep() expands to a diagnostic that fires only under CONFIG_DEBUG_ATOMIC_SLEEP, and on a production kernel it compiles away to nothing but a preemption point. The practical consequence is worth stating plainly, because it is a common source of confusion. Calling kmalloc(GFP_KERNEL) or mutex_lock() from softirq context is not detected and rejected at runtime; on a kernel built without the debug options it simply does the wrong thing, potentially scheduling from a context that must not schedule. The context macros are how the debug machinery notices the mistake, not how the kernel protects itself from it.

Softirq Types in Linux 7.0.12

Linux 7.0.12 defines 10 softirq types, up from the 6 in Linux 2.6:

Table 4-69. Softirqs used in Linux 7.0.12

SoftirqIndex (priority)DescriptionPresent in 2.6?
HI_SOFTIRQ0High-priority tasklets (via tasklet_hi_schedule())Yes
TIMER_SOFTIRQ1Timer wheel expiration (run_timer_softirq())Yes
NET_TX_SOFTIRQ2Network packet transmissionYes
NET_RX_SOFTIRQ3Network packet reception (NAPI polling)Yes
BLOCK_SOFTIRQ4Block device completion processingYes (was SCSI_SOFTIRQ)
IRQ_POLL_SOFTIRQ5IRQ polling for block I/O (replaces old blk-iopoll)No — new
TASKLET_SOFTIRQ6Regular tasklets (via tasklet_schedule())Yes (was index 5)
SCHED_SOFTIRQ7Scheduler load balancing across CPUsNo — new
HRTIMER_SOFTIRQ8High-resolution timer softirq mode (HRTIMER_MODE_SOFT)No — new
RCU_SOFTIRQ9RCU callback processing (always last)No — new

The index determines priority: a lower index means higher priority because handle_softirqs() processes them starting from index 0. RCU_SOFTIRQ is deliberately last — the enumeration carries the comment “Preferable RCU should always be the last softirq” — so that RCU callbacks, which can arrive in large batches, do not delay networking or timer processing.

The ordering is worth reading as a statement of the kernel’s priorities rather than as an accident of history. Tasklets scheduled through tasklet_hi_schedule() come first, ahead even of timers, because the drivers that use them asked for exactly that. Timers come next, then the two networking classes, then storage. A driver author choosing between tasklet_schedule() and tasklet_hi_schedule() is, in effect, choosing whether the work should run before or after the entire networking stack’s deferred processing — which is a far larger decision than the API names suggest, and one reason the tasklet interface is now discouraged.

The addition of SCHED_SOFTIRQ, HRTIMER_SOFTIRQ, and RCU_SOFTIRQ reflects the growing importance of deferred work that is too frequent and latency-sensitive for workqueues but needs the per-CPU, non-sleeping guarantees of softirqs.

Only core kernel subsystems should add new softirq types. Device drivers should use tasklets, workqueues, or threaded interrupts instead. The softirq API (open_softirq(), raise_softirq()) is intentionally not exported to modules.

Softirq Data Structures

The implementation of softirqs relies on three core data structures: the softirq_action structure and its global array softirq_vec[], and a per-CPU pending bitmap that records which softirq classes need service.

The softirq_action Structure and softirq_vec[] Array

The main data structure used to represent softirqs is the softirq_vec array, which contains NR_SOFTIRQS (currently 10) elements of type struct softirq_action. The priority of a softirq is determined by its index in the array — a lower index means higher priority, because handle_softirqs() scans the pending bitmap starting from bit 0.

In Linux 2.6, softirq_action contained two fields: an action function pointer and a data pointer to a generic data structure that the softirq function might need. In Linux 7.0.12, the structure has been simplified to contain only the action pointer — the data field was removed because no softirq handler ever used it:

/* include/linux/interrupt.h */
struct softirq_action
{
    void    (*action)(void);
};

The global array is defined in kernel/softirq.c:

/* kernel/softirq.c */
static struct softirq_action softirq_vec[NR_SOFTIRQS] __cacheline_aligned_in_smp;

The __cacheline_aligned_in_smp attribute ensures the array is aligned to a cache-line boundary on SMP systems, avoiding false sharing when multiple CPUs read different entries simultaneously. Because the array is declared static, it is not directly accessible outside kernel/softirq.c; subsystems interact with it only through open_softirq() (to register a handler) and the raise/handle functions (to schedule and execute handlers).

For debugging and /proc/softirqs output, the kernel also maintains a parallel name array:

/* kernel/softirq.c */
const char * const softirq_to_name[NR_SOFTIRQS] = {
    "HI", "TIMER", "NET_TX", "NET_RX", "BLOCK", "IRQ_POLL",
    "TASKLET", "SCHED", "HRTIMER", "RCU"
};

Table 4-70. Comparison of softirq_action between Linux 2.6 and 7.0.12

Feature Linux 2.6 Linux 7.0.12
action field void (*action)(struct softirq_action *) void (*action)(void)
data field void *data — generic pointer Removed
Array size 32 elements (6 used) NR_SOFTIRQS = 10 (all used)
Action parameter Receives pointer to its own softirq_action element Takes no arguments

The removal of the data field and the self-pointer parameter is a small but telling simplification: every softirq handler in modern Linux accesses its own data through well-known per-CPU variables or global structures rather than through a generic void * pointer.

The Per-CPU Pending Bitmap

The last crucial data structure is a per-CPU bitmask describing which softirqs are pending. Each bit position corresponds to a softirq index — bit 0 for HI_SOFTIRQ, bit 1 for TIMER_SOFTIRQ, and so on. When a subsystem raises a softirq, it sets the corresponding bit; when handle_softirqs() begins processing, it atomically reads and clears the entire bitmap.

The storage and access of this bitmap differs between architectures. On most architectures (including ARM64), the pending field is stored inside the irq_cpustat_t structure:

/* include/asm-generic/hardirq.h */
typedef struct {
    unsigned int __softirq_pending;
} ____cacheline_aligned irq_cpustat_t;

DECLARE_PER_CPU_ALIGNED(irq_cpustat_t, irq_stat);

On x86, performance optimization led to a different arrangement. The irq_cpustat_t structure is much larger (containing dozens of architecture-specific counters for APIC timers, TLB flushes, IPI counts, KVM interrupts, and more), and the __softirq_pending field is pulled out into its own dedicated per-CPU variable for better cache locality:

/* arch/x86/include/asm/hardirq.h */
typedef struct {
    /* No __softirq_pending here — it's separate for cache optimization */
    unsigned int __nmi_count;
    unsigned int apic_timer_irqs;
    unsigned int irq_spurious_count;
    /* ... many more architecture-specific counters ... */
} ____cacheline_aligned irq_cpustat_t;

/* Separate per-CPU variable, marked cache-hot for fast access */
DECLARE_PER_CPU_CACHE_HOT(u16, __softirq_pending);
#define local_softirq_pending_ref       __softirq_pending

Notice that on x86, __softirq_pending is only u16 (16 bits) rather than unsigned int (32 bits). With only 10 softirq types defined, 16 bits is more than sufficient and fits more efficiently in the CPU cache — this is a micro-optimization that matters because the pending bitmap is checked on every interrupt exit path.

Regardless of the underlying storage, the kernel provides a uniform set of macros to manipulate the pending bitmap:

/* include/linux/interrupt.h */
#define local_softirq_pending()     (__this_cpu_read(local_softirq_pending_ref))
#define set_softirq_pending(x)      (__this_cpu_write(local_softirq_pending_ref, (x)))
#define or_softirq_pending(x)       (__this_cpu_or(local_softirq_pending_ref, (x)))

These macros always operate on the current CPU's bitmap without locking, because softirq pending bits are strictly per-CPU: a softirq raised on CPU 0 is only serviced on CPU 0. This per-CPU design eliminates the need for cross-CPU synchronization on the hot path.

Table 4-71. Per-CPU softirq pending bitmap access

Macro Operation Typical caller
local_softirq_pending() Read the current CPU's pending bitmap handle_softirqs(), __irq_exit_rcu(), ksoftirqd_should_run()
set_softirq_pending(x) Write the entire bitmap (used to clear it) handle_softirqs() — clears before processing
or_softirq_pending(x) Atomically set specific bits __raise_softirq_irqoff() — sets 1UL << nr

The ksoftirqd Per-CPU Thread

Each CPU has a dedicated kernel thread, ksoftirqd/N, that serves as a fallback executor for softirqs when inline processing at interrupt exit would take too long or starve user tasks. The thread pointer is stored in a per-CPU variable:

/* kernel/softirq.c */
DEFINE_PER_CPU(struct task_struct *, ksoftirqd);

The ksoftirqd thread runs at normal scheduling priority and loops on two functions:

/* kernel/softirq.c */
static int ksoftirqd_should_run(unsigned int cpu)
{
    return local_softirq_pending();
}

static void run_ksoftirqd(unsigned int cpu)
{
    ksoftirqd_run_begin();
    if (local_softirq_pending()) {
        handle_softirqs(true);    /* true = called from ksoftirqd */
        ksoftirqd_run_end();
        cond_resched();
        return;
    }
    ksoftirqd_run_end();
}

The ksoftirqd thread is woken by wakeup_softirqd(), which is called from two places:

  1. From raise_softirq_irqoff() — when a softirq is raised outside interrupt context and should_wake_ksoftirqd() returns true.
  2. From handle_softirqs() — when the inline processing loop exhausts its time budget (MAX_SOFTIRQ_TIME = 2 ms) or restart count (MAX_SOFTIRQ_RESTART = 10 iterations), and more softirqs are still pending.

This dual-path design captures a fundamental trade-off: inline execution at interrupt exit is fast and cache-friendly (the data just touched by the ISR is still hot), but unbounded inline execution would starve user-space processes. The ksoftirqd thread provides a schedulable fallback that participates in normal scheduling fairness.

How the Data Structures Connect

The following summary shows how the three core data structures interact during the life of a softirq:

  1. Registration (boot time): A subsystem calls open_softirq(nr, handler), which stores handler in softirq_vec[nr].action.
  2. Raising (interrupt or process context): A producer calls raise_softirq(nr) or __raise_softirq_irqoff(nr), which calls or_softirq_pending(1UL << nr) to set bit nr in the current CPU's pending bitmap.
  3. Dispatch (interrupt exit or ksoftirqd): Either __irq_exit_rcu() notices local_softirq_pending() != 0 and calls invoke_softirq() → __do_softirq() → handle_softirqs(false), or ksoftirqd wakes up and calls handle_softirqs(true).
  4. Execution: handle_softirqs() reads and clears the pending bitmap with set_softirq_pending(0), then iterates with ffs(pending) to find each set bit, indexing into softirq_vec[vec_nr].action() to call the registered handler.
  5. Re-check: After one pass, handle_softirqs() re-reads local_softirq_pending(). New softirqs may have been raised during step 4 (since interrupts are re-enabled during handler execution). If pending and within budget, it loops; otherwise it wakes ksoftirqd.

Table 4-72. Major softirq classes in Linux 7.0.12

Softirq Typical producer Typical consumer work
HI_SOFTIRQ tasklets / BH work highest-priority tasklet-style deferred actions
TIMER_SOFTIRQ timer wheel / hrtimer handoff timer expiry callbacks
NET_TX_SOFTIRQ network transmit completion TX queue cleanup
NET_RX_SOFTIRQ NIC RX path / NAPI scheduling packet receive processing
BLOCK_SOFTIRQ block layer I/O completion follow-up
IRQ_POLL_SOFTIRQ polled block/network helpers poll fallback
TASKLET_SOFTIRQ classic tasklets serialized driver bottom halves
SCHED_SOFTIRQ sched_balance_trigger() from sched_tick(); nohz_csd_func() for NOHZ idle balancing periodic load balancing across CPUs
HRTIMER_SOFTIRQ high-resolution timers timer callbacks needing later context
RCU_SOFTIRQ RCU core callback invocation and grace-period progress

Registering and Raising Softirqs

Registering a Softirq with open_softirq()

The open_softirq() function takes care of softirq initialization. It receives two parameters: the softirq index nr and a pointer to the handler function. The function simply stores the handler in the corresponding entry of the softirq_vec array:

/* kernel/softirq.c */
void open_softirq(int nr, void (*action)(void))
{
    softirq_vec[nr].action = action;
}

In Linux 2.6, open_softirq() took three parameters — the index, the action function, and a data pointer. Since the data field was removed from softirq_action in modern kernels, the third parameter is no longer needed.

Because softirqs are statically defined at compile time, open_softirq() is called exactly once per softirq class, typically during subsystem initialization at boot. The following table shows every open_softirq() call site in Linux 7.0.12:

Table 4-73. Softirq registration sites in Linux 7.0.12

Softirq Handler function Registered in Init function
HI_SOFTIRQ tasklet_hi_action kernel/softirq.c softirq_init()
TIMER_SOFTIRQ run_timer_softirq kernel/time/timer.c timers_init()
NET_TX_SOFTIRQ net_tx_action net/core/dev.c net_dev_init()
NET_RX_SOFTIRQ net_rx_action net/core/dev.c net_dev_init()
BLOCK_SOFTIRQ blk_done_softirq block/blk-mq.c blk_mq_init()
IRQ_POLL_SOFTIRQ irq_poll_softirq lib/irq_poll.c irq_poll_setup()
TASKLET_SOFTIRQ tasklet_action kernel/softirq.c softirq_init()
SCHED_SOFTIRQ sched_balance_softirq kernel/sched/fair.c init_sched_fair_class()
HRTIMER_SOFTIRQ hrtimer_run_softirq kernel/time/hrtimer.c hrtimers_init()
RCU_SOFTIRQ rcu_core_si kernel/rcu/tree.c rcu_init(), only if use_softirq
RCU_SOFTIRQ rcu_process_callbacks kernel/rcu/tiny.c rcu_init() (CONFIG_TINY_RCU builds)

Two entries in that table are conditional, and both are instructive. RCU_SOFTIRQ appears twice because Tree RCU and Tiny RCU are mutually exclusive implementations, each with its own rcu_init() and its own callback processor. More interestingly, the Tree RCU registration is guarded by if (use_softirq). That variable is controlled by the rcutree.use_softirq boot parameter; setting it to zero moves RCU callback invocation out of softirq context entirely and into per-CPU rcuc/N kthreads. Real-time and latency-sensitive deployments do exactly that, so that a burst of RCU callbacks cannot delay an unrelated softirq — or a high-priority task — on the same CPU. When use_softirq is zero, softirq_vec[RCU_SOFTIRQ].action is simply never populated, and the bit is never raised.

No locking is required during registration because these calls happen during early boot, before the softirq machinery is active and before secondary CPUs are brought online. Once registered, a softirq handler is never unregistered — the set of softirq classes is fixed for the lifetime of the system. There is deliberately no close_softirq(): the array is indexed by a compile-time constant from a fixed enumeration, so an unregistered slot could only ever be reached through a bug, and leaving action as a stale pointer is safer than leaving it NULL and having to test it on the hot path.

Raising a Softirq

Softirqs are activated (raised) by means of the raise_softirq() function family. The kernel provides three variants with different interrupt-safety assumptions:

raise_softirq(nr) — the general-purpose entry point, safe to call from any context. It performs the following steps:

  1. Executes local_irq_save(flags) to save the current interrupt flag state and disable local interrupts. This ensures the pending bitmap manipulation is atomic with respect to interrupts on this CPU.
  2. Calls raise_softirq_irqoff(nr) to do the actual work (see below).
  3. Executes local_irq_restore(flags) to restore the previous interrupt state.
/* kernel/softirq.c */
void raise_softirq(unsigned int nr)
{
    unsigned long flags;
    local_irq_save(flags);
    raise_softirq_irqoff(nr);
    local_irq_restore(flags);
}

raise_softirq_irqoff(nr) — for callers that already have local interrupts disabled (e.g., from within a hardirq handler or after an explicit local_irq_save()). It performs two actions:

  1. Calls __raise_softirq_irqoff(nr) to set the pending bit.
  2. Checks whether ksoftirqd should be woken. The wakeup is performed only if the caller is not in interrupt context (!in_interrupt()) and should_wake_ksoftirqd() agrees. If the caller is in interrupt context — which is the common case for softirqs raised from hardirq handlers — there is no need to wake ksoftirqd because __irq_exit_rcu() will check the pending bitmap when the interrupt returns and process the softirq directly.
/* kernel/softirq.c */
inline void raise_softirq_irqoff(unsigned int nr)
{
    __raise_softirq_irqoff(nr);
    /*
     * If we're in an interrupt or softirq, we're done
     * (this also catches softirq-disabled code). We will
     * actually run the softirq once we return from
     * the irq or softirq.
     *
     * Otherwise we wake up ksoftirqd to make sure we
     * schedule the softirq soon.
     */
    if (!in_interrupt() && should_wake_ksoftirqd())
        wakeup_softirqd();
}

On a conventional kernel should_wake_ksoftirqd() simply returns true, so the !in_interrupt() test carries the whole decision. The function exists for CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT, where it becomes !this_cpu_read(softirq_ctrl.cnt): an RT kernel that is currently inside a bottom-half-disabled region must not wake the thread, because the region will run the pending softirqs itself on the way out. Reading the non-RT definition as though it were a meaningful policy check is a common misreading of this code.

__raise_softirq_irqoff(nr) — the lowest-level primitive. It asserts that interrupts are disabled (via lockdep_assert_irqs_disabled()), emits a tracepoint for ftrace / perf, and sets the bit in the local CPU's pending bitmap:

/* kernel/softirq.c */
void __raise_softirq_irqoff(unsigned int nr)
{
    lockdep_assert_irqs_disabled();
    trace_softirq_raise(nr);
    or_softirq_pending(1UL << nr);
}

Table 4-74. The three raise variants and when to use each

Function Interrupts must be disabled? Wakes ksoftirqd? Typical caller
raise_softirq(nr) No — disables them internally Yes, if outside interrupt context Process-context code (e.g., blk_mq_raise_softirq())
raise_softirq_irqoff(nr) Yes Yes, if outside interrupt context Code already holding local_irq_save() (e.g., net_tx_action(), tasklet_schedule())
__raise_softirq_irqoff(nr) Yes No — caller handles dispatch Hardirq paths that know __irq_exit_rcu() will process (e.g., napi_schedule_prep(), blk_mq_complete_request())

When Pending Softirqs Are Checked

Setting the pending bit is only half the story — the softirq will not execute until the kernel checks the bitmap and dispatches. In Linux 7.0.12, softirq checkpoints occur at several well-defined places:

  1. __irq_exit_rcu() — after every hardirq handler completes. This is the most common and performance-critical checkpoint. If the CPU is no longer in interrupt context and local_softirq_pending() is nonzero, it calls invoke_softirq().
  2. __local_bh_enable_ip() — when code calls local_bh_enable() to re-enable bottom halves. If the softirq disable count drops to zero and softirqs are pending, it calls do_softirq().
  3. ksoftirqd — the per-CPU kernel thread polls local_softirq_pending() in its ksoftirqd_should_run() callback and calls handle_softirqs(true) when bits are set.
  4. do_softirq() — explicitly called from certain code paths. It checks in_interrupt() to avoid reentrant softirq execution, then calls do_softirq_own_stack() if pending.

In Linux 2.6, the checkpoint list also included smp_apic_timer_interrupt() and CALL_FUNCTION_VECTOR IPI handlers. In 7.0.12, these are no longer separate checkpoints because all interrupt handlers now flow through the unified __irq_exit_rcu() path, which handles softirq dispatch generically.

Disabling and Enabling Softirqs

Driver code that must protect data shared with a softirq handler uses local_bh_disable() and local_bh_enable() to temporarily prevent softirq execution on the local CPU. These functions manipulate the softirq counter subfield of preempt_count:

/* include/linux/bottom_half.h */
static inline void local_bh_disable(void)
{
    __local_bh_disable_ip(_THIS_IP_, SOFTIRQ_DISABLE_OFFSET);
}

static inline void local_bh_enable(void)
{
    __local_bh_enable_ip(_THIS_IP_, SOFTIRQ_DISABLE_OFFSET);
}

local_bh_disable() adds SOFTIRQ_DISABLE_OFFSET to preempt_count, incrementing the softirq counter. While the counter is nonzero, in_softirq() returns true and softirq dispatch is suppressed.

The value of that offset is worth pausing on, because it looks like a mistake and is not. SOFTIRQ_OFFSET is 1UL << SOFTIRQ_SHIFT, or 0x100 — bit 8, the lowest bit of the softirq counter. But SOFTIRQ_DISABLE_OFFSET is defined as 2 * SOFTIRQ_OFFSET, or 0x200, so disabling bottom halves increments the counter by two, not one. The reason is that bit 8 is overloaded. in_serving_softirq() tests softirq_count() & SOFTIRQ_OFFSET — that one bit alone — and handle_softirqs() sets exactly that bit while a handler is running. Had local_bh_disable() incremented by one, a task that merely took a spin_lock_bh() would appear to the rest of the kernel to be executing a softirq handler. Stepping by two keeps the two conditions distinguishable in a single word: even values of the counter mean “disabled”, and the odd bit means “serving”. This is also why the counter is eight bits wide for what is nominally a boolean — it must count nesting, since local_bh_disable() may legitimately be called recursively.

local_bh_enable() subtracts the offset and, critically, checks for pending softirqs on the way out:

/* kernel/softirq.c — the !CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT definition */
void __local_bh_enable_ip(unsigned long ip, unsigned int cnt)
{
    WARN_ON_ONCE(in_hardirq());
    lockdep_assert_irqs_enabled();
#ifdef CONFIG_TRACE_IRQFLAGS
    local_irq_disable();
#endif
    /*
     * Are softirqs going to be turned on now:
     */
    if (softirq_count() == SOFTIRQ_DISABLE_OFFSET)
        lockdep_softirqs_on(ip);
    /*
     * Keep preemption disabled until we are done with
     * softirq processing:
     */
    __preempt_count_sub(cnt - 1);

    if (unlikely(!in_interrupt() && local_softirq_pending())) {
        /*
         * Run softirq if any pending. And do it in its own stack
         * as we may be calling this deep in a task call stack already.
         */
        do_softirq();
    }

    preempt_count_dec();
#ifdef CONFIG_TRACE_IRQFLAGS
    local_irq_enable();
#endif
    preempt_check_resched();
}

The subtraction is deliberately short by one: __preempt_count_sub(cnt - 1) leaves a single unit of preemption disable in place across the do_softirq() call, and only the trailing preempt_count_dec() releases it. The effect is that softirqs invoked from this path run non-preemptibly even though the caller was preemptible, which is what the softirq contract requires. Doing it with one arithmetic adjustment rather than a separate preempt_disable()/preempt_enable() pair keeps the fast path — the overwhelmingly common case where nothing is pending — down to a single read-modify-write on preempt_count.

Because the softirq counter is left below the “disabled” threshold while do_softirq() runs, softirqs raised during that window are processed too, and the guarantee is the one drivers rely on: softirqs raised while bottom halves were disabled are never delayed past the matching local_bh_enable(). The closing preempt_check_resched() then honours any TIF_NEED_RESCHED that accumulated while preemption was held off.

The naming "local_bh" is historical — it refers to "bottom halves," an older Linux deferred-work mechanism that was removed before Linux 2.6. The functions survived the transition because softirqs and tasklets inherited the BH semantics.

The do_softirq() and __do_softirq() Functions

In Linux 2.6, do_softirq() and __do_softirq() contained the entire softirq processing logic — disabling interrupts, switching to the softirq stack, iterating the pending bitmap, and enforcing restart limits.

In Linux 7.0.12, both functions still exist but are now thin wrappers around a new unified function, handle_softirqs():

/* kernel/softirq.c — do_softirq() is now a trivial entry point */
asmlinkage __visible void do_softirq(void)
{
    __u32 pending;
    unsigned long flags;

    if (in_interrupt())
        return;

    local_irq_save(flags);
    pending = local_softirq_pending();
    if (pending)
        do_softirq_own_stack();   /* switches stack, then calls __do_softirq() */
    local_irq_restore(flags);
}

/* __do_softirq() is a one-line delegation */
asmlinkage __visible void __softirq_entry __do_softirq(void)
{
    handle_softirqs(false);       /* false = not called from ksoftirqd */
}

The do_softirq() function retains the same guard as in 2.6: it returns immediately if in_interrupt() is true, preventing reentrant softirq execution. If softirqs are pending, it calls do_softirq_own_stack(), which switches to the per-CPU IRQ stack (to avoid overflowing the current task's kernel stack) and then calls __do_softirq(). The __do_softirq() function now simply delegates to handle_softirqs(false).

The refactoring was motivated by the need to share the processing logic between two callers: __do_softirq() (from interrupt exit) and run_ksoftirqd() (from the per-CPU thread). The bool ksirqd parameter tells handle_softirqs() whether it is running in ksoftirqd context, which affects RCU quiescent state handling.

On CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT kernels, do_softirq() and __do_softirq() are not used at all. Instead, invoke_softirq() always wakes ksoftirqd, and softirqs are processed entirely in thread context to preserve real-time scheduling guarantees.

handle_softirqs() Step by Step

handle_softirqs() in kernel/softirq.c:579-652 is the central interpreter for the pending bitmap. It begins by computing an end time jiffies + MAX_SOFTIRQ_TIME, saving the current task flags, and setting a restart budget MAX_SOFTIRQ_RESTART. It clears PF_MEMALLOC temporarily because the current task context is being borrowed for deferred work that may not semantically inherit reclaim privileges. Then it snapshots the local pending bitmap and enters the processing loop.

  • softirq_handle_begin() adds SOFTIRQ_OFFSET to preempt_count — the single odd bit that makes in_serving_softirq() true — while lockdep_softirq_start() and account_softirq_enter() establish lockdep and CPU-time-accounting state for softirq context.
  • The function clears the pending bitmap with set_softirq_pending(0) before re-enabling interrupts, so that raises arriving during this batch accumulate into a fresh bitmap rather than being lost or double-processed.
  • kstat_incr_softirqs_this_cpu(vec_nr) bumps the per-CPU, per-class counter that /proc/softirqs reports, and trace_softirq_entry/trace_softirq_exit bracket the call for ftrace and perf.
  • ffs(pending) finds the next set bit and h->action() runs the corresponding handler.
  • After one pass, interrupts are disabled again and local_softirq_pending() is re-read.

The dispatch loop deserves a closer look, because its index arithmetic is not obvious:

/* kernel/softirq.c */
h = softirq_vec;

while ((softirq_bit = ffs(pending))) {
    unsigned int vec_nr;
    int prev_count;

    h += softirq_bit - 1;

    vec_nr = h - softirq_vec;
    prev_count = preempt_count();

    kstat_incr_softirqs_this_cpu(vec_nr);

    trace_softirq_entry(vec_nr);
    h->action();
    trace_softirq_exit(vec_nr);
    if (unlikely(prev_count != preempt_count())) {
        pr_err("huh, entered softirq %u %s %p with preempt_count %08x, exited with %08x?\n",
               vec_nr, softirq_to_name[vec_nr], h->action,
               prev_count, preempt_count());
        preempt_count_set(prev_count);
    }
    h++;
    pending >>= softirq_bit;
}

The pointer h walks forward through softirq_vec while pending is shifted right by the same amount, so ffs() always operates on a bitmap whose bit 1 corresponds to whatever h currently points at. This is why the code says vec_nr = h - softirq_vec rather than reusing softirq_bit: after the first iteration, softirq_bit is a relative distance, not an absolute softirq index. The advantage of the shifting form is that the loop terminates as soon as the remaining bits are exhausted, without scanning the empty high end of the bitmap — and since ffs() compiles to a single bit-scan instruction on both x86-64 (bsf) and ARM64 (rbit plus clz), the common case of one or two pending softirqs costs almost nothing.

The prev_count != preempt_count() check is a safety net for a specific and nasty class of driver bug: a softirq handler that returns having taken a lock it did not release, or having called local_bh_disable() without the matching enable. Left uncorrected, the leaked count would make the CPU appear permanently non-preemptible and would silently suppress all further softirq processing. Rather than let that corruption propagate, the kernel logs the offending handler by name and forcibly restores the count with preempt_count_set(). The deliberately informal wording of the message — it begins “huh,” — has survived in the source for over two decades.

One more line sits between the loop and the re-check:

/* kernel/softirq.c */
if (!IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT) && ksirqd)
    rcu_softirq_qs();

This is the one place the ksirqd parameter is used. Reporting an RCU quiescent state is only legitimate when the CPU is genuinely not inside any RCU read-side critical section, and ksoftirqd — a plain kernel thread that has just finished a batch of handlers — qualifies. The inline path at interrupt exit does not: it borrowed a task’s context, and that task may well have been holding rcu_read_lock() when the interrupt arrived. Announcing a quiescent state there would let a grace period end early and free memory still in use.

If additional softirqs are pending, Linux does not loop forever. It restarts only while three conditions hold: the current time is still before end, the scheduler does not need a reschedule, and the restart budget has not been exhausted. Otherwise it wakes ksoftirqd and lets a schedulable kernel thread finish the backlog. This policy captures the core trade-off: direct execution is fast and cache-friendly, but unbounded softirq execution would starve ordinary tasks.

/* kernel/softirq.c */
if (pending) {
    if (time_before(jiffies, end) && !need_resched() && --max_restart)
        goto restart;
    wakeup_softirqd();
}

irq_enter_rcu(), irq_exit_rcu(), and invoke_softirq()

The hardirq and softirq subsystems connect at interrupt exit. irq_enter_rcu() increments the hardirq nesting state and updates tick and accounting information on the way in. __irq_exit_rcu() undoes that on the way out, and it is there that deferred work gets its chance to run:

/* kernel/softirq.c */
static inline void __irq_exit_rcu(void)
{
#ifndef __ARCH_IRQ_EXIT_IRQS_DISABLED
    local_irq_disable();
#else
    lockdep_assert_irqs_disabled();
#endif
    account_hardirq_exit(current);
    preempt_count_sub(HARDIRQ_OFFSET);
    if (!in_interrupt() && local_softirq_pending())
        invoke_softirq();

    if (IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_IRQ_FORCED_THREADING) && force_irqthreads() &&
        local_timers_pending_force_th() && !(in_nmi() | in_hardirq()))
        wake_timersd();

    tick_irq_exit();
}

The ordering here carries real meaning. preempt_count_sub(HARDIRQ_OFFSET) comes before the softirq check, and it has to. Only after the hardirq count has been decremented can in_interrupt() possibly be false, and that test is what makes nested interrupts safe: if this was an inner interrupt that preempted an outer one, the hardirq count is still nonzero after the decrement, the condition fails, and softirq processing is left to the outermost __irq_exit_rcu(). The same test also suppresses softirq processing when the interrupted code had bottom halves disabled, since in_interrupt() covers the softirq count as well. So this single condition encodes two quite different guarantees — no reentrancy, and no violation of a local_bh_disable() held by the interrupted task — which is why the deprecated-but-broad in_interrupt() is exactly the right macro in this one place.

The wake_timersd() call is the handoff to the ktimers threads described later in this section, and tick_irq_exit() is where a tickless CPU decides whether to go back to sleep — the point discussed in “Tickless Operation” earlier in this chapter. Between them sits invoke_softirq(), the hinge where immediate hardirq handling can roll directly into deferred softirq handling on the same CPU.

invoke_softirq() and the Stack Question

invoke_softirq() answers two questions at once: should softirqs run inline, and if so, on which stack?

/* kernel/softirq.c — the !CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT definition */
static inline void invoke_softirq(void)
{
    if (!force_irqthreads() || !__this_cpu_read(ksoftirqd)) {
#ifdef CONFIG_HAVE_IRQ_EXIT_ON_IRQ_STACK
        /*
         * We can safely execute softirq on the current stack if
         * it is the irq stack, because it should be near empty
         * at this stage.
         */
        __do_softirq();
#else
        /*
         * Otherwise, irq_exit() is called on the task stack that can
         * be potentially deep already. So call softirq in its own stack
         * to prevent from any overrun.
         */
        do_softirq_own_stack();
#endif
    } else {
        wakeup_softirqd();
    }
}

The first question is settled by force_irqthreads(), a static key enabled by the threadirqs boot parameter and forced on under CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT. When it is set, softirqs never run inline: the CPU wakes ksoftirqd and returns, so that every scrap of deferred work becomes a schedulable, priority-bearing entity. The || !__this_cpu_read(ksoftirqd) half of the test covers early boot, when the thread does not exist yet and inline execution is the only option available.

A CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT build does not even compile the function shown above. The entire block containing invoke_softirq(), do_softirq(), and ksoftirqd_run_begin() sits under #ifndef CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT, and the RT replacement is a single line — if (should_wake_ksoftirqd()) wakeup_softirqd(); — with no inline path and therefore no stack question to answer.

The second question — the stack — is where x86-64 and ARM64 part company, and the reason is a difference in how each architecture arranged its interrupt entry path.

Table 4-75. Softirq stack selection at interrupt exit

ArchitectureHAVE_IRQ_EXIT_ON_IRQ_STACKWhat invoke_softirq() callsEffect
x86-64Selected (arch/x86/Kconfig, if X86_64)__do_softirq()Already on the per-CPU hardirq stack, which is nearly empty at this point; no switch needed
x86-32Not selecteddo_softirq_own_stack()Explicit switch in arch/x86/kernel/irq_32.c
ARM64Not selecteddo_softirq_own_stack()call_on_irq_stack() switches to the per-CPU IRQ stack (arch/arm64/kernel/irq.c)

Both architectures run interrupt handlers on a per-CPU IRQ stack. The difference is where the switch back happens.

On x86-64 the entry code switches to the hardirq stack early and stays there for the whole of irq_exit(), so by the time invoke_softirq() is reached the CPU is already on a stack that was empty when the interrupt arrived and has since unwound all but a frame or two. Running softirqs there is safe and costs nothing.

ARM64 scopes the switch much more tightly — to the handler call alone:

/* arch/arm64/kernel/entry-common.c */
static void do_interrupt_handler(struct pt_regs *regs,
                                 void (*handler)(struct pt_regs *))
{
    struct pt_regs *old_regs = set_irq_regs(regs);

    if (on_thread_stack())
        call_on_irq_stack(regs, handler);
    else
        handler(regs);

    set_irq_regs(old_regs);
}

static __always_inline void __el1_irq(struct pt_regs *regs,
                                      void (*handler)(struct pt_regs *))
{
    irqentry_state_t state;

    state = enter_from_kernel_mode(regs);

    irq_enter_rcu();
    do_interrupt_handler(regs, handler);
    irq_exit_rcu();

    exit_to_kernel_mode(regs, state);
}

call_on_irq_stack() returns before do_interrupt_handler() does, so irq_exit_rcu() — and therefore invoke_softirq() — executes back on the interrupted task’s kernel stack. That stack may be arbitrarily deep: a syscall several layers into the filesystem, for instance. Adding an unbounded softirq call chain on top of it is exactly how stack overflows happen, so ARM64 cannot select HAVE_IRQ_EXIT_ON_IRQ_STACK and must route the call through do_softirq_own_stack(), paying for a second switch:

/* arch/arm64/kernel/irq.c */
#ifdef CONFIG_SOFTIRQ_ON_OWN_STACK
static void ____do_softirq(struct pt_regs *regs)
{
    __do_softirq();
}

void do_softirq_own_stack(void)
{
    call_on_irq_stack(NULL, ____do_softirq);
}
#endif

Note that both architectures end up running softirqs on the IRQ stack; there is no separate softirq stack in Linux 7.0.12. The difference is only whether a switch is needed to get there. This also explains why do_softirq() — the entry point used from local_bh_enable(), which runs in ordinary task context on a potentially deep stack — calls do_softirq_own_stack() unconditionally on both architectures. There, x86-64 has no head start to exploit.

The policy question that remains is whether to keep executing softirqs or hand off to ksoftirqd. Direct execution has excellent locality and avoids a wakeup. But if the CPU has already spent a long time in interrupt context, or if the system needs to reschedule, pushing the remaining work to ksoftirqd improves fairness. That is why the restart budget and time limit exist. They are the kernel’s compromise between throughput and latency.

The ksoftirqd Kernel Threads

The Problem: Balancing Throughput and Fairness

Softirq functions may reactivate themselves; in fact, both the networking softirqs and the tasklet softirqs do this. Moreover, external events, such as packet flooding on a network card, may activate softirqs at very high frequency. This creates a fundamental scheduling dilemma that developers faced early in Linux's evolution:

Strategy 1 — No re-checking: handle_softirqs() could determine what softirqs are pending at the start, execute them, and terminate without re-checking. This is fast, but in the worst case a re-raised softirq would not execute until the next timer interrupt — even if the machine is idle. The resulting latency is unacceptable for networking developers.

Strategy 2 — Continuous re-checking: handle_softirqs() could keep looping as long as any softirqs are pending. This satisfies latency requirements, but under a high-frequency softirq flow (such as a packet flood), the function would never return, and user-space programs would be virtually stopped.

The ksoftirqd/N kernel threads solve this trade-off. The handle_softirqs() function uses a bounded restart loop — it re-checks for pending softirqs up to MAX_SOFTIRQ_RESTART (10) times and for at most MAX_SOFTIRQ_TIME (2 ms). If the flow of softirqs does not stop within that budget, the function wakes ksoftirqd and returns. The kernel thread has normal scheduling priority, so user programs get a chance to run; but if the machine is idle, the pending softirqs are executed quickly.

Thread Creation and Registration

Each CPU's ksoftirqd thread is created early in the boot process by spawn_ksoftirqd(), which is registered as an early_initcall. The threads are managed by the smpboot per-CPU thread framework, which automatically creates a thread for each CPU — including CPUs brought online later via CPU hotplug:

/* kernel/softirq.c */
static struct smp_hotplug_thread softirq_threads = {
    .store              = &ksoftirqd,
    .thread_should_run  = ksoftirqd_should_run,
    .thread_fn          = run_ksoftirqd,
    .thread_comm        = "ksoftirqd/%u",
};

static __init int spawn_ksoftirqd(void)
{
    cpuhp_setup_state_nocalls(CPUHP_SOFTIRQ_DEAD, "softirq:dead",
                              NULL, takeover_tasklets);
    BUG_ON(smpboot_register_percpu_thread(&softirq_threads));
    return 0;
}
early_initcall(spawn_ksoftirqd);

The smpboot framework provides the main loop: it calls thread_should_run() to check whether work is pending, and if so, calls thread_fn() to perform it. Between iterations, the framework parks the thread and puts it to sleep. The .store field points to the per-CPU variable where the thread's task_struct pointer is saved — this is the ksoftirqd variable that wakeup_softirqd() reads.

The cpuhp_setup_state_nocalls() call registers takeover_tasklets() as a CPU hotplug teardown callback. When a CPU goes offline, this function migrates any pending tasklets from the dying CPU's per-CPU lists to the current CPU and raises the appropriate softirqs.

The ksoftirqd Main Loop

In Linux 2.6, the ksoftirqd loop was written explicitly with set_current_state(TASK_INTERRUPTIBLE), schedule(), and a while (local_softirq_pending()) loop calling do_softirq(). In Linux 7.0.12, the loop is handled by the smpboot framework, and the thread's logic reduces to two callbacks:

/* kernel/softirq.c */
static int ksoftirqd_should_run(unsigned int cpu)
{
    return local_softirq_pending();
}

static void run_ksoftirqd(unsigned int cpu)
{
    ksoftirqd_run_begin();
    if (local_softirq_pending()) {
        /*
         * We can safely run softirq on inline stack, as we are not deep
         * in the task stack here.
         */
        handle_softirqs(true);
        ksoftirqd_run_end();
        cond_resched();
        return;
    }
    ksoftirqd_run_end();
}

ksoftirqd_should_run() simply reads the pending bitmap. If any bits are set, run_ksoftirqd() is called. ksoftirqd_run_begin() disables local interrupts (on non-RT kernels) to prevent races with raise operations during the read of the pending bitmap. Then handle_softirqs(true) is called with the ksirqd parameter set to true, which tells the function to emit an RCU quiescent state (rcu_softirq_qs()) after processing — something that is not appropriate when running inline at interrupt exit. After processing, cond_resched() gives the scheduler a voluntary preemption point, ensuring ksoftirqd does not monopolize the CPU even if softirqs keep arriving.

Waking ksoftirqd

The wakeup_softirqd() function is the sole entry point for waking the thread:

/* kernel/softirq.c */
static void wakeup_softirqd(void)
{
    /* Interrupts are disabled: no need to stop preemption */
    struct task_struct *tsk = __this_cpu_read(ksoftirqd);

    if (tsk)
        wake_up_process(tsk);
}

The if (tsk) guard is necessary because ksoftirqd may not yet exist during very early boot — before spawn_ksoftirqd() has been called. The function is called from two places:

  1. raise_softirq_irqoff() — when a softirq is raised outside interrupt context. In this case, there is no upcoming __irq_exit_rcu() that would check pending softirqs, so the thread must be woken explicitly.
  2. handle_softirqs() — when the inline restart budget is exhausted but softirqs are still pending. This is the handoff from inline processing to threaded processing.

ksoftirqd vs. Inline Processing: When Each Path Runs

Table 4-76. Softirq processing paths

Scenario Processing path Why
Softirq raised in hardirq handler Inline at __irq_exit_rcu() Cache-hot; no wakeup needed
Inline processing exhausts budget ksoftirqd takes over Prevents user-space starvation
Softirq raised in process context ksoftirqd woken directly No interrupt exit to trigger inline
local_bh_enable() finds pending Inline via do_softirq() Immediate processing when BH re-enabled
CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT kernel Always ksoftirqd Softirqs must not run in interrupt context on RT

The ktimers Thread (PREEMPT_RT)

When the kernel is built with CONFIG_IRQ_FORCED_THREADING and force_irqthreads() is enabled at runtime, spawn_ksoftirqd() registers a second set of per-CPU threads, ktimers/N. It is worth being precise about the condition, because it is often misstated: this is not a CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT feature. force_irqthreads() is a static key that RT turns on unconditionally, but an ordinary kernel booted with the threadirqs parameter turns it on too, and gets ktimers threads as a result.

These threads handle TIMER_SOFTIRQ and HRTIMER_SOFTIRQ separately from ksoftirqd and run at elevated priority (SCHED_FIFO low priority via sched_set_fifo_low(), described in the source as “above SCHED_NORMAL to handle timers before regular tasks”). This separation ensures that timer callbacks — which are latency-sensitive — are not delayed behind a backlog of network or block softirqs in ksoftirqd.

The mechanism is a second, parallel pending bitmap. raise_ktimers_thread() sets bits in a per-CPU pending_timer_softirq word instead of the normal one, __irq_exit_rcu() calls wake_timersd() when that word is nonzero, and the thread merges the two before dispatching:

/* kernel/softirq.c */
static void run_ktimerd(unsigned int cpu)
{
    unsigned int timer_si;

    ksoftirqd_run_begin();

    timer_si = local_timers_pending_force_th();
    __this_cpu_write(pending_timer_softirq, 0);
    or_softirq_pending(timer_si);

    __do_softirq();

    ksoftirqd_run_end();
}

Because the timer bits are folded into the ordinary pending bitmap just before __do_softirq() runs, the dispatch machinery itself needs no special case — the separation exists entirely in when the bits become visible, not in how they are processed.

/* kernel/softirq.c — only on CONFIG_IRQ_FORCED_THREADING */
static struct smp_hotplug_thread timer_thread = {
    .store              = &ktimerd,
    .setup              = ktimerd_setup,    /* sched_set_fifo_low(current) */
    .thread_should_run  = ktimerd_should_run,
    .thread_fn          = run_ktimerd,
    .thread_comm        = "ktimers/%u",
};

Tasklet Internals

Tasklets are the preferred way to implement deferrable functions in I/O drivers — at least, they were in Linux 2.6. As already explained, tasklets are built on top of two softirqs named HI_SOFTIRQ and TASKLET_SOFTIRQ. Several tasklets may be associated with the same softirq, each tasklet carrying its own function. There is no real difference between the two softirqs, except that handle_softirqs() executes HI_SOFTIRQ's tasklets before TASKLET_SOFTIRQ's tasklets.

The tasklet API header in Linux 7.0.12 explicitly states: "This API is deprecated. Please consider using threaded IRQs instead." The subsystem remains present because many drivers still use it, but new code should generally prefer threaded IRQs, workqueues, or NAPI depending on the workload.

The tasklet_struct Data Structure

The tasklet descriptor is a data structure of type tasklet_struct:

/* include/linux/interrupt.h */
struct tasklet_struct {
    struct tasklet_struct *next;
    unsigned long state;
    atomic_t count;
    bool use_callback;
    union {
        void (*func)(unsigned long data);
        void (*callback)(struct tasklet_struct *t);
    };
    unsigned long data;
};

Table 4-77. Fields of struct tasklet_struct

Field Description
nextPointer to next descriptor in the per-CPU tasklet list
stateStatus flags: TASKLET_STATE_SCHED (scheduled) and TASKLET_STATE_RUN (running)
countDisable counter; the tasklet is enabled only when this is zero
use_callbackWhen true, the modern callback prototype is used; when false, the legacy func prototype
funcLegacy callback: void (*func)(unsigned long data)
callbackModern callback: void (*callback)(struct tasklet_struct *t)
dataLegacy opaque unsigned long argument passed to func

Table 4-78. Comparison of tasklet_struct between Linux 2.6 and 7.0.12

Feature Linux 2.6 Linux 7.0.12
Callback typeSingle void (*func)(unsigned long)Union of legacy func and modern callback
use_callback fieldNot presentSelects between func and callback
TASKLET_STATE_RUNOnly on SMPOn SMP and CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT
Deprecation statusPreferred for driver bottom halvesDeprecated; threaded IRQs recommended

The state field includes two flags:

  • TASKLET_STATE_SCHED — When set, this indicates that the tasklet is pending (has been scheduled for execution); it also means the tasklet descriptor is inserted in one of the per-CPU lists.
  • TASKLET_STATE_RUN — When set, this indicates that the tasklet is currently executing on some CPU. On uniprocessor systems without CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT, this flag is not used because there is no need to check whether a specific tasklet is running. The flag ensures that a given tasklet object will not run concurrently on two CPUs at once, though different tasklets may run in parallel.

Initializing Tasklets

Linux 7.0.12 provides several ways to create a tasklet:

tasklet_setup() — the modern initializer (preferred):

/* kernel/softirq.c */
void tasklet_setup(struct tasklet_struct *t,
                   void (*callback)(struct tasklet_struct *))
{
    t->next = NULL;
    t->state = 0;
    atomic_set(&t->count, 0);
    t->callback = callback;
    t->use_callback = true;
    t->data = 0;
}

The modern callback receives a pointer to the tasklet_struct itself, allowing the driver to use container_of() (via the from_tasklet() helper) to recover a pointer to its private data structure — the same pattern used by timer callbacks and work items.

tasklet_init() — the legacy initializer (for old-style callbacks):

/* kernel/softirq.c */
void tasklet_init(struct tasklet_struct *t,
                  void (*func)(unsigned long), unsigned long data)
{
    t->next = NULL;
    t->state = 0;
    atomic_set(&t->count, 0);
    t->func = func;
    t->use_callback = false;
    t->data = data;
}

Static declaration macros:

/* include/linux/interrupt.h */
/* Modern style — enabled at declaration */
DECLARE_TASKLET(name, callback_fn);

/* Modern style — disabled at declaration (count = 1) */
DECLARE_TASKLET_DISABLED(name, callback_fn);

/* Legacy style — for old void (*func)(unsigned long) signature */
DECLARE_TASKLET_OLD(name, func);
DECLARE_TASKLET_DISABLED_OLD(name, func);

The DISABLED variants set count to 1, so the tasklet must be explicitly enabled with tasklet_enable() before it can execute.

Scheduling Tasklets

To activate a tasklet, a driver calls either tasklet_schedule() (for normal priority) or tasklet_hi_schedule() (for high priority). The two functions are very similar; each performs the following steps:

  1. Checks the TASKLET_STATE_SCHED flag using test_and_set_bit(). If it is already set, the function returns immediately — the tasklet has already been scheduled and a second scheduling would be redundant. This guarantees that a tasklet appears in the per-CPU list at most once.
  2. Calls __tasklet_schedule() or __tasklet_hi_schedule(), which delegates to __tasklet_schedule_common().
/* include/linux/interrupt.h */
static inline void tasklet_schedule(struct tasklet_struct *t)
{
    if (!test_and_set_bit(TASKLET_STATE_SCHED, &t->state))
        __tasklet_schedule(t);
}

__tasklet_schedule_common() performs the actual queueing:

/* kernel/softirq.c */
static void __tasklet_schedule_common(struct tasklet_struct *t,
                                      struct tasklet_head __percpu *headp,
                                      unsigned int softirq_nr)
{
    struct tasklet_head *head;
    unsigned long flags;

    local_irq_save(flags);
    head = this_cpu_ptr(headp);
    t->next = NULL;
    *head->tail = t;
    head->tail = &(t->next);
    raise_softirq_irqoff(softirq_nr);
    local_irq_restore(flags);
}

The function:

  1. Disables local interrupts with local_irq_save().
  2. Appends the tasklet descriptor to the tail of the per-CPU list (in 2.6, it was added at the head — the change to tail-append preserves FIFO ordering).
  3. Raises either TASKLET_SOFTIRQ or HI_SOFTIRQ via raise_softirq_irqoff().
  4. Restores the interrupt state.

Tasklets and high-priority tasklets are stored in the tasklet_vec and tasklet_hi_vec per-CPU variables, respectively:

/* kernel/softirq.c */
struct tasklet_head {
    struct tasklet_struct *head;
    struct tasklet_struct **tail;
};

static DEFINE_PER_CPU(struct tasklet_head, tasklet_vec);
static DEFINE_PER_CPU(struct tasklet_head, tasklet_hi_vec);

Executing Tasklets: tasklet_action_common()

The softirq function associated with HI_SOFTIRQ is tasklet_hi_action(), and the function associated with TASKLET_SOFTIRQ is tasklet_action(). Both delegate to tasklet_action_common(), which performs the following steps:

  1. Detaches the list: Disables local interrupts, saves the head pointer of the per-CPU list into a local variable list, resets the per-CPU list to empty (head = NULL, tail = &head), and re-enables interrupts. This atomic detach-and-reset means new tasklets scheduled during processing will go into a fresh list.
  2. Iterates the detached list: For each tasklet descriptor in list:
    • (a) Attempts to lock the tasklet by calling tasklet_trylock(t), which atomically sets TASKLET_STATE_RUN. If the bit is already set (the tasklet is running on another CPU), the lock fails and the tasklet is requeued (see step 3).
    • (b) If locked, checks whether the tasklet is disabled (atomic_read(&t->count) != 0). If disabled, it unlocks (tasklet_unlock(t)) and requeues (step 3).
    • (c) If the tasklet is enabled, clears TASKLET_STATE_SCHED via tasklet_clear_sched(), invokes the callback (choosing t->callback(t) or t->func(t->data) based on use_callback), and unlocks.
  3. Requeue on conflict: If the tasklet could not be executed (already running or disabled), it is appended back to the per-CPU list and the softirq is re-raised with __raise_softirq_irqoff(). This defers the tasklet until the conflict is resolved.
/* kernel/softirq.c — simplified for clarity */
static void tasklet_action_common(struct tasklet_head *tl_head,
                                  unsigned int softirq_nr)
{
    struct tasklet_struct *list;

    local_irq_disable();
    list = tl_head->head;
    tl_head->head = NULL;
    tl_head->tail = &tl_head->head;
    local_irq_enable();

    while (list) {
        struct tasklet_struct *t = list;
        list = list->next;

        if (tasklet_trylock(t)) {
            if (!atomic_read(&t->count)) {
                if (tasklet_clear_sched(t)) {
                    if (t->use_callback)
                        t->callback(t);
                    else
                        t->func(t->data);
                }
                tasklet_unlock(t);
                continue;
            }
            tasklet_unlock(t);
        }
        /* Requeue: could not run this time */
        local_irq_disable();
        t->next = NULL;
        *tl_head->tail = t;
        tl_head->tail = &t->next;
        __raise_softirq_irqoff(softirq_nr);
        local_irq_enable();
    }
}

Notice that, unless the tasklet function reactivates itself, every tasklet activation triggers at most one execution of the tasklet function.

Disabling and Enabling Tasklets

The tasklet may be selectively disabled by invoking either tasklet_disable_nosync() or tasklet_disable(). Both functions increment the count field of the tasklet descriptor, but they differ in synchronization behavior:

Table 4-79. Tasklet disable/enable API

Function Behavior Can sleep?
tasklet_disable_nosync(t)Increments count; returns immediately even if the tasklet is runningNo
tasklet_disable(t)Increments count; waits until a running instance finishes (tasklet_unlock_wait)Yes
tasklet_disable_in_atomic(t)Increments count; busy-waits for running instance (tasklet_unlock_spin_wait)No
tasklet_enable(t)Decrements count; the tasklet becomes eligible when count reaches zeroNo
 
/* include/linux/interrupt.h */
static inline void tasklet_disable_nosync(struct tasklet_struct *t)
{
    atomic_inc(&t->count);
    smp_mb__after_atomic();
}

static inline void tasklet_disable(struct tasklet_struct *t)
{
    tasklet_disable_nosync(t);
    tasklet_unlock_wait(t);
    smp_mb();
}

static inline void tasklet_enable(struct tasklet_struct *t)
{
    smp_mb__before_atomic();
    atomic_dec(&t->count);
}

In Linux 2.6, tasklet_disable() used tasklet_unlock_spin_wait() (busy-wait) on all configurations. In 7.0.12, tasklet_disable() uses tasklet_unlock_wait(), which on SMP and PREEMPT_RT calls wait_on_bit() — a sleeping wait. The old busy-wait behavior is preserved only in tasklet_disable_in_atomic(), which is explicitly marked as "do not use in new code."

Killing Tasklets

To permanently remove a tasklet (e.g., when unloading a driver module), the kernel provides tasklet_kill():

/* kernel/softirq.c */
void tasklet_kill(struct tasklet_struct *t)
{
    if (in_interrupt())
        pr_notice("Attempt to kill tasklet from interrupt\n");

    wait_on_bit_lock(&t->state, TASKLET_STATE_SCHED, TASK_UNINTERRUPTIBLE);
    tasklet_unlock_wait(t);
    tasklet_clear_sched(t);
}

tasklet_kill() sleeps until the tasklet is neither scheduled nor running, then clears the TASKLET_STATE_SCHED bit. It must be called from process context (not from interrupt context or within the tasklet itself). After tasklet_kill() returns, the tasklet will not be executed again unless it is rescheduled.

PREEMPT_RT Considerations

On CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT kernels, tasklet execution is significantly different. Since softirqs run in ksoftirqd (a fully preemptible thread), tasklets also run in thread context. However, the original tasklet API assumes non-preemptible execution, which can lead to priority inversion on RT kernels.

To address this, Linux 7.0.12 adds a per-CPU tasklet_sync_callback structure with a spinlock that serializes tasklet callback execution on RT:

/* kernel/softirq.c — CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT only */
struct tasklet_sync_callback {
    spinlock_t   cb_lock;
    atomic_t     cb_waiters;
};

The tasklet_lock_callback() / tasklet_unlock_callback() wrappers acquire and release this per-CPU lock around the entire tasklet iteration loop. tasklet_callback_cancel_wait_running() provides the synchronization needed by tasklet_disable_in_atomic() under RT. On non-RT kernels, all these functions are empty stubs.

Per-CPU List Initialization

The per-CPU tasklet lists are initialized in softirq_init(), which is called early during boot:

/* kernel/softirq.c */
void __init softirq_init(void)
{
    int cpu;
    for_each_possible_cpu(cpu) {
        per_cpu(tasklet_vec, cpu).tail =
            &per_cpu(tasklet_vec, cpu).head;
        per_cpu(tasklet_hi_vec, cpu).tail =
            &per_cpu(tasklet_hi_vec, cpu).head;
    }
    open_softirq(TASKLET_SOFTIRQ, tasklet_action);
    open_softirq(HI_SOFTIRQ, tasklet_hi_action);
}

This function sets each per-CPU list to the empty state (tail points to &head) and registers the two tasklet softirq handlers.

Using Tasklets in a Driver: Summary

For drivers that still use tasklets, the typical workflow is:

  1. Declare or allocate a tasklet_struct — use DECLARE_TASKLET(name, callback) for static allocation, or call tasklet_setup(t, callback) for dynamic initialization.
  2. Schedule the tasklet from the ISR — call tasklet_schedule(t) or tasklet_hi_schedule(t).
  3. The callback runs in softirq context at a later point, on the same CPU that scheduled it.
  4. Disable/enable as needed for synchronization — use tasklet_disable(t) / tasklet_enable(t).
  5. Kill the tasklet on cleanup — call tasklet_kill(t) from the module's remove function.

Workqueues

Work queues were introduced in Linux 2.6, replacing the older "task queue" mechanism from Linux 2.4. They allow kernel functions to be activated (much like deferrable functions) and later executed by special kernel threads called worker threads.

Despite their similarities with softirqs and tasklets, workqueues are fundamentally different. The main difference is that deferrable functions run in interrupt context while functions in work queues run in process context. Running in process context is the only way to execute functions that can block (for instance, functions that need to access a block device or acquire a sleeping mutex), because no process switch can take place in interrupt context. Neither deferrable functions nor workqueue callbacks can access the user-mode address space of a process — workqueue workers are kernel threads with no user-mode mappings.

Since Linux 2.6, the workqueue implementation has evolved dramatically into the Concurrency-Managed Work Queue (CMWQ) model. In the old model (pre-3.x), each workqueue created its own dedicated set of worker threads (foo/0, foo/1, etc.). The CMWQ model shares worker pools across workqueues, grows or shrinks them according to demand, and provides sophisticated concurrency control — resulting in far fewer total kernel threads.

Work Queue Data Structures

The workqueue subsystem in Linux 7.0.12 uses four primary data structures:

struct work_struct — represents a single work item:

/* include/linux/workqueue_types.h */
struct work_struct {
    atomic_long_t data;
    struct list_head entry;
    work_func_t func;
#ifdef CONFIG_LOCKDEP
    struct lockdep_map lockdep_map;
#endif
};

Table 4-80. struct work_struct fields

FieldDescription
data (low bits)Encode work state flags: pending, inactive, color, and linked status
data (high bits)Encode the pool-workqueue identity when queued
entryLinks the work item onto the pool's active list or the inactive list
funcCallback function executed by the worker: void (*func)(struct work_struct *work)
Lockdep mapDebugging metadata for lock dependency tracking

In Linux 2.6, work_struct had separate pending, data, and timer fields. In 7.0.12, these are all encoded into the single atomic_long_t data word using bit manipulation — a space optimization that also makes atomic state transitions cheaper.

struct delayed_work — wraps a work_struct with a timer for delayed execution:

/* include/linux/workqueue.h */
struct delayed_work {
    struct work_struct work;
    struct timer_list timer;
    struct workqueue_struct *wq;
    int cpu;
};

struct worker — represents a worker thread:

/* kernel/workqueue_internal.h — principal fields only */
struct worker {
    union {
        struct list_head entry;
        struct hlist_node hentry;
    };
    struct work_struct *current_work;
    work_func_t current_func;
    struct pool_workqueue *current_pwq;
    struct list_head scheduled;
    struct task_struct *task;
    struct worker_pool *pool;
    unsigned int flags;
    int id;
    char desc[WORKER_DESC_LEN];
};

struct worker_pool — a pool of worker threads shared by workqueues. Each pool manages a set of struct worker instances. Bound pools are per-CPU: one normal-priority and one high-priority pool per CPU. The pool monitors concurrency, and when all of its workers are blocked it creates another one to ensure forward progress.

Unbound pools are allocated per pod, and the definition of a pod is configurable. Older accounts of CMWQ describe unbound pools as per-NUMA-node, which was true when the feature was introduced but is no longer the default. Linux 7.0.12 offers five affinity scopes:

/* include/linux/workqueue.h */
enum wq_affn_scope {
    WQ_AFFN_DFL,            /* use system default */
    WQ_AFFN_CPU,            /* one pod per CPU */
    WQ_AFFN_SMT,            /* one pod poer SMT */
    WQ_AFFN_CACHE,          /* one pod per LLC */
    WQ_AFFN_NUMA,           /* one pod per NUMA node */
    WQ_AFFN_SYSTEM,         /* one pod across the whole system */

    WQ_AFFN_NR_TYPES,
};

/* kernel/workqueue.c */
static enum wq_affn_scope wq_affn_dfl = WQ_AFFN_CACHE;

The default is WQ_AFFN_CACHE — one pod per last-level cache, not per NUMA node. On a modern server the two differ substantially: a single NUMA node may contain several LLC domains, so cache-scoped pods keep a work item and the data it touches within one shared cache rather than merely within one socket. The scope can be changed globally with the workqueue.default_affinity_scope boot parameter, or per workqueue through sysfs when the workqueue was created with WQ_SYSFS. This matters for the surrounding text as well: where the alloc_workqueue() documentation speaks of a per-node max_active, “node” should be read as “pod”.

struct pool_workqueue — connects a workqueue_struct to a worker_pool. This intermediary tracks max_active enforcement, the active/inactive work item lists, and flush/drain state. Each workqueue has one pool_workqueue per pool it uses.

Table 4-81. Data structure hierarchy: Linux 2.6 vs 7.0.12

Linux 2.6Linux 7.0.12Change
workqueue_struct → array of cpu_workqueue_structworkqueue_struct → pool_workqueue → worker_poolThree-level hierarchy; pools shared
One dedicated thread per CPU per workqueueShared per-CPU and per-NUMA worker poolsFar fewer total threads
work_struct.pending separate fieldState bits encoded in work_struct.dataSpace optimization
cpu_workqueue_struct.threadworker.task inside shared poolWorkers not owned by a single workqueue

Creating and Destroying Workqueues

alloc_workqueue() is the modern replacement for create_workqueue(). It takes a printf-style name format, flags, and a max_active limit:

/* include/linux/workqueue.h */
struct workqueue_struct *
alloc_workqueue(const char *fmt, unsigned int flags, int max_active, ...);

For bound workqueues, max_active applies per CPU. For unbound workqueues, the limit applies system-wide but is distributed per NUMA node. Passing max_active = 0 uses the default (WQ_DFL_ACTIVE = 1024). A min_active guarantee (default 8) prevents deadlocks when max_active is reached.

Ordered workqueues guarantee strict FIFO execution (one work item at a time):

/* include/linux/workqueue.h */
#define alloc_ordered_workqueue(fmt, flags, args...)  \
    alloc_workqueue(fmt, WQ_UNBOUND | __WQ_ORDERED | (flags), 1, ##args)

This replaces the old create_singlethread_workqueue(). Ordered workqueues are implemented as unbound workqueues with max_active = 1.

Table 4-82. Important workqueue flags for alloc_workqueue()

FlagEffect
WQ_BHExecute in bottom-half (softirq) context — new in 7.0.12
WQ_UNBOUNDWorkers not tied to the submitting CPU; good for throughput and NUMA balancing
WQ_HIGHPRIUse high-priority worker pools
WQ_FREEZABLEParticipate in suspend freezing
WQ_MEM_RECLAIMGuarantee a rescuer thread for memory-reclaim safety
WQ_CPU_INTENSIVELong CPU-bound work should not throttle unrelated work via concurrency management
WQ_PERCPUBound to specific CPUs for cache locality
WQ_POWER_EFFICIENTPrefer energy-sensitive placement when configured
WQ_SYSFSExpose workqueue attributes in sysfs for runtime tuning

destroy_workqueue() drains all pending work items and frees the workqueue.

Table 4-83. Old and new workqueue creation styles

Old APIModern equivalentWhy the change matters
create_workqueue(name)alloc_workqueue(name, __WQ_LEGACY | WQ_MEM_RECLAIM | WQ_PERCPU, 1)Unifies legacy wrappers under alloc_workqueue() semantics
create_singlethread_workqueue(name)alloc_ordered_workqueue(name, flags)Ordered execution is now an unbound max_active=1 policy
Ad hoc per-driver kthread poolShared CMWQ pools where possibleBetter scalability and lower thread count

Queueing Work Items

queue_work() inserts a work item into a workqueue. It receives a pointer to the workqueue_struct and a pointer to the work_struct:

/* include/linux/workqueue.h */
static inline bool queue_work(struct workqueue_struct *wq,
                              struct work_struct *work)
{
    return queue_work_on(WORK_CPU_UNBOUND, wq, work);
}

Both eventually reach queue_work_on(), which performs the following steps:

  1. Disables local interrupts with local_irq_save(), so that the pending-bit test and the queueing that follows it cannot be split by an interrupt that queues the same item.
  2. Tests and sets WORK_STRUCT_PENDING_BIT in work->data with a single test_and_set_bit(). If the bit was already set, the work item is already queued and the function returns false without doing anything further.
  3. Calls clear_pending_if_disabled(), which backs the operation out if the work item has been disabled by disable_work() or by an in-progress cancel_work_sync().
  4. Calls __queue_work(), which chooses the appropriate pool_workqueue based on the CPU and the workqueue’s flags.
  5. If the pool’s max_active limit has not been reached, the work item goes directly onto the pool’s active worklist and a worker is woken (or created if needed).
  6. If max_active is reached, the work item goes onto an inactive list and will be activated later when an active item completes.

Step 2 gives work items the same “at most once” property that TASKLET_STATE_SCHED gives tasklets, and the consequence is the same and equally easy to overlook. Queueing a work item that is already queued is silently a no-op; the callback runs once, not twice. A driver that queues an item per completed request and expects one callback per request will lose events under load. The return value exists to make this visible, and code that cares must check it.

queue_delayed_work() is similar but accepts a third parameter — a delay in jiffies. It arms the delayed_work's internal timer; when the timer fires, it calls delayed_work_timer_fn(), which queues the work item onto the workqueue. cancel_delayed_work() cancels a previously armed timer, preventing the work from being queued.

The Worker Thread Loop

Every worker thread continuously executes a loop inside the worker_thread() function (or rescuer_thread() for rescuer threads). The CMWQ worker loop in 7.0.12 works as follows:

  1. The worker sleeps on the pool's wait queue until work is available or the pool idle timeout expires.
  2. Once woken, it calls process_one_work(), which dequeues a single work item from the pool's active worklist.
  3. process_one_work() records the current work item in worker->current_work and worker->current_func, then invokes worker->current_func(work).
  4. Before calling the function, the worker marks itself as "running" in the pool's concurrency tracking. If the callback blocks (e.g., waits for I/O), the pool's concurrency manager detects this and may spawn a new worker to keep forward progress.
  5. After the callback returns, process_one_work() clears the worker's state and checks if more work is available.

This "spawn on block" behavior is the key innovation of CMWQ: the kernel creates additional workers only when all current workers in a pool are blocked, ensuring minimum thread count while guaranteeing forward progress.

Synchronization: Flushing and Cancelling

Sometimes the kernel must wait until work items have completed. Linux 7.0.12 provides several synchronization functions:

Table 4-84. Workqueue synchronization API

FunctionBehavior
flush_work(work)Block until a specific work item has finished executing
flush_workqueue(wq)Block until all currently queued work items on the workqueue have finished; warns at compile time if wq is a shared system workqueue
cancel_work_sync(work)Cancel a pending work item, or wait for it to finish if already running
cancel_delayed_work_sync(dwork)Cancel a delayed work timer and/or wait for running callback to finish
drain_workqueue(wq)Flush all work items and prevent new ones from being queued (used during teardown)

flush_workqueue() does not wait for work items added after the flush call — it uses a coloring mechanism (work items are tagged with a color at queue time, and the flush waits for all items of the current color to complete).

cancel_work_sync() is safe to call even if the work item is currently executing: it cancels the pending state and then flushes, so that it returns only once any in-flight callback has finished. This makes it the correct primitive for cleanup paths in driver remove() functions, where the driver is about to free the memory that the callback would otherwise dereference.

The wait is a sleeping wait, not a spin. __cancel_work_sync() opens with might_sleep(), so the function must be called from process context; the only exception is a BH work item, for which it busy-waits instead and merely warns if called from hardirq context. Calling cancel_work_sync() from a softirq, a timer callback, or while holding a spinlock is therefore a bug — and one that a kernel built without CONFIG_DEBUG_ATOMIC_SLEEP will not report.

A related trap concerns flush_workqueue(). In Linux 7.0.12 it is a macro that detects at compile time whether its argument is one of the shared system-wide workqueues and, if so, emits __warn_flushing_systemwide_wq() — a __compiletime_warning reading “Please avoid flushing system-wide workqueues.” The reason is a dependency hazard: flushing a shared queue waits for every unrelated subsystem’s work items as well as one’s own, which is both slow and an easy way to construct a deadlock. flush_scheduled_work(), the old shorthand for flushing the default queue, carries an unconditional version of the same warning and is documented as scheduled for removal. The modern advice is to flush a specific work item with flush_work(), or to own a private workqueue if a whole-queue barrier is genuinely required.

The Predefined System Workqueues

In most cases, creating a whole set of worker threads for a single driver function is overkill. The kernel provides predefined system workqueues that can be freely used:

Table 4-85. System workqueues in Linux 7.0.12

WorkqueueCharacteristicsUse case
system_wqAlias of system_percpu_wqDeprecated — the header marks it “use system_percpu_wq, this will be removed”
system_percpu_wqBound, per-CPU workersDefault for schedule_work()
system_highpri_wqHigh-priority worker poolsLatency-sensitive deferred work
system_long_wqPer-CPU, for long-running itemsWork that may block for extended time
system_unbound_wqUnbound, flexible CPU placementThroughput-oriented work
system_dfl_wqUnbound, not concurrency-managedWork needing no concurrency limits
system_freezable_wqPer-CPU, participates in suspendSuspend-aware deferred work
system_power_efficient_wqPer-CPU or unbound (configurable)Power-sensitive workloads
system_freezable_power_efficient_wqBoth freezable and power-efficientSuspend-aware, power-sensitive work
system_bh_wqExecutes in BH (softirq) contextBH work items (new in 7.0.12)
system_bh_highpri_wqHigh-priority BH contextHigh-priority BH work items

The schedule_work() and schedule_delayed_work() helper functions queue work onto system_percpu_wq:

/* include/linux/workqueue.h */
static inline bool schedule_work(struct work_struct *work)
{
    return queue_work(system_percpu_wq, work);
}

static inline bool schedule_delayed_work(struct delayed_work *dwork,
                                         unsigned long delay)
{
    return queue_delayed_work(system_percpu_wq, dwork, delay);
}

In Linux 2.6, the predefined workqueue was called events (stored in keventd_wq), and the helper was schedule_work(w) which mapped to queue_work(keventd_wq, w). The modern system_percpu_wq serves the same purpose but is part of the CMWQ pool infrastructure.

The rename from system_wq to system_percpu_wq is recent and still in progress, which is why both symbols exist. It accompanies a broader change of defaults: WQ_PERCPU became an explicit flag so that CPU binding is something a workqueue asks for rather than something it gets by accident.
