The motivation is spelled out in the flag definition — per-CPU placement gives better cache locality, but it also takes the CPU choice away from the scheduler, and a per-CPU work item queued from an interrupt handler on an idle CPU forces that CPU out of idle. On battery-powered and heavily consolidated systems that is a measurable cost. Driver code that does not genuinely need locality should prefer system_dfl_wq, the unbound default, and let the scheduler place the work.

The WQ_BH workqueues (system_bh_wq, system_bh_highpri_wq) are a notable addition in 7.0.12. They allow work items to be executed in softirq context, bridging the workqueue API with bottom-half execution and giving tasklet users a migration path that does not change the execution context their code runs in.

The implementation is disarmingly direct — BH work items are literally executed by the tasklet softirqs:

/* kernel/softirq.c */
static __latent_entropy void tasklet_action(void)
{
    workqueue_softirq_action(false);
    tasklet_action_common(this_cpu_ptr(&tasklet_vec), TASKLET_SOFTIRQ);
}

static __latent_entropy void tasklet_hi_action(void)
{
    workqueue_softirq_action(true);
    tasklet_action_common(this_cpu_ptr(&tasklet_hi_vec), HI_SOFTIRQ);
}

Note the ordering: workqueue_softirq_action() runs before tasklet_action_common(), so within each of the two tasklet softirqs, BH work items are drained first and tasklets second. That is a deliberate nudge — a subsystem that converts from tasklets to BH work items does not lose latency in the process, it gains a little. Because BH work executes in softirq context, such workqueues accept only the flags listed in __WQ_BH_ALLOWS: WQ_BH, WQ_HIGHPRI, and WQ_PERCPU. Everything that implies sleeping or CPU migration — WQ_UNBOUND, WQ_MEM_RECLAIM, WQ_FREEZABLE — is rejected at allocation time.

Using Workqueues in a Driver: Summary

The typical workflow for a driver using workqueues:

  1. Initialize a work item with INIT_WORK(&my_work, my_callback) or INIT_DELAYED_WORK(&my_dwork, my_callback).
  2. Queue the work from an ISR or other context: schedule_work(&my_work) or queue_work(my_wq, &my_work).
  3. The callback runs in process context in a worker thread — it may sleep, acquire mutexes, or perform I/O.
  4. Synchronize when needed: flush_work(&my_work) or cancel_work_sync(&my_work).
  5. Clean up on module removal: cancel_work_sync(&my_work) and, if using a custom workqueue, destroy_workqueue(my_wq).

Comparing Deferred-Work Mechanisms

Table 4-86. Deferred-work mechanisms compared

Mechanism Context Can sleep? Concurrency model Typical use Latency profile
Softirq interrupt/bottom-half No same class may run on many CPUs networking, timers, RCU very low, but bounded by fairness heuristics
Tasklet softirq-based atomic context No self-serialized per tasklet object legacy driver bottom halves low, but legacy and limited
Threaded IRQ dedicated kernel thread per action Yes one thread per IRQ action, optionally oneshot device follow-up needing blocking or long work higher than hardirq, still device-coupled
Workqueue worker thread Yes pool-managed, many policies general deferred kernel work scheduler-mediated, flexible
Timer callback hardirq/softirq depending on type Usually no per timer event schedule later action at time boundary time-driven rather than event-driven

The comparison table hides an important qualitative difference. Softirqs and tasklets borrow the interrupted CPU's urgency. Threaded IRQs and workqueues turn the deferred work into schedulable entities. That means the latter two integrate naturally with priority, cgroup accounting, CPU affinity, and the scheduler's fairness decisions. The price is extra latency and context-switch overhead. Linux 7.0.12 gives developers enough mechanisms that they can choose the right trade-off instead of forcing all drivers into one deferred-work style.

Returning from Interrupts and Exceptions

We will finish the chapter by examining the termination phase of interrupt and exception handlers. (Returning from a system call is a special case, described in Chapter 10.) Although the main objective is clear — to resume execution of some program — several issues must be considered before doing it:

  • Nesting of kernel control paths: If the interrupt or exception occurred while the CPU was already handling another interrupt in kernel mode, the return simply restores the previous kernel context. Only the outermost return can potentially reach user mode.
  • Pending process switch requests: If TIF_NEED_RESCHED or TIF_NEED_RESCHED_LAZY is set, the scheduler must be invoked before returning to user mode.
  • Pending signals: If a signal is sent to the current process, it must be delivered before the process resumes in user mode.
  • Single-step and debug state: If a debugger is tracing the execution of the current process, single-step mode must be restored.
  • Livepatch state: If the kernel has a pending livepatch transition (TIF_PATCH_PENDING), the task's patch state must be updated.
  • Uprobes: If a userspace probe is active (TIF_UPROBE), the probe handler must complete.
  • Restartable sequences: If an RSEQ critical section is in progress, the kernel may grant a slice extension to avoid preempting at the worst possible point.

In Linux 2.6, these checks were implemented as assembly language code at two entry points: ret_from_intr() and ret_from_exception(), with labels like resume_userspace, resume_kernel, work_pending, work_resched, work_notifysig, and restore_all. The old code also handled Virtual-8086 mode, which is no longer relevant on x86_64.

In Linux 7.0.12, the return path has been completely rewritten. The architecture-specific assembly is minimal — it saves and restores registers and executes the final return instruction (iretq on x86_64, eret on ARM64). All the decision logic has moved into generic C, shared across architectures. The key function is irqentry_exit(), which dispatches to either the user-mode exit path or the kernel-mode exit path.

That generic code lives in two places, and it is worth knowing which is which when you go looking for it. The out-of-line functions — irqentry_enter(), irqentry_exit(), raw_irqentry_exit_cond_resched(), and the work loop itself — are compiled into kernel/entry/common.c. Everything that must be inlined into the architecture's noinstr entry code, because a call would itself be traceable at a point where tracing is not yet safe, lives in the header include/linux/irq-entry-common.h: irqentry_exit_to_user_mode(), __exit_to_user_mode_prepare(), exit_to_user_mode(), and the arch_* hook defaults are all __always_inline definitions there. A reader who greps only kernel/entry/ will find the dispatcher but not the machinery it dispatches into.

Adoption of this machinery is not uniform. arch/Kconfig splits it into two options: GENERIC_IRQ_ENTRY covers the interrupt and exception entry/exit paths, while GENERIC_ENTRY additionally covers the system-call path and selects the former. x86 selects GENERIC_ENTRY and therefore uses the generic code everywhere; ARM64 selects only GENERIC_IRQ_ENTRY and still routes its user-mode exit through a compatibility wrapper the header frankly labels exit_to_user_mode_prepare_legacy(), above the comment "Temporary workaround to keep ARM64 alive." The unification described in this section is real but, five years after it began, still unfinished.

The Return Path: Overview

Figure 4-17 shows the return flow for Linux 7.0.12. The most significant change since Linux 2.6 is that the decision logic is now in generic C rather than architecture-specific assembly, and the user-mode work loop is a clean while loop rather than a chain of conditional jumps.

One structural point is easy to miss in both diagrams, because neither draws it: the loop is normally skipped altogether. Before entering it, __exit_to_user_mode_prepare() reads the thread flags once and tests them against EXIT_TO_USER_MODE_WORK under an unlikely() annotation. On the overwhelming majority of returns nothing is pending, the test fails, and the kernel goes straight to the architecture's final exit work. The loop in Figure 4-17 is the slow path; the fast path is a single load, a single mask, and a not-taken branch.

Table 4-87. How the return path changed from Linux 2.6 to 7.0.12

AspectLinux 2.6 (x86 32-bit)Linux 7.0.12 (generic + x86_64/ARM64)
Decision codeArchitecture-specific assembly (ret_from_intr, ret_from_exception)Generic C in kernel/entry/common.c and include/linux/irq-entry-common.h
Entry pointsTwo: ret_from_intr(), ret_from_exception()One: irqentry_exit(regs, state)
User-mode workJump chain: resume_userspace → work_pending → work_resched / work_notifysigwhile loop in __exit_to_user_mode_loop()
Kernel preemptionCheck preempt_count + TIF_NEED_RESCHED in assemblyraw_irqentry_exit_cond_resched() in C, plus an architecture veto
Preemption modelFixed at compile time by CONFIG_PREEMPTSelectable at boot/run time via CONFIG_PREEMPT_DYNAMIC (static call on x86, static key on ARM64)
Marking a preemptive schedule()PREEMPT_ACTIVE, bit 28 of preempt_countSM_PREEMPT, an argument to __schedule()
Big Kernel Lockpreempt_schedule_irq() saves and restores the BKL depthGone; the BKL was removed in 2.6.39
Fast pathMask the flags word and branch to restore_allMask the flags word against EXIT_TO_USER_MODE_WORK; the work loop is never entered
Virtual-8086 modeExplicit save_v86_state() checkNot applicable on x86_64
LivepatchNot presentTIF_PATCH_PENDING → klp_update_patch_state()
UprobesNot presentTIF_UPROBE → uprobe_notify_resume()
RSEQNot presentrseq_grant_slice_extension() may defer reschedule
Final instructioniret (32-bit)iretq (x86_64) / eret (ARM64)

The irqentry_exit() Function

irqentry_exit() is the unified return dispatcher. It receives the saved pt_regs and the irqentry_state_t recorded by irqentry_enter() at entry. The function performs a three-way branch:

/* kernel/entry/common.c */
noinstr void irqentry_exit(struct pt_regs *regs, irqentry_state_t state)
{
    lockdep_assert_irqs_disabled();

    /* Check whether this returns to user mode */
    if (user_mode(regs)) {
        irqentry_exit_to_user_mode(regs);
    } else if (!regs_irqs_disabled(regs)) {
        /*
         * Returning to kernel mode with IRQs enabled.
         * May need to preempt if CONFIG_PREEMPTION.
         */
        if (state.exit_rcu) {
            /* RCU was not watching on entry — fix up carefully */
            trace_hardirqs_on_prepare();
            lockdep_hardirqs_on_prepare();
            ct_irq_exit();
            lockdep_hardirqs_on(CALLER_ADDR0);
            return;
        }
        if (IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_PREEMPTION))
            irqentry_exit_cond_resched();
        trace_hardirqs_on();
    } else {
        /* Returning to kernel mode with IRQs disabled — nothing to do */
        if (state.exit_rcu)
            ct_irq_exit();
    }
}

The three branches correspond to:

  1. Return to user mode — the most complex case, requiring signal delivery, rescheduling, uprobe completion, and more. Delegates to irqentry_exit_to_user_mode().
  2. Return to kernel mode with IRQs enabled — may trigger kernel preemption via irqentry_exit_cond_resched(). This is the modern equivalent of the old resume_kernel + need_resched assembly path.
  3. Return to kernel mode with IRQs disabled — the simplest case. The interrupted kernel code was running in a critical section with interrupts off, so no preemption is possible. Only RCU state cleanup is needed.

Two details in this function repay a second look. The first is that the branch is taken on regs_irqs_disabled(regs) — the interrupt-enable bit saved in the interrupted frame, not the current one. Interrupts are hard-disabled right now on every path (the lockdep_assert_irqs_disabled() at the top says so), so asking about the present state would be useless; what the kernel needs to know is the state it is about to restore. On x86_64 this reads EFLAGS.IF from pt_regs; on ARM64 it inspects the saved PSTATE DAIF bits.

The second is the state.exit_rcu early return in the middle branch. irqentry_enter() sets that flag when it found RCU not watching — the interrupt landed in the idle task or in some other extended quiescent state — and had to call ct_irq_enter() to wake RCU up. Unwinding that requires the same delicate trace_hardirqs_on_prepare() / lockdep_hardirqs_on_prepare() / ct_irq_exit() / lockdep_hardirqs_on() ordering used on the user-return path, and it returns immediately afterwards: a CPU that was idle deeply enough for RCU to stop watching it has nothing worth preempting, so the conditional-reschedule call is deliberately skipped. Note also that the listing above elides the instrumentation_begin() / instrumentation_end() brackets that surround every instrumentable region; the function is noinstr, and those markers are what let objtool prove that no tracer is invoked before RCU and lockdep agree on the machine's state. The comments have likewise been condensed.

One path deliberately bypasses all of this. Non-maskable interrupts return through irqentry_nmi_exit(), which pairs with irqentry_nmi_enter() and does nothing beyond unwinding bookkeeping: ftrace_nmi_exit(), the conditional trace_hardirqs_on_prepare() / lockdep_hardirqs_on_prepare() pair, ct_nmi_exit(), lockdep_hardirq_exit() and finally __nmi_exit(). There is no work loop, no need_resched() test and no signal delivery, because an NMI can arrive at literally any instruction — including inside the scheduler, inside __schedule() with the runqueue lock held, or inside another NMI handler. Preempting from that context would deadlock instantly. An NMI therefore always resumes exactly what it interrupted; if it needs to cause a reschedule it does so indirectly, by setting TIF_NEED_RESCHED and letting the next ordinary return path act on it.

The Return Path to User Mode

If irqentry_exit() sees that the saved frame returns to user mode, it calls irqentry_exit_to_user_mode(regs). From there the path is a short ladder of inline wrappers, each of which exists to add exactly one step:

irqentry_exit_to_user_mode(regs)            /* include/linux/irq-entry-common.h */
 └─ irqentry_exit_to_user_mode_prepare(regs)
     ├─ __exit_to_user_mode_prepare(regs)
     │   ├─ tick_nohz_user_enter_prepare()  /* flush a pending rcuog wakeup */
     │   ├─ ti_work = read_thread_flags()
     │   ├─ if (unlikely(ti_work & EXIT_TO_USER_MODE_WORK))
     │   │       ti_work = exit_to_user_mode_loop(regs, ti_work);   /* SLOW PATH */
     │   └─ arch_exit_to_user_mode_prepare(regs, ti_work)
     ├─ rseq_irqentry_exit_to_user_mode()   /* arm the slice-extension timer */
     └─ __exit_to_user_mode_validate()      /* kmap_assert_nomap(), lockdep_sys_exit() */
 └─ exit_to_user_mode()                     /* tracing/lockdep/RCU: now in user mode */

The tick_nohz_user_enter_prepare() call comes before the flags are read, and the comment in the source explains why: it flushes any deferred wakeup of the rcuog kthread so that if such a wakeup sets TIF_NEED_RESCHED, the read that follows sees it. Reading the flags first would race with the very work being flushed.

Only if some bit in EXIT_TO_USER_MODE_WORK is set does the kernel enter exit_to_user_mode_loop(), and from there __exit_to_user_mode_loop(). The loop exists because pending work bits can change while some other work item is being processed with interrupts enabled. Linux therefore keeps reevaluating until none of the user-return work bits remain.

/* kernel/entry/common.c */
static __always_inline unsigned long
__exit_to_user_mode_loop(struct pt_regs *regs, unsigned long ti_work)
{
    while (ti_work & EXIT_TO_USER_MODE_WORK_LOOP) {
        local_irq_enable_exit_to_user(ti_work);

        if (ti_work & (_TIF_NEED_RESCHED | _TIF_NEED_RESCHED_LAZY)) {
            if (!rseq_grant_slice_extension(ti_work & TIF_SLICE_EXT_DENY))
                schedule();
        }
        if (ti_work & _TIF_UPROBE)
            uprobe_notify_resume(regs);
        if (ti_work & _TIF_PATCH_PENDING)
            klp_update_patch_state(current);
        if (ti_work & (_TIF_SIGPENDING | _TIF_NOTIFY_SIGNAL))
            arch_do_signal_or_restart(regs);
        if (ti_work & _TIF_NOTIFY_RESUME)
            resume_user_mode_work(regs);

        arch_exit_to_user_mode_work(regs, ti_work);

        local_irq_disable_exit_to_user();
        tick_nohz_user_enter_prepare();
        ti_work = read_thread_flags();
    }
    return ti_work;
}

The loop processes each category of pending work in priority order:

  1. Rescheduling (_TIF_NEED_RESCHED / _TIF_NEED_RESCHED_LAZY): Calls schedule() to let a higher-priority task run. The RSEQ extension may temporarily defer this (see below).
  2. Uprobes (_TIF_UPROBE): Completes userspace probe single-step work.
  3. Livepatch (_TIF_PATCH_PENDING): Synchronizes the task's livepatch transition state.
  4. Signals (_TIF_SIGPENDING / _TIF_NOTIFY_SIGNAL): Delivers pending signals via arch_do_signal_or_restart(). This is the modern equivalent of the old work_notifysig → do_notify_resume() path.
  5. Generic resume work (_TIF_NOTIFY_RESUME): resume_user_mode_work() clears the flag first — deliberately, so that a callback which queues more work can set it again and be seen on the next iteration — then runs the task-work queue via task_work_run(), applies memory-cgroup high-limit reclaim through mem_cgroup_handle_over_high(), and lets the block cgroup throttle the task in blkcg_maybe_throttle_current(). This is the general-purpose "do it just before user space" hook: any subsystem that needs to run code in a task's own context, with no locks held and sleeping permitted, queues it here.
  6. Architecture-specific work: arch_exit_to_user_mode_work(), whose generic definition is an empty stub. Of the two architectures this book follows, only ARM64 overrides it — to deliver SIGSEGV with SEGV_MTEAERR for a pending asynchronous Memory Tagging Extension tag-check fault, and to reload FPSIMD state when the CPU's copy belongs to some other task. x86_64 leaves the hook empty and does its per-architecture work elsewhere, as described below.

After each iteration, interrupts are disabled and the thread flags are re-read. This re-read is essential: processing any work item above may have caused new work to become pending (for example, delivering a signal may trigger a reschedule).

The loop condition deserves a note of its own, because it is not simply EXIT_TO_USER_MODE_WORK — the mask that gated entry to the loop — but a slightly narrower relative:

/* kernel/entry/common.c */
#ifdef CONFIG_HAVE_GENERIC_TIF_BITS
#define EXIT_TO_USER_MODE_WORK_LOOP    (EXIT_TO_USER_MODE_WORK & ~_TIF_RSEQ)
#else
#define EXIT_TO_USER_MODE_WORK_LOOP    (EXIT_TO_USER_MODE_WORK)
#endif

On an architecture that has adopted the generic thread-flag numbering — x86 selects HAVE_GENERIC_TIF_BITS, ARM64 does not — TIF_RSEQ gets a bit of its own (bit 11 in include/asm-generic/thread_info_tif.h) and is deliberately excluded from the loop condition. Without that exclusion the loop would spin forever whenever TIF_RSEQ were the only bit set, because nothing inside the loop body clears it. Restartable-sequence fixups are instead handled by the enclosing for (;;) in exit_to_user_mode_loop(), described later in this section. Where the generic numbering is not in use, TIF_RSEQ is defined as an alias for TIF_NOTIFY_RESUME, which is consumed inside the loop, so no exclusion is needed and none is made.

Note also the asymmetry in the interrupt handling at the top and bottom of the loop body. local_irq_enable_exit_to_user() takes ti_work as an argument while local_irq_disable_exit_to_user() takes none; the generic definitions ignore the argument and simply call local_irq_enable() and local_irq_disable(). The parameter exists so that an architecture can decide, from the pending work, exactly which interrupt classes to unmask — on a machine with a priority-masking interrupt controller, re-enabling everything may not be the right answer. Neither x86_64 nor ARM64 currently overrides it.

In Linux 2.6, this logic was a sequence of conditional jumps in assembly:

  • work_pending tested TIF_NEED_RESCHED, falling through to work_resched if it was set and jumping to work_notifysig if it was not
  • work_resched called schedule(), disabled interrupts, and jumped back to resume_userspace to re-test everything
  • work_notifysig called save_v86_state() if the interrupted program was in virtual-8086 mode, then do_notify_resume() for signals and single-stepping, and jumped to restore_all

Comparing the two carefully reveals a behavioural change, not just a stylistic one. The 2.6 code looped only for rescheduling: work_resched jumped back to resume_userspace and re-examined the flags, but work_notifysig jumped straight to restore_all. Signal delivery therefore happened exactly once per return, and any work that became pending while a signal handler frame was being built would not be noticed until the next entry into the kernel. The 7.0.12 loop re-reads the flags after every category of work, so a reschedule raised during signal delivery is acted on before the return completes. The modern C version is not merely cleaner, more extensible, and shared across all architectures — it also closes a window the assembly version left open.

Kernel-Mode Return and Preemption

If the interrupted context was kernel mode and interrupts were enabled, the kernel may preempt the current task. The raw_irqentry_exit_cond_resched() function checks two conditions:

/* kernel/entry/common.c */
void raw_irqentry_exit_cond_resched(void)
{
    if (!preempt_count()) {
        /* Sanity check RCU and thread stack */
        rcu_irq_exit_check_preempt();
        if (IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_DEBUG_ENTRY))
            WARN_ON_ONCE(!on_thread_stack());
        if (need_resched() && arch_irqentry_exit_need_resched())
            preempt_schedule_irq();
    }
}

There are in fact three conditions, not two, and one debug assertion:

  1. preempt_count() == 0: No spinlocks held, no softirq processing in progress, no hardirq or NMI nesting, and preemption not explicitly disabled. Recall from the previous section that all of these live in different bitfields of the same word, so this single comparison against zero tests them all at once — which is precisely why they were packed together.
  2. need_resched(): TIF_NEED_RESCHED is set, meaning the scheduler has determined that another task should run. Note that unlike the user-mode loop, the kernel-preemption path checks only the eager flag; a TIF_NEED_RESCHED_LAZY request is by definition allowed to wait for a more convenient moment, and returning into kernel code is not one.
  3. arch_irqentry_exit_need_resched(): an architecture veto, defaulting to true. x86_64 accepts the default. ARM64 overrides it, and both of its refusal cases are instructive; they are discussed below.

The WARN_ON_ONCE(!on_thread_stack()), compiled in only under CONFIG_DEBUG_ENTRY, guards against a subtle disaster: preemption calls schedule(), and schedule() switches stacks. If the CPU were still on the per-CPU IRQ stack at this point, the outgoing task's context would be saved onto a stack that the next interrupt will happily reuse. As discussed in the previous section, ARM64 unwinds to the task stack before irq_exit_rcu() precisely so that this holds, and x86_64's HAVE_IRQ_EXIT_ON_IRQ_STACK arrangement keeps the check honest by ensuring the exit path runs after the IRQ-stack epilogue. Its companion, rcu_irq_exit_check_preempt(), makes the parallel assertion for RCU: that the nesting counters have unwound far enough that a context switch is legal.

ARM64's veto, in arch/arm64/include/asm/entry-common.h, refuses preemption in two situations. The first is when any DAIF bit is still set on entry to the check. Normal IRQ handling clears DAIF.DA at the start, and when GIC priority masking is in use the irqchip driver clears DAIF.IF as well; so if anything remains set, the kernel must be unwinding a pseudo-NMI, and preempting out of an NMI is not permissible. The second is !system_capabilities_finalized(), and the comment explains it well: preempting a task from an IRQ leaves copies of PSTATE on its stack, and the CPU-feature enable calls that run during boot modify PSTATE. Resuming such a task later would restore the stale copy and silently undo the feature enablement. ARM64 therefore forbids preemption from interrupt context until the CPU capability set is frozen.

If all three conditions hold, preempt_schedule_irq() is called. Its body is worth reading, because its interrupt handling is the opposite of what the name suggests:

/* kernel/sched/core.c */
asmlinkage __visible void __sched preempt_schedule_irq(void)
{
    enum ctx_state prev_state;

    /* Catch callers which need to be fixed */
    BUG_ON(preempt_count() || !irqs_disabled());

    prev_state = exception_enter();

    do {
        preempt_disable();
        local_irq_enable();
        __schedule(SM_PREEMPT);
        local_irq_disable();
        sched_preempt_enable_no_resched();
    } while (need_resched());

    exception_exit(prev_state);
}

The function is entered with interrupts already disabled — the opening BUG_ON() makes that a hard requirement rather than a convention — and it enables them around the call to __schedule(), restoring the disabled state afterwards so that the caller's final return sequence runs with a stable interrupt state. Enabling interrupts here is deliberate: a context switch can take an appreciable amount of time, and the machine should not be deaf for its duration.

Preemption is disabled across the switch by preempt_disable() and re-enabled afterwards by sched_preempt_enable_no_resched(), the variant that decrements the counter without re-checking for a pending reschedule. The check is not skipped, merely relocated: it is the while (need_resched()) condition, which loops if another reschedule request arrived while the previous one was being serviced.

Finally, note the argument SM_PREEMPT. The scheduler distinguishes four entry modes — SM_IDLE, SM_NONE, SM_PREEMPT, and SM_RTLOCK_WAIT — and uses them to decide, among other things, whether the outgoing task is being involuntarily displaced (and so must stay runnable) or is voluntarily blocking (and so may be dequeued). This parameter is the modern replacement for Linux 2.6's PREEMPT_ACTIVE bit, which encoded the same one-bit fact by temporarily setting bit 28 of preempt_count. The information now travels as a function argument instead of as hidden global state.

In Linux 2.6, this logic was in assembly at the resume_kernel / need_resched labels. The key differences in 7.0.12:

  • The PREEMPT_ACTIVE flag from 2.6 has been removed. As described above, the fact it conveyed now travels as the SM_PREEMPT argument to __schedule(), which frees bit 28 of preempt_count and removes a class of bug in which the flag could be observed by code that had no business seeing it.
  • The Big Kernel Lock (BKL) manipulation from 2.6 is gone. In 2.6, preempt_schedule_irq() temporarily set the BKL counter to −1 and restored it afterwards; the BKL was removed from the kernel entirely in 2.6.39, and with it that whole dance.
  • CONFIG_PREEMPT_DYNAMIC allows the preemption model — none, voluntary, full, or lazy — to be selected with the preempt= boot parameter, or changed at run time through /sys/kernel/debug/sched/preempt, rather than being fixed at compile time. irqentry_exit_cond_resched is one of the handful of functions this switch rewires, and the two architectures do it by different means: x86 selects HAVE_PREEMPT_DYNAMIC_CALL and uses a static call, whose indirect target is patched to either raw_irqentry_exit_cond_resched() or a no-op; ARM64 selects HAVE_PREEMPT_DYNAMIC_KEY and instead compiles in dynamic_irqentry_exit_cond_resched(), which tests a static key — a jump-label branch patched between a fallthrough and a jump. Either way the cost when the model is none is nil rather than a runtime test, which is what makes the feature acceptable: a distribution can ship one kernel binary and let the administrator choose the model, without the non-preemptible configuration paying for the flexibility.

If the interrupted context had interrupts disabled (the third branch of irqentry_exit()), preemption is never attempted. This is the same rule as in 2.6: preempting a kernel path that was running with interrupts disabled could corrupt data structures protected by the interrupt-disabled critical section.

The Final Assembly Return

After the generic exit helpers finish, the architecture-specific assembly restores registers and executes the final return instruction. Before that, though, x86 has one more piece of C to run.

Where x86_64 Does Its Per-Architecture Work

The previous section noted that x86_64 leaves arch_exit_to_user_mode_work() — the in-loop hook — empty. It uses two other hooks instead, both in arch/x86/include/asm/entry-common.h, and the difference in when they run matters:

  • arch_exit_to_user_mode_prepare() runs once, after the loop has drained, from __exit_to_user_mode_prepare(). It is passed the final ti_work value and, if anything is set, calls arch_exit_work(), which fires user-return notifiers (TIF_USER_RETURN_NOTIFY, used by KVM to restore MSRs it borrowed), reprograms the TSS I/O permission bitmap (TIF_IO_BITMAP), and reloads FPU registers (TIF_NEED_FPU_LOAD). It then updates the FRED RSP0 pointer, clears TS_COMPAT for 32-bit tasks, picks a fresh random kernel-stack offset with choose_random_kstack_offset(rdtsc()), and — if the CPU requires it — issues an indirect branch prediction barrier. Running once rather than per iteration is the point: reloading the FPU inside the loop would be wasted work if the next iteration called schedule() and swapped it out again.
  • arch_exit_to_user_mode() runs from exit_to_user_mode(), later still, after lockdep and RCU have been told the kernel is leaving. On x86_64 it is a single call to amd_clear_divider(), which executes a dummy division to flush the AMD divider unit and prevent a previous kernel division's result from being speculatively leaked to user space.

Table 4-88. Architecture exit hooks and where each runs

HookInvoked fromHow oftenx86_64ARM64
arch_exit_to_user_mode_work()__exit_to_user_mode_loop()Once per loop iterationNot defined (empty stub)MTE async tag-check fault → SIGSEGV/SEGV_MTEAERR; fpsimd_restore_current_state()
arch_exit_to_user_mode_prepare()__exit_to_user_mode_prepare()Once, after the loopUser-return notifiers, I/O bitmap, FPU reload, FRED RSP0, kstack randomization, conditional IBPBNot defined (empty stub)
arch_exit_to_user_mode()exit_to_user_mode()Once, last C code before asmamd_clear_divider()Not defined (empty stub)
arch_irqentry_exit_need_resched()raw_irqentry_exit_cond_resched()Once per kernel-mode returnNot defined (defaults to true)Vetoes preemption from pseudo-NMI context and before CPU capabilities are finalized
ARCH_EXIT_TO_USER_MODE_WORKFolded into EXIT_TO_USER_MODE_WORKCompile-time mask0_TIF_MTE_ASYNC_FAULT | _TIF_FOREIGN_FPSTATE

The x86_64 Return Sequence

The return path must carefully manage GSBASE state. Kernel mode and user mode use different GS base addresses (the kernel uses it to reach per-CPU data, user mode for TLS), and the swapgs instruction exchanges the two. The full sequence in common_interrupt_return is more subtle than a single instruction, however, and the order is forced by dependencies that are worth spelling out:

/* arch/x86/entry/entry_64.S — abridged */
SYM_INNER_LABEL(swapgs_restore_regs_and_return_to_usermode, SYM_L_GLOBAL)
    IBRS_EXIT                       /* leave kernel IBRS mode */
    ALTERNATIVE "", "jmp .Lpti_restore_regs_and_return_to_usermode", X86_FEATURE_PTI
    STACKLEAK_ERASE
    POP_REGS
    add     $8, %rsp                /* discard orig_ax */
.Lswapgs_and_iret:
    swapgs                          /* kernel GSBASE -> user GSBASE */
    CLEAR_CPU_BUFFERS               /* VERW: MDS/TAA/RFDS buffer clear */
    testb   $3, 8(%rsp)             /* assert the frame really targets ring 3 */
    jnz     .Lnative_iret
    ud2
.Lnative_iret:
    iretq                           /* restore RIP, CS, RFLAGS, RSP, SS */

.Lpti_restore_regs_and_return_to_usermode:
    POP_REGS pop_rdi=0
    movq    %rsp, %rdi              /* switch to the per-CPU trampoline stack */
    movq    PER_CPU_VAR(cpu_tss_rw + TSS_sp0), %rsp
    /* copy SS, RSP, EFLAGS, CS, RIP and user RDI onto the trampoline stack */
    STACKLEAK_ERASE_NOCLOBBER
    SWITCH_TO_USER_CR3 scratch_reg=%rdi scratch_reg2=%rax
    popq    %rdi
    jmp     .Lswapgs_and_iret

Reading the two paths together yields the real ordering: speculation state first, then the KPTI page-table switch, then swapgs, then the buffer clear, and only then iretq. Each step is where it is for a reason.

IBRS_EXIT comes first because it writes an MSR, and MSR writes are best done while the kernel mapping and kernel GSBASE are both still available. When KPTI is active the kernel then cannot simply switch CR3 in place: the moment the user page tables are installed, the kernel stack it is standing on is unmapped. It therefore migrates to the per-CPU trampoline stack — a small region deliberately mapped in both page tables — copies the five-word IRET frame there, and only then calls SWITCH_TO_USER_CR3. That macro reads per-CPU data, which requires kernel GSBASE, so swapgs must come after it, not before as one might guess from the name of the entry label. CLEAR_CPU_BUFFERS — the VERW instruction that flushes the microarchitectural buffers exploited by MDS, TAA, and RFDS — is placed as late as possible, immediately before iretq, so that no kernel data touched after it can be left behind.

The ud2 after the ring check is not dead code but a deliberate trap: if the IRET frame does not describe user mode at a label named "return to usermode", something has corrupted the stack, and crashing immediately is far safer than executing iretq into an unknown privilege level.

Returning to kernel mode takes a much shorter route, restore_regs_and_return_to_kernel: no swapgs, no CR3 switch, no buffer clear — none of them are needed when the destination is the same privilege level and address space — just POP_REGS and a jump into the same iretq. A comment there records a second duty of that instruction: iretq is architecturally serializing, which is what allows x86 to implement ARCH_HAS_MEMBARRIER_SYNC_CORE by returning from an IPI handler. Finally, iretq itself can fault when returning to user mode — a bad SS, a non-canonical RIP, an LDT stack segment needing the ESPFIX64 workaround — which is why the instruction carries its own label, native_irq_return_iret, that the #DF and fixup_bad_iret handlers test for.

The ARM64 Return Sequence

ARM64 reaches its assembly epilogue through one of two short code blocks, ret_to_kernel or ret_to_user, each of which invokes the kernel_exit macro with an exception-level argument that switches large parts of the macro on and off. ret_to_user does two things first: it re-reads the thread flags and calls enable_step_tsk, which sets MDSCR_EL1.SS if TIF_SINGLESTEP is set — this is where a debugger's single-step request is finally reinstated — and, under CONFIG_KSTACK_ERASE, it erases the unused portion of the task stack.

/* arch/arm64/kernel/entry.S — kernel_exit macro, abridged */
    .macro  kernel_exit, el
    .if     \el != 0
    disable_daif                        /* returning to EL1: mask everything */
    .endif

    /* Restore the GIC priority mask first (pseudo-NMI support) */
    ldr     w20, [sp, #S_PMR]
    msr_s   SYS_ICC_PMR_EL1, w20
    dsb     sy                          /* redistributor must observe it */

    ldp     x21, x22, [sp, #S_PC]       /* one load: ELR and SPSR are adjacent */

    .if     \el == 0
    ldr     x23, [sp, #S_SP]            /* restore user SP_EL0 */
    msr     sp_el0, x23
    scs_save tsk                        /* save the shadow call stack pointer */
    ldr     x0, [tsk, THREAD_SCTLR_USER]
    clear_mte_async_tcf x0              /* drop faults raised inside uaccess */
    __ptrauth_keys_install_user tsk, x0, x1, x2   /* swap PAC keys to the user's */
    mte_set_user_gcr tsk, x0, x1        /* restore the user tag-generation mask */
    apply_ssbd 0, x0, x1                /* drop the kernel-only SSB workaround */
    .endif

    msr     elr_el1, x21                /* only now are ELR/SPSR written */
    msr     spsr_el1, x22
    ldp     x0, x1, [sp, #16 * 0]       /* ... restore x0-x29 ... */
    /* If KPTI is active, branch through the tramp_exit trampoline instead */
    ldr     lr, [sp, #S_LR]
    add     sp, sp, #PT_REGS_SIZE
    eret                                /* return to EL0 or EL1 */
    sb                                  /* speculation barrier after ERET */
    .endm

The ordering here is the mirror image of the entry macro, and two placements are deliberate. The ICC_PMR_EL1 restore comes first, before anything else is unwound, because on a kernel using GIC priority masking that register is the interrupt mask; it must be put back before the CPU can be allowed to take interrupts again, and the dsb sy ensures the GIC redistributor has observed the change (elided by an alternative on parts that implement relaxed PMR synchronization). Conversely msr elr_el1 and msr spsr_el1 come last, long after the values were loaded, because any exception taken in between would overwrite them. Loading early and writing late narrows that window to a handful of instructions.

The block guarded by \el == 0 is the per-task security state that only matters when leaving the kernel entirely: the shadow call stack pointer is saved, MTE asynchronous tag-check faults raised inside uaccess routines are discarded, the pointer-authentication instruction key is swapped from the kernel's to the user's (after which the comment warns that no kernel C function may be called), and the tag-generation mask is restored. The last of these, apply_ssbd 0, is the counterpart of the apply_ssbd 1 that kernel_entry issued on the way in: the Spectre-v4 firmware workaround is turned on for the duration of kernel execution and turned back off for user space — unless the task has TIF_SSBD set, in which case the macro skips the call and the mitigation stays active in user mode too. Returning to EL1 skips all of it, which is a large part of why kernel-to-kernel returns are so much cheaper.

ARM64 has a KPTI arrangement of its own, CONFIG_UNMAP_KERNEL_AT_EL0, and it too must solve the problem of unmapping the kernel while still executing kernel instructions. Its answer differs from x86's: instead of a trampoline stack, ARM64 branches to a trampoline page. kernel_exit installs the per-CPU vector table in VBAR_EL1, stashes x29 in FAR_EL1 as scratch space, and branches to tramp_exit, which unmaps the kernel via TTBR1, restores x29, and executes the eret from the trampoline page — the only kernel text still mapped at that instant.

The eret instruction atomically restores the program counter from ELR_EL1 and the processor state from SPSR_EL1, switching back to the appropriate exception level. It is followed by sb, a speculation barrier: without it, some implementations may speculatively execute instructions past the eret at the old, higher privilege level. x86's iretq needs no such companion because it is architecturally serializing; ARM64's eret is not, and the barrier makes up the difference.

TIF Flags Checked on Exit

The thread-information flags are the mechanism by which various kernel subsystems request work to be done before returning to user mode. They live in thread_info.flags, are set with ordinary atomic bit operations from anywhere in the kernel — including from another CPU — and are read exactly once per return, which is what makes the fast path so cheap.

Not every TIF bit is a return-path flag, and the distinction trips people up. Some are consumed at context switch time instead: on x86 the mask _TIF_WORK_CTXSW gathers TIF_NOCPUID, TIF_NOTSC, TIF_BLOCKSTEP, TIF_SSBD, TIF_SPEC_FORCE_UPDATE and (on SMP) TIF_SPEC_IB, and __switch_to_xtra() acts on them when tasks are swapped. The speculation-control flags belong to that group, not to this one: TIF_SSBD and TIF_SPEC_IB describe a property of the task that must be established before it runs at all, so they are applied at switch time and on mm changes in arch/x86/mm/tlb.c — never on the return path. Others, such as TIF_SYSCALL_TRACE and TIF_SECCOMP, are tested only on the system-call path covered in Chapter 10. Table 4-89 lists the flags that genuinely participate in returning from an interrupt or exception, and names the code that consumes each.

Table 4-89. Thread-information flags consumed on the interrupt/exception return path

FlagWhen it mattersConsumed byPresent in 2.6?
TIF_NEED_RESCHEDImmediate reschedule required__exit_to_user_mode_loop(); also raw_irqentry_exit_cond_resched() for kernel-mode returnsYes
TIF_NEED_RESCHED_LAZYLazy reschedule request; honoured on the way to user mode but not for kernel preemption__exit_to_user_mode_loop() onlyNo — new in 6.13
TIF_UPROBEUprobes single-step resume work pendinguprobe_notify_resume()No
TIF_PATCH_PENDINGTask must converge to the new livepatch stateklp_update_patch_state()No
TIF_SIGPENDINGTraditional signal pending for the taskarch_do_signal_or_restart()Yes
TIF_NOTIFY_SIGNALSignal-like notification with no actual signal (task_work, io_uring)arch_do_signal_or_restart()No
TIF_NOTIFY_RESUMEGeneric callback before returning to user; the delivery vehicle for task_work_add()resume_user_mode_work()Yes (unused on 80x86 in 2.6)
TIF_RSEQRestartable-sequence fixup or slice-extension bookkeepingThe outer for (;;) in exit_to_user_mode_loop() — deliberately excluded from the inner loop conditionNo
TIF_USER_RETURN_NOTIFYA subsystem (in practice KVM) registered a callback to restore MSRs it borrowedarch_exit_work(), x86_64 onlyNo
TIF_IO_BITMAPThe task's I/O permission bitmap must be reinstalled in the TSStss_update_io_bitmap(), x86_64 onlyNo (handled differently in 2.6)
TIF_NEED_FPU_LOADThe CPU's FPU registers do not hold this task's stateswitch_fpu_return(), x86_64 onlyNo
TIF_SINGLESTEPRe-enable single-step for user modeArchitecture assembly: enable_step_tsk in ret_to_user on ARM64; the signal path on x86Yes
TIF_MTE_ASYNC_FAULTAn asynchronous MTE tag-check fault must be reportedarch_exit_to_user_mode_work(), ARM64 onlyNo
TIF_FOREIGN_FPSTATEThe CPU's FPSIMD/SVE registers belong to another taskfpsimd_restore_current_state(), ARM64 onlyNo

The table reveals a broader trend. The number of return-path flags has grown because modern kernels have more subsystems that need to hook into the moment of leaving the kernel — livepatching, uprobes, restartable sequences, io_uring, hardware memory tagging — and because that moment is the only point at which a task is guaranteed to be in a known, quiescent state with no locks held. The generic __exit_to_user_mode_loop() provides a clean, extensible framework for adding such hooks without touching architecture assembly, which is exactly the property the 2.6 design lacked: there, every new user-return hook meant editing entry.S for every architecture.

The last five rows show the other half of the picture. Architectures do not add their flags to the generic loop; they publish a mask, ARCH_EXIT_TO_USER_MODE_WORK, which is folded into EXIT_TO_USER_MODE_WORK at compile time, and supply one of the hooks in Table 4-88 to act on it. ARM64 defines that mask as _TIF_MTE_ASYNC_FAULT | _TIF_FOREIGN_FPSTATE; x86_64 leaves it at zero and handles its flags after the loop instead. The generic code never needs to know what any of these bits mean.

RSEQ Slice Extension

Restartable sequences are a modern addition absent from Linux 2.6. They allow user-space code to perform short per-CPU atomic operations without using heavy synchronization primitives: the code is written so that it can be abandoned and retried from the top, and the kernel promises to redirect the instruction pointer to an abort label if the task is migrated or preempted mid-sequence. The scheme is cheap when it works and expensive when it does not, since an abort throws away all the work done so far.

Slice extension attacks the "does not" case directly. If a task is inside such a critical section and a reschedule request arrives, preempting it now is the worst possible timing: the section restarts, and whatever the scheduler gained is partly spent on redone work. rseq_grant_slice_extension(), called from the reschedule arm of the exit loop, can instead grant the task a few extra microseconds to reach the end of its section, on the understanding that it will yield promptly afterwards. This is a cooperative bargain between kernel and user space, and the kernel takes care to make it one it cannot lose.

The protocol runs through a field in the struct rseq that user space shares with the kernel. User space sets slice_ctrl.request before entering a critical section; when the kernel grants an extension it clears request and sets slice_ctrl.granted, which user space can read to know it is on borrowed time. The grant is hedged by every restriction the implementation could reasonably impose:

  • Interrupts only. The grant is conditioned on curr->rseq.event.user_irq, which irqentry_enter_from_user_mode() sets via rseq_note_user_irq_entry(). A reschedule discovered on the way out of a system call gets no extension — the task was in the kernel by its own choice, so it was not in a critical section.
  • Once only. If state.granted is already set, the request is not renewed but revoked: the kernel zeroes the user control word and returns false, so schedule() runs immediately. A task cannot chain extensions to monopolize a CPU.
  • Nothing else pending. The work_pending argument is ti_work & TIF_SLICE_EXT_DENY, where TIF_SLICE_EXT_DENY is EXIT_TO_USER_MODE_WORK minus the reschedule bits. Any signal, uprobe, livepatch, or notify-resume work denies the grant, because those must be delivered promptly and none of them is what the extension is for. On CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT the set of extendable reschedules narrows further to _TIF_NEED_RESCHED_LAZY alone, since on an RT kernel an eager reschedule is a latency guarantee and may not be deferred.
  • Bounded. The extension lasts rseq_slice_ext_nsecs, clamped to between 5 and 50 microseconds and defaulting to the minimum. rseq_exit_to_user_mode_restart() arms a per-CPU hrtimer for the deadline as the last thing it does, so an unresponsive task is preempted anyway. Because arming the timer makes that function return true, the outer for (;;) takes one more turn, re-reading the flags — a final chance to notice work that arrived while the grant was being set up.
  • Opt-in and switchable. The whole feature sits behind the rseq_slice_extension_key static key and requires CONFIG_RSEQ_SLICE_EXTENSION, RSEQ ABI version 2 registration, and a per-task prctl(PR_RSEQ_SLICE_EXTENSION_SET, PR_RSEQ_SLICE_EXT_ENABLE).

Of the two architectures this book follows, slice extension is available only on x86_64. CONFIG_RSEQ_SLICE_EXTENSION depends on GENERIC_ENTRY && HAVE_GENERIC_TIF_BITS (plus HIGH_RES_TIMERS), and ARM64 selects neither: it takes the generic interrupt entry code but not the generic system-call path, and it keeps its own thread-flag numbering. This is the same partial conversion noted at the start of the section, and it is a good illustration of why that conversion matters — an architecture that stops halfway does not merely keep some old code, it becomes ineligible for features built on the new.

Repayment is enforced on the way back in. Any system call issued while a grant is outstanding ends it: rseq_syscall_enter_work() cancels the timer, sets the reschedule flag, and clears the grant in both the kernel and the user control word. The polite way to settle up is the dedicated rseq_slice_yield(2) system call, which does exactly that and nothing else; the distinction between the two is recorded in the s_yielded and s_aborted statistics. Accepting any syscall as an end-of-grant signal is deliberate, and the comment explains why: in layered software the library that requests an extension often cannot control what the code inside the critical section does. If user space is caught having tampered with the control word, the kernel responds with SIGSEGV.

Separately from the grant machinery, the classic RSEQ fixup — the abort-label redirection — is applied by the outer loop. exit_to_user_mode_loop() wraps __exit_to_user_mode_loop() in a for (;;) that calls rseq_exit_to_user_mode_restart(), which checks whether a valid critical section is registered and whether the saved instruction pointer lies inside it; if so it rewrites the return address to the abort IP, and if the registration itself is malformed it terminates the task. Because rewriting the return address is a user-memory access that can fault and therefore sleep, it may itself make new work pending — which is precisely why it sits in an outer loop that re-reads the flags rather than inside the inner one. When the fixup cannot be completed inline it does not retry there: it sets TIF_NOTIFY_RESUME and returns true, deferring the work to the slow path on the next iteration. On ARM64, where rseq_exit_to_user_mode_restart() is a stub returning false, the outer loop always runs exactly once and RSEQ fixups reach user space through TIF_NOTIFY_RESUME alone.

/* kernel/entry/common.c */
__always_inline unsigned long exit_to_user_mode_loop(struct pt_regs *regs,
                                                     unsigned long ti_work)
{
    for (;;) {
        ti_work = __exit_to_user_mode_loop(regs, ti_work);
        if (likely(!rseq_exit_to_user_mode_restart(regs, ti_work)))
            return ti_work;
        ti_work = read_thread_flags();
    }
}

What Changed Since Linux 2.6

Compared with Linux 2.6, the concepts of this chapter are almost entirely intact. Interrupts still steal a CPU from whatever was running, handlers still must be short, and long work is still deferred. What changed is the engineering around those concepts. Three restructurings account for most of the difference, and each was driven by a defect class that the 2.6 design made easy to introduce.

Entry Code: From Assembly Stubs to IDTENTRY

In Linux 2.6 each x86 exception and interrupt began life in hand-written assembly. The stubs pushed an error code (or a dummy zero to keep the frame uniform), saved registers, and jumped to a C handler, with the assembly and C sides agreeing only by convention. Every new hardening feature — KPTI page-table switching, RCU state tracking, lockdep bookkeeping, context tracking for NO_HZ_FULL — meant editing dozens of nearly identical stubs, and an omission in any one of them produced a bug that appeared only under a specific configuration.

Linux 7.0.12 replaces that with the DEFINE_IDTENTRY* macro family in arch/x86/include/asm/idtentry.h. A single macro generates the noinstr wrapper, and the wrapper always brackets the real handler with irqentry_enter() and irqentry_exit():

/* arch/x86/include/asm/idtentry.h:53 */
#define DEFINE_IDTENTRY(func)                                           \
static __always_inline void __##func(struct pt_regs *regs);             \
                                                                        \
__visible noinstr void func(struct pt_regs *regs)                       \
{                                                                       \
        irqentry_state_t state = irqentry_enter(regs);                  \
                                                                        \
        instrumentation_begin();                                        \
        __##func (regs);                                                \
        instrumentation_end();                                          \
        irqentry_exit(regs, state);                                     \
}                                                                       \
                                                                        \
static __always_inline void __##func(struct pt_regs *regs)

The macro deliberately ends with a dangling function header rather than a complete definition, so a handler is written by appending a body: DEFINE_IDTENTRY(exc_divide_error) { ... } expands to the noinstr wrapper plus the opening line of __exc_divide_error(), and the author's braces close it. The effect is that a handler looks like an ordinary function while the entry and exit bookkeeping is generated around it and cannot be forgotten. Note also that instrumentation_begin() and instrumentation_end() are real calls, not commentary: they mark the exact window in which tracing, KASAN and other instrumentation are permitted.

The payoff is that the bookkeeping now lives in exactly two functions, irqentry_enter() and irqentry_exit(), defined in kernel/entry/common.c and declared in include/linux/irq-entry-common.h. Adding a new entry-time obligation means editing those two functions rather than auditing every stub.

It is worth being precise about how far that sharing extends, because the chapter has drawn the distinction repeatedly. arch/Kconfig offers two options: GENERIC_IRQ_ENTRY covers interrupt and exception entry and exit, while GENERIC_ENTRY adds the syscall path and selects the former. x86 selects GENERIC_ENTRY; ARM64 selects only GENERIC_IRQ_ENTRY. So ARM64 does share irqentry_enter() and irqentry_exit() — enter_from_kernel_mode() in arch/arm64/kernel/entry-common.c calls straight into them — but it keeps its own syscall entry and its own user-mode return path. The unification described here is real, and it is not yet complete.

The noinstr annotation is the other half of the change: it marks code that runs before RCU is watching, where a tracepoint or a KASAN callback would itself be a bug, and the compiler and objtool enforce that boundary.

From Per-Architecture IRQ Code to genirq

Linux 2.6 had a global, statically sized IRQ table and a great deal of duplicated per-architecture masking and acknowledgment logic. An IRQ number was essentially a hardware line number, which worked while machines had one interrupt controller and a couple of dozen lines. It did not survive systems with cascaded controllers, PCIe MSI/MSI-X vectors allocated by the thousand, and hierarchical ARM GIC topologies where the same number can mean different things at different levels.

The generic IRQ layer separates three concerns that 2.6 conflated:

  • struct irq_desc holds the per-line software state — the handler list, statistics, and status flags. With CONFIG_SPARSE_IRQ these are allocated on demand from a maple tree rather than sized at compile time.
  • struct irq_chip holds the controller operations (irq_mask, irq_unmask, irq_eoi, irq_set_affinity). A driver never calls controller code directly.
  • struct irq_domain translates a hardware interrupt specifier into a Linux IRQ number. Domains nest, which is what makes a GICv3 ITS → GIC → CPU chain expressible at all.

On top of these sit the flow handlers — handle_level_irq(), handle_edge_irq(), handle_fasteoi_irq(), handle_percpu_irq() — which encode the masking and acknowledgment sequence a given electrical signalling type requires. In 2.6 that sequence was open-coded per architecture; getting it wrong on a level-triggered line produced an interrupt storm. Today choosing the flow handler is the controller driver's single decision, and it is made once when the line is mapped.

Bottom Halves Became a Hierarchy

Older kernels deferred interrupt work through bottom halves, softirqs, and tasklets. The old fixed-vector bottom halves are long gone, and what replaced them is not one mechanism but a graded set of choices, each trading latency for context:

  • Softirqs remain the lowest-latency deferral, and the mechanism is unchanged in outline: a small, fixed set of statically numbered vectors, closed to drivers, running in atomic context and forbidden to sleep. The membership has turned over, though. Linux 2.6.11 defined six vectors — HI, TIMER, NET_TX, NET_RX, SCSI, TASKLET. Linux 7.0.12 defines ten: SCSI_SOFTIRQ is gone, absorbed by the block layer, and BLOCK, IRQ_POLL, SCHED, HRTIMER and RCU have been added. That SCSI could be retired at all is the point — a softirq vector is a scarce, kernel-wide resource, and one subsystem no longer gets to own one simply because it is latency-sensitive.
  • Tasklets are still built on TASKLET_SOFTIRQ, but are now discouraged for new code. Their serialization guarantee — the same tasklet never runs concurrently on two CPUs — is precisely what prevents them from scaling.
  • Threaded IRQs did not exist in 2.6. request_threaded_irq() splits a driver into a tiny hardirq handler that quiets the device and returns IRQ_WAKE_THREAD, plus a handler running in a dedicated kernel thread where sleeping is legal. Threading can also be imposed on drivers that never asked for it: under CONFIG_PREEMPT_RT, force_irqthreads() is unconditionally true, and on an ordinary kernel the threadirqs boot parameter flips a static key to the same effect. The exemption is IRQF_NO_THREAD, and the instructive case is IRQF_TIMER, which includes it: the timer interrupt cannot be deferred into a thread whose scheduling depends on the timer interrupt. Forced threading is the reason a fully preemptible kernel is now feasible — it converts almost every driver's interrupt work into something the scheduler can preempt.
  • Workqueues were rebuilt as concurrency-managed workqueues. Instead of one thread per workqueue, a shared pool of worker threads is sized dynamically, so a system with hundreds of workqueues no longer pays for hundreds of mostly idle threads. WQ_UNBOUND lets work migrate for throughput, and the newer WQ_BH flag allows a workqueue to execute in softirq context, giving tasklet-like latency through the workqueue API.

The practical guidance follows directly from the hierarchy: use a threaded IRQ unless the latency budget forbids it, use a workqueue for anything that may sleep or block, and reach for a softirq only in the few subsystems that already own one.

Summary of Changes

Table 4-90. Interrupt and exception handling: Linux 2.6 vs 7.0.12

AspectLinux 2.6.11Linux 7.0.12

Controller abstractionPer-architecture masking and ACK codestruct irq_chip operations
Hardware → IRQ translationImplicit; IRQ number was the line numberstruct irq_domain, hierarchical and nestable
Signalling semanticsOpen-coded per architectureFlow handlers (handle_level_irq(), handle_fasteoi_irq(), …)
Interrupt routing8259A PIC and I/O APICxAPIC/x2APIC, MSI/MSI-X, ARM GICv3 and ITS
Threaded handlersNot availablerequest_threaded_irq(); the default under PREEMPT_RT
Workqueue modelOne kernel thread per workqueue per CPUConcurrency-managed shared worker pools, WQ_UNBOUND, WQ_BH
TaskletsThe standard driver deferral mechanismStill present, but discouraged in favour of threaded IRQs and workqueues
Softirq types6 (HI, TIMER, NET_TX, NET_RX, SCSI, TASKLET)10 — drops SCSI, adds BLOCK, IRQ_POLL, SCHED, HRTIMER, RCU
Stack layoutShared kernel stack, limited IST usePer-CPU entry area with dedicated IST stacks for #DF, NMI, #DB, #MC and #VC
Page-table isolationNot applicableKPTI swaps CR3 on entry and exit; the entry area is mapped in both PGDs
RCU interactionAd-hocExplicit irqentry_enter() / irq_enter_rcu() state tracking
Return-to-user workA handful of TIF flags__exit_to_user_mode_loop() over signals, resched, uprobes, rseq, and more

Summary

Interrupts and exceptions remain the kernel's most timing-sensitive control paths. They are where hardware urgency meets software discipline, and where a single missing step — an unacknowledged controller, a stale RCU state, an unswapped page table — produces a failure that is silent, intermittent, and extremely hard to reproduce.

Linux 7.0.12 manages that risk by layering responsibilities so that each layer has one job. Hardware vectors and controllers choose the entry point; architecture stubs build a stable pt_regs frame; generic entry helpers take care of RCU, preemption, and context tracking; the generic IRQ layer dispatches according to the electrical semantics of the line; and a graded set of deferred-work mechanisms absorbs everything that does not belong in the hardirq top half. The twelve technical sections of this chapter follow that stack from the outside in, and then back out again along the return path.

  • Interrupts steal a CPU, they do not borrow one. A handler runs at the expense of whatever task was executing, in that task's kernel stack and accounting context, until the work is explicitly handed off to an IRQ thread, a softirq, or a workqueue. This is why "keep the handler short" is a correctness requirement and not merely advice.
  • Synchronous and asynchronous events share one mechanism but not one contract. Exceptions are produced by the instruction stream and are therefore reproducible and attributable; interrupts arrive from outside and are neither. The kernel routes both through the same descriptor table, but only exceptions may safely conclude that the current task caused the event.
  • Interrupt controllers are now a fabric, not a chip. The 8259A model of a single central router is gone. x86_64 combines per-core local APICs with I/O APICs and device-initiated MSI/MSI-X writes; ARM64 splits the GICv3 into a Distributor, per-CPU Redistributors, and a system-register CPU interface, with an ITS translating PCIe MSIs into LPIs.
  • The IDT is built in phases because the kernel needs handlers before it has stacks. Linux populates early_idts[], def_idts[], and apic_idts[] at different points in boot, because some exceptions must be catchable before the per-CPU TSS and the IST stacks exist. Task gates, which 32-bit Linux used for double faults, are unavailable in long mode and were replaced by the IST mechanism.
  • Hardware builds only part of the frame. The processor pushes a fixed architectural frame and jumps; the entry stub must complete it before the result is a valid struct pt_regs. Everything the kernel later does with the interrupted context — signal delivery, backtraces, ptrace, page-fault fixups — depends on that frame being complete and on the correct stack.
  • Exception handling is mostly about deciding whose fault it is. The same trap means "deliver a signal" when it comes from user mode and "consult the exception table, then panic" when it comes from kernel mode. The exception tables in arch/x86/mm/extable.c are what allow copy_from_user() to fault safely rather than oops.
  • The generic IRQ layer separates three things that used to be one. irq_desc holds per-line software state, irq_chip holds controller operations, and irq_domain translates hardware specifiers into Linux IRQ numbers. Flow handlers such as handle_level_irq() and handle_fasteoi_irq() encode the masking and acknowledgment sequence a given signalling type demands — the part that, when open-coded per architecture, produced interrupt storms.
  • Shared lines make handler return values load-bearing. Because several devices may share one line, __handle_irq_event_percpu() walks the whole irqaction list and each handler must honestly report IRQ_NONE or IRQ_HANDLED. The spurious-interrupt logic in kernel/irq/spurious.c exists precisely to disable a line whose drivers lie about this.
  • The timer interrupt is no longer a metronome. Under NO_HZ_IDLE the tick stops on idle CPUs, and under NO_HZ_FULL it stops even while a single user task runs. Code that assumed a fixed-rate tick — for accounting, for timeouts, for RCU progress — had to be rewritten to be driven by events instead.
  • IPIs are messages, not lines. A CPU reaches another CPU by writing the local APIC's ICR on x86_64, or by raising an SGI through the GICv3 Distributor on ARM64. The most frequent one, the reschedule IPI, has an almost empty handler: its entire purpose is to force the target CPU into the return path, where irqentry_exit() will notice TIF_NEED_RESCHED.
  • Deferred work is a hierarchy of trade-offs. Ten static softirqs give the lowest latency and forbid sleeping; tasklets build on them but serialize and therefore do not scale; threaded IRQs move the bulk of a driver into a schedulable context and are the default under PREEMPT_RT; concurrency-managed workqueues handle anything that may block. New driver code should reach for a threaded IRQ or a workqueue, not a tasklet.
  • Returning is where the real work happens. The instruction that leaves the handler is trivial; what precedes it is not. __exit_to_user_mode_loop() iterates until no pending work remains — signals, rescheduling, uprobes, rseq fixups — and only then does the kernel restore registers, swap CR3 for KPTI, and execute IRETQ or ERET.
  • The concepts survived; the engineering was rebuilt. Compared with Linux 2.6, the durable changes are structural: DEFINE_IDTENTRY in place of hand-written per-vector assembly, genirq in place of duplicated per-architecture controller code, and a graded deferred-work hierarchy in place of tasklets as the default answer. Each change removed a class of bug that the older design made easy to write.

The kernel source files this chapter works from are collected by topic in Source Trails.
